Pinned by a seam test that submits a battery block through `submit_block` (accepted, extending the tip) then rejects its resubmission as dcrd's "already have block" duplicate rule error, and an end-to-end HTTP check that `submitblock` over the live listener answers `null` on acceptance (the chain advancing to the submitted block) and `rejected: already have block` on the duplicate.  The fee estimator now runs in the daemon (dcrd `newServer` always building the estimator and handing it to both the mempool and the RPC server, with no CLI gate): one shared `Arc<Mutex<Estimator>>` is built over the network's minimum relay fee (dcrd's `EstimatorConfig` — buckets from the relay floor up to a hundred times it plus the extra wallet-compat bucket), installed on the pool through a new `FeeEstimatorSink` mempool hook (dcrd's `AddTxToFeeEstimation`/`RemoveTxFromFeeEstimation` config closures) that fires the add on every accepted mempool transaction with dcrd's `(hash, fee, int64(SerializeSize()), txType)` at the tail of `addTransaction` beside the exists-addr hook and the remove on every eviction inside `removeTransaction`, and read by `estimatesmartfee` through the RPC `FeeEstimator` seam; the chain handler drives it exactly as dcrd's notification cases do — the first accepted block enables it at that height only once the sync is current (`if !s.feeEstimator.IsEnabled() { Enable(block.Height()) }` sits at the end of dcrd's NTBlockAccepted case, guarded by the `!AllowUnsyncedMining && !IsCurrent() { return }` early-return at the top, so pre-sync mempool contents never skew the estimate; the port applies that gate at the same drain that relays the accepted announcement — `drain_pending_accepted_announcements`, where the enable is the last step under the identical is-current gate — because the callback runs under the chain mutex and cannot re-check is-current inline) and every connected block's regular and stake transaction hashes feed `ProcessBlock` before the mempool removal loop (dcrd's documented ordering, "because the mempool will alert the estimator of the txs that are leaving", with the coinbase and treasurybase ignored because they were never in the pool, and Enable-at-H-then-ProcessBlock-at-H early-returning for the enable block exactly as dcrd's `block_height <= best_height` guard does); the estimator starts empty each run because dcrd's leveldb bucket persistence is deferred (a fresh dcrd database re-primes identically over the first post-sync blocks), and `estimatefee` still echoes the minimum relay fee dcrd-faithfully.  `EstimateFeeError` gained dcrd's exact `Display`/`Error` strings so `estimatesmartfee` surfaces "not enough transactions seen for estimation" as an internal error (dcrd's `-32603`) over an unprimed estimator and rejects a non-conservative mode with dcrd's invalid-parameter error (`-8`) before consulting it.  Pinned by seam unit tests (the mempool sink recording and clearing a transaction, the block feed shifting a tracked transaction out of the mempool statistics into the mined counters, and the shared-estimator `Send` bound) and the end-to-end HTTP check that `estimatesmartfee` over the wired but unprimed estimator answers dcrd's estimation error and rejects the bad mode.  An adversarial review pass shaped the wiring: the signal handler arms at the top of `run()` before the database work so an interrupt aborts the index drops and catch-up gracefully (dcrd installs its shutdown listener before `loadBlockDB`), the RPC block runs before the peer-to-peer listeners so the notification callback is installed before any peer can connect a block (dcrd registers the handler at chain construction — a block landing in that window would otherwise desync the expected-height state machine and fail every later index update until the heights line up again), and every failed index update logs its error under INDX (dcrd's `log.Error(err)` in `handleIndexUpdates`, which keeps consuming notifications after its `s.cancel()`), so an index that fell out of step reports the missing-notification error for each later block instead of going silent.  `getrawtransaction`'s three-second sync wait delays only its caller, as dcrd's per-request goroutines do: each HTTP connection runs on its own thread over one shared, unlocked `Arc<Server<_>>` (`rpcrun.rs`'s module doc; `the_rpc_server_is_shareable_without_a_lock` keeps the server `Sync`, so a seam that would need the retired server-wide mutex back fails to compile), and the wait (`indexes.rs` `wait_for_index_sync`) polls the index tip against the chain tip every 100 ms, where dcrd's subscriber re-checks on a 500 ms ticker and after each processed notification, which changes how soon a caught-up index is seen; the wait only triggers while the index tip trails the chain tip by five blocks or fewer; while it trails by more, which the queue allows during a sync, the handlers answer `<index>: index not synced` at once, as dcrd's do.  The three seconds are not a bound, though: the handler's tip reads and every poll's take the chain mutex, and the deadline is checked only between polls, so a call made while a block connect's UTXO flush or an `invalidateblock` or `reconsiderblock` reorganization holds that mutex waits the hold out and can succeed after the point where dcrd's gives up with `<index>: index not synced` (see the Open entry on the newest-block hook and the RPC server reading the tip under the chain mutex).  A websocket client's own request still holds that client's reading thread for the wait (see the Open entry on websocket requests serviced on the reading thread).  Every log line goes to standard output and, unless `--nofilelogging` is set, to the rotating log file `dcroxide.log` in the network's log directory, through a port of `jrick/logrotate` v1.1.2's rotator (`logrotate.rs`; see the Accepted entry *The log file*).  The network-interface and cross-user home resolution used by a small part of the configuration pipeline are not yet wired.  Pinned by command line integration checks over the built binary (the version, help, debug-level show, and unknown-flag exits, plus a fresh-database `--nolisten` startup that opens the block database and loads the genesis chain state at height zero, and a `--listen=127.0.0.1:0` startup that announces it is serving peers on the bound ephemeral port), and by end-to-end dispatch checks over a genesis chain served through the full runtime (a negotiated peer's getheaders answered with the empty low-work headers message, a getdata served the genesis block from the chain with misses consolidated into notfound, an empty getdata request dropping the connection through the intake gate, the genesis committed filter served with its inclusion proof singly and batched, and the pre-stake-validation empty init state sent exactly once per connection).  Also ported (the threaded server runtime, translating `server.go`'s `Run`/`peerHandler` goroutines to OS threads and channels rather than an async runtime, per the project's concurrency decision): the listener backbone (`ListenerRuntime`) — it binds each parsed listener spec (expanding the wildcard host to the family-appropriate any-address for the `tcp4`/`tcp6` split, with `SO_REUSEADDR` set outside Windows on both families as Go's listeners set it, so a restart rebinds over the previous run's closing connections).  A spec that cannot bind is logged as dcrd's `initListeners` logs it (`Can't listen on <addr>: <err>`, SRVR warning) and skipped, and startup fails only when nothing binds, with `newServer`'s "no valid listen address", which the binary reports as `Unable to start server: ...`.  It accepts inbound connections on a dedicated non-blocking thread per listener, which announces itself with connmgr `listenHandler`'s `Server listening on <addr>` (CMGR info) and hands each accepted connection to a supplied handler, and coordinates a graceful shutdown by signalling those accept threads and joining them; the connection manager's outbound dialing and seeding, the peer version handshake, the per-peer input and output loops, the sync manager, and the RPC server plug into this same shutdown coordination, and the runtime is wired into the binary's `run` — all covered below.  Pinned by integration checks that bind ephemeral listeners, connect to the assigned ports, and confirm both single- and multi-listener inbound acceptance and a clean joined shutdown.  Also ported (the connection framing): `WireTransport` — the concrete `MsgTransport` the ported peer module's version handshake and per-peer message loops run over, framing whole wire messages over any byte stream (a TCP connection in the daemon, an in-memory stream in tests) with the ported wire codec — reading the fixed 24-byte header first and rejecting a header-declared payload length above the global cap before any payload allocation (so a hostile peer cannot force a huge read), and tallying the wire bytes moved in each direction for the peer loop's byte accounting; the idle read deadline dcrd sets before each read is applied by the peer loop on the underlying stream.  Pinned by unit checks (the framed bytes match the wire codec, an empty-payload and a payload-carrying message round-trip through an in-memory stream, and an oversized header length is rejected without reading a payload) and an integration check that round-trips messages both directions over a real loopback TCP connection.  Also ported (the peer environment and handshake): `NodePeerEnv` — the daemon's concrete `PeerEnv`, supplying the ported peer module's injected clock (the wall clock in unix nanoseconds), randomness (the process-wide `crypto/rand` generator for the version nonce, as dcrd's peer module draws it), and address shuffle (dcrd's `rand.ShuffleSlice` over that same generator) — together with the conversion from an accepted socket address into the wire network address a peer is associated with; with these a `Peer` runs the ported version negotiation straight over the `WireTransport`.  Pinned by unit checks of the environment (the clock is a recent positive time, the random source varies, the shuffle preserves the address multiset, and the socket conversion keeps the raw IP bytes) and an integration check where an inbound and an outbound peer complete the full version exchange over a real loopback TCP connection and each learns the other's advertised protocol version, services, and user agent.  Also ported (the per-peer input pump, `inHandler`): `classify_incoming` — the message-type dispatch as a decision core over the ported peer handlers (a second version or a second verack disconnects with dcrd's reason, a first verack marks the peer, a ping yields the matching pong reply, a pong records the ping round-trip, a sendheaders sets the wants-headers preference, and every other message is forwarded to the server handlers) with `send_verack` (dcrd `start`'s post-negotiation `QueueMessage(verack)`) and `run_peer_input`, the read loop that queues each immediate reply on the outbound queue and forwards each message until a read error (a closed connection or an idle read timeout) or a protocol disconnect.  dcrd's stall detector arrives later.  The read and write halves share the peer as a `&Mutex<Peer>` — the blocking read is taken without the lock held so the ping timer and the server make progress, and every write (the input pump's protocol replies included) goes through the outbound queue so all writes stay on the single output loop.  The keepalive is the ping schedule of `run_peer_timers`, the one timer thread a served peer runs for the keepalive and the stall check (`run_ping_timer` runs the ping schedule alone); every interval it draws a fresh nonce and queues a ping, until a shutdown signal or the queue closes (the ping ticker in dcrd's `outHandler`, `peer.go:1812-1814`).  The output loop stamps each ping as the outstanding one when it takes it off the queue to write it, whoever queued it (the keepalive or the `ping` RPC's broadcast), as dcrd's `outHandler` sets `lastPingNonce`/`lastPingTime` for every `MsgPing` (`peer.go:1776-1777`), so the answering pong is matched and its round trip excludes the time the ping spent queued.  Pinned by unit checks of the dispatch (ping-to-pong, the first-then-duplicate verack, the second-version disconnect, the sendheaders flag, and the pong clearing the outstanding ping); an integration check where the inbound peer, after the handshake, queues its verack, splits the socket, runs the output loop on its own thread, and runs the input loop over the shared peer — marking the remote's verack, answering a ping with the matching pong through the queue, and forwarding both messages in order — over a real loopback TCP connection, ending when the remote closes; a ping-timer check that the timer thread queues a keepalive ping and stops on the shutdown signal, and an output-loop check that a ping queued from anywhere is stamped when written (`a_ping_is_stamped_when_written_whoever_queued_it`).  Also ported (the per-peer output handler, `outHandler`): `OutboundQueue` — a cloneable queue handle (dcrd `QueueMessage`) the server, the input pump's replies, and the ping timer send through — and `run_peer_output`, the writer loop that drains the queue and writes each message to the connection until the queue is closed (a local shutdown) or a write fails, so all writes to a connection are serialized on one thread.  This is the plain message queue, and the send semaphore is a later refinement.  dcrd's inventory trickle (`QueueInventory` feeding `queueHandler`'s `invSendQueue`, `peer/peer.go:1604-1707`, `:1907-1927`) is ported with the relay state in `dispatch.rs`: one thread runs every registered peer's trickle timer (`trickleTimer`, re-armed after each firing with a uniform 100 to 500 ms period drawn through `rand.Duration`, `:1628-1631`), and a firing drains the peer's queue into inv messages of at most `maxInvTrickleSize` (1000) vectors in the order they were queued, skips what the peer has come to know since and a vector already placed (a stricter repeat check than dcrd's, which holds only while the first copy is among the 1000 vectors the known set keeps), and marks each message's vectors known once the message is queued.  A message the full queue refuses ends the firing and the rest is dropped unmarked, where dcrd's queueing fails only past `maxQueuedOutputBytes` and the peer goes; dcrd's timer starts with the peer's handlers once the handshake completes, the port's on the thread's first pass after the peer registers, at most 100 ms later.  Transaction, mix and rebroadcast relays and mempool replies take the trickle, and block announcements stay immediate, as dcrd's `RelayBlockAnnouncement` makes them.  Pinned by `trickled_inventory_waits_for_the_timer_and_goes_out_in_batches`, `the_trickle_skips_inventory_the_peer_came_to_know`, `a_refused_trickle_batch_is_dropped_unmarked`, `block_announcements_skip_the_trickle` and `the_trickle_thread_sends_relayed_inventory_and_stops`.  Pinned by an integration check that queues messages, drains them over a real loopback TCP connection, and confirms they arrive in the order queued and the loop shuts down cleanly when the queue closes, plus a check that queueing to a closed queue fails.  Also ported (the connection assembly): `run_peer_connection` — dcrd `peer.go`'s `start` plus the per-peer goroutine set as OS threads: it sets the read deadline to the idle timeout, splits the socket into read and write halves, runs the version handshake (inbound or outbound per the peer) over the read half before starting the loops — framing the handshake at the local maximum protocol version and then lowering both transports to the negotiated version, as dcrd re-reads the peer's protocol version per message — shares the negotiated peer as an `Arc<Mutex<Peer>>`, queues the verack, runs the output loop and the ping timer on their own threads, drives the input loop on the calling thread, and on disconnect shuts the socket down (so a write blocked against a peer that stopped reading unblocks, as dcrd's `Disconnect` closes the conn), signals the ping timer, closes the outbound queue, and joins both threads before returning why the connection stopped (a connection the server wants read ahead of its block handler has one more thread, which reads the socket in the input thread's place, is released at the disconnect and is joined after those two; *Reading ahead of the block handler* under Deliberate divergences).  Pinned by an integration check where the server side runs the whole connection — accept, associate, handshake, and serve — while a remote peer negotiates, exchanges verack, and pings, receiving the pong, then closes: the connection forwards every message in order and stops with a read error, with the ping interval and idle timeout set long so neither fires.  Also ported (serving inbound peers): `inbound_peer_handler` — the listener runtime's accept handler (dcrd `server.inboundPeerConnected`): each accepted connection is handed to its own thread, which builds a fresh inbound peer from a `PeerTemplate` (network, advertised services, user agent with dcrd `newPeerConfig`'s pre-release comment `(pre)`, the idle timeout and ping interval, the blocks-only `DisableRelayTx` flag, and the configured proxy), associates it with the remote address, and runs the whole connection; the server-handler dispatch (relay, inv, getdata) is a no-op until the peer-handler piece, so a served peer completes the handshake and is kept alive with ping and pong.  Each live connection is tracked in a `ConnectedPeers` registry (a socket handle per peer) so a shutdown can disconnect them all — shutting down each socket unblocks the peer's read loop so it winds down (the connected-peer half of dcrd's `peerState`); a drop guard deregisters a peer even when its serving thread unwinds from a panic, the registry's locks recover from poisoning so a crashed peer thread cannot break the shutdown sweep, and a transient `accept` error waits out a poll interval instead of killing the listener, logged as dcrd's `listenHandler` logs it (`Can't accept connection: <err>`, CMGR error, not during shutdown).  The would-block poll, EINTR and ECONNABORTED stay silent because Go's `Accept` retries those internally.  Accepted divergences: the error texts in `Can't listen on`/`Can't accept connection` are std's `io::Error` rendering ("Address already in use (os error 98)"), not Go's `*net.OpError` text ("listen tcp6 :9108: bind: address already in use").  A persistent accept error is retried every 50 ms, where dcrd retries at once, so the log gets at most 20 such lines a second.  Pinned by an integration check that starts a listener runtime with the serving handler, connects as an outbound peer, negotiates (learning the server's user agent), and confirms the server answers a ping with a pong before a clean shutdown; and a check that a served peer registers as live, then deregisters once `disconnect_all` tears its connection down.  The legacy mining-state exchange answers over the live chain (dcrd `OnGetMiningState`'s latch, its blank push that aborts on zero blocks so an early chain sends nothing, the vote-list truncation against the BLOCK limit kept bug for bug, and `OnMiningState` requesting the advertised blocks and votes through the sync manager), with the eligible parents drawn from the ported `SortParentsByVotes` — the same sorted, majority-vote-filtered list now also feeding getinitstate's head blocks.  The StakeShuffle mix messages now route through a shared pool: the daemon builds one `mixpool.Pool` over a chain adapter (`NodeMixChain` for the tip and params, `NodeMixUtxoFetcher` validating pair-request ownership against the live UTXO set through `Chain::fetch_utxo_entry`, exactly as dcrd's mixpool `utxoFetcher` over `FetchUtxoEntry`) and shares it between the getdata MIX serve path and the netsync manager (dcrd's one `s.mixMsgPool`); the eight `OnMix*` handlers ignore mix traffic entirely under --blocksonly (dcrd's `onMixMessage` blocksonly gate), mark each received message known to the sending peer before processing (dcrd's `sp.AddKnownInventory`, so the accept-time relay never echoes it back to the sender), and hand the wire mix message to that pool with the sync-manager lock held only for the rejected-message and request bookkeeping on either side of the acceptance, never across it (dcrd's `OnMixMsg` → `SyncManager.OnMixMsg` → `mixpool.AcceptMessage`, which takes only `requestMtx` around that bookkeeping; transaction intake has the same shape around `ProcessTransaction`), announcing every accepted message — the delivered one plus any orphan its acceptance un-orphaned — to the peers as MIX inventory and to the subscribed websocket clients (dcrd's `AnnounceMixMessages` → `relayMixMessages` + `s.rpcServer.NotifyMixMessages`, the latter a no-op under --norpc), requesting a referenced-but-missing pair request back from the sender on the orphan path (dcrd's `MissingOwnPR` → `RequestMixMsgFromPeer`), and disconnecting a peer whose message is a rule violation bannable for its advertised services; the getdata MIX serve path resolves each requested hash through the same pool's recent-message lookup (dcrd's `s.mixMsgPool.RecentMessage`, which also consults the LRU of messages just removed from the pool so a peer that advertised a now-confirmed message is still served it), queuing the served message in request order and missing a hash the pool never held into the consolidated notfound.  Pinned by a wire↔pool round-trip over all eight message variants (a mis-paired conversion arm surfaces as an inequality) and an end-to-end getdata check that an absent mix message misses into notfound.  The `addblock` and `promptsecret` tool commands are ported as workspace binaries.  `addblock` reads dcrd's bootstrap file format (`<network u32> <length u32> <serialized block>` records) and runs each block through the chain engine with bulk-import mode enabled (dcrd `EnableBulkImportMode`, skipping the script checks), creating the enabled transaction and exists-address indexes and catching them up before the import while leaving them untouched during it — dcrd's chain never notifies the index subscriber (only the daemon's server does, and addblock has no server), so its importer defers the imported blocks' indexing to the next daemon start's catch-up, and the port does the same — with dcrd's `loadConfig` gauntlet (network exclusivity, database type, per-network datadir namespacing, and infile existence with dcrd's exact error texts — including the quirk that `--help` exits nonzero because `realMain` treats the go-flags help error as a failure) riding the same go-flags scanner the daemon's pinned configuration pipeline uses (the scanner is parameterized over an option registry and value sink; the daemon's path is unchanged and its 52 syntax scenarios still pin it); the record reader keeps `readBlock`'s outcomes byte for byte (clean EOF only at the network field, Go's `unexpected EOF` on partial fields, the network-mismatch and payload-cap texts inside the `error reading from input file:` wrap), the importer keeps `processBlock`'s order (stats before the already-known skip, the does-not-link error printing the missing parent hash, the orphan and does-not-extend errors) and `logProgress`'s cadence (10ms-truncated durations, dcrd's pluralization, the per-record `lastHeight` counter, and the quirk that a zero interval logs every block despite the help text saying it disables announcements — the block time renders in pinned UTC where dcrd uses the host zone); dcrd's three importer goroutines run as one synchronous loop (only the read/process overlap is traded away) and its separate leveldb UTXO database has no equivalent in the port's single-database architecture.  A chain that fails to open is logged as dcrd's `Failed create block importer: %v` line (`cmd/addblock/addblock.go:110`), with the error's description; until 2026-10-08 the port printed the Rust `Debug` form there instead (`Corrupt("...")`), and a chain database an older dcroxide wrote is refused on that line with the directory to delete (see "Seven per-block buckets" under Known remaining gaps; `tests/db_version_refusal.rs` pins both).  Pinned by config/reader/importer unit tests, an end-to-end import of a battery prefix into a fresh regnet chain (the pre-created transaction index staying at its pre-import tip through the run and a fresh catch-up indexing the imported blocks, dcrd's deferred indexing) plus the already-known re-import and the gap/mismatch errors, and CLI checks over the built binary (the help/conflict/dbtype/infile exits and an empty-file mainnet import creating the database and reporting zero blocks).  `promptsecret` prompts on stderr, reads the secret with terminal echo disabled, writes it to stdout `-n` times, and zeroes the buffer, keeping Go's `flag` package exits (usage on `-h` with exit 0, parse errors with exit 2 and Go's normalized `parse error`/`value out of range` texts); the workspace forbids unsafe code, so the echo flip runs through `stty` on unix (failing on a non-terminal stdin exactly like Go's `ReadPassword` on a pipe) and reports echo control as unsupported on windows — a documented divergence.  Each `stty` call gets the tool's own stdin (`Command::output` would give it a null one, and the snapshot then failed on every terminal).  The secret is read a byte at a time and written through unbuffered duplicates of the standard descriptors, as Go's `os.Stdin`/`os.Stdout` reach them, so no std stream buffer keeps a copy after the zeroing.  The `gencerts` tool command is ported as a workspace binary over new certificate machinery in the certgen crate: dcrd's go-flags command line over the shared scanner (help exits 0, parse errors 1, a wrong argument count or unknown algorithm prints the help to stderr and exits 2 — with dcrd's `%q`-quoted unknown-algorithm text — `-C`/`-K` pairing, `-L` local-host appending, and the force checks with Go's stat-error-is-exists `fileExists`), key generation for the elliptic-curve and Ed25519 algorithms (P-256/P-384/P-521 by rejection sampling and Ed25519 — `p384` is a new RustCrypto dependency alongside the p256/p521 dependencies; dcrd's `RSA4096` is not offered, a documented divergence: the `rsa` crate carries the unfixed Marvin-attack advisory RUSTSEC-2023-0071 and no dcrd daemon component uses RSA), Go `newTemplate` (first-host-or-organization CN (left out of the subject when empty, and never a PrintableString when it holds `*` or `&`), Go `idna.ToASCII` (bare Punycode, case-preserving) hostnames split from parsed IPs into the SAN, a day of backdating, the 2049 UTCTime clamp, the already-elapsed refusal, 128-bit random serials, and the int64 nanosecond wrap of the years arithmetic), self-signed authorities and CA-issued certificates with Go `x509.CreateCertificate`'s behaviors (KeyUsage digitalSignature plus keyCertSign under `-S`, BasicConstraints with the CA boolean only for authorities, the SubjectKeyIdentifier (truncated SHA-256, Go 1.25's default) auto-computed for CA certificates only, the AuthorityKeyIdentifier copied from the parent only when the subject differs from the issuer, the SAN omitted without hosts, and per-curve signature hashes), PKCS#8 marshaling for every key, and the `tls.LoadX509KeyPair` slice the tool exercises (PEM decode, PKCS#8 and SEC 1 keys, the pair match check, the parent-cannot-sign refusal, and the issued validity clamped to the parent's NotAfter).  Documented divergences: dcrd's RSA4096 algorithm is omitted (the `rsa` crate's unfixed RUSTSEC-2023-0071 timing advisory, and no daemon component uses RSA), only the Decred-emitted key PEM forms are recognized, whole-second-UTC time rendering in the elapsed error vs Go's local-zone monotonic form, Rust OS-error texts vs Go's `*PathError`, and the hand-written help text without go-flags' default annotations.  Pinned by round-trip vectors (authorities across the algorithms re-loading through the CA loader, the issuance checks, the validity clamp, and a small-key RSA round trip) and CLI checks over the built binary culminating in a rustls/webpki-validated TLS handshake: a `-S -L` authority issues a localhost leaf that satisfies a real client trusting only the generated CA.  The Tor/proxy dial path runs in the daemon: a port of `decred/go-socks`'s `Proxy.DialContext` (the SOCKS5 greeting with optional RFC 1929 username/password authentication, the TCP CONNECT command always over a domain address so the proxy resolves the name, go-socks' exact reply-status error texts, and Tor isolation drawing fresh random hex credentials per connection) and of dcrd `addrmgr.TorLookupIP` (Tor's SOCKS RESOLVE extension, command 0xF0, with dcrd's error-code table), assembled into the routing dcrd's `loadConfig` closures select over the pinned configuration enums — `--proxy` routes every dial through SOCKS and resolution through Tor unless `--noonion`, `--onionproxy` gives `.onion` traffic its own proxy, `dcrdDial` routes addresses containing `.onion:` to the onion dialer and `dcrdLookup` routes `.onion` hosts to the onion resolver, and `--noonion` makes both onion paths fail with dcrd's "tor has been disabled" — wired into the outbound connection driver's dials (a hostname address a proxied dial resolves remotely simply skips the address-manager attempt mark, which keys on parsed IPs) and `getaddednodeinfo`'s DNS lookup.  go-socks' `proxiedConn` bound-address dressing is read and discarded (the peer runtime keys everything on the dialed address), and the HTTPS seeder routes through the same dial path when a proxy is configured (dcrd routes its seeder transport through `dcrdDial`): a `ProxySeederTransport` dials each seeder through the `NodeDialer` — SOCKS or direct — wraps the connection in TLS validated against the public web PKI roots, and reads a minimal `Connection: close` HTTP/1.1 GET capped at the connmgr response limit, so a proxied operator's seeder traffic no longer leaks outside the proxy; without a proxy the battle-tested `ureq` transport still does the direct dial.  That minimal client follows no redirects and decodes no chunked transfer encoding (the Decred seeders answer 200 with the JSON body directly), a documented simplification from Go's `http.Client`.  The whole proxied request (dial, TLS handshake, request and response) runs under one absolute one-minute deadline that caps every socket read and write, as dcrd's per-seeder `context.WithTimeout(ctx, time.Minute)` does, so a peer trickling the handshake cannot hold a seeding round.  The direct `ureq` transport is built with `proxy(None)`, so neither transport takes a proxy from `ALL_PROXY`/`HTTPS_PROXY`/`HTTP_PROXY`, matching dcrd's seeder `http.Transport`, whose `Proxy` is nil.  Pinned by fake-proxy tests: the anonymous and authenticated handshakes bridging to a live echo target, Tor isolation's distinct per-connection credential pairs, go-socks' error texts, the Tor RESOLVE exchange with its error table, the `.onion` routing split between the onion proxy and direct dials, and the --noonion failures.  The pipe IPC lifecycle is wired end to end: the `--pipetx` writer thread serializes every queued message in the ported encoding (`serviceControlPipeTx`, with the no-descriptor drain), the `--piperx` watcher discards input until end-of-file or an error and then requests the same graceful shutdown as an interrupt (`serviceControlPipeRx` — the parent closing its end is the shutdown request), and under `--lifetimeevents` the daemon sends dcrd's events at dcrd's points.  Startup DbOpen goes out before the block database loads.  Startup P2pServer goes out ahead of everything dcrd does in `newServer` (the fee estimator, the chain load with its UTXO catch-up replay, the index catch-up, the address manager, the mempool, the listeners), so a server that fails to build has sent it and sends no P2pServer shutdown event.  Startup-complete goes out before the idle wait.  The P2pServer shutdown event follows the teardown and the final UTXO flush, after `Server shutdown complete` (dcrd's defer after `svr.Run`).  The DbOpen shutdown event goes out as the block database closes, on every exit after it opened, failures and the index-drop exits included.  dcrd's queue is an unbuffered channel whose sends wait for the writer; the port's queue never blocks a sender, so at exit the daemon instead waits up to a second for the writer to write what is queued (`PipeNotifier::wait_written`).  The node crate forbids `unsafe`, so the inherited descriptors cannot be adopted with `from_raw_fd`.  On Linux the daemon duplicates them from itself with `pidfd_getfd`, which shares the open file description as `os.NewFile` does, so the socketpairs Node/Electron parents pass work.  A descriptor left non-blocking is waited on with `poll`, for the `--piperx` reads and the `--pipetx` writes alike, as Go's poller does, and a failed `--pipetx` write logs `Failed to write to pipe` as dcrd does.  Both loops log the error as dcrd does: Go's `*PathError` over the name dcrd gives the file (`|N`), with the OS error as `syscall.Errno` spells it, so a descriptor that is not open logs `Failed to read from pipe: read |N: bad file descriptor` (through the re-open fallback below, the path lookup's error instead) and a write that takes nothing `write |N: unexpected EOF`.  A number above 2^63 − 1, negative as Go's `int`, gets the nil file from `os.NewFile`, whose reads fail with a bare `invalid argument` (`os.ErrInvalid`), and the port fails it the same way.  On kernels before 5.6, or under a sandbox that filters the call, the daemon falls back to re-opening through `/proc/self/fd`, which serves pipes and files but not sockets (`ENXIO`; `--piperx` then shuts down).  Other unixes re-open through `/dev/fd`, whose open duplicates the descriptor.  On Windows the inherited pipe handles are taken by `dcroxide-winsvc` (`adopt_inherited_handle`), which duplicates each within the process (`DuplicateHandle` with the same access) and owns the duplicate, so the parent closing its end stops the daemon and the lifetime and bound-address events reach the parent.  The inherited handle itself is left open, which the parent cannot observe.  As with `os.NewFile`, `INVALID_HANDLE_VALUE` fails with `invalid argument` (Go's `os.ErrInvalid`, what every read on the nil file returns) and a number that names no open handle with `ERROR_INVALID_HANDLE`; both take dcrd's broken-descriptor path, so `--piperx` logs `Failed to read from pipe: invalid argument` or `Failed to read from pipe: read |N: The handle is invalid.` and shuts down.  Go asks `FormatMessage` for the US English message text first and Rust's std for the user's default language, so on a Windows system in another language the port can log the localized text where dcrd logs English.  A `--pipetx` handle that cannot be taken is warned about at startup (`Unable to open the pipetx descriptor`), as on unix, where dcrd logs `Failed to write to pipe` when the first message is written.  Pinned on Windows CI by `review_windows_pipes.rs` and the adoption tests in `dcroxide-winsvc`; nothing here builds the node for Windows, so those run only there.  Under `--boundaddrevents` the daemon sends one `p2plistenaddr` per bound peer-to-peer listener and then one `rpclistenaddr` per bound RPC listener (dcrd `initListeners` and `setupRPCListeners`, added in `fc017ce3`).  Each carries Go's `listener.Addr().String()`: IPv4-mapped addresses in dotted form, IPv6 bracketed, and a zoned link-local address with Rust's numeric scope where Go names the interface.  The RPC messages wait until the peer listeners have bound, which is the order dcrd's `newServer` binds the two in.  Before anything else, `main` raises the soft `RLIMIT_NOFILE` as the Go runtime does for dcrd before `main` runs (`syscall/rlimit.go`): to one below the hard limit.  On macOS Go clamps to `kern.maxfilesperproc`; with no safe sysctl, the port bisects for the largest value the kernel accepts, which is the same value for an ordinary user.  It then applies dcrd's `limits.SetLimits`: a soft limit above 2048 is kept, a lower one goes to 2048 (or to the hard limit if that is lower), and a hard limit below 1024 exits 1 with `failed to set limits: need at least 1024 file descriptors` before the configuration is read.  Until the review of 2026-09-23 the daemon raised nothing and ran at its inherited soft limit: 1024 under systemd, 256 in macOS shells.  A shutdown requested during startup is honoured at dcrd's `shutdownRequested` points.  The first is ahead of the block database load.  The second is after the load; dcrd has separate checks after `loadBlockDB` and after `LoadUtxoDB`, which are one here because the UTXO set lives in the block database.  The third is after the chain load and index catch-up, before any listener, dialer, seeder or background thread starts.  `Shutdown complete` is logged on every return, as dcrd's deferred log is.  The lifecycle log lines are dcrd's.  The block database load logs `Loading block database from '<datadir>/blocks_ffldb'` and `Block database loaded` (DCRD, `blockdb.go:139`, `:185`), so both come before every CHAN line of the chain open, as `loadBlockDB` runs before `newServer` builds the chain; the tip the chain loaded is reported by CHAN's closing `Chain state: ...` line alone, as in dcrd.  The `stop` RPC (only its first request is forwarded, as in dcrd), the `--piperx` close and the service stop log `Shutdown requested.  Shutting down...`, or `Shutdown requested.  Already shutting down...` for a repeat.  A Windows console close, logoff or shutdown logs `Received signal (terminated).  Shutting down...`, or the `Already shutting down...` form, as dcrd logs the SIGTERM Go's runtime makes of those events.  `Server shutting down` (SRVR, WRN) opens the teardown and `Server shutdown complete` (SRVR) closes it, with up to two lines of the port's own just before it: one when a connection was read ahead (`Read-ahead: ...`, SRVR; *Reading ahead of the block handler* under Deliberate divergences) and after it one when blocks were pre-verified (`Signature pre-verification: ...`, SRVR; *Signature pre-verification ahead of the block thread* under Deliberate divergences), followed by `Gracefully shutting down the block database...` and `Shutdown complete`.  `--allowoldforks` logs `Processing forks deep in history is enabled` (SRVR), then `--assumevalid` logs `Assume valid is disabled` or `Assume valid set to <hash>` (SRVR), in `newServer`'s order.  A bad `--assumevalid` value is `Unable to start server: invalid hex for --assumevalid: ...`.  The missing-config warning is logged under DCRD.  dcrd's `Gracefully shutting down the UTXO database...` has no counterpart, because the port keeps the UTXO set in the block database.  Under `--regnet` an existing block database is removed before it is opened, as dcrd's `loadBlockDB` does so that every regression test run starts from genesis, with dcrd's `Removing regression test database from '<path>'` (DCRD) ahead of `Loading block database from`; that one removal also clears the UTXO set and the indexes, so dcrd's second line, `Removing regression test UTXO database from '<path>'` (CHAN) for `<datadir>/utxodb`, has no counterpart either.  `newServer`'s order is kept where a start can fail: the peer-to-peer listeners bind, with their `p2plistenaddr` events and the local-address registration, and a `--maxpeers` dcrd's relay queue refuses is refused, before the fee estimator and the chain, as `initListeners` and the server literal come first upstream (the listeners accept nothing until the server runs); the persistent peers are added before the RPC listeners are set up, and a persistent peer that cannot be added, or an RPC certificate or TLS configuration that fails, is `Unable to start server: ...` under DCRD before any RPC bind.  The fee estimator is built ahead of the chain, as in `newServer`, and a configuration it refuses fails startup as `Unable to start server: ...` with exit status 1.  After the peer listeners bind, the tail of dcrd's `initListeners` registers the node's own addresses with the address manager.  Every `--externalip` goes in at manual priority, its port defaulting to the network's; unparsable ports and unresolvable or unroutable hosts are skipped with dcrd's SRVR/AMGR warnings.  Without `--externalip`, every bound listener goes in at bound priority, and an unspecified bind expands to the same-family interface addresses: the route netlink dumps Go's `InterfaceAddrs` reads on Linux, none elsewhere, the same enumeration gap the RPC certificate records.  Not ported from the lifecycle, each deliberate: the Go soft-memory-limit tuning (`GOMEMLIMIT`, garbage-collector headroom with no Rust analog, so dcrd's `Soft memory limit:` and `Ignoring GOMEMLIMIT` INFO lines have no counterpart either, `dcrd.go:98-113`) and the pprof profile servers (`--profile`/`--cpuprofile`/`--memprofile` validate but serve nothing, so the startup failures that come of serving are skipped with them: dcrd exits 1 when the `--profile` server cannot bind or the `--cpuprofile` or `--memprofile` file cannot be created, `dcrd.go:115-149`, where the daemon neither listens nor creates a file; a `--profile` address refused before the bind ends the daemon as it ends dcrd, below).  The `startprofiler` and `stopprofiler` RPCs reach the daemon's `profiler::ProfileServer` (dcrd's `profileServer`, `profiler.go`), which makes every check `Start` makes before its first `net.Listen`, in dcrd's order and with dcrd's texts: `portToLocalHostAddr`, `validateProfileAddr` (Go's `SplitHostPort` errors and the port range), `normalizeAddresses` with `normalizeInterfaceAddrs` (a host that names an interface stands for each of its addresses, read over route netlink on Linux; elsewhere no host names an interface, as for `addnode`), the loopback check over `netip.ParseAddrPort` and `IsLoopback` without `allownonloopback`, and `parseListeners`.  Where dcrd would then bind and serve, it binds nothing and refuses, and `handleStartProfiler` wraps the refusal as it wraps dcrd's own failed starts: -8 `unable to start profile server: the profile server is not part of this port of dcrd`, where dcrd answers the listeners it bound.  So no `startprofiler` succeeds and `stopprofiler` always answers -26 `profile server is not started`.  With `--profile` set, `run_node` makes dcrd's start (`dcrd.go:118-126`, `ProfileServer::start_configured`): the same checks, with addresses that are not loopback allowed, after the configuration has run only `portToLocalHostAddr` and `validateProfileAddr`.  A host `parseListeners` refuses, such as `localhost:6060`, ends the start as it ends dcrd's: the WRN line `unable to start profile server: 'localhost' is not a valid IP address`, exit 1 before the block database loads, and `Shutdown complete`.  An interface with no address records dcrd's server with no listener from startup, so every `startprofiler` answers -32603 (QK-0032).  For an address dcrd listens on, dcrd's server runs from startup, so its `startprofiler` answers -26 `profile server is already running` and its `stopprofiler` stops the server; here nothing starts, `startprofiler` checks the address and `stopprofiler` finds nothing running.  What dcrd does once it binds is not reproduced either: its `unable to listen on` errors, and the listeners a bind that fails after another succeeded leaves recorded with no server.  The one address that passes every check and leaves dcrd nothing to bind, an interface with no address, gets dcrd's replies (QUIRKS QK-0032).  Until 2026-10-23 the daemon handed the RPC server the `()` stand-in, so every `startprofiler` answered -8 `unable to start profile server: RPC server seam start is not wired in this build`, the refused addresses included, and `--profile` skipped dcrd's start, so `localhost:6060` left the daemon running.  Pinned by `tests/profiler_vectors.rs`: 65 `Start` calls from `tools/profilergen`, which runs dcrd's own `profileServer` in its package main (at `382ff4dd`, and at `release-v2.1.7`, which gives the same file; `profiler.go` is unchanged at the pin), replayed through the manager and the RPC dispatch; the replies a dcrd 2.1.7 node gave dcrctl for the four addresses it refused, and the deliberate refusal for the two it listened on; the limited user's refusal; and the server recorded with no listener.  `tests/review_profiler_daemon.rs` sends refused and accepted addresses to the daemon binary's RPC server and starts it with `--profile` set to an address dcrd serves and to two it refuses; `profiler::tests::the_profile_flag_start_allows_addresses_that_are_not_loopback` pins the `--profile` start, the interface with no address included.  UPnP is not a gap: dcrd master removed port mapping and `--upnp` only logs that the option is no longer supported (`config.go:176`, `dcrd.go:74-78`), a warning the daemon reproduces verbatim.  The misbehaving-mix-peer observer is ported and runs: a ticker thread wakes at every mix epoch boundary (UTC time truncated to the epoch plus one epoch, dcrd `Observer.waitForEpoch`), skips the first boundary because no full epoch has finished, and hands each completed epoch to the pool's `check_prev_epoch` (dcrd `Server.Run` running `s.mixObserver.Run`), feeding the strike set behind `misbehaving_block` and `misbehaving_tx`; the transaction pool consults the second for every transaction it accepts (dcrd's `MisbehavingMixSpend`, `3c8a1734`); the winning-tickets notification path consults that observer and refuses to prompt votes on a block spending misbehaving mix inputs (dcrd's `MisbehavingBlock` break in the same gated case).  The Windows service wrapper is ported over the `windows-service` crate in its own `dcroxide-winsvc` crate, which denies rather than forbids unsafe code: the service-entry macro expands an FFI shim into it, and it is the workspace's one audited exception for the console control handler and the pipe-handle adoption, each unsafe block allowed individually with a `SAFETY` comment (see the crate documentation): the dispatcher detects SCM invocation (dcrd `svc.IsWindowsService` folded into the failed-connect error) and runs the whole daemon under the service with dcrd's start-pending → running → stop-pending → stopped transitions, SCM stop/shutdown controls request the same graceful shutdown as an interrupt (dcrd `dcrdService.Execute`'s `shutdownRequestChannel` send, with a stop arriving before the daemon's shutdown channel exists latched and honored on arming), and the `-s/--service` commands install (manual start, dcrd's display name and description), remove, start (passing the process arguments through), and stop (waiting up to ten seconds for the stopped state) — with the option group registered only on Windows exactly like dcrd's `newConfigParser`, so the flag stays an unknown-option error on other platforms, pinned by the flags vectors.  As in dcrd's `loadConfig`, only the config-file pre-parse and the final parse carry the group, not the help pre-parse (dcrd's bare `flags.NewParser(&cfg, helpOpts)`, where `--service` is skipped as unknown).  The command runs straight after the version check, on that pre-parse alone: before the home directory or default config file is created, the config file read or anything validated.  It prints any error and exits 0.  Until the review of 2026-09-23 it ran only after a successful full load, so a bad config file blocked `--service=stop`, and on Windows the help pass took the option too, so `--bogus --service=stop` ran a command dcrd's pre-parse never reaches.  The `-h` help text is generated byte-for-byte as go-flags v1.6.1 renders it (the usage line, the Application Options section with the description column two past the longest long name and the value marker squeezing into the padding, `wrapText`'s last-space-within-width splitting with exact-width remainders kept whole and its hyphenated splits followed by a blank line, all over bytes as Go indexes them, so a split inside a multibyte character of a default path writes the raw partial bytes as dcrd does, the `(default: X)` note go-flags adds for every argument-taking option whose value in dcrd's defaulted config is not zero — rendered as `convertToString` does: the path or string, Go `Duration.String`, `FormatFloat` 'g' shortest, decimal integers, with the four `default-mask:"-"` passwords hidden — the `[$ENV]` annotations, and the Help Options tail with the trailing blank line of dcrd's `Println` over the help error), wrapped at the width go-flags' `getTerminalColumns` reads: the columns of the terminal on stdin (`TIOCGWINSZ` on descriptor 0, which the port reads through `stty size` over its own stdin because the workspace forbids unsafe code), or 80 when stdin is not a terminal or reports zero columns.  The descriptions are extracted verbatim from dcrd's config struct tags; dcrd's dedicated help pre-parse never adds the Windows service group, so neither does the render.  `tools/helpgen` regenerates the pinned vectors from the same struct filled with `loadConfig`'s defaults over a fixed `/home/user/.dcroxide` home: the 80-column one with stdin from /dev/null, and a 140-column one from a 140-column terminal.  On Windows go-flags wraps at the stdout console's `MaximumWindowSize.X` (`termsize_windows.go`), which needs an unsafe console call, so the port always wraps at 80 there; the Windows help is not dcrd's in any case, since go-flags renders the Windows option style there (`/V, /version`, `[%DCRD_APPDATA%]`, a `/?` entry) and the port the POSIX one (see *Open*).  The Windows event log half (`eventlog.InstallAsEventCreate`, the start-of-day message) is not ported.  A process the SCM starts has no standard handles, and std discards every write to them, so a daemon run as a service logs to its log file alone, and under `--nofilelogging` keeps no log at all.  The SCM behavior compiles on Windows in CI but is exercised only by the command-dispatch and identity tests; running under a real service control manager is untested.  The console close, logoff and shutdown events are held as Go's runtime holds them for dcrd (`runtime/os_windows.go` `ctrlHandler`, which maps them to SIGTERM and blocks): Windows ends a console process once a handler returns TRUE for one, and ctrlc's returned at once, so the process used to be ended before its shutdown began.  `dcroxide-winsvc`'s handler, registered after ctrlc's so Windows asks it first, requests the shutdown with the SIGTERM log line above, waits until `main` reports the daemon has shut down (block database closed, `Shutdown complete` logged, the service stopped when run as one), and then returns TRUE.  Ctrl-C and Ctrl-Break it passes on to ctrlc.  Go's handler blocks for good, while this one returns TRUE after 30 seconds without the report, longer than the grace Windows gives by default (five seconds for a close, at most twenty for logoff and shutdown), so under the default timeouts it is still Windows that ends a slow shutdown.  The handshake is pinned by unit tests on every platform and the registration runs in CI's Windows tests, but nothing automated raises a real close, logoff or shutdown event, which needs an interactive Windows console.  Documented daemon-layer divergences deferred from the cross-piece review of pieces 120-152: the accepted-block relay, fee-estimator enable, and background-generator gates evaluate netsync `IsCurrent` through a shared gate over the sync manager's is-current flag and sync height (dcrd's `isCurrent` is an `atomic.Bool` for the same cross-goroutine reads; the gate replicates `maybeUpdateIsCurrent` — current once latched, else the chain believing itself current with the best height at the sync height — without taking the manager lock, which those paths cannot); the peer-transport per-message read deadline is absolute (dcrd's `SetReadDeadline(now + IdleTimeout)` before each `readMessage`): every receive runs under a socket timeout of the remaining budget, capped at the 1 s `READ_POLL_INTERVAL` slice and re-armed only when that slice changes, covering the header and payload of one message under a single wall-clock bound — during the handshake, whatever remains of the single negotiate deadline that bounds the whole exchange (dcrd's `Handshake` select on `time.After(negotiateTimeout)`, reported as `protocol handshake timeout` with the `ErrHandshakeTimeout` kind, pinned by `the_negotiate_timeout_bounds_the_whole_handshake`), and the idle timeout after — so a byte-dribbling peer cannot stretch a message read (for a frame a `peer-reader` thread reads ahead of the input thread the bound runs from the moment the input thread began waiting for that frame, which is when dcrd would have armed it; *Reading ahead of the block handler* under Deliberate divergences); the RPC request-read phase is bounded the same way plus a per-connection watchdog that closes the socket at the deadline (Go's `http.Server` `ReadTimeout` is an absolute bound at the conn level, under TLS too, where the rustls record loop cannot be bounded by re-armed receive timeouts alone), disarmed once the request is read; the RPC response write stays unbounded exactly as dcrd's `http.Server` without a `WriteTimeout` leaves it, and websocket connections live past the deadline by design (dcrd sets no read deadline on them), with the shutdown-time force-close bounding both; and the addrmgr upkeep now runs: a ticker thread dumps peers.json every ten minutes (the ticker half of dcrd addrmgr's `addressHandler`, with `savePeers`' dirty gate and the final save still at shutdown), and each outbound dial performs dcrd `attemptDcrdDial`'s full bookkeeping — the host/port split and net-address conversion over the configured lookup failing the dial like dcrd's, `AddAddresses` with the target as its own source so a `--connect`/addnode address the manager never learned joins it for gossip and persistence, then the `Attempt` mark.  The RPC listener drains on shutdown: per-connection handler threads register in a wait group entered on the accept thread before each spawn, and `RpcListener::shutdown` joins the accept threads (dcrd `Run`'s `wg` covers exactly its accept goroutines), lets the handlers finish through a one-second grace — websocket serving loops observe the shutdown flag within a poll interval, the port's analog of dcrd cancelling the request context — then force-closes any connection still alive (dcrd's per-client context watcher calling `wsClient.Disconnect`), so a mid-frame websocket stall or a zero-window reader cannot wedge the shutdown; the final wait is bounded, abandoning a still-stuck thread exactly as dcrd leaves in-flight standard handlers detached.  The RPC request parser decodes chunked `Transfer-Encoding` bodies the way Go's `internal/chunked` reader and `body.readTrailer` decode them (1.26.2's; 1.27.1's differ only in the trailer's value cap): size lines end in CRLF, with no other CR; sizes are `parseHexUint`'s bare hex digits, at most 16; an extension is cut after trimming only spaces and tabs; the net non-data allowance is 16 KiB, in Go's wrapping `int64` arithmetic; a size line and the trailer section are each bounded at 4096 bytes; and trailer fields get the MIME-field checks, and at most 500 values, a cap that is Go 1.27's: 1.26.2 reads any number.  It reads through a 4 KiB buffer rather than a byte per syscall pair, under the same authenticated read limit and deadline as `Content-Length` bodies.  A body the reader refuses is answered `400 error reading JSON message: <err>` with Go's error text, as dcrd's `jsonRPCRead` answers it, pinned by `chunked_framing_follows_gos_reader` and `chunked_overhead_is_bounded_where_go_bounds_it`, every row of which is a real Go 1.27 server's answer.  Replayed against a go1.26.2 server, every row agrees but four, whose malformed MIME field line 1.26.2 writes unquoted (`net/textproto/reader.go:544`, `:556-564`) where 1.26.4 and later, 1.27 and the port quote it.  Following 1.26.2 on the cap and the quoting is pending (ADR-0001's 2026-10-26 addendum on the reference Go).  It ignores the `Content-Length` of a chunked message per RFC 7230, and ignores `Transfer-Encoding` altogether below HTTP/1.1 as Go does (issue 12785, `transfer.go:646-649`); from HTTP/1.1 on it answers any other transfer encoding 501, exactly as Go's `net/http` does, with `chunked` matched by `ascii.EqualFold` over the untrimmed value.  Not reproduced: a body read cut short by the client's EOF or by the read deadline, outside the trailer, which dcrd answers `400 error reading JSON message: unexpected EOF` (or the timeout) and the port closes without an answer, in both framings (see Known remaining gaps).  The HTTPS seeder's shutdown no longer waits out an in-flight round: each seeder reports over a channel the retry loop polls against the stop signal, so a stop abandons the round immediately — dcrd cancels the requests through the daemon context, aborting them outright, while the port's abandoned request threads run to the one-minute transport timeout at most and die with the process, a documented divergence in the requests' fate but not in the shutdown latency.  The seeder host lookup for the source address, and the addition, run on that seeder's own thread, as in dcrd's `seed` goroutine (`server.go:3361-3392`), so a Tor RESOLVE under `--proxy` never holds the retry loop or the shutdown, and a seeder that answers after the stop adds nothing where in dcrd the cancelled context fails its request.  A failed seeder logs dcrd's `seeder '<name>' error: <err>` at info level under SRVR (`server.go:3368`): the bad-status and parse texts are `SeedAddrs`'s own, and a transport failure carries the port's HTTP client text (`seeder request failed: ...`) where dcrd shows Go's `http.Client` text (`Get "<url>": ...`).  Deferred from the P3 sweep of the same review: the UTXO flush is atomic: the entry writes land in the same database transaction as the utxo-set-state marker everywhere the cache flushes (dcrd's backend `PutUtxos` coupling) — the clean-shutdown flush additionally folds the modified block index rows and the best-state marker into that one transaction, the stats-time forced flush couples its marker the same way, and each disconnect's forced flush now records the parent tip as the set state exactly as dcrd's `MaybeFlush` call does, with the block's database spend-journal record removed in its own transaction only after that flush succeeds (dcrd's `dbRemoveSpendJournalEntry` intentionally after the forced `MaybeFlush`), so a crash between the best-state write and the flush still finds the journal record the cache needs to recover.  Manual peer control (`addnode`, `node connect`/`remove`) is wired end to end: the RPC connection-manager seams run dcrd's `rpcConnManager.Connect`/`RemoveByID`/`RemoveByAddr` over a control channel into the outbound dial driver (duplicate/resolve/max-peers checks in dcrd's order and error texts, the resolution on the RPC thread like `addrStringToNetAddr`, persistent-peer removal stopping the connmgr redial with the `CancelPending` fallback for a still-dialing added peer), with the `--connect` permanents resolved at startup exactly where dcrd's `newServer` resolves them (failing the start on an unresolvable address); dials run on per-request dialer threads reporting back to the manager (dcrd's `Connect` goroutines), so the dial scheduling — and the peer-control commands riding the same event loop — never block on an in-flight dial.  Target resolution follows the pin's `addrStringToNetAddr` and connmgr's `stdlibNetAddrToAddrMgrNetAddr`.  A Tor v3 host stays unresolved (dcrd's `simpleAddr`), so `--connect`/`--addpeer`/`addnode`/`node connect` accept it.  It is dialed over the onion route and served as an onion peer, and so are auto-dialed TorV3 addresses, which were previously torn down after connecting.  Every other host, IP literals included, resolves through the configured `dcrdLookup` routing, which under `--proxy` without `--noonion` is a Tor RESOLVE through the proxy.  The port parses with `strconv.ParseUint(port, 10, 16)`'s texts.  The seeders' source-address lookup (`querySeeders`' `dcrdLookup(seeder)`) takes the same routing, so a proxied daemon sends no clear DNS for peer or seeder names.  Where that routing ends at the system resolver (Go's `net.LookupIP`), a `.onion` name is never handed to it and comes back with no addresses: `no addresses found for <host>` for a connect target.  This is Go's `avoidDNS` (RFC 7686), which matches without regard to case and ignores one trailing dot.  Go's resolver additionally answers such a name from `/etc/hosts` and queries its `resolv.conf` search-list forms, so with a search domain configured dcrd answers `lookup <host> on <server>: no such host` instead.  The port sends no query at all.  The 2.2 campaign (upstream master `452c1a6c`) also lands the server smalls: the live ban flow — the misbehavior handlers now record bans in a shared banned-host map through dcrd `BanPeer`'s semantics (whitelist and disabled-banning short-circuits, host-keyed entries lifting after `--banduration`) at every dcrd ban site the dispatch serves: the threshold-tripped mempool/getdata/notfound scores (the notfound intake is newly dispatched, scoring per dcrd and forwarding survivors to the sync manager), dcrd 2.2's direct committed-filter ban (replacing the old +100 score, with master's reason text), and the direct empty-getdata/empty-inventory/empty-address-list/invalid-addrv2/malformed-mix bans — the direct sites keeping dcrd's only-disconnect-inside-`BanPeer` semantics so whitelisted peers and disabled banning stay connected; `addBanScore` follows dcrd 2.2 in accumulating whitelisted peers' scores (visible through getpeerinfo) while never banning them; and both inbound and outbound admission refuse banned hosts before any handshake work, clearing expired bans as they are consulted — pinned end-to-end by a dispatch test driving a ban and asserting the recorded host and the refused reconnect (peers whose frames fail wire decoding are banned too — the transport's read errors are typed so the read loop mirrors dcrd `OnRead`'s `wire.ErrorCode` ban, pinned by an end-to-end test sending a malformed frame and asserting the recorded host and refused reconnect); a ban is stamped and checked on the monotonic clock (`server::ban_clock_nanos`, pinned by `tests/review_ban_clock.rs`), because dcrd's `time.Now().Add(BanDuration)` and `time.Now().Before(banEnd)` compare monotonic readings (`server.go:2763`, `:2193`), so a wall-clock step neither lifts nor stretches a ban; dcrd 2.2's retirement of the legacy mining-state exchange — getminings requests and miningstate messages from peers whose negotiated protocol version covers getinitstate ban with dcrd's exact reasons, as do repeated getminings/getinitstate requests (previously trace-ignored) and repeated initial state messages in either form, behind a new received-state latch, with the initstate intake now forwarded to the sync manager like dcrd `OnInitState`; and the ADXR logging subsystem removed to match master's `log.go` (the config vectors' resolved level maps and the supported-subsystems error updated in place).  The serve-signal plumbing carries owned disconnect reasons (`Cow<'static, str>`) so the dynamic 2.2 ban reasons flow without leaking |

## Deliberate divergences from dcrd: safety controls Go's runtime made unnecessary

dcrd's behaviour is the specification, and everywhere the two can agree they
do. The exceptions below are places where reproducing dcrd's *structure*
faithfully would reproduce a resource bound that only holds because of the Go
runtime. Each is a considered divergence, not an oversight, and each is
covered by a test that fails if the control is removed.

Two rows are of another kind. *The header's size and a proven data commitment*
changes no bound but what the chain holds after a block dcrd also refuses. It
refuses no block dcrd accepts, and after a copy of a valid block it accepts
the valid block, which dcrd, having marked its header for the copy, refuses.
*UTXO cache entry for an output added again after a disconnect* changes which
row the database keeps after a restart or an eviction: the port deletes a row
that a restarted dcrd keeps, so it rejects a later block spending that row, as
a dcrd that kept the entry cached does, where a restarted dcrd accepts it.

The recurring shape: a goroutine costs a few KiB and is scheduled by Go's own
runtime, so dcrd can spawn one per connection, queue into an unbounded
channel, and check its limits inside the handler. An OS thread costs a
multi-megabyte stack and a kernel scheduling slot, and a Rust channel that is
unbounded really is unbounded. Where dcrd's limit is enforced *after* the
expensive step, dcroxide moves it before.

| Area | dcrd | dcroxide | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Orphan eviction source | first entry of a `range` over a Go map — unpredictable per process, and dcrd's comment leans on that ("an adversary would have to be able to pull off preimage attacks … to target eviction of specific entries").  Unpredictable, but not uniform, and dcrd's comment is inaccurate about the mechanism: Go randomises the starting *slot*, not the starting *entry* (`internal/runtime/maps/table.go:710`, `:757-758`, `:1025`), so an entry is yielded first in proportion to the run of empty slots before it and the table is at most 7/8 full.  Measured over 300,000 ranges of a 100-entry map, the favoured entry led about 5.5x more often than uniform and the least favoured about 0.75x; eight entries is the one size that comes out uniform, because eight fill a group exactly | an index drawn from the injected `PoolChain::random_u64` (the OS CSPRNG in the daemon) into a slot vector that holds each orphan once, so the pick costs O(1) like dcrd's first-entry map range. Walking the ordered map to the index cost time linear in the orphan count, under the pool lock, on every admission to a full pool (review finding M1-p#3) | The port stores orphans in a `BTreeMap`, which has no equivalent of Go's seeded map iteration: the first entry is always the numerically smallest transaction hash. Grinding a *large* hash costs milliseconds, so reading the first entry would let an attacker keep their own orphans resident indefinitely while honest low-hash orphans churn — inverting the property dcrd's comment claims. Drawing the index restores it. Any choice is conformant, dcrd's own being explicitly unspecified. Pinned by `eviction_follows_the_random_draw_not_the_hash_order`, `every_orphan_is_reachable_by_some_draw` and `eviction_slots_follow_removals`. |
| Rival orphan trial order | the inner `map[chainhash.Hash]*wire.MsgTx` of `orphansByPrev` ranged in Go's per-execution randomized order (`mempool.go:2155-2177`), which is what makes tracking several children of one output the anti-griefing device dcrd describes it as | the collected rivals shuffled with Fisher-Yates from the injected `PoolChain::random_u64` before the acceptance loop | Only one child of a given output can be accepted — the loop takes the first that succeeds and breaks — so whatever orders the candidates decides which rival spend enters the pool. `orphans_by_prev`'s inner map is a `BTreeMap` keyed by child hash, so iterating it would hand the win to the numerically smallest hash every time, and an attacker reaches that by grinding a nonce for a handful of tries. This is the same hazard as the *Orphan eviction source* row above, at the selection site rather than the eviction site, and it takes the same remedy. Reported as RVW-019 by an external review of `382864f5`. Pinned by `trial_order_follows_the_draw_not_the_input_order`, `every_rival_can_be_tried_first` and `the_shuffle_preserves_every_rival`; those pin the shuffle itself, not `process_orphans_internal` end to end, which would need two rival transactions the fake chain accepts and the replayed dcrd corpus has no such scenario. |
| Rejected block bodies in memory | bodies are written to the database only (`process.go:321-327`), whose comment says keeping doomed-but-proof-of-work-valid blocks on disk is deliberate because proof-of-work makes filling the *disk* prohibitive; the sole in-memory copy is a fixed `recentBlockCacheSize = 12` LRU (`chain.go:41-46`) | the body is stored in the in-memory mirror by `maybe_accept_block_data` and dropped again by `forget_rejected_block_body` the moment a contextual or connect check rejects the block, when a database is attached, and also when a contextual `ErrBadMerkleRoot` leaves it unmarked, as in dcrd (row below). A restart mirrors again the stored body of every block on a chain tip's branch within 288 blocks of the tip's height (`process.rs:1596-1625`), a rejected block's among them, since the load cannot tell an unmarked rejected block from a valid side branch's; such a body leaves again when a check rejects the block again or the sweep passes it | The port mirrors bodies in memory where dcrd does not, and `maybe_accept_block_data` stores before the contextual and connect checks run, so a rejected block was already resident. `prune_chain_memory` only reaches heights below `tip - 288`, and a peer feeding proof-of-work-valid blocks that fail a later check never moves the tip, so nothing it sent crossed the horizon and the map grew for as long as the flood ran — free on the reduced-difficulty networks, proof-of-work-gated on mainnet. Dropping at the point of rejection costs a database read on the rare re-request, which `block_data` already serves through `db_fetch_stored_block`; a chain with no database keeps the body, since there the map is the only copy. Reported as RVW-014 by an external review of `382864f5`. Pinned by `blocks_that_fail_validation_do_not_keep_their_bodies`, which replays dcrd's own battery: 102 of 104 validation-failed bodies stay resident without the drop, none with it. No battery block fails its contextual merkle check; the unmarked case is pinned by `tests/review_merkle_variant_stall.rs` `a_copy_committing_to_the_variant_not_in_force_does_not_stall_its_sibling`, whose copy stays resident without that drop, and the reload and the drop at the reorganization by `a_branch_on_the_copy_after_a_restart_does_not_stall_the_chain`. |
| Reorganization past a block whose contextual merkle check fails | `maybeAcceptBlocks` and `reorganizeChainInternal` leave a block that fails `checkMerkleRootsContext` unmarked (`process.go:367-374`, `chain.go:1209-1215`), so it stays a best chain candidate. When an attempt fails at it, `reorganizeChain` asks `FindBestChainCandidate` for a new target, is handed the same block, or the branch on it, while that is still the best candidate, and stops (`chain.go:1338-1341`), leaving the tip where the attempt left it. Such a block can be stored only where the data preconditions cannot settle the header commitments agenda and so accept data matching either merkle variant (`validate.go:2037-2048`): on regnet before a tally has found the activation, and again after each restart until one does; never on simnet; on mainnet and testnet3 only on a branch that forks below the activation anchor and extends past it, which the fork rejection checkpoint refuses once it is set (`process.go:64-75`), unless old forks are allowed (`validate.go:1298-1312`). New in `ddd3b6d2` and `release-v2.1.7`; `release-v2.1.6` marked the block (`process.go:377-381`, `chain.go:1220-1224` there) | the same checks, errors and statuses, and the block is still never marked. When `reorganize_chain_internal` fails at it, `drop_unconnectable_candidates` takes it, and every candidate descending from it, out of the candidates (`BlockIndex::remove_best_chain_candidates_from`: the candidates `mark_block_failed_validation` would remove), so the loop's next `find_best_chain_candidate` yields another target and the reorganization falls back in the same call, as it does after any failure that marks a block. When that leaves no candidate, the tip, the block's parent, is made one again: dcrd's candidates always hold at least the tip, which `RemoveLessWorkCandidates` asserts (`blockindex.go:1279-1284`), and there the unmarked block and the branch on it keep that true by staying candidates | In dcrd a valid sibling of equal work, which lost the tie on received order (`blockindex.go:469-473`), is rejected with the unmarked block's `ErrBadMerkleRoot`, and the tip stays at the fork point until a branch with strictly more work arrives; a block on the unmarked one, before or after a restart, or `reconsiderblock` on it, starts the stall again. The block reaches the attach loop's contextual checks only when it is not validated, its merkle check depends on nothing but the block and its ancestors, and its hash never enters the recent merkle checks, so every reorganization to it or to a descendant fails at it: dropping them skips only attempts that must fail, and whatever the fallback then tries is checked by the same rules. What differs: the call in which the attempt fails returns dcrd's errors, then any the fallback meets, and ends on the best remaining branch rather than where the attempt stopped. A later call tries the block again only when it makes it a candidate again (a block on it, `invalidateblock`'s repopulation of the candidates, `reconsiderblock`), and then falls back in that call too, so the sibling connects when it arrives, with no error, and a valid branch of less work is adopted. Without the tip's return, fallbacks that took every candidate left the set empty, and a forced head reorganization then panicked on that assertion where dcrd, stalled at another block, refuses it as made on the wrong chain. The failed mark is still not set, for dcrd's reason: data that matches the header only under the variant not in force does not show the header invalid, since other data could match it under the variant in force, and marking would let such data brand a valid header. The data stays stored, so sending it again is still `ErrDuplicateBlock`, and the header is still accepted. Pinned by `tests/review_merkle_variant_stall.rs`: `a_copy_committing_to_the_variant_not_in_force_does_not_stall_its_sibling`, `a_branch_on_the_copy_does_not_stall_the_chain`, `a_branch_on_the_copy_after_a_restart_does_not_stall_the_chain` and `reconsidering_the_copy_does_not_stall_the_chain`, each of which fails without the removal, and `the_tip_stays_a_candidate_when_the_fallbacks_drop_every_other` and `a_later_sibling_of_equal_work_does_not_replace_the_tip_after_the_fallbacks`, which fail without the tip's return; `process::tests::only_a_contextual_bad_merkle_root_drops_unmarked_candidates` pins that no other failure of the check removes a candidate. |
| The header's size and a proven data commitment | `checkBlockDataPreconditions` counts the data commitment as proven once the merkle check passes under an agenda state the block's position decides, as it does for every mainnet, testnet3 and simnet block (`internal/blockchain/validate.go:2009-2034` at `382ff4dd`), and the header's commitment to the block's size is checked only after that, in `checkBlockDataSanity` (`:910-914`), whose refusal of a block with a proven commitment marks the header as having failed validation (`process.go:496-513`). A tree level with an odd number of nodes pairs its last node with itself (`blockchain/standalone/merkle.go:56-60`), so repeating the last nodes of such a level keeps the tree's root: the shape of CVE-2012-2459, which `blockchain/fullblocktests/generate.go:2164-2174` sets aside because the header names the block's size. A copy of a valid block with such a repeat, under the block's own header, therefore has its commitment proven, is refused with `ErrWrongBlockSize`, and has the header marked; the valid block is then refused with `ErrKnownInvalidBlock` (`process.go:461-468`), and blocks built on it with `ErrInvalidAncestorBlock` (`process.go:50-53`, `:191-194`). Any peer a block is asked of can answer with such a copy. The same on master through `3360ae4d` and in `release-v2.1.7`. This is dcrd's code as read: no copy of a valid block has been sent to dcrd. A run of dcrd has shown the marking it rests on: in dcrd's `ProcessBlock` at `6f6cf21b`, with the agenda forced to "no", the battery's `bmf7`, whose header names a byte more than its block, left its header marked (status 4) and was refused again with `ErrKnownInvalidBlock` (the status replay in `tests/review_process_reorder.rs`), and `process.go` and the checks that path makes before a block's connect are the same at `382ff4dd` | `check_block_data_preconditions_with_leaves` (`validate.rs`) compares the header's size with the block's serialized size once the merkle check passes under a decided agenda state, before it reports the commitment as proven, and so before the chain records the block in its recent merkle checks, and refuses a mismatch with the sanity checks' own `ErrWrongBlockSize`, kind and text, the commitment not proven, so nothing is marked. The size is that of the block the chain was handed, through `process_block`, `process_shared_block` and `process_hashed_block` alike, a block read ahead of the block handler included, and for a template. The error is the sanity checks' own but for a block that also fails a check they make before the size, the proof of stake or the presence of a regular transaction (`validate.go:884-895`), or for a template one of the header's sanity checks (`:4674`): such a block is now refused for its size instead, where dcrd gives that check's error; in processing, with the commitment proven, dcrd marks its header and the port does not. With the agenda state undecided nothing is proven, and the order is dcrd's. A header marked this way by a build before the change stays marked in the block index on disk until `reconsiderblock` clears it | dcrd's reasoning, that the header's size keeps two blocks from sharing a hash, covers the hash but not a refusal charged to the header: a node that marks the valid block's header refuses the valid block, and the blocks built on it, for what a peer sent in its place. The change refuses no block dcrd accepts: dcrd refuses every block whose header names another size, a valid block passes the comparison, and short of breaking the hash every other set of trees with a valid block's roots is larger. What changes is where such a block is refused, in the preconditions rather than the sanity checks, and that its header is no longer marked; its error changes only where it also fails one of the checks the previous column names. The battery's `bmf7`, a header that names another size, was marked with the agenda forced to "no" and is not now (`battery_rejections_leave_dcrds_statuses`, `tests/review_process_reorder.rs`): a body sent for such a header is refused each time, as one is with the agenda undecided, rather than answered with `ErrKnownInvalidBlock`. A block whose header names the size with the repeats is still marked, through `ErrDuplicateTx`, since that header can be no valid block's. Pinned by `the_block_is_accepted_after_a_copy_of_it_with_a_repeated_merkle_tail` (`tests/repeated_tail_copies.rs`: a stake tree of five and a regular tree of three, each with its last leaf repeated, and a regular tree of six with its last two repeated, the copy and then the valid block through the three entries, with the agenda forced inactive, forced active and undecided, and with the header unknown and accepted first), `bad_shapes_are_refused_alike_however_the_block_arrives` (`tests/hashed_block_bad_shapes.rs`), `a_header_that_names_another_size_is_refused_for_it_before_other_data_checks` (`tests/review_process_reorder.rs`: `bsd0`, a ticket below the stake difficulty, under a header that names a byte more), and the preconditions' unit tests in `validate.rs`, `process.rs` and `blockhashes.rs`; each fails with the comparison taken out |
