| Multisig builder size-limit error kind | `MultiSigScriptV0` returns `builder.Script()`'s error unchanged, which is txscript's `ErrScriptNotCanonical` -- a different package's error type crossing the boundary (`stdscript/scriptv0.go:848`) | re-labelled as stdscript's `ErrUnsupportedScriptVersion`, since the port's `Result<Vec<u8>, StdScriptError>` carries one error type and has no counterpart kind | Reached only with enough keys to exceed `MAX_SCRIPT_SIZE`, which is unspendable regardless because `OP_CHECKMULTISIG` caps the count far below it. Matching dcrd would mean the constructor's error carrying a txscript error across the package boundary, an API change for a script nobody can spend. The departure was already documented at the call site; it is recorded here so a ledger check finds it rather than re-deriving it, as RVW-022 of an external review of `382864f5` did. |
| Absurd amount conversion (`--minrelaytxfee`, RPC amounts) | `dcrutil.NewAmount` multiplies by 1e8 and converts to `Amount`. Go leaves an out-of-range float-to-int conversion implementation-defined; on amd64 `CVTTSD2SI` yields `MinInt64`. For the flag, dcrd ends up with a negative relay fee, and that stops it starting: `newServer`'s `MaxBucketFee` is `100 * MinInt64`, which wraps to 0, so `fees.NewEstimator` passes its first check and refuses with "minimum bucket fee rate cannot be <= 0". The same conversion takes client-supplied floats over RPC: `createrawtransaction`'s input `amount` (converted with no range check first, `rpcserver.go:819-826`) and `createrawssrtx`'s input `amount` and `fee` | `(f + 0.5) as i64` saturates, so the port ends up at `i64::MAX` (both copies of `new_amount`, `dcroxide-node/src/config.rs` and `dcroxide-rpc/src/handlers.rs`). Only a *positive* out-of-range amount differs; a negative one is `MinInt64` either way. For the flag, the estimator's maximum is dcrd's wrapping `100 *` product (`fees::new_shared_estimator`), which for `i64::MAX` wraps to -100, so `Estimator::new` refuses with "maximum bucket fee should not be lower than minimum bucket fee", logged as `Unable to start server: ...` with exit status 1. Over RPC, what clients see differs in three places. `createrawtransaction` serializes the input's `ValueIn` as `0x7fffffffffffffff` where dcrd/amd64 writes `0x8000000000000000`. `createrawssrtx`'s not-equal-to-ticket-amount error renders the saturated amount where dcrd/amd64 renders a negative one. Before the automatic-revocations agenda, its `fee` reaches `CreateRevocationFromTicket` as `MaxInt64`, not `MinInt64`. `createrawtransaction`'s output amounts fail the `MaxAmount` range check with the same text either way, and the `stdDev` fee helper cannot overflow | For the flag: local operator input only -- it takes a configured fee large enough to overflow the conversion, which no remote party supplies. Both nodes refuse to start; only the error text differs, because the conversion does. For the RPC sites: authenticated requests carrying amounts beyond the money supply, which get a garbage transaction or an error either way. In both cases there is no single dcrd behaviour to reproduce: Go's result is platform-defined, and on arm64, whose `FCVTZS` saturates, dcrd gets the port's `MaxInt64`, so a port matching amd64 would diverge from dcrd on another architecture. This row used to say a saturated fee rejects everything and a negative one accepts everything; neither node gets that far. Until the review of 2026-09-23 the port reached the refusal through an `expect` and aborted. Reported as RVW-011 by an external review of `382864f5` and closed deliberately rather than reconciled; the RPC sites were added to the row after the review of 2026-09-23. |
| `--authtype=clientcert` with an empty `--clientcafile` | no rule: `newTLSConfig` skips client verification when `clientCAsPath == ""` (`server.go:3689`), and the RPC server grants admin to clientcert sessions, so the endpoint serves unauthenticated admin RPC | `loadConfig` refuses the combination with `loadConfig: --authtype=clientcert requires --clientcafile`, but only when the RPC server will run; with `--norpc` the configuration loads as dcrd's does | Reproducing dcrd would hand every admin RPC to anyone who can reach the listener. The rule is scoped to where there is an endpoint to protect (`tests/review_config_notices.rs`, `clientcert_without_a_ca_file_is_refused_only_with_rpc_enabled`). |
| Non-UTF-8 config file values and environment variables | go-flags' ini reader keeps raw bytes (`ini.go` `readIni`), and `os.Getenv` returns a variable's raw bytes (WTF-8 on Windows), so a Latin-1 `rpcpass`, a non-UTF-8 `datadir`, or a non-UTF-8 `DCRD_APPDATA`, `HOME` or `$VAR` in a path is taken byte for byte | Lines dcrd skips (comments) may hold any bytes. An applied assignment whose line is not valid UTF-8 is refused with `Error parsing config file: <file>:<line>: the value of option <name> is not valid UTF-8`. An environment variable dcrd would read that is not UTF-8 is refused with `invalid UTF-8 in environment variable <NAME>: <value>` (followed by the usage line) before the load acts on it. Those variables are `HOME` for the default home, when neither `--appdata` nor `DCROXIDE_APPDATA` names another (dcrd reads it at package init, but `loadConfig` then replaces everything derived from it, `config.go:692-727`), `HOME` again as the `~` of `--datadir`/`--logdir` when the password file has no entry for the user, `DCROXIDE_APPDATA` and `DCROXIDE_ALT_DNSNAMES` when the option is not set, and a `$VAR` in `--datadir`/`--logdir` (`flags::getenv_utf8`, `config::load_config_from_argv_with_home_refusal`, `tests/review_appdata_home.rs`). Beside a home that is named, no path given is taken for a default the refused `HOME` would have produced, since dcrd's defaults hold bytes no argument can spell: `--appdata=.` names a home, and `--configfile=dcroxide.conf` names a file, where the `.` the lookup fell back to matched both (`config::REFUSED_DEFAULT_HOME`). A variable dcrd leaves unread is never refused, with one exception: on Apple platforms and Windows the `~` is looked up through std, which reads a set `HOME` (on Windows `%USERPROFILE%`) before the directory service Go asks (`getpwuid_r`, `GetUserProfileDirectory`), so there a bad value is refused as the `~` of `--datadir`/`--logdir` where dcrd would resolve it, rather than the `~` taken for the current directory. `-h`/`-V` still exit as dcrd's do. addblock refuses a non-UTF-8 `HOME` the same way once its configuration parses, when its data directory is the default one: a `--datadir` of its own, `./data` included, leaves `HOME` unused, as in dcrd. This is the policy argv already follows (`flags::args_after_program`). | Config values are `String`s. The earlier lossy decode silently replaced each bad byte with U+FFFD, turning such a secret into a different one and such a path into a different directory. The environment lookups read such a value as unset, running the daemon on the default home, or for `HOME` on the current directory. Carrying raw bytes would need `OsString`/`Vec<u8>` fields throughout (`tests/review_config_notices.rs`). |
| `submitblock` bytes past the block | `dcrutil.NewBlockFromBytes` (`dcrutil/block.go:357-366`) decodes a block from the front of the submission and keeps the *whole* submission as the block's serialized bytes. ffldb's `StoreBlock` writes `block.Bytes()`, so any trailing bytes reach the flat files, and `getblock <hash> false` (hex of `blk.Bytes()`) echoes them back. P2P cannot produce this, because `ReadMessageN` rejects unconsumed payload bytes (`ErrTrailingBytes`), and peers are served a re-serialized `MsgBlock` | trailing bytes are dropped at decode: the block is stored in its canonical serialization, which `getblock <hash> false` hands back as stored, without decoding and re-serializing it (`RpcChain::block_bytes_by_hash`), as dcrd hex-encodes `blk.Bytes()` | Persisting bytes chosen by the submitter serves no purpose; `submitblock` is on `rpcLimited`, so limited credentials suffice to do it. Reproducing it would mean carrying the raw submission through the sync manager and chain into the block store. Consensus never reads `Bytes()`, and `getblock`'s verbose `size` comes from the header, so only the raw hex and the flat-file contents differ. Pinned by `submitblock_trailing_bytes_are_not_kept` (`crates/dcroxide-rpc/tests/review_rpc_handler_parity.rs`). |
| Per-peer outbound queue | `outputQueue` (a 5000-slot `chan`) drained into `queueHandler`'s unbounded `pendingMsgs` slice; since `589dd4c5`, `queueOutMsg` charges each message its `msgSize` against the 40 MiB `maxQueuedOutputBytes` and disconnects a peer past it | `sync_channel(128)`, each message charged its framed size (dcrd's `msgSize`: `MessageHeaderSize` + `SerializeSize`, computed without encoding) against a 4 MiB `MAX_OUTBOUND_QUEUE_BYTES` budget released as the writer drains; a full queue — either ceiling — drops the message and logs once per congestion episode | An unbounded queue against a peer that stops reading is unbounded memory, and a count bound alone was coarse: 128 max-size blocks is ~46 MB per peer, so the byte charge makes the bound mean what it exists to bound (pinned by `outbound_queue_is_byte_capped_before_the_depth_cap`). The 4 MiB and 128-message ceilings are the port's own hardening, not dcrd's 40 MiB bound ported. Dropping rather than disconnecting is deliberate: an earlier revision that disconnected cut off honest peers on slow links. Producers must treat a refused send as not sent, so nothing records inventory the peer was never told about. A refused netsync getdata, getheaders, getinitstate or getminingstate goes back to the sync manager (`SyncManager::on_request_not_sent`). The queue hands the refused message back (`OutboundQueue::queue_message_or_return`), so nothing is copied in case of a refusal. The manager re-requests each item from another peer known to hold it, or else forgets the request, rebuilding its needed-blocks list for a block. It clears the getheaders duplicate filter, and it re-arms the once-per-peer initial state request, which it makes again with that peer's next inv or headers message. When the sync peer gave back a block request (re-requested from another holder or forgotten) and has no blocks left in flight, the chain sync moves to the best candidate that has not refused in the same dispatch, the one with the highest last block, as `updateSyncPeerState` picks, and that peer is asked for the next blocks at once. With no such candidate the sync peer is kept, and the next download round asks it again. dcrd reaches the re-requests and the new sync peer only after its stall handler disconnects a peer that never answered (`stallResponseTimeout`, 30 s) and `OnPeerDisconnected` runs `startChainSync`, which it does for a departing sync peer whether its blocks were re-requested or forgotten. The port gets there without the wait and without disconnecting the refusing peer. Each refused request is logged at DEBUG (`Outbound queue for peer P is full -- dropping the CMD request`), so the queue's latched WARN stays the one line per congestion episode. Pinned by `crates/dcroxide-netsync/tests/review_request_not_sent.rs` and `a_refused_sync_request_is_handed_back_for_the_manager`. The getdata serve worker never drops a reply the peer asked for. It waits for queue room, as dcrd's serve goroutine waits on its `maxPendingSend` semaphore, and stops only when the peer goes away. It charges each payload its real framed size (`a_refused_reply_waits_for_the_queue_to_drain`, `small_mix_replies_never_stall_the_send_pipeline`). While a reply waits, the worker logs one DEBUG line (`Outbound queue for peer P is full -- waiting to send CMD`), not the queue's `-- dropping` warning. **Reconciled against `589dd4c5`:** dcrd now bounds this too, tracking queued bytes per peer against a 40 MiB `maxQueuedOutputBytes` and *disconnecting* on exceed, so the divergence is no longer one of kind but of value (4 MiB vs 40 MiB) and action (drop vs disconnect). Both halves are kept deliberately. The value: 40 MiB across the default `maxpeers` of 125 is ~5 GB, the same order as the ~5.7 GB this row's byte charge exists to eliminate, so taking upstream's threshold would undo the bound rather than tighten it. The action: dropping still beats disconnecting for a merely slow peer, and the case dcrd's disconnect targets — a peer that has stopped reading altogether — is already handled here by the per-message write deadline in the *Peer socket writes* row below, which tears that connection down long before 40 MiB could pile up. What `589dd4c5` retires is only the old rationale that dcrd never disconnects on a full queue: it does now, at a threshold this port reaches by a different route. |
| Peer socket writes | 20 s plus one second per 256 KiB of the framed message, armed before every write (`peer/peer.go:90`, `:96`, `:1032-1039`, added by `62fd529a`); nothing at all before that commit | the same deadline, armed around every `write_message` (`WriteStallPolicy::dcrd()`, `transport.rs`) | Reproduced rather than divergent since `62fd529a`. Before it dcrd blocked in `Write` unbounded, which a goroutine can afford and an OS thread cannot; pinned by `the_write_deadline_matches_dcrds_formula`. A blocked goroutine costs a few KiB; a blocked OS thread costs a stack and never returns to the pool. |
| Per-peer threads and thread exhaustion | goroutines (`inHandler`, `queueHandler`, `outHandler` with its ping ticker, `stallHandler`, `serveGetData`, and the connmgr's dial goroutines and `time.Timer`s), which cannot fail to start | three OS threads per served peer (input, output, and one timer thread for the keepalive and the stall check, `run_peer_timers`) plus the getdata worker, started on first use, and, on a connection that is read ahead of its block handler, one more, `peer-reader`, for which dcrd has no goroutine (*Reading ahead of the block handler*). Every thread a remote peer's arrival causes (inbound serve, dial, outbound serve, the per-peer loops, the drop-log reset, the reader of a connection that is read ahead) starts through `runtime::spawn_conn_thread`. A refusal drops that connection before the server sees it (`DisconnectReason::ThreadRefused`) or fails that dial through the ordinary outcome path, and is logged at warn. A refused reader thread drops nothing: the connection goes on being read by its input thread, as it was until then, the refusal is logged at warn and the thread is not asked for again. One that did start is joined before the connection returns, after the output and timer threads. On the RPC side, each websocket connection's writer thread, which an upgrade needing no credentials reaches, starts through `Builder::spawn_scoped` (`websocket.rs` `spawn_writer`): a refusal drops that client unserved, frees its `rpcmaxwebsockets` slot and is logged at warn, where `Scope::spawn` had panicked. The outbound driver's retry backoff and fill pause are deadlines its event loop waits on, not a sleeping thread each | `std::thread::spawn` panics when the OS refuses a thread (`EAGAIN` under `RLIMIT_NPROC` or a cgroup `pids.max`, `ENOMEM` for a stack), and the release profile aborts on panic. A unit whose task limit was sized for dcrd therefore turned one arriving peer into a crash loop. With every outbound slot held, the permit poll re-armed every 5 s, which meant a new OS thread every 5 s for the life of the node. Pinned by `a_refused_thread_drops_the_connection`, `a_refused_serving_thread_releases_the_admission`, `a_refused_dial_thread_reports_a_failed_dial`, `the_fill_wake_is_a_deadline_the_event_loop_keeps` and `a_refused_writer_drops_the_connection_and_frees_the_slot`, and for the reader thread by `a_refused_reader_thread_leaves_the_connection_reading_inline` and, on Linux, `the_reader_thread_is_joined_before_the_connection_returns`. |
| Script validation worker start | `txValidator.Validate` starts its goroutines anew for every batch, at most `NumCPU()*3` and no more than the batch has items, and a goroutine cannot fail to start (`internal/blockchain/scriptval.go:117-133`) | Two kinds of worker; the row below says which trees and callers run on each. The script pool's helpers, which check the trees of the blocks the chain connects that the pool takes and pre-verify the signatures of blocks read ahead (*Signature pre-verification ahead of the block thread*), start through `thread::Builder::spawn` when a batch or that pre-verification first wants them and live as long as the process (`Pool::global`, `scriptpool.rs:571`). The first helper the OS refuses ends that creation: the pool goes without it and without the rest that batch asked for, the calling thread and the helpers there are cover the batch, and no creation is tried again before the 1,024th batch after that one (`SPAWN_RETRY_BATCHES`, `:166`). The scoped path (`validate_items`, `validate.rs:5867`), which checks every other batch, starts one worker per core via `Builder::spawn_scoped` for a batch of 16 items or more and joins them before it returns. The first thread the OS refuses stops the spawning, and the calling thread drains the remaining items through the same work-stealing index | `Scope::spawn` panics when a task limit (RLIMIT_NPROC, cgroup `pids.max`, `TasksMax`) refuses a thread, and release builds abort on panic, so reaching a limit mid-block took the node down. A refusal costs speed only: the verdicts are identical however many workers or helpers start, which `validate::tests::validate_items_survives_refused_worker_threads` and `scriptpool::tests::a_refused_helper_thread_leaves_the_caller_to_do_the_work` pin. A pooled batch's verdict rests on two more checks made in every build: with no failure held, `Ok` is returned only when the batch was not stopped and the checks that passed number exactly the items, and an error is always one a check of that batch returned (`scriptpool::tests::result_refuses_ok_before_every_item_passed`, `result_refuses_ok_for_a_stopped_batch`), so a fault in the pool can stall or stop the node and cannot accept a block. A node that has checked a connected block's scripts, or pre-verified a block read ahead, keeps the helpers it created, at most one fewer than the logical CPUs the process may run on, parked and using no CPU while idle, and those limits count them for the rest of its life (`scriptpool::tests::the_process_wide_pool_is_one_helper_short_of_the_cpus`). |
| Script checks of a connected block | `txValidator.Validate` starts its goroutines anew for every batch, at most `NumCPU()*3` and no more than the batch has items (`internal/blockchain/scriptval.go:117-133`). A transaction (`ValidateTransactionScripts`, `:183-219`) and each transaction tree of a block that is connected or checked as a template (`checkBlockScripts`, `:224-274`, called from `checkConnectBlock`, `validate.go:4515`, `:4607`) are one batch each. With two or more invalid inputs it returns whichever error comes off its result channel first (`scriptval.go:141-163`), so which input it names, and with it the error's kind (`ErrScriptMalformed` or `ErrScriptValidation`), depends on scheduling. No goroutine is kept for a later batch: on the first error `Validate` cancels the rest and returns without waiting, and a goroutine inside a script exits when that script ends (`scriptval.go:49-54`, `:156-160`, `:165`) | The chain checks the scripts of the blocks it connects on helper threads that live as long as the process (`Pool::global`, `scriptpool.rs:571`): at most one fewer than the logical CPUs the process may run on, so that with the calling thread, which always checks too, a batch has at most one worker per CPU. They are created when a batch, or the pre-verification of blocks read ahead, first wants them, named `scriptval-N`, pre-verify the signatures of blocks read ahead while no batch wants them (*Signature pre-verification ahead of the block thread*), are parked while there is nothing to check and are never joined. The first helper the OS refuses ends that creation: the pool goes without it and without the rest that batch asked for, and the calling thread, with the helpers there are, covers the work; no creation is tried again before the 1,024th batch after that one. A connected block's tree goes to the pool when it has 4 script items or more (`MIN_POOLED_ITEMS`) and its previous scripts, which are copied into one buffer for it, come to at most 1 MiB (`MAX_POOLED_SCRIPT_BYTES`, `validate.rs:6099`), where the process may run on more than one logical CPU and in a build with the `std` feature (`check_block_scripts_inner`, `validate.rs:6403`). Every other tree and every other caller goes to the scoped path of the row above (`validate_items`, `validate.rs:5867`): a connected block's tree of fewer than 4 items, one whose previous scripts exceed 1 MiB, every tree where the process may run on only one logical CPU or without `std`, the template check the mining generator asks for (`Chain::check_connect_block_template`, through the public `check_connect_block`), the public `check_block_scripts`, and a single transaction (`validate_transaction_scripts`, which checks its first input on the calling thread and the rest on that path). **Which input is named** when more than one is invalid. A tree the pool runs names its invalid input of lowest index in block order, on every run, whatever the number of helpers (the argument is in the module documentation of `scriptpool.rs`). The scoped path checks a batch of fewer than 16 items in index order on the calling thread, and names the lowest too; one of 16 or more it fans out over the cores, where the process may run on more than one, and names whichever failure is recorded first, as dcrd does. So whichever failure is recorded first is still named for a connected block's tree of 16 items or more whose previous scripts exceed 1 MiB; for trees of 16 items or more in the template check and in the public `check_block_scripts`; and among the inputs after the first of a transaction with more than 16 inputs to check. A template check and a connect of the same invalid block can therefore name different inputs, and different kinds. The verdict, accept or reject, is the same on every path | An OS thread costs far more to create than a goroutine, and the scoped workers create and join theirs for every batch they fan out, up to two for every connected block. Naming the lowest index makes the error of every tree the pool runs reproducible, and it costs the pool nothing it did not already do: the calling thread waits for every helper inside its batch in any case, and an item once claimed is always checked to its end. The scoped path, which the single-transaction relay path shares, still names whichever failure it records first when it fans out, as dcrd does; giving it the pool's rule would also settle which input the relay path names, where dcrd's depends on scheduling. Pinned for the lowest index by `the_lowest_failure_is_kept`, `a_batch_with_two_failures_names_the_lower_one`, `a_failure_at_a_higher_index_waits_for_a_lower_one_still_running` and `an_item_claimed_before_a_failure_is_still_checked` (`scriptpool::tests`) and `a_pooled_tree_names_its_lowest_failing_input` (`validate::tests`); for where a tree runs by `pooled_and_scoped_block_scripts_agree` and `oversized_previous_scripts_use_the_scoped_workers` (`validate::tests`) and `a_connected_blocks_scripts_are_offered_to_the_script_pool`, `a_template_check_offers_the_script_pool_nothing`, `every_block_a_call_validates_is_checked_with_a_pooled_runner` and `a_block_with_its_hashes_and_its_record_is_processed_as_it_arrived_over_a_database` (`process::tests`); for the helpers by `the_process_wide_pool_is_one_helper_short_of_the_cpus`, which also reads their names, and `a_refused_helper_thread_leaves_the_caller_to_do_the_work` (`scriptpool::tests`). |
| RPC connection admission | goroutine per connection; `RPCMaxClients` checked inside the handler, after the TLS handshake | pre-authentication slot claimed on the accept thread against a two-tier budget — soft = `clamp(rpcmaxclients + 8, 32, 128)`, hard = `min(2 * soft, 256)`, both derived from a 512-thread process-wide ceiling at two threads per unauthenticated connection. At the soft tier one connection is disconnected to make room, chosen oldest first within three classes: first a connection already answered (the 401 or the 503) that is only discarding its declared body, since closing it loses nothing; then a connection that has not yet sent a request head; and as a last resort the oldest connection outright. Before the 2026-09-23 review answered connections counted as having sent a head and were protected, so a slow flood parked in the drain made a handshaking client the victim. The tier exists to shed arrivals that stall, and evicting a connection mid-service would leave it with no answer at all (pinned by `eviction_takes_an_answered_connection_before_a_handshaking_one`, `eviction_prefers_a_connection_that_has_not_sent_a_request` and `eviction_falls_back_to_the_oldest_when_all_have_sent_a_request`; until RVW-010 of an external review of `382864f5`, the selection ignored the head distinction); at the hard tier the arrival is refused with no reply | Connections that stall before sending a request never reach dcrd's 503, so under a flood each one costs a thread. The ceiling sits far above any admissible client count, so every state dcrd can reach — including its 503 — is reached identically. |
| RPC listen addresses | `setupRPCListeners` runs `parseListeners` (an empty host gives tcp4 and tcp6; a non-IP host gives "'%s' is not a valid IP address"). It binds each address with `net.Listen`/`tls.Listen`, which set `IPV6_V6ONLY` for tcp6 and `SO_REUSEADDR` outside Windows (`setDefaultListenerSockopts`). On a bind failure it warns 'Can't listen on %s: %v' and continues. `newServer` fails only with 'no usable rpc listen addresses'. | The same since the 2026-09-23 review (`start_rpc_listener` over `config::parse_listeners` and `runtime::bind_listener`). The daemon then logs 'RPC server listening on %s' once per listener, and reports a failure as dcrd's 'Unable to start server: %v'. The tcp6 bind sets `SO_REUSEADDR` outside Windows, as std's `TcpListener::bind` does for tcp4. Before a follow-up in the same review it did not: a restart within the `TIME_WAIT` period after IPv6 RPC traffic then lost the `::1` listener, and an IPv6-only rpclisten failed to start. The P2P tcp6 listener shares the helper and was affected the same way. | The warning's `%v` is Rust's `io::Error` text, not Go's `listen tcp4 ...: bind: ...`. The accept loop waits on listener readiness with a 50 ms cap, so a connection is accepted as soon as it arrives. Pinned by `review_rpc_listen` (including `a_restarted_v6_listener_rebinds_over_time_wait`) and by `review_rpc_startup::rpc_startup_logs_match_dcrd`. |
| Error-response body discard | discards after the response is flushed (`server.go:1701-1717`), gives up unread above `maxPostHandlerReadBytes` (`transfer.go:1002-1004`), and reads nothing at all when the request said `Connection: close` (`transfer.go:996-998`) | discards after the response and above the same cap, but discards a body already in flight even when the request said `Connection: close` | dcrd is reached through Go's own client stack; a raw client that sends its body and then blocks reading the reply loses it, because closing over an unread body resets the connection and the reset takes the queued response with it. Discarding a small, already-arrived body first is what makes the close clean, and costs only bytes no client can observe. The two arms that are reproduced exactly are the load-bearing ones: answering first, and reading nothing above the cap, without which a client that declares a `Content-Length` and never sends it is waited out until the handshake watchdog closes the socket and is answered with nothing at all. Pinned by `an_undelivered_body_does_not_swallow_the_401`. The mux's own answers (the 307, the `OPTIONS *` 200 and asterisk 400, the CONNECT 404), the `/ws` 401 and the 417 discard the same way, since Go runs `finishRequest` after them too (pinned by `mux_answers_discard_the_declared_body`). Two differences remain: Go's global options handler reads up to 4 KiB of an `OPTIONS *` body *before* its 200 (`server.go:4164-4175`), and so also sends a requested `100 Continue` there, where the port answers first and discards afterwards; and gorilla's handshake refusals (the 400/403/405 written by `serve_websocket`) still close without discarding. |
| Authenticated request body limit | `jsonRPCRead` reads through `io.LimitReader(r.Body, rpcReadLimitAuthenticated)` and never compares the declared length (`rpcserver.go:5682-5684`). It then calls `r.Body.Close()` before answering. Go's `body.Close` with `doEarlyClose` (`net/http/transfer.go:993-1019`) reads on through a chunked body for up to 256 KiB + 1 more decoded bytes. It reads on through a Content-Length body only when the request did not ask to close and at most 256 KiB remain. Otherwise it leaves the rest for the hijacked connection's close. `Close`'s error is dropped. | The same since the 2026-09-23 review. A Content-Length body is read up to `min(declared, 8 MiB)`. A chunked body stops at the limit, also when the limit falls on a chunk's end. `drain_body` or `discard_chunked_rest` then reads on under the read deadline as `body.Close` does, with `HttpHead::close` as Go's `Request.Close` (`shouldClose`). The two plain-text `400 request too large` answers are gone. | A valid request padded past the limit is served, and a truncated one gets -32700 inside a 200, as dcrd answers. Where Go reads the remainder, the close is now a FIN rather than a reset, and the answer waits on the remainder up to the read deadline, as dcrd's does. The handshake watchdog stands down before that discard, because at the deadline it shuts the socket both ways and would lose the answer. The per-read deadline still bounds the discard, except for a TLS record an authenticated client dribbles in. One timing-dependent case remains: Go's chunked reader parses the CRLF and the next size line in the same `Read` when they are already in its buffer, so a malformed line arriving with the last byte before the limit can still get dcrd's 400. The port always stops at the limit. Pinned by these tests in `review_rpc_listen`: `an_oversized_content_length_is_truncated_and_served`, `an_oversized_chunked_body_is_truncated_and_served`, `a_keep_alive_body_just_past_the_limit_is_read_to_its_end`, `a_chunk_ending_at_the_limit_is_served_whatever_follows` and `a_stalled_body_past_the_limit_is_answered_at_the_read_deadline`. Also pinned by `rpcrun::tests::request_close_is_gos_should_close`. |
| Websocket handshake carrying a body | gorilla inspects the hijacked reader and closes the connection outright on any byte that arrived alongside the handshake -- "client sent data before handshake is complete" (`gorilla/websocket@v1.5.1 server.go:186-191`) -- which dcrd surfaces only as a log line, never as a response (`rpcserver.go:6013-6019`) | refuses when the request *declared* a body in either framing, closing the same way and with the same silence | `read_http_head` takes the head one byte at a time and stops on the blank line, so nothing is ever buffered past it and there is no arrival to inspect -- the declared framing is the testable equivalent. It is stricter for a body declared but not yet sent (gorilla upgrades) and looser for bytes pipelined with no `Content-Length` (gorilla refuses), and identical for the only shape that can desync a front end: a `Content-Length` a proxy forwards as a request body while this server reads those bytes as the first RFC 6455 frames. Without the check the upgrade is answered 101 and the body is consumed as frame data. Pinned by `a_websocket_upgrade_declaring_a_body_is_refused`. |
| Request head size cap | `MaxHeaderBytes` is never set (`rpcserver.go:5921-5938`), so Go's default applies: heads up to `DefaultMaxHeaderBytes + 4096` -- a megabyte and change -- are served, and only past that is it `errTooLarge` -> 431 (`server.go:939`, `:960-962`, `:2067-2076`) | 431 with Go's exact wording, but at `MAX_HEAD_SIZE` = 8 KiB | the head is read byte at a time into a buffer held for the whole handshake, and a pre-authentication slot is held with it, so the cap is a live per-connection memory bound multiplied by the admission ceiling rather than a Go-runtime detail. A megabyte apiece across the hard budget is 64 MiB an unauthenticated client can pin. The status and the body are Go's. The threshold is not, so a head between 8 KiB and a megabyte -- a large `Authorization`, a long cookie jar, a proxy stacking `Via`/`Forwarded` chains -- is refused here and served by dcrd. Nor is the check's position in the order: the port reads the whole head, up to its blank line, before it parses a single line (`read_http_head`), where Go parses the request line and then each field line as it reads it (`request.go:1105-1118`, `textproto/reader.go:555-578`). A head that runs past 8 KiB is therefore a 431 here even when an earlier line -- a malformed request line, a field with no colon -- draws Go's 400 first, and that holds past Go's own megabyte too. Pinned by `connection_level_errors_are_go_shaped`. The 500-value cap is the next row's. |
| RPC mux surface | Go 1.22's routing tree behind `ServeMux`: wildcards, `{$}`, method and host patterns, the trailing-slash redirect, and 405 with `Allow` (`net/http/server.go`, `routing_tree.go`, `pattern.go`) | the same *observable* pipeline reduced to the two patterns dcrd registers (`rpcserver.go:5940`, `:5965`): the asterisk arm, `EscapedPath`, `cleanPath` over the escaped string, the canonicalizing 307, and a per-segment decoded comparison against `/ws` | the tree's other arms are unreachable with two literal, method-less, host-less patterns. The reduction is checked rather than assumed: `tests/data/muxroute.tsv` holds 3,836 targets and the answer a real `http.Server` carrying dcrd's exact registrations gave each one, and `routing_matches_go_over_the_generated_corpus` replays every row. **A third registered pattern invalidates the reduction.** Not reproduced, and unreachable from any HTTP client: a raw non-UTF-8 byte in the target, which the head parser refuses because the head is held as `&str`. The request target goes through a port of Go's `url.ParseRequestURI` (the same text in 1.26.2 and 1.27.1): the control-byte refusal, `getScheme`, the opaque form, and `parseAuthority`/`parseHost` with netip's IPv6 grammar and the http/https strict-colon rule. CONNECT's `http://`-prefixed authority form is ported too, and `Request.Host` is taken from an absolute-form target's host. `request_targets_parse_as_gos_request_uri` replays a second, probe-generated table of 155 targets together with the `URL.Host` each names. The head is read as `readRequestLimit` and textproto read it: lines end at a bare LF, as `bufio.ReadLine` ends them; a first field line that begins with a space or tab is refused, with a bare 400, or the 431 once it runs past the 80 bytes Go reads to quote it; a later line that begins with a space or tab continues the field before it (obs-fold), each line trimmed and the lines joined with one space; the colon must be on a field's first line, and the name must be non-empty; at most 500 values are read before the 431; and the checks Go makes once the header map is read follow in Go's order — a second Host, then the transfer encoding, the content length and `fixTrailer`'s trailer keys, then the version, then a missing Host (excused for the bare `PRI * HTTP/2.0` preface, per `isH2Upgrade`), a malformed Host and a space in a field name. Pinned by `field_lines_fold_as_textproto_folds_them`, `the_head_carries_at_most_gos_value_count` and `head_errors_rank_as_gos_do`; every row is a real server's answer. The corpus, those tables and the port all reflect Go 1.27, not the reference Go, 1.26.2 (ADR-0001's 2026-10-26 addendum on the reference Go). A dcrd built with Go 1.26, the reference and the other half of dcrd's CI matrix, answers differently in three places, each checked against a go1.26.2 server, and the last two against Go 1.26.5's source as well. The redirect: 1.26 builds `url.URL{Path: ...}` from the cleaned escaped path (`net/http/server.go:2695` in 1.26.2), whose `String` escapes it again, so `//%2f/ws` redirects to `/%252f/ws` and `//!/ws` to `/%21/ws`, where 1.27 and the port send `/%2f/ws` and `/!/ws`; replayed against go1.26.2, 1,634 of the corpus's 3,836 rows, all of them redirects, come out so. The value cap: 1.26 caps neither the head's nor the trailer's header values (`DefaultMaxHeaderValueCount` is new in 1.27: `api/go1.27.txt`, go.dev/issue/79936), and reads any number within its 1 MB header limit. The long leading-space line: 1.26 answers a leading-space first field line over 80 bytes with a bare 400, since mapping textproto's "message too large" to the 431 (`request.go:1152-1157`) is also new in 1.27. By that addendum's rule the port is to follow 1.26.2 in all three; it does not yet. |
| Connection reuse, and the `Connection` header that advertises it | keeps the connection alive after the five answers that carry no `Connection` header -- the mux's 307, its CONNECT-authority 404, the `OPTIONS *` 200, and the `/ws` 401 and gorilla handshake refusals -- and closes after every `/` answer, where the handler sets `Connection: close` itself (`rpcserver.go:5941`). The read deadline is per request and resets, so a reused connection lives indefinitely | one request per connection everywhere, so every reply names `Connection: close` and the socket is then closed | measured, not assumed. Reuse was designed and then declined: it converts a pre-authentication surface that costs an attacker a TCP (and TLS) handshake per reply into a bandwidth-limited reflector -- **6.2x amplification measured**, 30-byte requests to 186-byte replies, unlimited pipeline depth, no authentication -- and lets one unauthenticated client hold a connection open forever at roughly 4 B/s by resetting the per-request deadline (48 s observed, resettable without limit). dcrd absorbs both because a goroutine is cheap and `/ws` traffic is not counted by `numClients` at all (`rpcserver.go:5449-5465`); this port has a pre-auth slot budget precisely because it cannot make that trade, which is the same reasoning as the admission row above. Reuse would also raise slot-seconds for a `/ws` handshake refusal by about four orders of magnitude (a released slot at ~1 ms becomes one held for the full 10 s), leave the shutdown drain about 50 ms of margin against `HANDLER_DRAIN_GRACE`, and require `Connection: close` detection to reproduce the *union* of two different Go scanners (`httpguts.HeaderValuesContainsToken` over every copy for `r.Close`, and `net/http`'s `hasToken` over the first copy only). The header is downstream of the behaviour: omitting it while still closing would advertise a reuse that never happens and leave a client to meet the close as an unexpected EOF. Reachability is the other half of the trade -- browsers do not follow redirects on a `ws://` handshake and gorilla does not retry a 401 on the same connection, so no real client reaches the five reusable answers. `Date` *is* reproduced, on exactly the answers Go writes through its `ResponseWriter`. It is on none of the ones written straight to the socket: the connection loop's own answers, and the JSON-RPC reply dcrd writes by hand on the hijacked connection (`rpcserver.go:5862`). That reply also carries no `Content-Length` and runs to the close. Pinned by `the_date_header_follows_who_wrote_the_response`. |
| Websocket handshake refusals to an HTTP/1.0 or HEAD request | gorilla's refusals go through `http.Error` (`returnError`). The status line mirrors an HTTP/1.0 request's protocol (`server.go:1562`, `:1586-1600`), and a HEAD gets the `Content-Length` but no body (`server.go:377-384`, `:1382`) | `write_handshake_error` in `websocket.rs` answers `HTTP/1.1` and sends the body whatever the request | Every answer `rpcrun.rs` writes follows the request's method and protocol: the JSON-RPC 200, the 401, 503, 404 and 307, the asterisk answers, the chunked-body 400 and the 417 (pinned by `answers_follow_the_request_method_and_protocol` and `head_and_http_1_0_answers_are_shaped_as_gos`). The websocket module's own writer was outside that fix. A websocket client sends neither HTTP/1.0 nor HEAD, so no real client reaches the difference. |
| Wire payload allocation | `readMessageN` checks the global cap, magic, command, and the per-type maximum before `make([]byte, hdr.length)` | same order, via `wire::read_message_header` called before the buffer resize | Not a divergence but a *correction*: the port had been bounding the allocation by the 32 MiB global cap alone, letting a peer name 32 MiB in 24 bytes. `read_message` now delegates to the same function so the order stays single-sourced. |
| Websocket client registration | `defer` removes the client | RAII `ClientRegistration`; whole handler inside one `catch_unwind`, recovery replies with a null id | Go's `defer` runs on panic; a trailing statement does not. A panic on the request id previously re-panicked in the recovery arm and leaked the client slot permanently. |
| Unwired RPC seams | every method is implemented | The seam traits' defaults exist for the handler test doubles. Most of `RpcChain`'s answer a JSON-RPC internal error and three answer a neutral value where the signature cannot fail, but ten still panic with `unimplemented!` (`best_snapshot`, `best_header`, `is_current`, `locate_headers`, `main_chain_has_block`, `next_threshold_state`, `check_live_ticket`, `check_live_tickets`, `fetch_utxo_entry`, `tspend_count_votes`), as do defaults in most of the other seam traits, and `panic = "abort"` turns such a panic into a stopped node. The daemon's `NodeRpcChain` overrides every `RpcChain` method, each other daemon adapter overrides every panicking default of its trait, and `NodeRpcConnManager` overrides `broadcast_message`. Until the 2026-09-23 review, fourteen chain seams and the broadcast were left at their defaults: getstakedifficulty, getblocksubsidy at or below the tip, estimatestakediff, ticketvwap, getstakeversions, getstakeversioninfo, getvoteinfo, gettreasurybalance, ticketsforaddress, invalidateblock and reconsiderblock always failed; a mined tspend read as unknown to gettreasuryspendvotes; getchaintips answered `[]`; and `ping` and ws `rebroadcastwinners` sent nothing. The three mixing seams (`mix_pooler`, `accept_mix_message`, `relay_mix_messages`) were at their defaults too and now run over the shared mixpool (see the RPC handlers row). The profile server seam was at its default until 2026-10-23, so `startprofiler` answered its unwired-seam error; the daemon's `ProfileServer` now answers it (see the daemon row). getchaintips now sorts as dcrd's `sort.Reverse(nodeHeightSorter)` does: descending height, with ties broken by descending raw hash bytes. | A remote caller must never be able to unwind a handler thread, so no default that panics may be reachable from the daemon: every one is overridden. Pinned against the real adapter by `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/review_rpc_seams.rs` (including `equal_height_chain_tips_sort_by_descending_hash`) and `rpcrun::tests::broadcast_message_queues_to_every_peer_and_stamps_pings`, and the profile server seam through the daemon binary by `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/review_profiler_daemon.rs`. |
| Autogenerated secret files | `0600` file in a `0700` directory (`config.go` 471/506, `server.go` 3592) | same, applied at `open(2)`/`mkdir(2)` time rather than by a follow-up `chmod`, and a key that cannot be placed is fatal | Also a correction rather than a divergence: `fs::write` has no mode argument, so the port had been creating both the credential file and the TLS private key world-readable. |
| Per-peer stall detector | `stallHandler` goroutine fed by a `stallControl` channel | shared `Mutex<StallDetector>` ticked by the per-connection timer thread it shares with the keepalive ping (`run_peer_timers`) | The state is the point, so it lives behind its own mutex; the I/O loops never block on a control channel and dcrd's `select { stallControl <- msg; <-quit }` race disappears. Which peers get disconnected, and when, is dcrd's. The input thread reports each message to the detector as it takes it, whether it read the message itself or a `peer-reader` thread read it ahead (*Reading ahead of the block handler*), so a block waiting in that thread's queue settles nothing until its turn, like one still in the socket (`a_block_read_ahead_settles_its_deadline_only_when_it_is_handled`, `a_slow_handler_with_blocks_queued_does_not_stall_an_honest_peer`). |
| getdata ban score | `numNewReqs*99/wire.MaxInvPerMsg` in Go integer division, so batches of 505 items or fewer are free | identical, truncation included | An earlier revision carried the remainder so repeated 505-item batches were not free. Reverted: at 0.00198 points/item against dcrd's 60s half-life and threshold of 100, the equilibrium is ~583 items/s — and dcrd requests blocks in batches of at most `maxInFlightBlocks` (16), as this port does under `DCROXIDE_BLOCK_WINDOW=dcrd`, while by default this port's window requests at most its count in one getdata: 128 blocks, and never more than 505, which is 50,000 / 99 in integer division (*Block download window*). All of those truncate to zero, so dcrd charges an honestly syncing peer nothing while a carry charges the full rate. At ~1 KiB early-chain blocks that is ~0.6 MB/s of upload, i.e. ordinary IBD. The getdata path is bounded by the pending-batch/pending-item gates and the send pipeline instead. The requests of a departing peer that move to another peer known to hold the data go out in one getdata that is not held to the window's count, in dcrd (`internal/netsync/manager.go:917-939`) and here. Pinned by `the_getdata_ban_score_matches_dcrds_truncating_rate` and, for the largest window, `the_largest_block_window_costs_no_ban_score` (`crates/dcroxide-node/tests/srvgetdata_vectors.rs`). |
| Repeated items within one `getdata` | `handleServeGetData` walks `invVects` verbatim (`server.go:532`), so a hash listed N times is fetched N times, queued N times, and appears in the consolidated `notfound` N times; only the continuation inventory is emitted once, because `continueHash` is cleared after the first match. The dcrd-generated `dupcontinue` row in `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/data/srvservedata_vectors.txt` records exactly that: `block, inv, block`. | the serve worker (`GetDataWorker::serve_batch`) resolves and serves each `(type, hash)` at most once per batch. A repeat is still charged the same single `numPendingGetDataItemReqs` decrement it would have been charged had it been served, so the counter the intake gate reads stays bit-for-bit dcrd's; unknown inventory types are excluded from the dedupe, so dcrd's decrement-free `continue` for them is untouched. The pure `serve_get_data` fold in `server.rs` deliberately does **not** dedupe — it is the model checked against the dcrd oracle. | A 50,000-item `getdata` costs 1.8 MB on the wire; naming one hash 50,000 times makes dcrd return up to 50,000 x `MaxBlockPayload` (1.25 MB), a ~36,000x amplification, and dcrd's 12-block `recentBlocks` LRU (`internal/blockchain/chain.go:46`) is never populated by the serve path, so any older hash is a full database read and deserialize per copy. Pinned by `getdata_serves_repeated_items_once`. |
| Peer stall deadlines | `stallHandler` goroutine fed by a `stallControl` channel, `pendingDeadlines` holding `pendingData` per `wire.InvVect` plus `pendingCmds` per command | same two tables, behind a shared `Mutex<StallDetector>` ticked by the per-connection timer thread it shares with the keepalive ping (`run_peer_timers`) | Ported from master, replacing the `release-v2.1.5` command-keyed table where any single delivery settled every request a `getdata` was waiting on — a peer could answer one block just inside the timeout and keep all 16 sync slots pinned. Master's `maxBurst` refusal is ported too. Two shape differences: the mixing-message identity hash is computed by the thread that read the message (the connection's input thread, or its `peer-reader` thread where it has one) and passed in rather than cached on the wire type (dcrd caches it in `readMessage`; dcroxide keeps mix hashing in `dcroxide-mixing`), and the burst refusal is logged where it happens because ending the output loop shuts the socket down, so the connection's own teardown reason reports the resulting end of stream instead. A deadline is settled when the input thread takes the message that answers it, not when a `peer-reader` thread read that message ahead of it (*Reading ahead of the block handler*). |
| Block download window | at most `maxInFlightBlocks` (16) blocks requested from the sync peer. They are topped up to 16 at the start of the chain sync (`internal/netsync/manager.go:708`), at the end of every headers message once the headers are synced (`:1786`), and after a block accepted from the sync peer only when fewer than `minInFlightBlocks` (10) block requests remain in all, whichever peer they were asked of (`:31-38`, `:490-546`, `:1360-1372`), so through a sync the requests are 16 and then 7 each time nine are left. The blocks come from a list of the next 512 needed (`nextBlocksBuf`, `:372`), rebuilt when the best header changed or when one to nine remain and the last is not the best header (`:434-448`), and left as it is when a request is forgotten: a departing peer's requests move, whole and in one getdata, to another peer known to hold the data, or are dropped (`:876-894`, `:917-939`), and a notfound drops the request (`:1804-1829`). The window is never narrowed or widened, and nothing is logged about it | `SyncManager::new` keeps dcrd's window on dcrd's code paths (`BlockWindow::Dcrd`, `crates/dcroxide-netsync/src/manager.rs:127-134`, `manager.rs:1018`), and the dcrd-generated vectors run at it. The daemon's manager is built with a window budgeted in bytes in its place (`BlockWindow::Budget`, `manager.rs:221`; `new_sync_manager`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/sync.rs:667-692`). **Limits.** At most 128 blocks requested from the sync peer (`BUDGET_WINDOW_BLOCKS`; or the count `DCROXIDE_BLOCK_WINDOW` gives, from 16 to `MAX_BUDGET_WINDOW_BLOCKS`, which is 505, 50,000 / 99 in integer division, the largest getdata that costs no ban score) and, once 16 are requested from it, at most 3,145,728 bytes of them by the sizes their headers carry (`MAX_BUDGET_WINDOW_BYTES`, 3 MiB, eight maximum-size main network blocks). The bytes are eight times the network's smallest maximum block size and never more than that figure, which is what they come to on mainnet, testnet, simnet and regnet (`BlockWindow::budget`, `manager.rs:239`; `default_block_window`, `sync.rs:569`). dcrd's 16 are the floor: below 16 requested the bytes are not consulted, so a run of large blocks is requested as dcrd requests it. **Requests** (`fetch_next_blocks_budgeted`, `manager.rs:1445`). Blocks are taken from the front of the list of needed blocks, in order, while the blocks requested from the sync peer stay within the count and, once 16 are requested from it, their header sizes, the candidate's included, stay within the bytes (the budget itself may be reached). A block requested from the sync peer outside the window counts as one block of no bytes. A block whose header the chain cannot supply counts as larger than any budget, so it is requested only within the first 16. One getdata goes out, and only when fewer than 10 blocks are requested from the sync peer (its requests alone, not all requests as in dcrd) or at least 7 fit (dcrd's refill, 16 - 10 + 1); no getdata of the window is larger than the count in force. This is tried after every block accepted from the sync peer (`manager.rs:2346-2357`) and wherever dcrd fetches: at the start of the chain sync and at the end of every headers message once the headers are synced. **List** (`maybe_update_next_needed_blocks`, `manager.rs:1286`; `next_blocks_need_update`, `:300`). It is 512 plus twice the full count long (768 at 128, 544 at 16, 1,522 at 505), without the blocks still requested, each entry with its header size. It is rebuilt when the best header changed; when fewer than the full count remain, none included, unless it already ran to the best header; and after any block request is forgotten: its peer left, or the outbound queue refused the request, and no other peer is known to hold the block, or the manager was told notfound. Length and threshold go by the full count whether or not the window is narrowed. **Narrowing** (`on_peer_disconnected`, `manager.rs:1754`, at `:1781` and `:1860-1863`; `narrow_block_window`, `:1093`). When the sync peer leaves after the initial header sync is done, every block request attributed to it at that moment is counted, before its requests are moved or forgotten: the window's own, requests that moved to it when another peer left, blocks it announced by header once the chain was current (dcrd `internal/netsync/manager.go:1752-1777`) and those of `request_from_peer` (dcrd `RequestFromPeer`, `:2087-2121`). If that is more than 16 and the window in force is above 16 blocks, both limits are halved once, the number of narrowings goes up by one, the count toward a widening is discarded and the narrowed line below is logged; then the next sync peer is chosen and asked. After h halvings the count in force is max(floor(full count / 2^h), 16) and the bytes are floor(full bytes / 2^h): by default 128 and 3,145,728, then 64 and 1,572,864, 32 and 786,432, 16 and 393,216. A full count of 16 is never narrowed; 17 to 33 reach 16 in one halving, 34 to 67 in two, 68 to 135 in three, 136 to 271 in four and 272 to 505 in five. At 16 in force the bytes are no longer consulted and the requests are 16, then 7 each time nine are left, as under dcrd's window. It is still not dcrd's rule: the refill counts the sync peer's requests alone, the list keeps its length and its rebuild rule, and a forgotten request rebuilds it. As in dcrd, each request of the departing peer that another peer is known to hold moves to that peer (here the lowest id that holds it), whole, in one getdata that is not held to the window. The rest are forgotten, the list is rebuilt, and the next sync peer is asked for the lowest missing blocks first, within the halved limits and counting what moved to it. Nothing is narrowed by: a peer that is not the sync peer leaving; a sync peer leaving with 16 or fewer requested from it, or leaving while the count in force is already 16, neither of which is counted as a narrowing; a departure before the initial header sync is done; a request the outbound queue refused, even when that hands the chain sync to another candidate; a notfound the manager is told about, after which the block is asked for again at the next fetch; or anything under dcrd's window. The manager is not told why a peer left, so these do narrow: a sync peer that left for a reason of its own; one the server banned, for one over a notfound for six or more blocks (20 a block against the default ban threshold of 100), which the server, like dcrd's (`server.go:1890-1896`), then does not pass on to the manager; one with more than 16 requested none of which the window made, so a node that is current can log a narrowing with no window in flight; and each of several sync peers lost one after another with nothing delivered in between, as when the node's own link drops, which takes 128 to 16 in one incident and counts three narrowings. **Widening** (`maybe_widen_block_window`, `manager.rs:1124`). One halving is undone at a time, once one sync peer has had as many blocks accepted since the window last changed as 16 (`WINDOW_RECOVERY_WINDOWS`) times the full count times the number of narrowings since the window was selected: by default 2,048 after the first narrowing, 4,096 with two counted, 6,144 with three. A widening does not reduce the number of narrowings. A block accepted from the sync peer counts, on the main chain or a side chain, whichever path asked for it. A rejected block, a duplicate block, a block from another peer and another peer leaving neither count nor restart the count. The count belongs to one connection and starts again with another sync peer, after a narrowing and after a widening. The block that completes it widens the window before the request it triggers. At full width nothing is counted. **Lifetime.** The halvings in force, the number of narrowings and the count are held in memory by the manager, and only selecting a window puts them back, which the daemon does at startup alone. So the number of narrowings never goes down while the process runs, the end of the initial chain sync changes nothing, and a restart begins at full width with none. Once the chain is current a block is asked of whichever peer announces it, so only the blocks the sync peer was first to announce count toward a widening: at the main network's target of 288 blocks a day, 2,048 blocks for each narrowing counted are at least 7.1 days of them. A window still narrowed when a sync ends therefore stays so for at least that long, unless the daemon is restarted or syncs that many blocks from one sync peer again. **Selection** (`DCROXIDE_BLOCK_WINDOW`, read once at startup; `parse_block_window`, `sync.rs:589`; `apply_block_window_setting`, `:629`; `with_block_window_setting`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/bin/dcroxide.rs:1989`). Unset: the budgeted window at 128 blocks and 3,145,728 bytes, and nothing is logged. `dcrd`, in any letter case: dcrd's window exactly. A whole number written in digits only, from 16 to 505: the budgeted window with that full count and the same bytes, for measurement; 16 is not dcrd's rule, and 128 is the default but is logged. Surrounding whitespace is ignored in both. Anything else, the empty string and a value that is not valid Unicode included: a warning, and the default is kept. **Log lines**, verbatim. `SYNC`, INF: `Block download window narrowed to at most N blocks in flight: the sync peer left with M requested` (N the count in force after the halving, M the count that narrowed it) and `Block download window widened to at most N blocks in flight after K blocks from the sync peer` (N the count in force after the widening, K the blocks it took, printed without separators, for example 2048). These two lines are this port's own: dcrd logs neither. `DCRD`, INF, at startup: `Block download window set to dcrd's 16 blocks in flight by DCROXIDE_BLOCK_WINDOW` and `Block download window set to at most N blocks in flight by DCROXIDE_BLOCK_WINDOW` (N the full count: this line never reports a narrowed width, which only the two `SYNC` lines do). `DCRD`, WRN, at startup: `DCROXIDE_BLOCK_WINDOW=V is neither dcrd nor a whole number of blocks from 16 to 505; using the default` (V the value without its surrounding whitespace). Unchanged from dcrd: the header sync, the announce path once the chain is current, `request_from_peer`, the stall detector and the serving side | Under dcrd's window the round trip to the sync peer, not the machine, sets the pace of a sync over a link with a round trip of tens of milliseconds. Measured on m3 on 2026-10-11 through a relay that delays every chunk, in fixed-time runs on a machine that was not quiet, one run each: dcroxide `7d31abb` moved 12.2, 13.0 and 13.6 blocks per round trip at round trips of 20, 50 and 150 ms, and dcrd v2.1.6 12.6 at 50 ms. **Measured with this window**, on m3 on 2026-10-12, one binary and one run per arm. Through the same relay for 300 s from the start of the process, on a quiet machine with the node on four cores: at a 50 ms round trip the node reached block 68,031 under `DCROXIDE_BLOCK_WINDOW=dcrd` and block 532,079 under the default; at 150 ms, 18,445 and 158,440. No arm logged a narrowing, a widening or a stalled peer. The relay delays and does not limit bandwidth. In five outbound-only starts of 300 s against the public main network, on a machine that was not quiet and with the peers and links the network offered at the time: under dcrd's rule the node reached block 24,455 180.7 s after its first block arrived and block 41,191 after 220.3 s; under the default, block 188,469 after 215.7 s and block 93,095 after 161.8 s; a third start under the default fetched no block, its header sync being about 68% done when the 300 s ended. None logged a narrowing, a stalled peer or a ban. Over loopback, on a quiet machine, one default sync to block 1,116,035 each: 612.4 s with the window against 607.7 s for the code before it (`3a367e5`), 4.7 s or 0.8% more, and one run each does not settle whether the window costs anything over loopback. **Cost**, read from the code and not measured: two deadlines that a wide window runs into far more often than dcrd's does. (1) The node's own request deadline. A requested block is given 30 s from the moment its getdata is written, checked every 15 s with the node's own handling credited for the latest check only (dcrd `peer/peer.go:1106-1123`, `:1260-1279`, and the port's stall detector, which this window leaves as it was), and a block settles its deadline only when the peer's input thread takes it, which is once every block ahead of it has been processed, however early a `peer-reader` thread read it off the socket (*Reading ahead of the block handler*). So the last block of a window has to be taken 30 to 45 s after it was asked for. A path that carries less than 70 to 105 kB/s (3,145,728 bytes in that time; the sync peer's uplink counts as much as the node's own link), or a host that processes fewer than 3 to 4 blocks a second (128 blocks in that time), has its sync peer dropped as stalled while the peer is still delivering. (2) The serving peer's write deadline: 20 s plus one for each 256 KiB of the message, in dcrd since `62fd529a` (`peer/peer.go:86-96`, `:1032-1040`) and in this port (*Peer socket writes*). A node that does not read its sync peer's socket for that long, through a long cache flush for one, while more of the window is unsent than the socket buffers of the two ends hold, is disconnected by such a peer, and neither end logs a stall. How often a window is that heavy, as counts over the header sizes of main network heights 1 to 1,116,035, taking the window that starts at each height: more than 512 KiB is requested (512 KiB standing in for the socket buffers, which the host decides) at 98.05% of heights at full width (128 blocks and 3,145,728 bytes), 82.75% after one halving, 51.55% after two and 5.26% at 16, which is also the figure under dcrd's window; more than 2 MiB at 52.60%, 0.09%, 0.09% and 0.09%. So one halving takes the rate the first deadline needs down to 35 to 52 kB/s and leaves the exposure to a pause in reading almost whole, and only the third reaches dcrd's. **What bounds it**, also read from the code. Each loss of a sync peer with more than 16 requested halves the window, so three losses reach dcrd's 16 from the default and five from 505, and up to a window of blocks delivered but not yet handled (unread in the socket, or read ahead and still queued) is discarded with the connection each time and requested again, where dcrd loses at most sixteen. After that the node loses one more sync peer each time a wider window is tried and fails, and the tries grow further apart. The floor is dcrd's window. Every departure of a sync peer with more than 16 requested narrows, whether or not a block arrived since the last one, so a node whose own link drops during a sync, or that loses three sync peers in a row with a window requested, is at 16 with three narrowings counted, and each step back then takes 16 x 128 x 3 = 6,144 blocks from one sync peer, 18,432 for the three (from 505: five narrowings, 40,400 a step, 202,000 in all). A model's figures, not measurements (arithmetic over header sizes up to height 1,116,035 for a constant-rate link with no processing time; no sync has been run at such a rate): the sync peers lost over the whole chain to a 30 s deadline, under dcrd's window / this rule / the budget with no narrowing, come to 4,122 / 4,129 / 33,497 at 20 kB/s, 1,461 / 1,467 / 25,168 at 26 kB/s, 220 / 234 / 11,293 at 50 kB/s, 122 / 134 / 6,967 at 70 kB/s, 57 / 56 / 57 at 105 kB/s and 26 / 29 / 28 at 150 kB/s. **Memory.** Requested blocks that have arrived wait in the kernel socket buffers of the two ends, as before, except for up to sixteen frames of a connection that is read ahead, which wait decoded in that connection's queue (*Reading ahead of the block handler*): from every other connection the node reads one message at a time. The window requests at most the larger of 3,145,728 bytes and sixteen blocks, by header size, from the sync peer, so the main network's worst case is unchanged at 5,876,343 bytes (heights 1,025,094 to 1,025,109), and by the same counts the mean rises from 16 blocks and 275,104 bytes to 124.5 blocks and 2,065,658 bytes. Requests that moved to a peer are on top of that and are not held to the window. **The way back** is `DCROXIDE_BLOCK_WINDOW=dcrd`: dcrd's window is never narrowed or widened. It is a variable and not a flag because the generated `-h` output is pinned byte for byte against dcrd's. **Pinned by** `crates/dcroxide-netsync/tests/block_window.rs` (55 tests of the sync manager under both windows, among them a whole sync of 2,000 blocks held inside the window, four tests of whole syncs through sync-peer departures, at full counts of 17, 128 (two of the four) and 505, that hold every request, narrowing and widening to the rule, and tests that hold dcrd's window to dcrd's rule where the two differ); the unit tests `budget_is_half_of_sixteen_maximum_blocks_and_the_count_is_clamped`, `a_budgeted_window_is_halved_down_to_dcrds_count`, `the_needed_blocks_list_is_rebuilt_before_a_window_can_run_dry` and `request_map_sizes_follow_their_entries` in `manager.rs`; `the_default_block_window_is_budgeted_on_every_network`, `block_window_settings_parse`, `the_daemons_sync_manager_has_the_default_block_window` and `a_block_window_setting_is_applied_to_the_manager` in `crates/dcroxide-node/src/sync.rs`; `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/block_window_env.rs` (four tests: the daemon reads the variable and logs each startup line, and says nothing when it is unset); `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/block_window_sync.rs` (one test, on Linux: two daemons each sync 80 simnet blocks over loopback from a third, one through each window, which is a single request under the default window, so no refill, list rebuild or narrowing happens between daemons); and `the_largest_block_window_costs_no_ban_score` in `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/srvgetdata_vectors.rs`. The dcrd-generated netsync vectors are unchanged and run at dcrd's window. **Not verified.** No sync has been run under this window over a link held to a set rate or with a reader made to stop for 20 s or more, so neither of the two cases in which it costs sync peers has been measured: the public-network starts above ran over whatever links their peers had, at rates that were not recorded, and the longest flush of the loopback sync above took 3.5 s. No whole sync has been run over a delayed link, or over a real one with a longer round trip than a LAN's: the only whole syncs over a real link, on m3 on 2026-10-18 and 2026-10-19 at the default window, took their blocks from a dcrd v2.1.7 node on another machine on the same LAN (docs/bench-ledger.md, "Rungs from a block server on the LAN, two changes held for their memory, and dcrd v2.1.7" and "One commit thread for the life of the store, a sync that checks every script, and dcrd v2.1.7"). No recorded run of the daemon has narrowed or widened its window: both are exercised at the level of the sync manager only. The figures for slow links are a model's. |
| Reading ahead of the block handler | `inHandler` is one loop per connection: it tests its disconnect flag, reads one message, reports it to the stall handler, runs the message's listener, and reads the next message only when the listener has returned (`peer/peer.go:1535-1598`: the flag at `:1551`, the read at `:1555`, the listener through `processInboundMessage` at `:1583`). Each read arms a fresh deadline of the idle timeout as it begins (`readMessage`, `:963-964`), adds its bytes to the peer's counter (`:970`), hashes a mixing message (`:972-978`) and passes the count to `OnRead` (`:980-982`), which bans for a malformed message and adds the bytes to the server's totals (`server.go:1851-1860`); `lastRecv` is stamped as the read returns (`peer/peer.go:1571`). For a block the listener is `OnBlock`, which wraps the block, computing its hash and keeping the payload it was read in (`NewBlockFromBlockAndBytes`, `server.go:1359`, `dcrutil/block.go:386-392`), and returns only once the sync manager has had the chain process it. Its comment says the further receives are held back on purpose until the block is known good or bad, so that a malicious peer cannot queue up bad blocks and waste memory (`server.go:1356-1371`, the comment at `:1365-1369`; the comment on `SyncManager.OnBlock`, `internal/netsync/manager.go:1211-1213`, says the same of further reads). A block's transaction hashes are made lazily, when the chain first asks for its transactions (`Block.Transactions` and `STransactions`, `dcrutil/block.go:162-220`, through `NewTx`, `dcrutil/tx.go:94-101`), its two merkle trees are built inside the merkle check (`checkMerkleRoots`, `internal/blockchain/validate.go:1909-1952`), its signature operations are counted inside the contextual check (`checkBlockContext`, `internal/blockchain/validate.go:2378-2408`), and the context-free checks of its data are made as the chain processes it, once the header's commitment to the data has been checked and before the data is accepted (`checkBlockDataSanity`, `internal/blockchain/process.go:501-515`, `validate.go:878-965`). `StoreBlock` writes the payload the block was wrapped with (`ffldb/db.go:1175`, `dcrutil/block.go:54-58`), and `writeBlock` takes the record's checksum as it writes it at the commit (`blockio.go:466-489`). No connection is read ahead of its handler | On by default, on one kind of connection. **Where and when** (`ServerPeerHandler::handle_block`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/dispatch.rs:2741`, at `:2760-2768`; `run_input_loop`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/peerloop.rs:392`, at `:557-559`). After every block the input loop has handled and kept the connection for, and at no other time, it asks the server whether the frames behind that block should be read ahead (`ServeHooks::read_ahead_wanted`, `peerloop.rs:276`, whose default is no). The daemon's server answers yes when the setting is on, the peer is the sync manager's current sync peer and the initial chain sync is not done, all read under the sync-manager lock once that block has been processed. On the first yes with the connection's teardown flag down, the input thread hands the connection's read transport to a new thread named `peer-reader` (`ReadAheadSource::block_handled` and `start_reader`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/readahead.rs:951`, `:802`), and that thread reads the socket until the connection ends. So the first block of a connection is always read by its input thread; the block that starts the thread need not be one the chain accepted, only one the peer was not disconnected over; and a connection that is never the sync peer during the initial chain sync never has the thread. A peer that is not the sync peer can deliver blocks too, ones it announced in its initial state or requests moved to it when another peer left, and its connection is read as dcrd reads every connection. **The thread** (`reader_loop`, `readahead.rs:642`). It reads a frame, checks its checksum and decodes it (`WireTransport::read_message_paced`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/transport.rs:832`, which is also what the input thread's own read runs), hashes a mixing message as the input thread's read does, and for a block computes the block hash, makes the one prefix pass and one witness pass over its transactions that the chain otherwise makes, builds the block's two transaction merkle trees from those hashes, keeping their roots, where dcrd builds the trees inside its merkle check, and counts the block's signature operations once for each answer the treasury agenda can give, keeping the two sums, where dcrd counts inside its contextual check (`HashedBlock::new`, `crates/dcroxide-blockchain/src/blockhashes.rs:425`; `BlockMerkleRoots`, `:277`; `BlockSigOps`, `:302`). It then takes the frame the read kept for the block (`WireTransport::take_block_frame`, `transport.rs:469`: a read made for a reader thread reserves four bytes behind the payload and keeps its buffer when it decoded a block), serializes the block once and compares the payload with the serialization, and when the two are equal lays the payload out as the block's block-file record in the frame's own buffer: the network and the block's length over the last eight bytes of the message header, the CRC-32C appended, and one more BLAKE-256 of the 180 header bytes, which the store checks the hash it is handed against (`HashedBlock::keep_wire_bytes`, `blockhashes.rs:527`; `BlockRecord::lay_out`, `crates/dcroxide-database/src/blockfile.rs:186`). The payload is not copied. A payload that does not compare equal is dropped and the block goes on without a record: nothing is logged and nothing is decided. The comparison is made in every build, so what reaches the block files is the block's serialization whatever frame a block was paired with. It then queues the frame for the input thread. A failed read is the last thing it queues, and it reads nothing after one. It is given the transport, the queue, the process's signature pre-verifier, if there is one, and the two values of the chain parameters the data sanity checks read, if the server gave them, and nothing else: it takes no lock but the queue's and, as it hands a block to the pre-verifier before queueing it while reading ahead is wanted (*Signature pre-verification ahead of the block thread*), the script pool's, never the two at once; it calls no hook, logs nothing, sends nothing to the peer and acts on nothing it reads. No merkle root is compared with a header on it: the choice of commitment the agenda makes, the DCP0005 combination and both comparisons stay on the input thread (`check_merkle_roots_with_leaves`, `crates/dcroxide-blockchain/src/validate.rs:2327`). No sum of signature operations is compared with the limit on it either: the treasury agenda's answer, the comparison with the limit and, where the sum is over it, the count that gives the error and its text stay on the input thread, in the contextual check made as the block is accepted (`check_block_sig_op_limit`, `validate.rs:4140`). One rule is applied on it, and its verdict goes with the block unread: the context-free checks of the block's data, dcrd's `checkBlockDataSanity`, made with the two values of the chain parameters they read, `MinimumStakeDiff` and `MaxTxSize` (`DataSanityParams`, `validate.rs:715`). The server gives the connection those values once, as its message source is made (`ServeHooks::data_sanity_params`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/peerloop.rs:290`, whose default is none; the daemon's `ServerPeerHandler::data_sanity_params`, `dispatch.rs:2807`, answers with the network's), and the thread keeps each block's verdict with them (`HashedBlock::check_data_sanity`, `blockhashes.rs:462`). The chain returns that verdict at the point where it makes the checks of a block that arrives without one, after the duplicate, known-invalid, header and merkle checks and before the data is accepted, with the same error, kind and text, and marks the block for a failure where the data commitment is proven, as the checks' own failure does; it reads the verdict only where the two values equal those of the parameters it processes the block with, and makes the checks itself otherwise (`check_block_data_sanity_with_verdict`, `validate.rs:762`, called at `process.rs:5418`). Under debug assertions it makes the checks again where it reads a verdict and compares. **Early reads** (`Shared::may_read`, `readahead.rs:381`). The thread starts a frame before the input thread is waiting for it only when three things hold: the frame it read last was a block (or it has just been started, behind the block that started it), the server's latest answer was yes, and fewer than sixteen frames (`READ_AHEAD_MAX_ITEMS`, `:273`) have been read and not yet taken by the input thread, the one being read included. Otherwise it starts a frame only once the input thread is waiting with nothing queued, which is when `inHandler` begins its read. So the queue holds at most sixteen frames; at most one of them is not a block, and that one is the last; and nothing behind a frame that is not a block is read until the input thread has handled that frame and waits. **The thread stays.** When the answer becomes no, because the initial chain sync is done or the sync manager has taken another sync peer, the thread is not stopped. Until the connection ends it reads each frame only once the input thread waits for it, at two thread wake-ups a message, and it goes ahead again if the answer becomes yes again. Every block it reads, ahead of the input thread or in turn, is hashed, given its merkle trees, counted, checked for its data's sanity where the server gave the values, and laid out as its record on it, also after the initial sync on a connection that keeps its thread. **The input thread.** One loop body serves both sources of messages (`MsgSource`, `readahead.rs:186`): the input thread's own read, made when it is ready for the message, and the reader thread's queue. It takes one item at a time, in the order the frames ended on the wire, asks for the next only when it has handled the one before, and handles each with the code that handled it before this change: the stall detector is told and the handler-active window opened (`peerloop.rs:477-490`), the frame's bytes and the time go to the peer's `bytesrecv` and `lastrecv` (`:506-508`), any protocol-level reply is queued and the server's hook runs. A block from the reader thread arrives with its hashes, its merkle roots, its counts of signature operations, the verdict of its data sanity checks where they were made and, when its payload was kept, its record (`Received::Block`, `readahead.rs:168`) and gets what a `block` message gets, with the block hash it carries in place of one computed there. The server passes it to the sync manager as `ArrivedBlock::Hashed` (`crates/dcroxide-node/src/sync.rs:66`), which takes the block hash it carries for its own (`SyncChain::block_hash`; the `internal/netsync` row), and the chain takes it through `Chain::process_hashed_block` (`crates/dcroxide-blockchain/src/process.rs:5266`), which makes the checks of `Chain::process_block` in the same order and reads the block hash, the transaction hashes and the roots where that computes them, and the verdict of the data sanity checks where that makes them (*Each transaction hash goes through a heap buffer* under Known remaining gaps; the `internal/blockchain` row, *The block hash*). It stores a block that carries a record through `Transaction::store_block_record`, and the commit writes the record as it stands (`BlockStore::write_block_record`; the `database` row), the same bytes at the same place; a block without one is serialized as it is stored, and its record is built and checksummed at the commit.
