What still differs from dcrd there: the payload is kept only after the comparison, where dcrd keeps it unconditionally; a block a connection's own input thread reads is still serialized and checksummed on the block thread; the checksum is taken before the block is judged, where `writeBlock` takes it at the commit; and the payload is dropped once the block is handled, where dcrd holds it for as long as the `dcrutil.Block` lives. A failed read is acted on only after every frame before it. The connection's teardown flag is tested before each item is taken (`Shared::next`, `readahead.rs:506`), as `inHandler` tests its flag before each read. **The idle budget** (`BudgetStart`, `transport.rs:485`; `Shared::waiting_since`, `readahead.rs:490`). A frame the reader thread reads times out one idle timeout after the moment the input thread began waiting for that frame with nothing left to take, which is when `inHandler` would have armed its deadline. The budget does not run while the input thread is busy or has an earlier frame to take, so a frame begun behind a block that is still queued gets a budget of its own when the input thread comes back for it, as each `readMessage` arms a fresh deadline. A read the input thread makes itself keeps its one fixed deadline. **Not changed.** Which messages are accepted, rejected, answered, banned for or logged, and in what order. What the stall detector is told: it hears of a message when the input thread takes it and never when it was read, so its handler-active window covers a block waiting in the queue exactly as it covers one still in the socket. When the peer's `bytesrecv` and `lastrecv` move, which is as a message is taken. Every result of the chain. The bytes in the block files and their checksums, the block index rows, the write cursor and its rollover, the store's error texts and everything read back. **Observable.** (a) Up to sixteen frames leave the socket before the frames ahead of them have been handled. (b) `getnettotals` counts a frame's bytes when the reader thread reads it, and the peer's `bytesrecv` and `lastrecv` count it when the input thread takes it. While frames are queued the total leads that peer's counter by up to sixteen frames, and frames still queued when the connection ends are in the total and never in the peer's counter. A failed read's bytes are in the total only, as before. (c) Up to sixteen frames from a sync peer that then misbehaves are checksummed, decoded and, for blocks, hashed, given their two merkle trees, counted twice for their signature operations, checked for their data's sanity, serialized once, compared with their payload and laid out as block-file records (one CRC-32C over the payload and eight bytes, and a second BLAKE-256 of the header) before the node acts on the earlier one, and the blocks among them above the script floor have their signatures of the guessed shape checked on the script pool's helpers until the handler takes them (*Signature pre-verification ahead of the block thread*): work that dcrd's `OnBlock` holds back. (d) Every block the reader thread reads gets that work before the sync manager has checked that it was requested and before the chain's duplicate, known-invalid and header checks, where a block the input thread reads is hashed after them, has its trees built inside the merkle check, has its signature operations counted inside the contextual check and is serialized and checksummed as it is stored. A block refused by one of those had that work done for nothing and is refused exactly as before. A block whose regular root fails the original commitment has had its stake tree built too, where dcrd, and the chain for a block that arrives without hashes, stop at the regular root and never build it; the verdict and its text are the same. A block whose count of signature operations is over the limit under the treasury agenda's answer is counted on the reader thread and again on the input thread, which gives the error and its text, where dcrd counts it once; the verdict and its text are the same, and so is the transaction at which the count stops. A block the reader thread checked for its data's sanity had the checks made before the sync manager checked that it was requested and before the chain's duplicate, known-invalid, header and merkle checks, where dcrd makes them after those; a block refused by one of those had them made for nothing, is refused exactly as before, and its verdict is not read. A block that fails them is refused at the same point, with the same error, kind and text, and marked as before. (e) Once the connection's teardown flag is up, nothing queued is handled, banned for or logged: a malformed frame read behind a message that got the peer disconnected earns no ban, as when it was still unread in the socket. (f) When another thread tears the connection down while the input thread is parked on an empty queue, the connection's internal reason text is `the connection was torn down locally`, where an inline read whose receive the shutdown ended reports `failed to fill whole buffer`. Neither is logged, and only the reasons of a failed handshake are read. (g) The connection has one more thread beside its input, output and timer threads, and one more descriptor, a duplicate of the socket handle that the input thread keeps to end the connection with. (h) The setting and the log lines that follow. **Selection** (`DCROXIDE_READ_AHEAD`, read once at startup; `read_ahead_setting`, `readahead.rs:981` and `crates/dcroxide-node/src/bin/dcroxide.rs:2024`). Unset: on, and nothing is logged. `off`, in any letter case, or `0`: off, and every connection is read by its input thread alone, as dcrd reads, from the same binary. Surrounding whitespace is ignored. Anything else, `on`, `1`, the empty string and a value that is not valid Unicode included: a warning, and it stays on. **Log lines**, verbatim, none of which dcrd has. `DCRD`, INF, at startup: `Read-ahead of blocks disabled by DCROXIDE_READ_AHEAD`. `DCRD`, WRN, at startup: `DCROXIDE_READ_AHEAD=V is neither off nor 0; using the default` (V the value without its surrounding whitespace, with U+FFFD for what is not valid Unicode). `PEER`, DBG, as a reader thread starts: `Reading ahead of the block handler for peer P` (P the peer's address and direction, as the other peer lines name it). `PEER`, WRN, when the OS refuses the thread: `Unable to start a read-ahead thread for peer P: E -- reading on the input thread`. `PEER`, DBG, when the socket handle cannot be duplicated for it: `Unable to share the connection of peer P with a read-ahead thread: E -- reading on the input thread`. After either of those two the connection goes on being read by its input thread and the thread is not asked for again. `SRVR`, INF, as the daemon shuts down, just before `Server shutdown complete`, or before the pre-verification's line when there is one (*Signature pre-verification ahead of the block thread*), and only when a reader thread has made at least one read, a failed one included (`shutdown_line`, `readahead.rs:1099`): `Read-ahead: N blocks (B bytes) on C connections; checksum and decode X s and hashing Y s done off the block thread; the reader waited G s for the block thread; the block thread waited W s for the reader; at most Q items queued; index queue full for I s; block-file records laid out for R blocks in Z s, K written as laid out`. N and B are the blocks the reader threads read, handed over or not, and their wire bytes; C the connections that started a reader thread; X and Y sums over those threads, the socket reads not being timed; G the time those threads were parked with the queue full while reading ahead was wanted; W the time input threads waited on an empty queue for what proved to be a block while reading ahead was wanted; Q the most frames any one queue held; and I the time the threads that hand blocks to the index thread waited for room in its queue (`notify_blocked_nanos`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/indexes.rs:333`), which is summed whether or not any connection is read ahead and printed in this line only; R the blocks the reader threads handed over with their payload laid out as a record, which is below N only for payloads that were not kept, which is not expected; Z the time those threads spent serializing those blocks, comparing the payloads with the serializations and taking the records' checksums, dropped payloads included; and K the records the block database wrote as they were handed to it (`Database::records_written_as_laid_out`), below R by the blocks the chain did not store, and 0 beside an R above 0 when the database laid every record out again, as it does for a record laid out for another network. Y times `HashedBlock::new` and `HashedBlock::check_data_sanity`, so it includes the merkle trees, the two counts of signature operations and the data sanity checks. The line no longer ends with the index figure. The times are in seconds, to the millisecond | During the initial chain sync the input thread of the sync peer's connection is the thread that connects blocks, and between two blocks it also read the next one off the socket, checked its checksum, decoded it, hashed its transactions, built its merkle trees, counted its signature operations, made its data sanity checks, and serialized it and checksummed its record to store it. dcrd does all of that on one goroutine and holds further reads back on purpose. The port moves that work to a thread of its own, bounded at sixteen frames on one kind of connection, and one verdict with it, that of the data sanity checks, which the chain on the input thread returns where it would have reached it; `DCROXIDE_READ_AHEAD=off` is the way back to dcrd's reading. It is a variable and not a flag because the generated `-h` output is pinned byte for byte against dcrd's. **Measured** on m3 on 2026-10-12, on a quiet machine with the node held to eight of the machine's cores (16 threads), with one binary under both settings: default mainnet syncs to block 1,116,035 from a dcrd v2.1.6 server over loopback, in the order off, on, off, on, with other runs between the two pairs. With `DCROXIDE_READ_AHEAD=off`: 597.6 s and 599.0 s. With read-ahead on: 494.6 s and 490.8 s, which is 103.0 s and 108.2 s less, 17.2% and 18.1%. The shutdown line of the two runs with it on reported 1,116,034 blocks, 19,215,875,473 bytes and 1 connection; checksum and decode 45.829 s and 45.886 s; hashing 47.209 s and 47.246 s; the reader waited 382.350 s and 378.719 s for the block thread, and the block thread 0.271 s and 0.273 s for the reader; at most 4 items queued; index queue full for 3.191 s and 3.265 s. Peak resident memory was 2,967 and 2,921 MiB with it on against 3,273 and 3,256 MiB with it off, and the node's user CPU time 1,028.3 s and 1,027.6 s against 1,045.2 s and 1,042.5 s; neither difference is explained. The timed work that left the block thread, 93.0 s and 93.1 s, is less than what the syncs gained: the socket reads moved too and are not timed, and what the rest is was not established. **Those four runs are of the build made before the review of 2026-10-12 and its fixes.** The fixes changed production code in two places: one condition in `Shared::waiting_since`, the idle budget of a frame read behind a block that is still queued, and `BlockMerkleLeaves::compute` calling a private `hash_block` (`blockhashes.rs:842-851`; the same result). That day the code as committed had not been run in a sync. The runs with it off are of a build that also holds the block hand-off of the commit before this one (`Chain::process_shared_block`); the commit before that took 612.4 s in one run that day (*Block download window*). No sync was run of the hand-off alone, and the 14.8 s and 13.4 s between that run and the two with read-ahead off are not attributed. Those figures of 2026-10-12 are of builds without the merkle trees and the record on the reader thread. **Measured again on 2026-10-13**, in another session, in which the block server read 15.7 to 18.0 GB from the drive in every run, where it read none in the four runs above, so the times are not to be set beside theirs: on m3, in default mainnet syncs to block 1,116,035 from a dcrd on the same machine, a series of builds added one change or group of changes at a time. The step that added the record took 446.5 s against 458.1 s, one run each, 10.8 s less with the flushes' commit seconds taken out and 9.3 s less by the node's clock less its flush time (good to 3.5 to 6 s either way), and its shutdown line reported records laid out for 1,116,034 blocks in 7.078 s and 1,116,034 written as laid out; the reader thread's user time rose by 7.0 s and the block thread's CPU time outside its flushes fell by 9.2 s, so that work moved to the reader rather than going away. The next step added the merkle trees and the carried block hash together: 441.9 s and 442.2 s against that 446.5 s, 7.1 s less with the commit seconds taken out and 8.3 s less by the node's clock less its flush time (good to 3 to 5 s either way), with the hashing figure at 52.536 s and 52.562 s against 47.369 s, the reader's user time 5.1 s higher and the block thread's CPU time outside its flushes 8.3 s lower. The reader, fed by a dcrd on the same machine, waited 332 s for the block thread in the first of those runs and 322 s in each of the other two, and the block thread waited 0.82 s for the reader with the record and 1.04 s and 1.01 s with the trees as well, against 0.54 s before either. The harness's hash of the block files, the first 64 bits of one SHA-256 over their names and bytes, was the same in all 27 syncs of that session, dcrd's own included. In that session the read-ahead as `b4ceb1c` committed it ran inside `4700cf1`, in three default syncs and one with `--noexistsaddrindex`, without the record and the trees; every build that held those also held a developer variable this tree does not have, and none held the threads kept for a connected block's scripts (*Script checks of a connected block*). The read-ahead with the record and the trees was first run in a sync as committed inside `8fc9160`, four times on 2026-10-16 at a median of 430.7 s, where its own step was not measured apart; `88c46c3` was run so on 2026-10-17, four times at the defaults at a median of 396.25 s, the block thread waiting 0.84 to 0.86 s in all for the reader and the reader 275 to 278 s for the block thread in each, and its own step was again not measured apart (docs/bench-ledger.md). The counts of signature operations were first run in syncs on 2026-10-18, on m3, from a dcrd v2.1.7 node on another machine on the same LAN, in builds that were states of a worktree and not the tree of a commit, in a series not comparable with the ledger's loopback figures. In the build that added them, together with the change that writes GCS filter bits a word at a time, which runs nothing on the reader, the reader's time on a processor rose 3.9 s against the build below it and the shutdown line's hashing figure from 52.52 to 52.94 s in the builds below it to 56.70 and 57.00 s, and the block thread's time fell 8.9 s for the two changes together (docs/bench-ledger.md, "Rungs from a block server on the LAN, two changes held for their memory, and dcrd v2.1.7", build B). The data sanity checks were run on the reader in syncs that afternoon, in another session of the same series: the build that added them took 315.45 s against 318.77 s for the build below it by the means of two runs and three, 3.32 s less, which that session's spread of about ±6.5 s does not resolve, with 3.28 s of it before the assume-valid block and none after it; the block thread's time on a processor fell 5.3 s and the reader's rose 5.2 s, and the shutdown line's hashing figure rose from 57.96 to 57.98 s in the runs of the build below it to 63.03 and 63.06 s (the same section, build H). **The depth.** The queue held at most four frames until syncs of 2026-10-19, on m3, from a dcrd v2.1.7 node on another machine on the same LAN, compared four, sixteen and thirty-two in one build of `797a213`'s code with a developer variable for the depth, which this tree does not have, and with the pre-verifier's bound following the depth as it does here (*Signature pre-verification ahead of the block thread*); sixteen was taken. In that series, one of its own and not comparable with the ledger's loopback figures, two syncs at sixteen took 263.4 s and 263.4 s against 273.3 s and 272.9 s at four in the same build, 9.70 s or 3.6% less, beyond that queue's spread of about ±4.8 s for a step between two runs and two, 4.2 s of it before the assume-valid block and 5.5 s after; a third at sixteen, in a later queue, took 264.8 s. At sixteen the block thread waited 0.45 s and 0.51 s in all for the reader, against 3.60 s and 3.62 s, the pre-verifier checked 66.2% and 65.5% of the inputs it guessed for ahead of the block thread, against 51.7% and 52.2%, and node CPU time fell 14.15 s by the means. Two syncs at thirty-two took 267.2 s and 263.5 s, 1.95 s more than at sixteen, which that spread does not resolve. Each setting queued at most its depth, by the shutdown line, and peak resident memory, read when the clock stopped, was 2,466 to 2,471 MiB at sixteen against 2,451 and 2,460 MiB at four (docs/bench-ledger.md, "Where `797a213`'s block thread waits, a deeper read-ahead and a rung of four changes"). Sixteen was compared with four only in that build, on `797a213`'s code, not with the four changes of R1, the build the `internal/blockchain` row's *Transaction types* describes, so whether its gain adds to R1's is not known. With R1's changes it has since run in release builds of `8bda92e`, `b49145e` and `58bd107`, never beside a run at four: `8bda92e`'s in four syncs from the LAN server on 2026-10-22, compared only with each other and with a diagnostic build of the same session (docs/bench-ledger.md, "`8bda92e` in a directory without copy-on-write, and block records on fresh pages"). **Memory and threads.** One more OS thread and one more descriptor for each connection that has started a reader thread, until that connection ends: one connection in each measured run. A connection that stops being the sync peer keeps its thread, which then reads only in turn, so more than one connection can have one at a time; how many do was not counted. The thread starts through `runtime::spawn_conn_thread` and is joined before the connection returns (*Per-peer threads and thread exhaustion*). The queue holds at most sixteen decoded frames beyond the one the input thread is handling. The download window still bounds what the node requests from the sync peer (*Block download window*), so for an honest peer the queue only moves up to sixteen of those frames from the socket buffers into decoded memory; a hostile peer is the worst case estimated next. The frames themselves are bounded exactly: sixteen queued and one being handled, each at most the largest block message and 28 bytes, 22,282,716 bytes. The rest are estimates from struct layouts, not measurements: about 880 kB for sixteen main network blocks of mean size, and at worst about 211 MB held from a hostile sync peer sending blocks of the largest size made of the smallest transactions (sixteen queued and one being handled, each with its frame: the frames above and about 189 MB of decoded blocks), with about 1.3 MB more while a frame is compared with the block's serialization, against about 12.5 MB for one such block without the queue. The pre-verifier holds those same blocks, shared and not copied, at most 22,282,240 bytes of them by their serialized size, with a list of 12 bytes for each input it guesses for, which an input of at least 94 bytes keeps under about 2.8 MB, in an allocation grown by doubling that stays under about 5.7 MB (*Signature pre-verification ahead of the block thread*); while a block is hashed one tree's leaves are copied at a time, at most 1,398,144 bytes, and a `HashedBlock` holds 64 bytes more for its roots, 16 more for its two counts of signature operations and 8 more for its data sanity verdict, which a block the reader checked holds in one allocation of 48 bytes, with the error's description where it failed (`a_carried_verdict_is_small`). At four frames, the bound before sixteen, those figures were about 220 kB and about 62 MB, and before the frames were kept about 150 kB and about 57 MB (four queued, one being handled and the payload buffer). That the decoder accepts the smallest transactions inside a block is now checked, up to its limit of 43,691 in each tree: a block of 43,691 five-byte transactions in each tree decodes, and one more in either tree is refused. The byte estimates were not recomputed against that limit. What a peer can make the reader spend on a block's trees is less than what its leaves already cost: a tree of n leaves has at most n - 1 + ceil(log2 n) inner nodes, each one hash of 64 bytes, two BLAKE-256 compressions, where each leaf takes at least four compressions and two serializations of its transaction, so the block whose trees cost the most, with a payload of 437,096 bytes, takes 174,792 compressions for its 87,396 inner nodes beside at least 349,528 for its leaves (`the_trees_of_the_fullest_block_stay_within_the_stated_bound`, `a_tree_has_no_more_inner_nodes_than_the_stated_bound`). **Pinned by** `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/read_ahead.rs`, nine tests over real loopback connections whose hooks ask for read-ahead after every block. The transcript: `the_handler_sees_the_same_calls_with_and_without_read_ahead` (300 seeded streams of blocks and nine other kinds of message, ending in a close or a malformed frame, with scripted disconnects, teardowns and bans, each run once with the input thread reading and once read ahead: the same calls with the same contents, mix hashes and byte counts in the same order, and the same end of the connection, text included; the peer's replies the same where the peer ends the stream, and a prefix of what is owed where the node does), `a_bad_frame_behind_good_blocks_bans_after_them` and `pings_are_answered_in_stream_order_while_blocks_are_queued`. The bound and the early reads, in `readahead.rs`: `at_most_the_bound_is_read_ahead_and_the_next_read_waits`, `items_come_out_in_the_order_they_were_read`, `the_frame_after_a_block_is_read_while_the_block_is_handled`, `a_frame_after_a_message_that_is_not_a_block_waits_for_the_handler`, `when_read_ahead_is_no_longer_wanted_each_frame_waits_for_the_handler`, `a_failed_read_is_delivered_after_the_items_before_it_and_ends_the_stream` and `ten_thousand_items_pass_through_in_each_mode_without_a_stall`. The flag: `nothing_queued_is_handed_over_once_the_connection_is_cancelled`, `a_waiting_handler_notices_the_flag_within_the_poll_interval` and `a_bad_frame_behind_a_disconnecting_block_is_ignored`. The idle budget: in `transport.rs`, `a_read_started_while_the_waiter_is_busy_does_not_run_its_budget`, `the_budget_runs_from_the_moment_the_waiter_began_waiting`, `a_dribbled_read_still_ends_at_the_budget_once_the_waiter_waits` and `an_inline_read_keeps_its_fixed_deadline`; in `readahead.rs`, `the_budget_start_is_none_while_the_handler_is_busy_and_set_when_it_waits`, `the_budget_start_does_not_move_while_the_handler_keeps_waiting` and `a_frame_behind_a_block_the_handler_waited_for_gets_a_budget_of_its_own`; and over a connection, `a_silent_peer_is_dropped_one_budget_after_the_handler_went_idle` and `the_frame_behind_a_block_that_arrived_late_in_its_budget_has_its_own_budget`. The byte counts: `a_paced_read_counts_bytes_as_an_inline_one_does` and `each_message_carries_its_own_byte_count`. The stall detector: `a_block_read_ahead_settles_its_deadline_only_when_it_is_handled`, `a_slow_handler_with_blocks_queued_does_not_stall_an_honest_peer` and `a_hashed_block_is_handled_as_its_message_is`. Who gets a reader thread: `read_ahead_is_wanted_only_from_the_sync_peer_during_the_initial_chain_sync` (`crates/dcroxide-node/tests/dispatch.rs`), and in `peerloop.rs` `read_ahead_starts_only_after_a_kept_block_while_wanted`, `hooks_that_do_not_ask_are_never_read_ahead`, `a_reader_no_longer_wanted_ahead_reads_only_when_the_handler_waits` and `a_refused_reader_thread_leaves_the_connection_reading_inline`. The teardown: `a_dropped_peer_read_ahead_is_disconnected_before_the_server_lets_it_go`, `disconnecting_releases_a_reader_parked_on_a_full_queue`, `leaving_wakes_a_parked_reader_and_one_blocked_in_a_read`, `a_reader_thread_that_panics_ends_the_stream_for_the_handler`, `a_paced_read_stops_at_the_flag`, `shutdown_with_blocks_queued_ends_the_connection_without_handling_them` and, on Linux, `the_reader_thread_is_joined_before_the_connection_returns` (`tests/read_ahead_join.rs`). End to end: `blocks_read_ahead_over_a_served_connection_are_hashed_once` (`tests/dispatch.rs`: the listener, the server handler, the sync manager and the chain, with the setting on and off) and, on Linux, `tests/read_ahead_sync.rs` (one test: two daemons each sync 64 simnet blocks over loopback from a third, one reading ahead and one with the setting off). The shutdown line: `the_shutdown_line_reports_the_figures_of_a_known_stream` (`tests/read_ahead_stats.rs`), `the_shutdown_line_reports_what_the_readers_did` and `only_a_wait_for_room_in_the_index_queue_is_timed` (`indexes.rs`). The setting: `read_ahead_settings_parse` and `tests/read_ahead_env.rs` (four tests: the daemon reads the variable, logs each startup line, and says nothing when it is unset). The chain's side is pinned where the hashes are described (*Each transaction hash goes through a heap buffer*). The record: `crates/dcroxide-blockchain/tests/wire_record_battery.rs` (seven tests: every block of dcrd's full block battery is kept as the bytes dcrd serialized it in, and stored as that record leaves the block files and index rows that storing the decoded block leaves; the battery gives the same results and files however its blocks arrive; encodings that are not the block's serialization, a count written longer than it need be among them, are dropped and the block is stored as ever; a record for another network is not written), in `transport.rs` `a_block_read_for_a_waiter_keeps_the_frame_it_was_decoded_from` and `the_largest_block_is_laid_out_as_its_record_where_it_was_read`, in `readahead.rs` `a_block_is_handed_over_with_its_frame_laid_out_as_its_record`, the transcript test's 300 seeded streams, which fault any block a reader thread read that arrives without a record equal to its serialization, the store's side in the `database` row, and in `tests/read_ahead_sync.rs` the block files of the daemon reading ahead against those of the one that does not, with the shutdown line's two counts equal to the blocks read. The trees: in `blockhashes.rs` `carried_merkle_roots_give_the_verdicts_of_roots_built_by_the_check`, `hashing_a_block_ahead_is_one_pass` and the two tests of the bound above; `process::tests::a_hashed_block_is_processed_without_building_its_merkle_trees`; and `tests/hashed_block_bad_shapes.rs` (three tests, which hold `process_hashed_block` to the fork length, error and text of `process_block` and `process_shared_block` under each answer the agenda query can give). The counts: in `blockhashes.rs` `carried_sig_op_counts_are_the_counts_of_the_block_check` (every block of dcrd's full block battery under each answer the treasury agenda query can give: the carried count is dcrd's count written out in the test and, where the check's own count passes, the sum it ends on, and the limit checked with the counts and without gives the same verdict, error and text), `carried_sig_op_counts_at_the_limit_and_by_agenda_answer` (blocks at the limit, one over it, over it in the middle of a tree, over it only once `OP_TSPEND` counts or once a version 1 coinbase is no longer one, a treasurybase and a vote: the error names the sum at which the check stops), `only_a_count_at_most_the_limit_is_within_it`, `a_sum_that_overflows_its_accumulator_is_none` and `a_transaction_whose_own_count_overflows_makes_the_count_none` (a transaction of more than 214 million bytes of script, which no block that can arrive holds: the path alone); `process::tests::the_contextual_check_counts_by_its_treasury_agenda_answer` (a block of the battery made one transaction longer, at the limit until `OP_TSPEND` counts and two over it once it does, passes the contextual check over the battery's chain and, shaped for the treasury agenda, fails it with the agenda forced active, with the counts and without), `a_hashed_block_is_processed_without_counting_its_sig_ops`, `the_battery_counts_a_hashed_block_only_over_the_limit` (five of the six rows dcrd refuses for too many signature operations are refused through the count; the sixth, `bshso1`, has a count within the limit and is refused as it is connected, by the count that includes pay-to-script-hash inputs) and `a_block_linked_by_its_parent_is_counted_by_the_check`; `process::tests::hashes_are_only_reused_for_the_very_block_they_were_filed_with`, which now covers the counts filed with the hashes; and `tests/hashed_block_battery.rs`, which now requires rows refused with `ErrTooManySigOps` among those it compares. The data sanity verdict: in `blockhashes.rs` `a_carried_data_sanity_verdict_is_the_checks_own` (every block of dcrd's full block battery with the regression network's parameters and every block of dcrd's block sanity vectors with mainnet's: the carried verdict is the checks' own, error, kind and text, and is the kind dcrd recorded for each of the 138 vector rows whose header passes its own checks; the verdicts cover the ten kinds the full block battery and the vectors refuse a block's data with; a verdict is not read with the other network's values, and reaching it makes none of the checks the chain counts), `the_kinds_no_vector_reaches_are_carried_as_the_checks_reach_them` (a transaction over the largest size, a fraud proof amount out of range, a block over the largest the wire protocol carries), `data_sanity_params_hold_the_two_values_the_checks_read` and `a_carried_verdict_is_small` (on a 64-bit target the verdict's allocation is 48 bytes and a `HashedBlock` 192, with the verdict behind a pointer); in `process.rs` `a_checked_block_is_processed_without_its_data_sanity_checks` (with and without full validation, against a twin handed the same blocks plain), `the_battery_returns_a_carried_verdict_where_the_checks_are_made` (every row the same result, no row checked by the chain; the thirteen rows the checks refuse refused with their verdicts, and again when they arrive a second time), `a_failing_verdict_is_not_read_where_an_earlier_check_refuses_the_block` (a duplicate block, a merkle root that does not commit to the data), `a_block_refused_by_its_verdict_is_known_invalid_when_it_arrives_again` and `a_verdict_reached_with_other_values_is_not_read`; `tests/hashed_block_battery.rs` `the_battery_gives_the_same_outcomes_through_checked_hashed_blocks` (checked with the chain's values and with mainnet's); on the node's side `blocks_read_carry_the_sanity_checks_made_with_the_values_given` (`readahead.rs`: ahead of the handler and in turn, and none without values), `the_reader_checks_blocks_with_the_values_the_server_gives` (`peerloop.rs`, over a connection), `read_ahead_is_wanted_only_from_the_sync_peer_during_the_initial_chain_sync` (`tests/dispatch.rs`, which now delivers blocks checked with the handler's values through the sync manager to the chain) and `blocks_read_ahead_over_a_served_connection_are_hashed_once` (`tests/dispatch.rs`, which now also requires that the chain checked only the block the input thread read, `Chain::arrivals_checked`, and every block with the setting off); and, under debug assertions, every test that processes a checked block, those two and `tests/read_ahead_sync.rs` among them, the chain making the checks again where it reads a verdict and comparing. **Not verified.** The read-ahead's gain in `88c46c3`, which was run in syncs on 2026-10-17 and 2026-10-18 with it on only, or in any code since: the four runs of 2026-10-12 are of the build before the fixes; on 2026-10-13 the read-ahead of `b4ceb1c` ran inside `4700cf1`, without the record and the trees, and every build that held those also held a developer variable this tree does not have. A sync with read-ahead over a delayed link, or over a real one with a longer round trip than a LAN's (the syncs of 2026-10-18 and 2026-10-19 from a dcrd v2.1.7 node on another machine on the same LAN had it on; docs/bench-ledger.md, "Rungs from a block server on the LAN, two changes held for their memory, and dcrd v2.1.7" and "One commit thread for the life of the store, a sync that checks every script, and dcrd v2.1.7"), where the round trip and not the block thread may set the pace and reading ahead may gain nothing, and where the reader, onto which the record and the trees moved about 7 s and 5 s of the block thread's work in the runs of 2026-10-13, may have no time to spare. The counts of signature operations apart from the change they were run with, from a server on the same machine, and where the reader has no time to spare: it counts each block twice, once for each answer the treasury agenda can give. The data sanity checks on the reader: what they gain, which their one step in a sync did not resolve, from a server on the same machine, and where the reader has no time to spare. A node on fewer cores than the eight of the four runs. `--assumevalid=0` and `--noexistsaddrindex` against a run with the read-ahead off: the read-ahead was on in one sync with `--assumevalid=0`, on 2026-10-19 from a server on the same machine, whose wall is not a timing (docs/bench-ledger.md, "One commit thread for the life of the store, a sync that checks every script, and dcrd v2.1.7"), and with `--noexistsaddrindex` the read-ahead of `b4ceb1c` was run once, inside `4700cf1` on 2026-10-13, `88c46c3` twice on 2026-10-17 and later builds five times from the LAN node on 2026-10-18 and 2026-10-19, none of them against a run with the read-ahead off. The memory bound: sixteen frames is the most the tests allow, the byte figures other than the frames' are estimates, and no sync has filled the queue with the largest blocks. macOS and Windows: nothing of the read-ahead has been run there, neither a sync nor any of its tests, and two of the tests (`tests/read_ahead_join.rs`, `tests/read_ahead_sync.rs`) are Linux-only. The late-block case over a connection in an optimized build: `the_frame_behind_a_block_that_arrived_late_in_its_budget_has_its_own_budget` reaches it only where checking, decoding and hashing its block, one close to the largest a block may be, takes tens of milliseconds, which is an unoptimized build, and passes without reaching it in a release-mode test run; the unit test `a_frame_behind_a_block_the_handler_waited_for_gets_a_budget_of_its_own` holds the rule in every build. Every arm of the server's dispatch for a dependence on when bytes leave the socket: the handler has no access to the read transport, and the transcript test covers ten kinds of message, not all of them. And no test counts the reader thread's calls to `HashedBlock::new` or `HashedBlock::check_data_sanity`, so hashing or checking a block twice there would change no result and fail no test. |
| Signature pre-verification ahead of the block thread | No counterpart. A signature reaches the signature cache only from a verify that succeeded while a script was executed with the cache attached (`sigCache.Add` in `opcodeCheckSig` and `opcodeCheckMultiSig`, `txscript/opcode.go`; `txscript/sigcache.go`): at mempool acceptance, at a template check, or while the chain connects a block on `txValidator`'s goroutines (`internal/blockchain/scriptval.go:117-166`). During the initial sync the mempool is empty, so every signature of a block after the assume-valid block is verified by the connect checks themselves | On by default, for the blocks a reading thread reads while its connection's handler wants reading ahead, which on the sync peer's connection it does until the initial chain sync is done (*Reading ahead of the block handler*); the reading thread a connection keeps after that hands over nothing (`Shared::preverify`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/readahead.rs:414`). **What is checked** (`crates/dcroxide-blockchain/src/preverify.rs`). The reading thread hands each block it has hashed to the process's pre-verifier before it queues the block (`Preverifier::submit`, `preverify.rs:467`; `reader_loop`, `readahead.rs:642`, at `:701`) when the block's header height is above the height at and below which the chain checks no script (`Chain::script_skip_floor`, `crates/dcroxide-blockchain/src/process.rs:9134`: `i64::MAX` in bulk import, the assumed valid node's height clamped as `is_assume_valid_ancestor` clamps it while that node is on the best header's branch, -1 otherwise), which the thread that processes blocks works out again after a block whenever the best header, the assumed valid node or the bulk import mode has moved (`NodeSyncChain::process_block`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/sync.rs:246`, at `:275`). For each input whose signature script is exactly two canonical pushes, a signature of 1 to 75 bytes whose last byte, its hash type, is `SigHashAll`, and a public key of 33 or 65 bytes (`sig_and_key`, `preverify.rs:143`), it guesses the script the input spends: pay to the hash of that key, behind `OP_SSTX` for output 0 of a stake transaction spent from the stake tree by a transaction that is not a ticket purchase (a vote or a revocation spending its ticket), behind `OP_SSRTX` for outputs 0 and 1 of a stake transaction otherwise, behind `OP_SSGEN` for its outputs 2 and up, and behind none for an output of the regular tree (`guessed_tag`, `:164`). No other script is guessed, and each input gets one guess. **Where** (`scriptpool.rs`, *Lanes*; `take_lane_job`, `crates/dcroxide-blockchain/src/scriptpool.rs:1005`). The script pool's helpers (*Script checks of a connected block*) take the inputs of the oldest queued block one at a time when no batch of a connected block wants them, by default at most two fewer at once than the physical cores the process may run on plus one for every two logical CPUs beyond those cores, counting at most one beyond each core (where the cores are read, two fewer than them without SMT or with one hardware thread of each core allowed, and ten with two hardware threads to each of eight cores; where they cannot be read, as on Windows and macOS, the cores are taken to be half the logical CPUs, so that sixteen logical CPUs give ten with or without SMT), at least one from two cores, never more than the pool's width (`default_helpers`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/preverify.rs:104`; `DCROXIDE_PREVERIFY` sets the most instead), and run each through the script engine against its guess with the chain's signature cache and the transaction's hash attached, under every agenda's flags (`check_input`, `crates/dcroxide-blockchain/src/preverify.rs:184`). Every batch queued for helpers bumps a counter under the pool's mutex, and a helper inside a pre-verified block compares it before each input and leaves for the batch once it has moved (`lane_work`, `scriptpool.rs:1043`), so a batch waits for a pre-verifying helper at most the one input that helper holds. **When it stops.** The thread that processes blocks withdraws a block, and every block queued before it, as it takes that block from the reading thread's queue, and from then on the block's inputs that were not reached are checked by the connect checks alone, whatever they find (`Shared::next`, `readahead.rs:506`, at `:528`); the blocks still queued when the connection ends are withdrawn with it (`Shared::leave`, at `:571`), and a block read as the handler left is withdrawn as it is dropped (`Shared::push`, at `:439`). The daemon shuts the pre-verifier down right after it stops block processing, before it disconnects the peers: the queued blocks are let go and the call returns once no helper holds one (`Preverifier::shutdown`, `crates/dcroxide-blockchain/src/preverify.rs:526`; `LaneHandle::close`, `scriptpool.rs:970`). **Bounds.** A block is pre-verified only while it waits in the reading thread's queue, by at most the pre-verifier's helpers at once. An input costs one hash of its transaction's witness serialization, a byte for each other input, and at most one signature verification: under `SigHashAll` the signature hash takes the transaction's prefix from its hash, which the reading thread computed, where any other hash type serializes the whole prefix again for each input. The pre-verifier holds at most 22,282,240 bytes of serialized blocks, the read-ahead's sixteen frames and one more of the largest block message, and refuses a block past that (`max_queued_bytes`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/preverify.rs:173`; `LaneHandle::submit`, `scriptpool.rs:879`); the blocks are the reading thread's own allocations, shared, not copied. **Not changed.** The connect checks, which neither know nor ask whether an input was pre-verified, every verdict and its error, and the signature cache's own rules: an entry exists only after the engine's own parse and verify of that signature hash, signature and public key succeeded (`Engine::verify_sig_with_cache`; `SigCache::add` stays private to the script crate, and the `len` and `is_empty` it gains only read). **Observable.** (a) The signature cache holds entries for signatures of blocks not yet connected, of blocks that are then rejected, and of signatures made over a guessed script the input does not spend; each is a signature that verifies over that hash under that key, and random eviction removes them as it removes any other. (b) Up to that many helper threads are busy for the blocks read ahead. A block from the sync peer is pre-verified while it waits, whatever the chain then finds; a peer whose block the chain rejects stays connected, as in dcrd, and its later blocks are read ahead and pre-verified alike, so the work is bounded per block, as above, and by the time each block waits, not per peer. (c) At startup the setting's line, and the number of helpers in effect or why none was started (`started_line`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/preverify.rs:241`), at info level when `DCROXIDE_PREVERIFY` names a number and at debug level otherwise; one line at shutdown, after the read-ahead's and just before `Server shutdown complete`. (d) With `--assumevalid=0` the floor is -1 from the start and every block read ahead is pre-verified from the first; pay-to-script-hash spends, common before the assume-valid block, get no guess. (e) `DCROXIDE_PREVERIFY=off` (or a whole number that is zero, such as `0`, `00` or `+0`), `DCROXIDE_READ_AHEAD=off` and `--sigcachemaxsize=0` each leave the node as it is without pre-verification | After the assume-valid block the thread that processes blocks checks the scripts of every block it connects, with the script pool's helpers, and a hit in the signature cache at connect skips the key parse and the verify of that signature. No verdict can change: the engine adds an entry only after its own verify of the identical signature hash, signature and public key succeeded, and a connect check hits only those identical bytes, so a hit skips only a verify that would have succeeded, which is the argument dcrd relies on for the signatures its mempool verified; a wrong guess gives another signature hash, and its entry, if the signature verifies over it, is under a hash no connect check of that input computes. The flags do not enter an entry: the verify reads none of them, and the strict encoding checks run before the lookup on every path. Only `SigHashAll` signatures are guessed, and pay-to-script-hash spends not at all, so that an input costs the helpers one witness hash, a byte for each other input, and at most one verification, whatever the block holds: a transaction's signature hashes grow with the square of its inputs under any hash type, by about a byte for each pair of inputs under `SigHashAll`, where another hash type would serialize the whole prefix again for each input, which a block the chain refuses for its size before running any script could make far costlier; and a redeem script can hold many signature operations where the connect checks refuse a block over the sig-op limit before they run any script. Pre-verification was measured in syncs on 2026-10-18, on m3, from a dcrd v2.1.7 node on another machine on the same LAN, in builds that were states of a worktree and not the tree of a commit, in a series not comparable with the ledger's loopback figures: the build that added it took 7.95 s less than the build below it by the means of two runs each, beyond the morning session's 95% interval of about ±3.5 s, 8.50 s less after the assume-valid block and 0.55 s more before it, where no script is checked, and the block thread's time on a processor fell 13.9 s and that of the script pool's threads 29.0 s. That step is on top of the changes below it, one of which was measured there and not kept, and is not its effect alone; the same build without that change ran three times in a later session, beside no build without pre-verification. One build of `88c46c3`'s code with pre-verification and counters of what it did, one run with it on and one with `DCROXIDE_PREVERIFY=off`, took 7.4 s less with it on, 11.1 s less after the assume-valid block and 3.7 s more before it, where it does nothing, which shows how far one pair can stray; with it on, 60.5% of the connect checks' lookups of the signature cache hit, and in each run 11 of the inputs the connect checks ran spent a pay-to-pubkey-hash output with two pushes, a signature and then a public key, and were signed with another hash type than `SigHashAll`. The three end-state checks of that section, of build G and of a later build that holds it, each ended on the UTXO set of every full sync of 2026-10-17 (docs/bench-ledger.md, "Rungs from a block server on the LAN, two changes held for their memory, and dcrd v2.1.7", build G, "The prototype pair" and "The end state (v18)"). The default's count beyond the cores rests on one comparison, on m3 on 2026-10-19 with the node held to eight cores and their SMT siblings, in a diagnostic build of `797a213`'s code with the read-ahead's depth at sixteen frames: with `DCROXIDE_PREVERIFY=10` two syncs took 258.1 s and 259.6 s, 5.02 s less by the means than three at six, the default then, about ±2.6 s, 6.2 s less after the assume-valid block and 1.2 s more before it, where nothing is pre-verified; the pre-verifier checked 90.2% and 89.3% of the guessed inputs ahead of the block thread against 65.5% to 66.2%, and node CPU time rose 29.83 s by the means and the script pool's threads' 30.64 s, while the block thread was on a processor for 204.5 s and 202.7 s against 207.6 to 207.9 s (docs/bench-ledger.md, "Where `797a213`'s block thread waits, a deeper read-ahead and a rung of four changes"). No other count was compared, nor any machine without SMT, with another core count or with more than two hardware threads to a core. Ten helpers on the code as committed have run, at `8bda92e`'s default with the node held to eight cores and their SMT siblings, in four of the benchmark harness's syncs of 2026-10-22, those of `8bda92e`, and in its sync of `b49145e` on 2026-10-23 (docs/bench-ledger.md, "`8bda92e` in a directory without copy-on-write, and block records on fresh pages" and "Per-block comparison with dcrd, genesis to block 1,116,035"), never beside six. In none of the runs with ten helpers was it recorded which of the node's hardware threads they ran on. Pinned by `pre_verified_blocks_get_the_verdicts_they_get_without` (with the connect checks leaving a pre-verified cache as they leave a cold one), `a_forged_or_mismatched_signature_is_never_cached`, `a_signature_over_the_guessed_script_does_not_pass_for_the_spent_one`, `pooled_connect_checks_racing_the_lane_give_the_same_verdicts`, `the_guess_is_made_for_the_one_template` (the shape, the hash type and the tag table), `a_withdrawn_block_hands_out_no_more_inputs`, `blocks_at_or_below_the_floor_are_not_taken`, `no_helper_means_no_preverifier` and `shutdown_with_blocks_queued_lets_go_of_every_block` (`preverify::tests`); `a_lane_job_is_done_by_the_helpers`, `a_lane_holds_no_more_helpers_than_its_width`, `a_batch_takes_a_helper_away_from_a_lane_after_its_unit`, `withdrawn_jobs_stop_and_leave_the_lane`, `a_lane_refuses_past_its_bytes_and_when_closed`, `closing_a_lane_waits_for_the_helper_inside_and_drops_queued_jobs` and `a_panicking_lane_unit_leaves_the_helper_serving` (`scriptpool::tests`); the five tests of `tests/preverify_vectors.rs` (dcrd's script corpus; the connect battery's script rows; dcrd's full block battery on three chains, one without, one with each block pre-verified first, and one beside a pre-verifier, which is handed no block because the battery holds no input of the guessed shape; block 432,100's mainnet signatures; and the floor against `is_assume_valid_ancestor` along the headers-first battery); in the node `blocks_read_ahead_are_preverified_above_the_floor_until_taken`, `a_handler_that_leaves_lets_preverification_go_of_its_blocks` and `blocks_read_while_read_ahead_is_not_wanted_are_not_preverified` (`readahead::tests`), `the_preverifier_floor_follows_the_chain` (`sync::tests`), `preverify_settings_parse`, `the_off_switch_and_the_cache_decide_whether_one_starts` and `the_started_line_says_what_is_in_effect` with the default's test, `the_default_is_two_under_the_cores_plus_half_of_at_most_one_cpu_beyond_each` (machines of each shape, and every count of cores and of logical CPUs up to 256), and the core count's and the queue bound's tests (`preverify::tests`), and `the_installed_preverifier_fills_the_chains_own_cache` (`tests/preverify_install.rs`, a process of its own: nothing started off or without a cache, the helpers capped at the pool's width, the chain's own cache filled). dcrd's full block battery and the connect battery's script rows hold no input of the guessed shape (dcrd's generator pays to script hashes of `OP_TRUE`), so on them the tests show only that nothing changes; the signed blocks of `preverify::tests` and block 432,100 carry the signatures. |
| Address-manager bucket key, connmgr CSPRNG seed | `crypto/rand` | `getrandom` | The port had been deriving both from the clock, making bucket assignment predictable from a node's approximate start time. Anyone who ran an affected build should delete `peers.json`. |
| Mempool orphan-eviction draw | nothing: `limitNumOrphans` takes the first entry of a `range` over a Go map (`internal/mempool/mempool.go:581-592`), and dcrd's mempool imports no randomness package at all | a `SystemCsprng` stream seeded at `NodePoolChain` construction | The port stores orphans in ordered containers, so the unpredictability dcrd gets free from map iteration has to be asked for. What is borrowed is the *source's* failure contract, not the seam: fatal at initial seeding, infallible afterwards. Reading the kernel per draw instead — as this did — put a fallible call on a path a peer paces by pushing orphans past `max_orphan_txs`, and under `panic = "abort"` an entropy failure there is an outage dcrd's map walk has no way to cause. |
| `Prng` stream lifetime | rekeys every 4 MiB or 20 s (`crypto/rand/prng.go:20-21`, `:84-104`) | rekeys every 4 MiB only | The byte budget is not hygiene here, it is a bound on a reachable panic: the port's `chacha20::ChaCha20` is the 96-bit-nonce variant with a `u32` block counter, and `apply_keystream` panics rather than erroring at `(2^32-1)*64` bytes. The 20-second reseed is not reproduced — it is unobservable, and implementing it would put a clock read on every draw where the byte budget alone already removes the panic. Note also that `PRNG.seed` draws its 32-byte mix off the outgoing cipher without charging it (`prng.go:72`, counter cleared at `:78`), so one cipher yields `maxCipherRead + 32` bytes; reproduced rather than corrected, and nothing upstream documents a per-cipher budget for that to contradict. |
| Entropy failure after startup | impossible to observe: `crypto/rand.Read` calls `runtime.fatal` and kills the process rather than returning an error on any toolchain dcrd can build with (`go.mod` requires 1.25), so `PRNG.seed`'s `if err != nil && p.t.IsZero()` guard (`prng.go:68-70`) is dead code | `Prng::reseed` ignores a failed `getrandom` and rekeys from the outgoing keystream | Deliberate, and the one place this port is more available than upstream. dcrd's *documented* contract is the infallible draw ("Read never errors", `prng.go:84`; "will never panic after package init", `README.md:18`), which is what the 4 MiB rekey exists to provide; aborting instead would trade an unreachable divergence for a reachable process kill. Unpredictability is unaffected — the replacement key is a function of cipher state an observer without the old key cannot compute. |
| Reduction of a draw to a range | `ShuffleSlice`/`IntN` reduce through the bias-free `Uint64N` (`crypto/rand/uniform.go:102-148`): Lemire multiply-shift against a `-n % n` rejection threshold | `dcroxide_crypto::rand::Prng::uint64n` is that same reduction, line for line, and the peer environment's address shuffles go through it via `rand::shuffle_slice`. `dcroxide_connmgr::SystemCsprng::uint64n` and `dcroxide_addrmgr::SystemRng::int_n` reach the same distribution by rejection sampling instead, and `dcroxide-mempool/src/pool.rs` `limit_num_orphans` still reduces a full-width draw by plain modulo | Enumerated rather than counted, because three successive attempts to state this as a closed set were each wrong. Raw modulo over a random draw, in production code, is exactly two, both in the mempool: `dcroxide-mempool/src/pool.rs` `limit_num_orphans` (orphan eviction) and `shuffle_trial_order` (rival trial order). **Neither is a divergence, and neither is going to be converted.** dcrd draws nothing at either site — it ranges over a Go map — so there is no upstream reduction to converge on, and the *Orphan eviction source* row above records what that map walk actually distributes: off uniform by a factor of several, where this modulo is off by about one part in `2^57`. Adopting `Uint64N` here would buy distance from dcrd's real distribution while appearing to close a gap. It would also put a rejecting, redrawing loop behind `PoolChain::random_u64`, which this crate's test doubles answer with a constant: `2^64 mod n` is nonzero for every non-power-of-two bound, including the default `max_orphan_txs` of 100, so such a source would never clear the threshold and the pool would hang rather than fail. The modulo's totality is a property of this seam, not an oversight, and the trait's doc comment now says so. Converted where dcrd's own arithmetic was the target: the peer address shuffles, the mining-address pick, the rebroadcast timer's `rand.Duration` (`rebroadcast.rs` `rand_duration`, now `dcroxide_crypto::rand::duration` over `Prng::uint64n`, where it used to reject-and-retry over kernel draws), and the seeder's backdating, which was the largest of them — four days in nanoseconds (`~2^48`) is a bias near one part in `2^16`, roughly `2^41` times the mempool's. Bias-free but not Lemire, and so still divergent in arithmetic and draw count though not in distribution: `dcroxide_connmgr::SystemCsprng::uint64n` and `dcroxide_addrmgr::SystemRng::int_n`, which both reject-and-retry. What remains is those two rejection loops, which are bias-free but map stream bytes to outputs differently from Lemire and consume a different number of draws (unobservable: the input is an unpredictable keystream, so nothing outside the process can tell which arithmetic produced a value), and the two mempool modulo reductions above, whose bias is `2^64 mod n` in `2^64`: about one part in `2^57` for the default 100 orphans, a limit that also bounds the rival lists `shuffle_trial_order` permutes. |
| Metadata store after a failed durable write | no latch: `dbCache.flush` returns the commit error without clearing the cached treaps (`database/ffldb/dbcache.go:501-509`), so the next flush retries the same dirty set and can return success | the failure latches the store fatal and every write on that handle that checks the latch after it is refused with `ErrorKind::Fatal` (`ErrFatal`, the one error kind with no dcrd counterpart); reads keep answering from whatever redb can still read. After an I/O error redb fails every uncached page with `PreviousIo` until restart; the UTXO reads report that as an error, and the ffldb-layout reads of rows answer absence, as ffldb does; the lookup of a nested bucket reads nothing and finds the bucket (*Nested bucket lookup, and the bucket index at open*). A failing commit made on the committing thread latches before it releases the writer semaphore. A failure on the thread a flush is handed to in the daemon (*Where a cache flush's commit runs*), in syncing the flush's block files, writing its rows, the index's or the commit, is met with no semaphore held, and latches first, before the write cache's accounting is restored, before its `BCDB` line and before the participant is told. The latch is checked by `begin` for a writable transaction, by a commit's own check before it writes a block, at `flush_locked`'s entry and again after it has waited for a commit under way, by `Database::flush`, `Database::flush_in_background` and `Database::close` after they have waited, and by `Database::is_fatal_settled`, so a writer queued behind the failure cannot re-run the failed flush and nothing is captured again. `begin` does not wait for a commit under way. A commit that waited for a failing commit is refused with the first failure's cause. A transaction that committed between a flush's hand-off and that flush's failure reported success: its rows were published to the write cache and never acknowledged as durable, and a restart loses them with the failed batch. `Database::flush`, `Database::close` and a commit whose flush ran on the committing thread never report success once a durable write has failed (ADR-0009, addendum of 2026-10-16, restates condition 1 so) | A retry that succeeds after a failed commit is how a store loses data silently: the engine reports durability the log will not honour, and `Chain::flush` writes block index rows, UTXO entries and both state markers in one transaction precisely so a crash cannot leave them disagreeing. Not a runtime artifact either — the trade is availability for integrity, which is the right way round for a daemon that already runs under a supervisor because release builds abort on panic. Reads are deliberately not latched: bytes that reached disk are still valid, and refusing to serve them turns a write fault into a total outage for no gain. `Database::is_fatal_settled`, which first waits for a commit under way (*Classifying a refusal beside a commit under way*), is threaded into the block-processing result (`storage_failed` in `NodeSyncChain::process_block` and `process_block_header`, `sync.rs`, handed to `combine_process_block_result`, and in `reconsiderblock`) so a storage fault is not reported as a consensus rule error against the peer that sent a perfectly good block, and a failed shutdown flush exits `FAILURE` rather than success. Pinned by the latch's own tests, the source-scanning durability test, and the ENOSPC fault injection. |
| Shutdown UTXO flush failure | `ShutdownUtxoCache` discards `MaybeFlush`'s error (`internal/blockchain/utxocache.go:1073`), so `dcrdMain` exits 0 | logs `Unable to flush the block database: ...` and exits 1; `Server shutdown complete`, both shutdown events and the database close still follow | By then the store has latched fatal (row above), and exit status 0 would tell a supervisor the node's state reached disk. Nothing on disk is inconsistent: the store holds the last completed flush, and the next start replays forward from it (`initialize_utxo_state`). |
| Commit that trips a cache flush | `commitTx` flushes and then writes the transaction's own rows straight to the persistent database with `commitTreaps` (`database/ffldb/dbcache.go:558-582`), so the triggering write is durable when the commit returns | the flush publishes first and the transaction's rows land in the overlay after it, so the commit that trips a flush returns before its own rows are durable. In the daemon it returns earlier still: once the cache is captured, what the block-file sync owes is taken, redb's write transaction is open and the exists-address index has set apart the keys the flush will write; the block-file sync, the flush's rows, the index's rows and the commit are then made on a thread of its own (*Where a cache flush's commit runs*), so that commit returns before the rows of the flush it tripped are durable too, and a failure in any of them is reported to the next write, flush or close (*Metadata store after a failed durable write*). A failure before the hand-off (opening the write transaction, a write-cache row under the index's prefix) still fails the commit that tripped the flush, with its block files rolled back; a failed block-file sync, which dcrd's `commitTx` reports to the commit whose flush made it, has been reported the same later way since 2026-10-19, a divergence from dcrd's error timing that ADR-0009's third addendum of that date records | Nothing in the chain treats a returning commit as durable. The barriers are the explicit `Database::flush` that ends a forced UTXO cache flush (each connect once the chain is current, the latch to current, every disconnect, `gettxoutsetinfo`), `Chain::flush` at shutdown and `Database::close`; a periodic UTXO cache flush asks for a durable commit without waiting for it, which the daemon makes on the commit thread as it does a commit's own flush, and counts only once that commit has landed (*Periodic UTXO cache flush interval*); since 2026-10-11 a UTXO cache flush that only the size limit tripped is itself a commit that returns before its rows are durable (the next row). Recovery tolerates losing any suffix of the commit order, because every durable commit is a prefix of it, and the triggering transaction is the first transaction of that suffix. Writing through instead would let a reader that seeds between the flush's commit and its retirement observe the triggering transaction partially applied, and hold that snapshot for its lifetime; the RPC block-region path seeds outside the chain lock, which is what the split flush exists to allow. Real parity here is a reorder, not a write-through: publish the rows before starting the flush so they ride inside the captured layers. That is gated on the fatal-latch fix, because a failed flush would otherwise leave the rows cached while their block bytes rolled back. |
| UTXO cache flush and the durable commit | every UTXO cache flush first makes the block database durable (`flushBlockDB`, `utxocache.go:717-725`, wired to `db.Flush` at `server.go:4007`), whatever tripped it: the size limit, the two-minute interval (`shouldFlush`, `utxocache.go:665-679`) or a current chain (`chain.go:728`) | a forced flush ends in `Database::flush`, as dcrd's does. A periodic one ends in `Database::flush_in_background`, which captures the write cache and, in the daemon, hands the writing of its rows and its commit to the commit thread as the commit that trips a flush does, and returns; with `DCROXIDE_DB_SYNC_COMMIT` it writes and commits before it returns, as `Database::flush` does. A flush that only the size limit tripped, which is nearly every flush of an initial sync, writes its rows and the set state in one transaction and returns; they become durable with the next durable commit, whichever trigger makes it: the metadata write cache's size or time, the exists-address participant's, or the next forced or periodic flush (`maybe_flush_utxo_cache`, `flush_utxo_cache_with`); the chain counts the flush for the periodic interval at the first connect after that commit has landed (`utxo_settle_durable`) | dcrd flushes the block database first because its UTXO set is a separate database that must never run ahead of the block index. Here both are rows of one store and every durable commit is a prefix of the commit order. The rows and the set state are written in one transaction, so on disk they always describe the same block; that block's connect is in the same durable state; and the best state is at or past it, except across an interrupted disconnect, where the set state names the disconnected block and its spend journal row is still there. `initialize_utxo_state` replays or detaches whatever lies between, as it already did after a commit the write cache made on its own. What changes is what an unclean stop during initial sync loses and downloads again: about 120 s of processing while blocks connect, and in the daemon what is processed while the periodic flush's commit is under way on the commit thread, where it was one fill of the UTXO cache; every trigger is decided as a block connects or a transaction commits, so the bound is in work and not in time. On m3 on 2026-10-11 three default syncs with the change took a median of 728.7 s with 54 flushes, where the code before it took 775.6 s with 133 in one run that afternoon; peak resident memory rose from 2,635 MiB to 3,090–3,285 MiB with the exists-address index on, and was 2,138 MiB with it off (docs/bench-ledger.md, "Shared commits for UTXO-cache flushes, the filesystem's share and a delayed link"). |
| Periodic UTXO cache flush interval | two minutes since the last flush of any kind (`lastFlushTime`, `utxocache.go:678`, `:769`) | two minutes since the last UTXO cache flush whose rows a durable commit has carried (`utxo_last_durable_flush_nanos`). A forced flush (each connect once the chain is current, the latch to current, every disconnect, `gettxoutsetinfo`) and the shutdown flush (`Chain::flush`) end in their own `Database::flush`, carry every flush before them, and stamp the time that call returned, as before. The periodic flush, which asks for its commit with `Database::flush_in_background` and which the daemon writes and commits on the commit thread while blocks go on connecting, and a flush the size limit tripped, whose rows wait for the next durable commit, whichever makes it, are stamped once a commit that carries them has landed (`Database::commit_mark`, `Database::is_durable`): at the first `maybe_flush_utxo_cache` after it, which each connect calls (`utxo_settle_durable`), or at once when the periodic flush's commit was made before `flush_in_background` returned. The time they are stamped with is read as the chain's flush returns: for the periodic flush once `flush_in_background` has returned, where it was read when that flush ended in `Database::flush`, and for a size-triggered one once its own transaction has committed. No periodic flush is due while the last one's commit has not been seen to land. A periodic flush can still follow a size-triggered one sooner than dcrd's would, when no durable commit has carried that one yet | A flush that is not durable must not postpone the one that is: with dcrd's stamp, size-triggered flushes on a slow link could keep the periodic flush from ever firing, and the durable cadence would fall to the write cache's 300 s. One that a durable commit has carried is as safe to count from as dcrd's, every one of which follows a durable flush of the block database, so it now postpones the periodic flush as dcrd's does; and the periodic flush no longer holds the thread that connects blocks for its commit. What an unclean stop during the initial sync can lose is about two minutes of processing and what is processed while the periodic flush's commit is under way. With `DCROXIDE_DB_SYNC_COMMIT` the periodic flush commits before it returns and is stamped then, as before. Measured on m3 on 2026-10-18, in default mainnet syncs from a dcrd v2.1.7 node on another machine on the same LAN, on top of the change that writes a flush's rows on the commit thread, with a thread made for each flush: the build that added the change took 266.90 s against 274.00 s by the means of three runs and two, 7.10 s less, at the edge of that session's ±6.5 s for a step between two runs and three, 4.93 s of it after the assume-valid block, and the flushes' foreground time (the flush log's `fg_ms`) fell to 2.6 to 3.2 s a sync from 5.5 and 6.4 s (docs/bench-ledger.md, "Rungs from a block server on the LAN, two changes held for their memory, and dcrd v2.1.7", builds I and J). On 2026-10-19 the code the repository holds, as build K, made 52 flushes inside the clock in each default sync from that server, every one of them handed over (docs/bench-ledger.md, "One commit thread for the life of the store, a sync that checks every script, and dcrd v2.1.7"). Whether the periodic flush came due in those syncs at all, and how often a size-triggered flush now postpones it, were not counted: the flush log's `explicit` no longer marks the periodic flush. Pinned by the chain's stamp tests (`a_periodic_flush_hands_its_commit_over_and_is_stamped_once_it_lands`, `a_durable_commit_that_carries_a_size_triggered_flush_moves_the_stamp_to_that_flush`, `a_size_triggered_flush_is_not_counted_until_its_own_transaction_is_durable`, `a_failed_periodic_commit_never_moves_the_stamp_and_the_next_write_is_refused`, `a_stamped_periodic_flush_survives_a_power_cut`), the database's commit-mark and `flush_in_background` tests (`tests/background_commit.rs` and the database's own tests) and the periodic sweeps of `tests/utxo_flush_crash_sweep.rs`, in both commit modes. |
| Metadata write cache default | 100 MiB (`defaultCacheSize`, `database/ffldb/dbcache.go:22`) | 300 MiB (`DCROXIDE_DB_OVERLAY` sets it) | At 100 MiB the cache's own trigger, at 70 MB retained, would fire about as often as the UTXO cache flushes did, so the row above would gain little; that was reckoned from the flush logs and not run. One sync each of a prototype on m3 on 2026-10-11 took 724.6 s at 300 MiB and 736.9 s at 200 MiB with the same peak memory, against 775.6 s for the code before it. With the exists-address index on, in one run on 2026-10-11, the ceiling tripped only the early commits of the sync, the index's participant most of the rest and the 120 s rule about one in ten (11, 38 and 5 of 54); since 2026-10-19 that rule counts from the last UTXO cache flush any durable commit carried, and how often it comes due in a sync has not been counted (*Periodic UTXO cache flush interval*). The larger default should also mean fewer durable commits where no UTXO cache flush happens and the rows go through the write cache, as in the header sync, down to the cache's 300 s interval (not measured); `DCROXIDE_DB_OVERLAY=100` restores the old ceiling there. A catch-up of the exists-address index is paced by its participant and does not change. `addblock` has no such setting and takes the default. While a flush's commit is under way on a thread of its own (the next row), the captured layers stay in the overlay beside the new rows, so the overlay can hold up to `max(cache_max_size + T, trigger + 2T)`, T being the largest single transaction and the trigger two thirds of the ceiling, where it held the trigger plus one transaction; a commit that would pass the ceiling waits for the commit under way, holding the writer, rather than flushing. |
| Where a cache flush's commit runs | the goroutine that holds the database's write lock commits every flush, the one `commitTx` makes included, before it releases the lock (`database/ffldb/dbcache.go:558-582`) | in the daemon, a flush that a commit's own check trips, or that the chain's periodic UTXO cache flush asks for (`Database::flush_in_background`), writes its rows and makes its redb commit on a thread named `dbcommit`, one for the open store, started by the first flush handed over and kept, asleep between flushes, until the close, with at most one flush under way on it (the `database` row). The committing thread captures the write cache, takes what the block-file sync owes, opens redb's write transaction and has the exists-address index seal the keys the flush will write, under the writer, and returns; the thread then syncs the block files, writes the write cache's rows into the transaction, then the index's, and commits, so the block files still reach the disk before the metadata that names them, as dcrd's `syncBlocks` does inside the same flush. Blocks the committing thread writes meanwhile are left owed to the next flush's sync: the list of files owed a sync holds each file's latest write generation, and a sync discharges a file only when no write has reached it since the plan was taken (`BlockStore::finish_sync`, `blockfile.rs:574`). The next capture, a `Database::flush` and the close wait for the flush under way on it, and the close then stops it. `Database::flush`, the close, a flush in which a participant has work without letting its `contribute` and `finished` be deferred and a flush for which the operating system refuses the thread write and commit on the calling thread. `DCROXIDE_DB_SYNC_COMMIT`, read once at startup: unset, the default, and nothing is logged; a positive whole number puts every commit back on the thread that ran the flush, under the writer, as dcrd places it, and starts no `dbcommit` thread; any other value, `0` included, is a warning and the default, and a value that is not valid Unicode reads as unset, with no warning, as `DCROXIDE_DB_CACHE`'s does. **Log lines**, verbatim, none of which dcrd has. `BCDB`, ERR, when syncing a flush's block files, writing its rows, the index's or its commit fails on that thread: `Background commit of the metadata write cache failed: E` (E the error). `DCRD`, INF, at startup: `Metadata write cache commits on the committing thread by DCROXIDE_DB_SYNC_COMMIT`. `DCRD`, WRN, at startup: `DCROXIDE_DB_SYNC_COMMIT=V is not a positive whole number; committing the metadata write cache on a thread of its own` (V the value without its surrounding whitespace). The library's default is the committing thread (`Options::background_commit` false), so addblock, `dcroxide-bench` and the tests that do not ask keep dcrd's placement | With a flush's rows and its redb commit on the committing thread every other writer, the thread that connects blocks and the index thread among them, waits for them, and in a sync those two make nearly every flush. What stays on the writer is what the commits made beside the flush depend on: the plan of a block-file sync that covers every block the captured rows name, and the index's keys set apart from those later commits hand over. Everything else moves: the block-file sync, writing the rows, the index's merges and journal, the commit, and the report of a failure in any of them (the two rows above), so a failed fsync, a failed insert or a damaged index row found while merging is now reported like a failed commit, by the next write, flush or close. The rows go into the transaction in the same order as on the committing thread, the overlay's and then the index's. Nothing is acknowledged as durable before its sync returned: every commit is still `Durability::Immediate`, and the calls that acknowledge durability wait for the flush under way on the thread; the chain counts a periodic UTXO cache flush only once `Database::is_durable` says its commit has landed. The 2026-08-16 form of this lost 9.5% on m1, where flushes took 68–71% of a sync and commits ran back to back; where they do again, `DCROXIDE_DB_SYNC_COMMIT=1` is the way back, and the flush log's `wait_ms` shows the condition. **Measured** on m3 on 2026-10-16, in default mainnet syncs to block 1,116,035 from a dcrd block server over loopback, as part of a build that is not the code of a commit (`4700cf1` with the changes of the seventeen commits to the node's code after it, the validation pool among them, as measured on 2026-10-13), against that build without it, four interleaved runs each: 389.55 s against 429.45 s by the medians, 39.9 s or 9.3% less and less in every pairing, at 27 s more process CPU time and about 256 MiB more peak resident memory to the tip (docs/bench-ledger.md). The code as committed, `88c46c3`, was measured on 2026-10-17 in the same way against itself with the variable set, four default runs against two: 396.25 s against 429.2 s by the medians, 32.95 s or 7.7% less and less in every pairing, at 32.3 s and 33.7 s more process CPU time and 205.5 and 254.5 MiB more peak resident memory to the tip, and both arms ended with the same UTXO set (docs/bench-ledger.md, "`88c46c3`, the background commit as committed, dcrd v2.1.7 and two preloaded allocators"). Only that machine's storage was measured. Writing the rows on the thread as well was measured on m3 on 2026-10-18, in default mainnet syncs from a dcrd v2.1.7 node on another machine on the same LAN, with a thread made for each flush: the build that added it took 274.00 s against 315.45 s for the build below it by the means of two runs each, 41.45 s less, beyond that session's ±7.1 s, and the flushes' foreground time (the flush log's `fg_ms`) fell to 5.5 and 6.4 s from 45.3 to 47.0 s in the runs of the two builds below it, but that build peaked at 6,835 and 7,200 MiB of resident memory against 3,195 and 3,338 MiB (docs/bench-ledger.md, "Rungs from a block server on the LAN, two changes held for their memory, and dcrd v2.1.7", builds G', H and I). The thread is kept for the life of the store rather than made for each flush for that memory: the rows and the commit it writes allocate most of the pages a flush leaves in redb's page cache, which outlive the flush, and a thread made for each flush could take a different allocator arena from one flush to the next and leave what the earlier flushes allocated stranded in the arenas it left; one thread kept for the store allocates from one arena. That is inferred: no run records which arena a thread used. With the thread kept, the rows on it were measured only together with the other change the bench ledger held on 2026-10-18, which hands the periodic UTXO cache flush to the thread too and counts it once its commit has landed (*Periodic UTXO cache flush interval*); the code the repository holds has both. On 2026-10-19, from the same server, a build of that code, K, peaked at 2,456 and 2,470 MiB in two runs, against 6,730 to 6,933 MiB in three runs of the same code with a thread made for each flush, alternated with them, and took 271.10 s against 273.63 s by the means, a difference that session's ±3.9 s does not resolve. Its four default runs took a mean of 271.68 s against 350.2 s and 350.6 s for its two with `DCROXIDE_DB_SYNC_COMMIT=1`, 78.7 s or 22.5% less, and peaked lower, at 2,456 to 2,470 MiB against 2,959 and 2,964 MiB (docs/bench-ledger.md, "One commit thread for the life of the store, a sync that checks every script, and dcrd v2.1.7"). The rows on the kept thread without that other change were not measured. The block-file sync on the thread was first run in syncs on 2026-10-19, in R1, the build the `internal/blockchain` row's *Transaction types* describes, which holds it and three other changes: in R1's four runs from the LAN server every one of the 52 flushes inside the clock was handed over, their foreground time was 0.016 to 0.030 s a run against 2.38 to 2.69 s in the four of a build of the tree of `797a213` alternated with them, and their block-file syncs, timed on `dbcommit`, took 2.59 to 2.98 s; R1's step, 6.80 s or 2.5% less by the means, is the four together and not the sync's own (docs/bench-ledger.md, "Where `797a213`'s block thread waits, a deeper read-ahead and a rung of four changes"). `DCROXIDE_DB_SYNC_COMMIT` is a variable and not a flag because the generated `-h` output is pinned byte for byte against dcrd's.
