Pinned by `tests/background_commit_env.rs` (the default, the variable, a malformed value, and on Linux that the default keeps one `dbcommit` thread, the same at every look from the first tripped flush until the daemon is stopped, and that the variable starts none) and the database's own tests (the `database` row). |
| Classifying a refusal beside a commit under way | the sync manager classifies a refused block or header as it comes back from `ProcessBlock` or `ProcessBlockHeader`, by whether it is a `blockchain.RuleError` (`internal/netsync/manager.go:1260-1265`, `:1639-1646`), and no commit of the database is under way then: dcrd's flush commits on the goroutine that holds the write lock | when the chain refuses a block, a header or a `reconsiderblock`, the node asks the store whether it has failed with `Database::is_fatal_settled` (`storage_failed`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/sync.rs:323`, called with the chain released), which first waits for a metadata flush under way on a thread of its own. An accepted block asks nothing and waits for nothing | redb refuses every uncached read from the first failed write of a flush's transaction on, in its rows or its commit, and the store is latched only when that flush has ended; a block connected in between can fail on a read that reports its error, and a latch read at once would call that a rule violation and blame the peer that sent a good block. A failed read of the UTXO set never reaches this: the chain aborts the process on it (the `database` row), as it does beside a failing commit of the index thread's flush. The wait moves such a refusal's report by up to the time the commit thread takes for one flush (its rows, the index's and the commit) and the hold of whichever writer is ahead of it, since the answer first takes the writer, and only for a refusal. Pinned by `tests/background_commit_latch.rs`: a block, a header and a reconsidered block refused during a failing commit are storage failures, a refusal beside a commit that lands is still a rule error, and an accepted block does not wait. |
| Nested bucket lookup, and the bucket index at open | `bucket.Bucket` reads the bucket's index row, `bidx<parent ID><name>`, through `fetchKey` on every call (`database/ffldb/db.go:598-614`, `:1073-1087`), and `dbCacheSnapshot.Get` answers a leveldb read error as a missing key (`database/ffldb/dbcache.go:308-311`), so a lookup whose row cannot be read finds no bucket. Of the metadata, an open reads only the write cursor row (`reconcileDB`, `database/ffldb/reconcile.go:62-77`, called last in `openDB`, `database/ffldb/db.go:2114`), through the same `Get`, so damage in the bucket index's pages is first met by a lookup, as a missing bucket | `Bucket::bucket` answers from a copy of the bucket index held in memory (`crates/dcroxide-database/src/bucketindex.rs`). It is read from the store when the database is opened or created, after the reconciliation; each commit applies its own bucket index rows to it under the hold of the cache lock that publishes its other rows to the overlay; a transaction takes it with its overlay and store snapshots under that lock; it is never written, so no row, table or file is added. While the store works a lookup answers what a read of the row through the same transaction's view answers, a writer's own uncommitted buckets included, which it still reads from its pending changes (held against that read by `a_lookup_is_the_read_of_its_row_over_random_histories`, `a_lookup_is_the_read_of_its_row_in_every_state` and `the_map_is_the_stores_after_a_power_cut_at_every_storage_operation`). Two answers differ from dcrd's. **(a) A row that cannot be read.** Where the row itself cannot be read from the disk, dcrd's lookup finds no bucket; the lookup here reads nothing and finds it. Reads of rows in the bucket meet the store's errors as any read does: once a read, a write other than a best-effort one, a length query or change, or a sync of the metadata store has failed, redb refuses every read of a page it does not hold for the rest of the handle's life (redb-4.3.0 `cached_file.rs:198-261`), and the first read of a row in the bucket that needs such a page meets the error: absence from `Bucket::get`, as ffldb reads it, the end of the store's side of a `Bucket::for_each` walk, and an error from `Bucket::try_get` and `Bucket::try_for_each`. After a failed operation on the metadata store that did not touch the row (a failed read of another page, a flush whose write or sync of that store failed: ENOSPC, a failed `fsync` of its file), dcrd's read of the row still succeeds and finds the bucket, as the lookup does; the read of the row this replaced answered "no such bucket" there whenever the row was in neither the transaction's pending changes nor the write cache and a page on its path was not in redb's cache. What still differs from dcrd there is redb's refusal of the rows themselves, recorded under "Metadata store after a failed durable write". `create_bucket` and `delete_bucket` still read the row, as before this change, and take a failed or refused read for a missing row, so within one write transaction (none begins after a failed flush) they can disagree with the lookup: `delete_bucket` reports `ErrBucketNotFound` for a bucket `bucket` finds, and `create_bucket` does not report `ErrBucketExists` for it but goes on to the bucket ID counter, creating the name again under a new ID or failing with `ErrCorruption` where the counter cannot be read either. `create_bucket_if_not_exists` looks the bucket up first and returns it, where with the read of the row it went on to that create; against dcrd's `CreateBucketIfNotExists` that is a difference only where the row itself cannot be read. **(b) The open.** `Database::open` and `Database::create` read the bucket index rows once and fail with the store's error if they cannot, classified as any store error is (`storage_error`, `crates/dcroxide-database/src/lib.rs:2635`: `ErrDriverSpecific` for the failed read itself, `ErrCorruption` for a store redb reports corrupted or one that has already failed an I/O operation), where dcrd's open has read only the write cursor. The daemon then logs `Unable to load block database: unable to open database: ` and the error (`unable to create database: ` for a database it was creating) and exits (`crates/dcroxide-node/src/bin/dcroxide.rs:2498`, `:2496`): only `ErrDbDoesNotExist` leads it to create a database, so a failed load cannot cause a re-creation | A nested bucket's ID changes only when a bucket is created or deleted, and the read of its row cost every caller that names a bucket for each row it reads or writes a search of the transaction's pending changes, of every overlay layer and of redb. Once redb has met a failure neither difference changes anything durable: redb then fails every write and sync, so no commit made with either answer reaches the store. A row whose page goes bad after the open sets off no failure if nothing else reads that page: the lookup finds its bucket, where dcrd's finds none, and commits that use it reach the store. The answer (a) gives is the one a read of the row already gave whenever the row was in the pending changes or the write cache, or its pages in redb's cache, and after a failure that did not touch the row it is dcrd's. Neither can be avoided while the lookup reads nothing: a load that left out the rows it could not read would answer "no such bucket" for buckets the store holds for the life of the handle, and falling back to reads of the rows after a failed load would not restore dcrd's answers, the failed read having left redb refusing every page it does not hold. Pinned in `bucketindex::tests` (`crates/dcroxide-database/src/bucketindex.rs`): (a) by `a_lookup_reads_nothing_from_the_store` (no store read for a lookup by a reader or a writer; with reads failing, the read of the row finds no bucket, the lookup finds it, and the reads of a row in it meet the error), `a_read_error_is_a_missing_row_to_a_create_and_a_delete_and_not_to_the_lookup` and `after_a_failed_flush_the_lookup_finds_a_bucket_whose_row_is_refused`; (b) by `an_open_that_cannot_read_the_bucket_index_fails` (an open whose reads start failing at any point either fails or holds the whole bucket index; no test makes a create's load fail), and that the load the open and the create make reads the bucket index and not the rows that sort before it, by `the_load_reads_the_bucket_index_and_not_the_rows_before_it`. |
| `invalidateblock` / `reconsiderblock` RPCs | live handlers; the chain's notifications drive mempool, index and websocket maintenance whoever reorganized | Live handlers since the 2026-09-23 review. `NodeRpcChain` calls `Chain::invalidate_block`/`reconsider_block`, then drains the daemon's `ChainNtfnHandler` with the chain mutex released, as the sync adapter does after a processed block. | An operator's `invalidateblock`/`reconsiderblock` therefore reaches the flush placement recorded under Open ("`invalidateblock` and `reconsiderblock` flush the block index before their final reorganization"), not only `force_head_reorganization`. Pinned by `review_rpc_seams::invalidate_and_reconsider_move_the_tip`. |
| Tor SOCKS RESOLVE: two implementations, one live | one `TorLookupIP` (`addrmgr/tordns.go:44-137`), reached through `cfg.lookup`/`cfg.onionlookup` with `context.Background()` and no socket deadline, reading each reply head with a single unchecked `conn.Read`, and accepting any IPv6 answer longer than the six-byte minimum — `if replyLen <= 4+2 { ... }` then `addr = net.IP(reply[0:replyLen-2])`, so a nine-byte reply yields a seven-byte `net.IP` | two. `dcroxide-addrmgr`'s `tor_lookup_ip` reproduces all of the above exactly over a `TorTransport` seam, returning `Vec<Vec<u8>>` so an off-length answer is representable; it is reached only by its frozen vectors. The daemon calls `dcroxide-node`'s own (`src/socks.rs`), which reads the reply heads with `read_full`, carries one absolute deadline across the whole exchange, the proxy connect included (a proxy name with several addresses has the deadline split among them as Go's `partialDeadline` would split a context deadline, which dcrd's `context.Background()` lookup never has), and rejects an IPv6 answer whose address half is not exactly sixteen bytes — with dcrd's own "invalid IPV6 address" text | The daemon's lookup returns `std::net::IpAddr`, which cannot hold a seven-byte address, so the length check is forced by the type rather than chosen; dcrd's malformed `net.IP` fails to render or connect downstream anyway, so the observable difference is an error here against a useless address there. The deadline and the checked reads run in the port's favour and are deliberate: dcrd sets no deadline, so its Tor lookup can pin a caller indefinitely. The port's callers pass the bound: a minute for connect targets, seeder sources and `getaddednodeinfo`, 30 s for the server's other host lookups, `--externalip` names among them, and the dial timeout when marking a dial attempt. A lookup that runs it out fails with the dial timeout text or Go's `i/o timeout`, which dcrd never reports there. Both halves pinned — the daemon's by `the_daemon_resolver_refuses_an_ipv6_answer_dcrd_would_accept`, dcrd's by `seed_tor_vectors`. |
| Mixpool key exchanges per identity | a key exchange needs only its sender's own pair request known and may reference arbitrary other hashes (`acceptKE`, `mixing/mixpool/mixpool.go:1697` at `382ff4dd`), so one accepted pair request can mint sessions, each held until the pair request goes; since `ffb42adf` an identity has one key exchange per session, and since `38811b0b` at most 896 messages over its pair request's life (`maxMessagesPerIdentity`, `:46-51` at `382ff4dd`, counted over `messagesByIdentity` with removed messages included), which leaves room for 128 sessions' worth; since `3ce5cc41` and `874f6772` a key exchange's epoch must also be a whole minute of the last twenty (`:1685-1692` at `382ff4dd`), which bounds when sessions can be minted, not how many | both of dcrd's limits, and `MAX_KES_PER_IDENTITY` = 64 key exchanges held per identity at once; the 65th is refused with a non-bannable rule error, neither pooled nor relayed | A tighter memory bound per identity than dcrd's: with the same-type rule on un-orphaned messages (next row) a session holds at most about 1 MiB of one identity's messages, so the cap holds an identity to about 65 MiB, where dcrd's 896-message limit admits about 447 MiB, mostly slot reservations un-orphaned in bulk. Raised from 32 to 64 for honest headroom: a wallet forms one run per epoch tick for its pair request's life plus in-epoch reruns, and each run's key exchange is held until the pair request goes, which comes to about 26 runs on mainnet (13 blocks, about six and a half epochs with room for four runs each) and, since dcrd's client moved testnet3 to 10-second stages (`55892be8`), about 60 on testnet3 (31 blocks, about 20 epochs with room for three). All of these figures are arithmetic from the limits and timings, not observed. Pinned by `key_exchange_flood_stops_at_the_per_identity_cap` and `sixty_runs_of_one_pair_request_fit_under_the_cap` (`crates/dcroxide-mixing/tests/mixpool_expiry.rs`). |
| Un-orphaned mix messages | `reconsiderOrphans` accepts every orphan by the identity carrying the new key exchange's session ID (`mixpool.go:1605-1654` at `382ff4dd`) without the direct path's same-type conflict check (`:1310-1323` at `382ff4dd`), stopping only at the identity's 896-message limit (`:1641-1644` at `382ff4dd`), so up to an orphan pool's worth (`maxOrphans` = 250) of one message type is pooled and relayed for one identity and session | dcrd's message limit is checked first and leaves the orphan in place, as in dcrd; then the conflict check: the earliest-received orphan of each type is accepted, and later ones of a type the identity already holds in the session are dropped from the orphan pool; orphan key exchanges stay orphans there, as in dcrd | Without it the per-identity key exchange cap would bound the other messages only through dcrd's 896-message limit: about 447 MiB of slot reservations per identity rather than about 65 MiB (arithmetic), held for about an hour. Checking dcrd's limit first keeps the two answers equal wherever the limit decides. Honest senders send one message of each type per session and never reach it. Residual, as in dcrd: one UTXO can back several identities (a pair request that claims an outpoint another pair request already claims is accepted when its expiry is later), and the recently-removed cache (8,500 entries for a minute) is bounded by count, not bytes. Pinned by `un_orphaned_messages_keep_one_per_type_and_session` (`crates/dcroxide-mixing/tests/review_mixpool.rs`) and, for the order of the two checks, `orphans_stay_orphans_once_the_limit_is_reached` (`crates/dcroxide-mixing/tests/mixpool_acceptance_rules.rs`). |
| Mixpool scheduled expiry | `ExpireMessagesInBackground` latches the *first* height armed (`if p.expireHeight == 0`, `mixpool.go:434-436`) and its goroutine sleeps to the second epoch boundary after the call (`waitForExpiry`, `:447-452`), so an expired pair request survives at least one full epoch after the block that expired it | the latch keeps the *newest* height and the daemon's epoch ticker drains it at the next boundary (`run_epoch_tick`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/mixnode.rs`) | One shared ticker can coalesce several block connects into one pass, and a first-wins latch would then expire against a stale height; tracking the newest height only removes pair requests already unusable at the tip. Consequence: an expired pair request and the sessions built on it can be dropped up to one epoch sooner than dcrd drops them, so for that epoch the port may stop serving messages dcrd peers still relay. dcrd's own mix client expires its pool on every epoch tick with the latest height (`mixing/mixclient/client.go:938`, `:1108-1109`), so wallets stop pairing them at the same boundary. Pinned by `expiry_latch_tracks_the_newest_height` (`crates/dcroxide-mixing/tests/mixpool_expiry.rs`). |
| UTXO cache entry for an output added again after a disconnect | `UtxoCache.addEntry` marks an entry fresh, as never written to the backend, whenever its map holds nothing for the outpoint (`internal/blockchain/utxocache.go:276-292`, the test at `:288-289`, at `382ff4dd`). `disconnectTransactions` builds each output of an undone transaction that the view lacks from the transaction itself, without looking at the cache or the backend (`utxoviewpoint.go:444-503`), and a later transaction of the same view can add it again: a block that disapproves its parent and reincludes a transaction of it (the undo, `disconnectDisapprovedBlock`, `:582-611`, runs before the block's own tree), or the detach of such a block, whose redo of the parent adds the parent's copy back (`:1064`). After a restart, or once a flush has evicted the entry by height, the cache holds nothing for the output while the backend holds the undone block's row, so the entry added again is fresh, and a spend of it before the next flush records no delete (`utxocache.go:408-414`) | `UtxoView::disconnect_transactions` records every output it marks spent, and `Chain::commit_view` adds such an output without the fresh bit when the cache has no slot for it (`cache_add_entry`, `crates/dcroxide-blockchain/src/process.rs`). Over an explicit spent marker or a cached entry, and for every output no disconnect marked spent, the commit follows dcrd. The view's entries and the zero-confirmation set are not touched | The observable difference: after a restart, or after a flush that evicted the entry, dcrd can keep a row that a node that kept the entry cached has deleted. The row reads as unspent again after the flush, so the restarted dcrd node accepts a later block that spends it, with fraud proof data naming the undone block's copy, and a node that kept the entry cached rejects that block. The port keeps the two consistent: a spend before the flush leaves a tombstone, and the flush deletes the row. Which blocks a node that did not restart accepts is unchanged, since the view that validation reads is the same, and a spent tombstone and an absent marker both read as spent until the flush. On the disconnect side the live detach forces a flush before anything can spend the entry added again, so there only the startup catch-up's detach, which flushes on the usual triggers, can reach it. When the backend has no such row, the flush's delete removes nothing (`Bucket::delete` of a missing key is not an error, and redb's `remove` returns `None`). Cost: one set insert per output a disconnect marks spent, and a probe of an empty set per added entry for a block that approves its parent. Pinned by `after_a_restart_a_reincluded_output_spent_before_the_next_flush_leaves_the_backend`, `after_an_eviction_a_reincluded_output_spent_before_the_next_flush_leaves_the_backend`, `after_a_restart_a_detached_reinclusion_spent_before_the_next_flush_leaves_the_backend` (a live detach with the flushes off, which reaches the commit the catch-up's detach makes) and the control that keeps the entries cached (`crates/dcroxide-blockchain/tests/disapproval_round_trips.rs`), and by `process::tests::an_output_a_disconnect_marked_spent_is_not_fresh_over_a_missing_slot` and `utxoview::tests::a_disconnect_records_the_outputs_it_marks_spent_until_the_commit`. |

### Known remaining gaps

Tracked rather than hidden; see [SECURITY.md](../SECURITY.md).

Two kinds of entry live here and they call for opposite actions, so each now
sits under its own heading. Reading the whole section as a work list sent
three separate investigations at settled entries -- the SOCKS isolation
credentials, `--maxsameip`, and nearly the mempool's modulo reductions -- each
of which ended in "this already matches dcrd, leave it" only after a full dig.
An entry moves between the two subsections when the code moves, not when the
prose is rewritten.

#### Accepted: the port matches dcrd here

Closing one of these would introduce a divergence, which this project treats as
a security bug in its own right (SECURITY.md, *Scope*). They are recorded
because the behaviour is worth knowing about -- an unbounded structure, a
resource cost, a deliberate departure that is settled -- not because anyone
should act on them. Where a bullet reads like a complaint, the complaint is
against upstream and the port is faithful.

- **Panic policy: abort, deliberately unlike dcrd.** `sync.Mutex` does not
  poison and dcrd recovers per goroutine, so a panicking goroutine there
  costs one request. Rust's mutexes poison, so one thread's panic takes
  down every other consumer of that lock — and the RPC layer's
  `catch_unwind` then kept the process alive answering canned errors, so
  the node wedged while looking healthy and `Restart=on-failure` never
  fired. `[profile.release]` now sets `panic = "abort"`: a consensus
  daemon cannot reason about state a panic left half-mutated, so a clean
  restart beats continuing. Dev and test builds keep unwinding (the
  harness needs it, and several suites use `#[should_panic]`), which also
  makes the remaining `catch_unwind` guards inert in release. Pinned by
  `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/panic_policy.rs`, which fails if the
  setting is dropped. Operators must run under a supervisor. The helpers
  of the script pool (*Script checks of a connected block* under
  Deliberate divergences) hold `dcroxide-blockchain`'s first
  `catch_unwind` outside its tests (`scriptpool.rs:1248`), inert under
  `panic = "abort"` like the rest: in a release build a panic in a script
  check aborts on a helper as on any other thread, as a panic in dcrd's
  `validateHandler`, which nothing recovers, ends the process. In an
  unwinding build the helper catches the panic of a check, stops the
  batch, hands the payload to the submitting thread and keeps serving.
  The submitting thread then panics anew with
  `a script check panicked on a pool helper: <message>` when the payload
  is a `&str` or a `String`, so the panic hook runs twice, on the helper
  with the original location and on the submitting thread, where a test's
  output is collected; any other payload is resumed unchanged. A batch in
  which a check panicked returns no verdict, whatever another of its
  checks returned. Pinned by `a_panic_in_a_check_reaches_the_submitter`,
  `a_helper_panic_is_raised_again_with_its_message` and
  `a_recorded_failure_does_not_stand_in_for_a_helper_panic`
  (`scriptpool::tests`); the abort on a helper is not exercised by a test.
  A second `catch_unwind` guards the units of a lane (`lane_work`,
  `scriptpool.rs:1043`), inert under `panic = "abort"` too: in an
  unwinding build a unit of signature pre-verification that panics
  withdraws its block and the helper keeps serving, the panic hook having
  run on the helper; nothing waits for the block and no verdict depends on
  it (*Signature pre-verification ahead of the block thread*). Pinned by
  `a_panicking_lane_unit_leaves_the_helper_serving` (`scriptpool::tests`).
  The UTXO view's and cache's invariant panics carry dcrd's texts (the
  view's missing input and the commit's zero-confirmation check since
  `e60cedc5`, the grandfathered output's removal since `96a55a74`, and the
  view's already-spent input, every check of `assertBlockUtxoView` and the
  commit's unspent cache entry check since `22fd2431`), but they do not
  reach the log: dcrd's `panicf` writes the text to its log at the
  critical level before it panics (`internal/blockchain/chain.go:61-65`),
  so dcrd's log ends with a `[CRT] CHAN` line, while the port's text
  reaches the operator only through Rust's default panic hook on standard
  error, as the chain's `LogLevel` has no critical level
  (`notifications.rs`).

- **A `SigHashAll` signature hash at block connect takes its prefix hash from
  the transaction hash, a path dcrd ships switched off.** dcrd's
  `calcSignatureHash` takes a cached prefix hash and its three signature
  opcodes would pass `vm.tx.CachedTxHash()` for `SigHashAll`, behind
  `optimizeSigVerification`, a constant set to `false` with a warning that the
  optimization has not been vetted (`txscript/opcode.go:27-32`, `:2484-2489`,
  `:2717-2722`, `:2865-2873`; `sighash.go:296-305`). So dcrd serializes and
  hashes a transaction's whole prefix once per signature. The port takes the
  path in the block connect checks, where every transaction of the block has
  already been hashed, by the processing call or, for a block that a
  connection's `peer-reader` thread read, before the block reached the chain
  (*Reading ahead of the block handler* under Deliberate divergences), and
  nowhere else. Verdicts, error kinds and texts,
  and signature cache keys are unchanged. Two things differ from dcrd's
  disabled code: the hash is an argument and never a cache on the transaction
  (dcrd's `CachedTxHash` is stale once a transaction is modified), and it is
  keyed exactly as dcrd's condition is (masked hash type `SigHashAll`,
  `SigHashAnyOneCanPay` clear), not on every hash type for which the bytes
  happen to coincide. Under debug assertions `BlockTxHashes::assert_for`
  re-hashes the block and every engine compares its hash with its
  transaction's; a release build checks that there is one hash per
  transaction and otherwise rests on the pairing rule of `blockhashes.rs`.
  For a tree the script pool runs (*Script checks of a connected block*
  under Deliberate divergences), every build also compares by address the
  block and the hashes the pool is handed with the ones being checked
  (`ScriptRunner::assert_for`, `validate.rs:6038`), and each item's
  transaction and hash with the tree's own at the item's position and its
  input index with its transaction's inputs (`OwnedBatch::new`, `:6144`),
  since a helper rebuilds each item from those positions. Pinned by
  `the_shared_block_must_be_the_block_being_connected` and
  `the_shared_hashes_must_be_the_hashes_being_checked` (`process::tests`)
  and by `a_wrong_transaction_index_is_refused`,
  `a_hash_from_the_other_tree_is_refused`,
  `an_item_without_its_hash_is_refused`,
  `a_hash_from_a_copy_of_the_hashes_is_refused`,
  `an_item_of_a_copy_of_the_block_is_refused` and
  `an_input_index_out_of_range_is_refused` (`validate::tests`). Measured on
  m3 on 2026-10-12, one default mainnet sync each: 607.7 s with the path
  against 628.0 s for the code before it, the 89,438 blocks validated in full
  taking 121.6 s against 140.3 s and the node 1,058.0 s of user CPU time
  against 1,298.6 s. Pinned by: a property test over random transactions, all
  256 hash type bytes and every input index including out of range (same hash
  or same error with and without the hash); a negative test that a wrong hash
  changes the result for exactly `0x01`, `0x21`, `0x41` and `0x61`;
  `sighash.json` and 3,000 oracle rounds through the hash-taking path;
  `script_tests.json`, `tx_valid`, `tx_invalid` and the engine and signing
  differentials each run both ways; mainnet signatures
  (`tests/mainnet_signatures.rs`); a hand-signed block with several signatures
  per transaction in both trees, a bad signature rejected with the same error
  either way; and the full-block battery, whose signature hashes go through
  the path with each engine asserting its hash (4,995 of them by a count made
  once with a probe, which no test keeps). CI runs the two crates once more
  with debug assertions off, so that the arms showing what a release build
  does with a hash that is not the transaction's are executed.

- **`dbCache` overlay layering.** dcrd's `dbCache` is an immutable treap, so a
  reader's snapshot is O(1) and shares structure with the writer. The port
  reaches the same property with a layered overlay rather than a treap: an
  ordered stack of immutable `BTreeMap` layers, newest first, where a snapshot
  clones only the (one-to-three-deep) list of layer handles. A commit writes
  into the newest layer in place while the cache is its only owner; a reader
  holding a snapshot freezes that layer and the commit seals a fresh one above
  it, so no transaction ever copies the overlay to make progress. The first
  commit that finds no snapshot naming the frozen layer folds the fresh one
  back into it, unless a flush in flight captured that layer, and goes on
  folding while the next layer down is unnamed too, so above any layers a
  flush in flight captured the stack is one layer whenever no reader overlaps
  a commit. While readers keep layers frozen, layers merge back geometrically
  (the newest merges down once it reaches half the entry count below it).
  Either merge walks the smaller of its two layers into the larger, and both
  keep the lookup path — every layer is probed before it can answer "not
  cached" — shallow rather than pinned at the eight-layer ceiling. Because a
  sealed layer cannot be mutated, a key rewritten under a reader is held once
  per layer, so the size accounting counts *retained* entries, not the
  overlay's logical size; a fold drops the older copy at the first commit no
  reader overlaps, so the count usually comes back to a single treap's sooner
  than under the geometric merge alone, though at some commits it sits higher,
  where a geometric merge comes later because the layer below grew by a fold,
  and a flush the size triggers can come at a different commit than it did
  before the fold. Each entry is counted at dcrd's treap node size:
  `nodeFieldsSize` (72) plus its key and value bytes, for tombstones as for
  live values. For a single layer that is exactly the figure dcrd's
  `needsFlush` gates the 100 MiB ceiling on. The 72 is near this port's memory
  without matching it: a `BTreeMap` overlay entry holds about 97 bytes beyond
  its key and value, 74 before each key also held its first sixteen bytes
  inline for the layer's searches (`LayerKey`, which compares those bytes as
  two big-endian words and falls through to the whole key when they are equal,
  an order identical to the bytes'). The counted figure stays dcrd's. Counting
  a rewritten key once (as a single treap legitimately does) put the figure
  ~5× under the bytes actually held. Counting key and value bytes without the
  per-entry term put small-row overlays 2–4× under (an `existsaddridx` row was
  a 25-byte key and an empty value before layout 3). Pinned by `dbcache.rs`'s
  own suite, which sizes entries as treap nodes
  (`entries_are_sized_as_dcrds_treap_nodes`), asserts the running total
  against what the layers hold, exercises the flush against a genuinely
  three-deep stack, fails if the merge rule is deleted, and fails if a layer a
  reader split off is not folded back once the reader lets go, or a stack
  split three deep is not folded back to one layer in one commit
  (`a_layer_a_reader_split_off_folds_back_once_the_reader_lets_go`,
  `a_stack_three_deep_folds_to_one_at_the_first_commit_no_reader_overlaps`,
  `a_reader_over_one_commit_a_block_leaves_one_layer_to_capture`), or if a
  merge takes another map or reclaims other bytes than the plain merge
  (`a_merge_walks_the_smaller_layer_and_the_newer_entry_wins`), or if layers
  committed beside a flush in flight do not fold among themselves above the
  layer it captured (`layers_committed_beside_a_flush_fold_among_themselves`),
  or a reader loses its view while the layers around it fold
  (`readers_keep_their_view_while_the_layers_around_them_fold`), or if a
  layer's key order departs from its bytes' or a lookup or walk departs from a
  map of byte keys (`layer_keys_order_as_their_bytes_do`,
  `a_layer_looks_up_and_walks_as_a_map_of_byte_keys`). One boundary: a
  reader's snapshot can name a layer the stack has already merged away, and
  those bytes stay allocated until the reader drops — uncounted, because they
  belong to a transaction's lifetime rather than the overlay's, which is where
  dcrd's accounting draws the same line.

- **One redb table where dcrd has two databases, at 1.738x its own payload
  against goleveldb's 1.081x.** dcrd keeps chain metadata in a goleveldb at
  `<datadir>/blocks_ffldb/metadata` and the utxo set in a second goleveldb at
  `<datadir>/utxodb`; the port keeps the whole ffldb keyspace, utxo set
  included, in one redb table per accepted
  [ADR-0004](adr/0004-storage-backend.md). At the mainnet tip that costs
  disk: 32.06 GiB against dcrd's 23.73 GiB, measured 2026-07 under redb 2.6.3,
  and 33.58 against 23.69 when the pair was re-measured 2026-08-15 under
  4.1.0 with composition verified on both sides. With the height-first
  keys and the exists-address index's own layout (both below) it was
  27.90 GiB against dcrd v2.1.6's 24.16 at block 1,116,035 on m3 in
  [bench-ledger.md](bench-ledger.md)
  (2026-10), 1.15x, with a 10.01 GiB `metadata.redb`. The breakdown that
  follows is of the 2026-07 and 2026-08 stores and has not been repeated
  for the current layout.
  The block bytes are consensus data and match to within a mebibyte
  (17.579 GiB against 17.580), so the whole 8.33 GiB gap is metadata — one
  14.483 GiB `metadata.redb` against dcrd's 6.045 + 0.108 GiB. None of it is
  data dcroxide keeps and dcrd does not: on matched composition the payload is
  equal across fifteen buckets to a 54-byte residual on 6.06 GB (as measured
  2026-08-11, before the 2026-10-08 height-first keys added four bytes to each
  key in five per-block buckets, at most about 22 MB at the tip; see "Seven
  per-block buckets" below and
  [ADR-0010](adr/0010-height-first-block-keys.md)). Compression
  is not the reason either: dcrd opens its chain databases with
  `opt.NoCompression` (`database/ffldb/db.go:2095`,
  `internal/blockchain/utxobackend.go:365`), so both sides store raw bytes
  and the gap is per-key structural overhead. Over 5.65 GiB of payload in
  76,302,003 rows redb's live B-tree is 9.79 GiB on the 2026-07 store and
  9.82 on the matched-composition one — 0.69 GiB of per-pair and
  branch overhead plus 3.44 GiB of intra-page slack at 64.86% fill — which is
  the 1.738x above, 1.726x on dcrd's own write schedule. The 4.69 GiB of
  allocated-but-free pages is reused working space and is withdrawn as a
  comparison metric: it swings several-fold between runs of the same
  configuration while the live tree stays pinned. That is also why the live
  tree is the figure to quote and the whole file is not — matched
  composition has produced 14.00, 14.48 and 16.00 GiB apparent, all of them
  under redb 2.6.3 and none re-measured since the 4.1.0 upgrade. Compaction is
  not a way out: its yield varies by more than an order of magnitude
  between stores of the same chain (33x on apparent size, 20x on the
  consumed bytes this file no longer quotes)
  (0.12 GiB in 598.5 s on one, 3.950 GiB apparent in 137.9 s on another)
  because it relocates pages forward and truncates rather than repacking —
  fill is 0.646166 before and after, to the digit — so it is not an operator
  knob. A sorted copy-out rebuild shrinks the file only by clearing free
  pages, and packs *worse* doing it: 58.29% fill, live tree 10.92 GiB against
  9.79. The 2.48 GiB it was once credited with was recorded in consumed bytes,
  which the 2026-08-12 correction rejects. The throughput cost is a separate
  engine property: a copy-on-write B-tree does no background work, so commit
  cost tracks tree size, where goleveldb's LSM commit is O(dirty) and defers
  the rest to background compaction. Syncing mainnet from genesis in
  2026-07 the port
  spent 80.1% and 82.4% of wall time (two runs) inside progress stalls over
  20 s, while dcrd stalled 0 times in 754 windows. The node was flush-bound
  under fast ingest, which was the leading explanation for the initial block
  download gap — re-measured 2026-08-15 at **1.29x**, superseding the ~2.2x
  quoted through 2026-07. (That is m1, from genesis, with the
  exists-address index in dcrd's layout. On m2, slower storage, dcrd
  synced from genesis 1.40x as fast as the port with the height-first
  keys below. The
  index's own layout, also below, then took the 200,000-block tail after
  block 916,000 to a median of 482 blk/s with the index on, where dcrd ran
  it at 146–148 blk/s two days earlier. That cross-session pair is
  context, not a ratio. From genesis with both changes, on m3 on
  2026-10-09 and 2026-10-10, the port synced to block 1,116,035 in a
  median of 17.6 minutes against 47.7 for dcrd v2.1.6 (then the latest
  release; `6f6cf21b`, then the pin, took 47.8 and 48.5 minutes there
  later on 2026-10-10), 2.71x as
  fast, with 15–16% of its wall time in metadata flushes
  ([bench-ledger.md](bench-ledger.md), "Daemon against daemon, each
  from the other"). With the exists-address index off in both, one run
  each, the port was 1.42x as fast, behind dcrd up to the assume-valid
  block and ahead after it (the same ledger, "The parity commit, the
  index off, two levers and a full replay"). On 2026-10-11, after six
  changes to block processing and to when the node commits, three
  default syncs took a median of 12.1 minutes with 15% of the wall time
  in flushes, and one with the index off 10.2 minutes; dcrd v2.1.6 took
  48.1 minutes in one run that evening, 3.96x in one session, and 3.93x
  and 2.04x against its figures above are across sessions (the same
  ledger, "Five changes on the block-processing
  thread" and "Shared commits for UTXO-cache flushes, the filesystem's
  share and a delayed link"). On 2026-10-12, on a quiet machine,
  through eleven further commits (`7f99184` to `b4ceb1c`: the batch
  of four in the `database` row, the three in the
  `internal/blockchain/indexers` row, the `SigHashAll` prefix hash
  above, the block download window, the block hand-off and the
  read-ahead stage), one default sync at each of four steps took
  673.8 s (`a83f9a1`), 628.0 s (`6271436`), 607.7 s (`3a367e5`) and
  612.4 s (`540b203`), where the code before them (`b4a5d2d`) took
  728.5 s in one run that morning. Two default syncs of the
  read-ahead build as it was before its review's fixes (*Reading
  ahead of the block handler* under Deliberate divergences) then took
  494.6 s and 490.8 s, 8.2 minutes, with 21% of the wall time in
  flushes. The code as committed at `b4ceb1c` has not been run in a
  sync. dcrd was not run that day: against its 48.1 minutes of the
  evening before, those two are 5.83x and 5.88x as fast, across
  sessions (the same ledger, "Eleven changes, a background commit not
  kept and a pool gate"). On 2026-10-17 `88c46c3` took a median of
  396.25 s, 6.6 minutes, over four default syncs against 2,944.4 s and
  2,926.4 s for dcrd v2.1.7, 7.43x and 7.39x in one session, its flushes
  holding the threads that sync for 11.9% of the wall (the same ledger,
  "`88c46c3`, the background commit as committed, dcrd v2.1.7 and two
  preloaded allocators"). On 2026-10-19, from a dcrd v2.1.7 node on
  another machine on the same LAN, in a series of its own, a build of
  the code of `797a213` took a mean of 271.68 s over four default syncs
  against 3,014.6 s and 3,033.4 s for dcrd v2.1.7, 11.13x in one
  session, its flushes in the foreground on the threads that sync for
  2.5 to 2.8 s of each (the same ledger, "One commit thread for the life
  of the store, a sync that checks every script, and dcrd v2.1.7"). On
  2026-10-25, from the same server and with transparent huge pages at
  `always` where those runs had `madvise`, a build of `dfa77fe`, whose
  node code is that of `f37d763`, took a mean of 251.58 s over four
  default syncs against 2,997.7 s and 2,972.5 s for dcrd v2.1.7, 11.87x
  in one session, its flushes in the foreground on the threads that sync
  for 0.02 to 0.03 s of each (the same ledger, "`dfa77fe` against dcrd
  v2.1.7, with the exists-address index on and off"). So
  the rest of this passage explains the gap as it was measured on m1 in
  2026-08. It has not been re-measured there, and on m3 the node is not
  flush-bound.) A task-state decomposition on 2026-08-15 measured the
  mechanism: the port drives **11.7x** the kernel-side storage work dcrd does
  (mostly dm-crypt writeback) and blocks **30x more per GiB written**, while
  dcrd writes 1.16x more bytes at 1.74x the rate — so it is the write shape,
  not the volume, and most of the cost is paid in kernel threads rather than
  the port's own. Its share of sync wall time is measured too: the port is
  **fully stalled — zero runnable threads — for 34.6% of block-sync wall
  time** against dcrd's 0.9%, rising to 50.9% above block 900,000 as the tree
  grows, and removing all of it would bring both implementations to the same
  ~346 blk/s. That supersedes the 18% derived from an identical-engine
  `--addrindex` replay (863 s of 4,767), which validates every block where the
  daemon skips ~93% under assume-valid. Attributed 2026-08-16 by pairing the
  sampler with dcroxide's own flush observer: **90–98% of the stalled time is
  inside a metadata-flush window**, and the stall is **48% of wall** once the
  sampler's own starvation is corrected (34.6% was a count-weighting
  artifact). The counterfactual is not quotable as a prize — removing only the
  commit stall projects a rate faster than dcrd's, so the model is too
  generous. The
  free pages are copy-on-write churn and nothing more: an earlier note here
  suspected that `Database::begin` taking a redb read transaction for the life
  of every ffldb transaction (`lib.rs:3321`) pinned them, since redb will not
  reclaim a freed page past the oldest live read. Measured, that shape costs
  nothing — a reader opened before a write and still live at its commit
  produces a byte-identical file to no reader at all, because the next
  commit's reader is younger. A control run with no reader in the process
  reached a larger free fraction than the real file does. Only a reader
  spanning *many* commits hurts, and none exists: no type in the workspace
  stores a `Transaction`, so every read transaction's lifetime is bounded by
  one call frame. ADR-0004's 2026-07-26 amendment carries the full
  measurement record: both data directories side by side, the four-way
  throughput matrix, the per-bucket payload breakdown, the compaction and
  rebuild results, and the redb 2.6.3 mechanisms that bound the fill.

- **Seven per-block buckets are keyed by big-endian height first, so the
  chain database is version 15 where dcrd's is 14.** dcrd keys four of
  them by block hash and two by little-endian height, and ffldb keys its
  internal block index by hash. Each of the seven gains a row per block,
  so in dcrd's layout the few thousand blocks of one metadata flush land on
  about one cold B-tree leaf per block per bucket, and redb copies every
  leaf a flush touches, branch path included. The port keys all seven so
  that consecutive blocks are neighbours and a flush appends at each
  bucket's right edge ([ADR-0010](adr/0010-height-first-block-keys.md)).
  The rows' values are dcrd's, byte for byte.

  | bucket | dcrd key | dcroxide key | encoder |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `spendjournalv3` | hash (`chainio.go:767`, `:814`, `:821`) | height (BE) ‖ hash | `chaindb::block_row_key` |
  | `gcsfilters` | hash (`chainio.go:851`, `:877`, `:885`) | height (BE) ‖ hash | `chaindb::block_row_key` |
  | `hdrcmts` | hash (`chainio.go:980`, `:997`) | height (BE) ‖ hash | `chaindb::block_row_key` |
  | `treasury` | hash (`treasury.go:235`, `:244`) | height (BE) ‖ hash | `chaindb::block_row_key` |
  | `stakeblockundo` | height (LE) (`internal/ticketdb/chainio.go:483`, `:498`, `:509`) | height (BE) | `stakedb::height_key` |
  | `ticketsinblock` | height (LE) (`internal/ticketdb/chainio.go:572`, `:588`, `:599`) | height (BE) | `stakedb::height_key` |
  | `ffldb-blockidx` | hash (`database/ffldb/db.go:1142`, `:1239`, `:1717`) | height (BE) ‖ hash | `block_idx_key` in `dcroxide-database` |

  The height-first key is dcrd's own `blockidxv3` key (`blockIndexKey`,
  `chainio.go:311`). The reason is measured, not modelled: syncing with
  these keys ran **26% faster** over a 200,000-block tail (a median of 98.5
  against 78.1 blk/s, all four runs faster than all five base runs) and
  **1.74x** as fast from genesis to block 916,000, writing about 10% fewer
  bytes, with the data directory no larger
  ([bench-ledger.md](bench-ledger.md), "Height-first per-block keys").
  The format is internal to this store. No wire message, RPC result or
  file another program reads carries these keys, and nothing but dcroxide
  reads the store: it already refuses a dcrd data directory (the
  `database` row). The height costs four bytes on each key of the five
  hash-keyed buckets, at most about 22 MB at the mainnet tip, so the
  payload the "One redb table" entry above measured equal to dcrd's on
  2026-08-11 is no longer equal to the byte. Every lookup now needs the
  block's height. Every caller has the block's index node or the block,
  whose header records it (a node's height is its header's), and the one
  that has neither, the RPC transaction lookup, whose index entry names
  only the block hash, takes it from the block index
  (`indexes::NodeRpcDb`). A lookup under another height misses rather
  than reading another block's row. No code walks any of the seven
  buckets in key order, so the new order changes no result.

  **The version.** `CURRENT_DATABASE_VERSION` is 15: dcrd's 14
  (`chainio.go:29`) plus this layout. So CHAN's startup line reads
  "Blockchain database version info: chain: 15, compression: 1, block
  index: 3, spend journal: 3" where dcrd's reads "chain: 14". Bumping the
  version, rather than recording the layout in a row of its own, makes a
  build from before the change refuse a directory written after it,
  through the downgrade check every build has: "the current blockchain
  database is no longer compatible with this version of the software
  (15 > 14)". The other direction is the port's own. Where dcrd runs
  `upgradeDB` on an older version (`chainio.go:1676`), the port refuses
  it before reading anything else, with `ChainDbError::OlderVersion`.
  The daemon and addblock name the directory to delete: "the blockchain
  database in '<dir>' is version 14, which an older dcroxide wrote, and
  this version of the software reads only version 15 -- there is no
  in-place upgrade, and the chain is not damaged: delete '<dir>' and start
  again to build a new one from genesis (see docs/operating.md)". Pinned
  by `dcroxide-blockchain/tests/review_db_versions.rs` (both directions,
  and that a refusal changes no row),
  `dcroxide-node/tests/db_version_refusal.rs` (the daemon's and
  addblock's lines and exit status, and that neither changes a row),
  `dcroxide-blockchain/tests/rekey_layout.rs` (every bucket's walk order,
  with keys at heights whose little-endian bytes would sort otherwise, and
  every reader answering the same through the recent window, after
  pruning it and after a restart), `rekey_reorg.rs` (the full block
  battery, invalidations and reconsiderations replayed with a restart and
  an emptied window before each step, every row equal to an in-memory
  reference's), `prune_memory.rs`'s detach of a disconnect the UTXO set
  never saw, the database's `crash.rs` power cut over stored blocks, the
  node's filter-serving test over a pruned window
  (`dispatch.rs`, `serving_reads_filters_outside_the_window_under_each_blocks_height`)
  and the encoders' unit tests.

  **If dcrd ships a version 15 of its own**, port its change and take the
  next free number here, 16, keeping the refusal of every older version.
  dcrd's `upgradeDB` step for it is not ported, as none is: the port
  writes the new format from genesis. The startup line then still reads
  one more than dcrd's.

- **The exists-address index stores dcrd's address set in its own layout,
  as index version 3, written inside the metadata flush.** dcrd stores one
  empty-valued row per address ever seen (`existsAddrIndexVersion` 2,
  `internal/blockchain/indexers/existsaddrindex.go:371-475`). In redb, a
  copy-on-write B-tree, every new address then dirties its own leaf of a
  66-million-row bucket, and with the index off the node synced a
  200,000-block tail 3.4x faster. In the port's layout the same tail,
  with the height-first keys, synced at a median of 482 blk/s with the
  index on, against 94 in dcrd's layout and 501 with the index off, and
  wrote 15.5 GB against 137.4 GB and 12.6 GB
  ([bench-ledger.md](bench-ledger.md), "Exists-address index layout
  3"). The port keeps dcrd's set, tip row,
  subscriber, catch-up and recovery, and stores the set as a memtable in
  memory, a per-flush journal and per-partition sorted runs of 4 KiB
  chunks, all written by a flush participant inside the metadata flush's
  own durable commit ([ADR-0011](adr/0011-exists-address-layout-3-and-the-flush-participant.md);
  `crates/dcroxide-indexers/src/existsaddr/store.rs:297`). A connect's
  keys reach the memtable from an `on_commit` hook that runs once the
  commit's own flush has captured the write cache and sealed the keys it
  will write, and before its tip row is published
  (`crates/dcroxide-indexers/src/existsaddrindex.rs:894`), so the keys and
  the tip row are durable together and visible together. The memtable's
  sorted vectors and the keys of every chunk and journal row are in the
  order of the keys' bytes, which is part of the on-disk format. The
  memtable's lookups, merges and per-partition sort, the journal's batch
  sort, the union of runs, the order check on a decoded row and the commit
  hook's check that a partition's keys were handed in strictly ascending
  order compare keys with `cmp_keys`
  (`crates/dcroxide-indexers/src/existsaddr/mod.rs:64`), which reads a
  21-byte key as three big-endian words and compares them inline: the same
  order, computed without the call to the C library's `memcmp` that the
  array's own comparison makes at every step of a search or a merge. The
  format and every answer are unchanged; pinned by
  `the_inline_key_comparison_is_the_byte_order` and
  `every_place_that_compares_keys_keeps_the_byte_order`
  (`crates/dcroxide-indexers/src/existsaddr/tests.rs`). Each memtable
  partition keeps a Bloom filter of the keys it holds
  (`crates/dcroxide-indexers/src/existsaddr/bloom.rs`), and a lookup, a
  connect's filter and the hook's search look in the partition's sorted
  vectors only for a key the filter lets through: a key it rules out is
  not held, so every answer is the search's. The filter is in memory only,
  at most two bytes a key and one 64-byte block a partition; pinned by
  `the_filter_answers_as_a_search_does_through_every_change`
  (`crates/dcroxide-indexers/src/existsaddr/memtable.rs`). The filter was
  first run in syncs on 2026-10-18, on m3, from a dcrd v2.1.7 node on
  another machine on the same LAN, in builds that were states of a worktree
  and not the tree of a commit, in a series not comparable with the ledger's
  loopback figures: the build that added it took 7.70 s less than the build
  below it by the means of two runs each, beyond the morning session's 95%
  interval of about ±3.5 s, and the index thread's time on a processor fell
  60.9 s, or 46%, while the block thread's rose 7.5 s, which the records do
  not explain; a step on top of the changes below it and not its effect
  alone ([bench-ledger.md](bench-ledger.md), "Rungs from a block server
  on the LAN, two changes held for their memory, and dcrd v2.1.7", build D).
  The index's contents were not checked after any of
  those syncs. A connect the daemon's index thread runs does its address
  extraction, partition hashes, memtable filter and sort before its write
  transaction begins and keeps the bucket check, the tip row and the hook
  inside it, where dcrd does all of a connect inside the `dbTx`; the
  `internal/blockchain/indexers` row has that step, the one thing about it
  that depends on timing, its tests, and the sync measured on 2026-10-12
  with it and the inline comparison. Every answer is dcrd's except these:

  - **A mempool address never answers `false` while its block is indexed.**
    dcrd's connect drains `mpExistsAddr` into its write
    (`existsaddrindex.go:451-456`) and its lookups read the database before
    the map (`:301-364`), so a lookup between the drain and the commit
    misses the key in both. The port copies the map when the connect is
    queued for the index thread (at the connect itself under inline
    delivery, catch-up and the vectors) and removes
    exactly those keys in the commit hook, after inserting them, and
    its lookups read the overlay, then the memtable, then the store
    (`existsaddrindex.rs:237`): the order keys move in, each move inserting
    before it removes. Only that race's outcome differs; a connect that
    rolls back also leaves the map intact, where dcrd's drain loses its
    keys. Pinned by `lookups_never_miss_a_key_handed_from_the_mempool` and
    `lookups_never_miss_a_key_moving_into_the_runs`, whose controls reading
    the overlay last, or handing keys over after the tip is published, do
    see `false` (`crates/dcroxide-indexers/src/existsaddr/tests.rs`), and
    end to end by `existsaddr_rpc_lag.rs`.
  - **A mempool address is persisted with the first block connected after
    it was seen.** dcrd's goroutine drains `mpExistsAddr` when it reaches a
    block (`existsaddrindex.go:451-456`), so an address seen while that
    block waited in the channel is written with it. The daemon copies the
    overlay when it queues the connect, so the address goes with the next
    block connected instead, and while both run the answer is `true`
    either way. After a stop or a crash that lands in between, dcrd
    answers `true` for it on restart and the port answers `false` until
    it is seen again; dcrd's own answer there depends on how far its
    goroutine lagged, and the port pins the outcome of no lag. Pinned by
    the copy tests in `crates/dcroxide-indexers/src/existsaddr/tests.rs`,
    `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/index_thread.rs` and
    `existsaddr_powerloss.rs`.
  - **A store read error during a lookup is an RPC error, not `false`.**
    dcrd's ffldb `Get` reads an error as absence, so a failing disk answers
    "never seen". The port's run probes use `Transaction::try_first_after`
    and return the error, which the unchanged handlers turn into
    rpcInvalidError "Could not query address: ..."
    (`crates/dcroxide-rpc/src/handlers.rs:2726`, `:2759`; dcrd
    `internal/rpcserver/rpcserver.go:1652`, `:1709`). dcrd's answer is a
    one-line change in `ExistsAddrQuery::store_pass`
    (`existsaddrindex.rs:275`). Pinned by
    `a_store_read_error_during_a_lookup_is_an_error_not_false`
    (`crates/dcroxide-indexers/tests/existsaddr_v3.rs`), swept over every
    read a lookup makes.
  - **The drop's "Deleted N keys (M total)" lines count rows, not
    addresses.** The drop is dcrd's flat drop over the bucket
    (`common.go:298`), and layout 3's bucket holds meta, journal and run
    rows, modelled at 0.4-0.5 million for mainnet's 49-68 million
    addresses.
    Counting addresses would cost a full extra pass over the runs.
  - **A lookup the memtable misses reads up to two 4 KiB leaves**, the
    partition's delta run and then its base run, where dcrd's reads one
    row, so a large `existsaddresses` costs about twice the I/O. The answer
    is the same.
  - **An index of any other version is refused, never migrated.** A
    directory built before layout 3 (version 2, dcrd's layout) stops index
    startup with "exists address index: on-disk version 2 is not supported
    by this build; run once with --noexistsaddrindex --dropexistsaddrindex,
    then restart to rebuild it from genesis"
    (`existsaddrindex.rs:497-519`), leaving every row as it was. dcrd
    writes the version row and never reads it back. A build from before
    layout 3 reads no version either, and run with the index on over a
    layout-3 index it adds layout-2 rows and moves the tip; a start then
    finds them first in the bucket and refuses with the same remedy
    (`crates/dcroxide-indexers/src/existsaddr/store.rs:467`). A corrupt meta
    or journal row stops the start with a Corruption error naming the
    remedy, never an empty index. A corrupt run row is found by the first
    lookup that probes it, which fails with the remedy, or the first flush
    that merges its partition, which latches the store with it, so the node
    commits no further blocks. `--dropexistsaddrindex` drops either
    layout.
  - **Two developer-only tuning values have no dcrd counterpart.** The
    memtable and merge limits (`crates/dcroxide-indexers/src/existsaddr/policy.rs:63`)
    change the pages a flush writes and the memory the index holds, up to
    about 4 million keys (84 MB) while syncing, never an answer. While a
    flush's commit is under way on a thread of its own, up to `u_max`
    more keys can arrive before the index's flush request makes the next
    commit wait for it, so the bound at the defaults is `k0_hard` plus
    twice `u_max`, 6.0 million keys (126 MB), where it was `k0_hard` plus
    `u_max`, 4.5 million (94.5 MB). Those megabytes count the keys,
    21 bytes each; the memtable partitions' Bloom filters add at most
    2 bytes a key and one 64-byte block a partition, up to about 8 MB at
    4 million keys and 12 MB at 6.0 million. The
    `DCROXIDE_EXISTSADDR_MEMTABLE_KEYS` environment variable sets the
    memtable target for measurements (`exists_addr_policy`,
    `crates/dcroxide-node/src/bin/dcroxide.rs:2238`).
    The index's partitions are placed by SipHash-2-4 under a key each
    index draws when it is created, so nobody choosing addresses can crowd
    one (`policy.rs:159`); dcrd has no partitions. The memtable
    partitions' Bloom filters use a hash with no key, so whoever pays to
    addresses can grind ones a filter lets through; each then costs a
    lookup the search every key cost before the filters, plus one probe,
    and no answer depends on the hash.

  The answers are pinned by dcrd's own indexer session
  (`indexers_vectors.rs`, run at four merge settings), by a differential
  test against dcrd's two sets under random connects, reorgs, mempool
  transactions, flushes, restarts and drops (`existsaddr_v3.rs`), and by
  the RPC's lag behaviour over the live index (`existsaddr_rpc_lag.rs`:
  the not-synced error at six blocks behind, the wait and the answer from
  the caught-up index within five, the three-second timeout). The
  durability by power cuts at every storage operation of scripted runs,
  each reopened, recovered and checked against the keys of the connects
  whose tip rows survived, among them runs ending in the daemon's clean
  shutdown and drops of the index, with controls a broken index fails
  (`crates/dcroxide-indexers/src/existsaddr/tests.rs`), and by SIGKILLs of
  a mining simnet daemon under `tools/powerloss`, each replayed and
  restarted, after which every address answers as the surviving blocks
  say (`crates/dcroxide-node/tests/existsaddr_powerloss.rs`). The lock
  rules by `review_index_locks.rs`: the commit hook and the participant
  finish while the index, chain and pool mutexes are held elsewhere.

- **The index readers never take the index mutex.** dcrd's index readers
  take no index-wide lock. `ExistsAddress`/`ExistsAddresses` open a database
  view directly and take `unconfirmedLock` only for the mempool overlay.
  `AddUnconfirmedTx` takes `unconfirmedLock` alone. `Entry`/`Tip`/`Name`
  read the database or return a constant
  (`internal/blockchain/indexers/existsaddrindex.go` 301-364, 571-575;
  `txindex.go`). The port shares each index behind a mutex because the
  daemon hands it to several threads. Reproducing dcrd's structure naively
  therefore coupled every reader to the index writer, in both directions. In
  one direction, a caller-sized `existsaddresses` (limited-credential, up to
  the 8 MiB body cap) held the mutex across its reads. The writer claims the
  database's writer semaphore before it waits on that mutex, so one such
  call stalled block connection for its whole duration. In the other
  direction, the writer holds the mutex through its wait for the database
  writer and a whole block's index work. The mempool's `add_unconfirmed_tx`
  hook (which runs under the pool mutex), every `existsaddress` and every
  `getrawtransaction` waited for it, as long as any metadata or UTXO flush
  elsewhere. The overlay now sits behind its own `RwLock`, and every reader
  holds a handle the daemon takes once when it wires the index up. Lookups
  and the tip go through `ExistsAddrQuery`, the mempool hook through
  `ExistsAddrUnconfirmed`, and `getrawtransaction` through `TxIndexQuery`;
  the seams' `name` is a constant. This is dcrd's locking shape reached
  through different machinery. The subscriber takes the index guard before
  opening its write transaction rather than after, so nothing waits on
  another lock while holding the writer semaphore. It also reads the chain
  tip for its sync check before retaking the guard, so the index mutex is
  never held across a chain-lock wait. Pinned by
  `dcroxide-node/tests/b6_indexlock.rs`,
  `dcroxide-node/tests/review_index_locks.rs` and
  `the_sync_check_reads_the_chain_tip_without_the_index_lock` in
  `dcroxide-indexers/src/subscriber.rs`.

- **The websocket notification pipeline is unbounded, as dcrd's is.** Two
  accumulators sit between a chain event and a client's socket: the manager's
  `std::sync::mpsc::channel()` (`websocket.rs:377`) and the per-client
  `OutboundQueue`, plus three `Vec` staging queues in the chain callback
  (`chainntfns.rs:103`, `:106`, `:110`). The callback queues everything except
  the early checked-block peer relay, because it runs inside the chain's
  critical section. The per-client half is two deques rather
  than the one this entry used to describe, because dcrd's is two: `items` is
  `sendChan`, carrying every reply and at most one notification, and
  `held_notifications` is `pendingNtfns`, holding the rest behind a `waiting`
  flag (`websocket.rs:109`, `:112`, `:118`). dcrd is unbounded in the same two
  places — the manager's `q []any` slice behind `queueHandler`'s select loop and
  each client's `pendingNtfns [][]byte` behind `notificationQueueHandler` — with
  no drop policy, no slow-client disconnect, and no write deadline on the
  data-frame write. So a client that stops reading grows node memory in both
  implementations; the cost of a slow reader is borne by the node, and no
  notification is ever dropped or reordered. Capping it with drops or a
  slowness disconnect was considered and rejected: it would diverge from dcrd
  *and* punish an honest client on a slow link, which is the failure mode this
  campaign kept finding. dcrd does know the pattern — its mining-template feed is
  a bounded channel with an explicit drop policy — and deliberately does not
  apply it here.

  Where the port is **stronger** than dcrd: a stalled client cannot block writes
  to other clients — each connection has its own writer thread and its own
  stream mutex, so no lock is shared across clients — and cannot block the
  chain-notification callback, so it cannot stall block processing, where dcrd's
  single fan-out goroutine head-of-line blocks every client behind one stalled
  TCP peer. That writer thread, and the `pendingNtfns` throttle above, landed
  after this entry was first written (`a6b8650`, `96e9eb5`, `4609e44`); its
  citations pointed at unrelated code until this commit, and it described the
  port as writing notifications inline from the delivery thread, which it no
  longer does. The port used to be **weaker** in one respect, since corrected: the
  server was shared as a single `Arc<Mutex<Server<NodeRpcChain>>>` held for the
  whole of any one client's request, so a multi-thousand-block `rescan`
  serialized every other client's request *and* notification construction. dcrd
  has no such lock — `wsClient.serviceRequest`
  (`internal/rpcserver/rpcwebsocket.go`) calls the handler directly, and
  `rpcserver.Server` carries only three fine-grained mutexes, each guarding a
  single field (`hmacMu` at `rpcserver.go:5234`, `statusLock` at `:5239`,
  `blake256HaserMu` at `:5248`). The port now matches that shape: every handler
  seam takes `&self`, the server is shared as a plain `Arc<Server<…>>`, and the
  only interior locks guard one field each — `work_state` (dcrd's `workState`
  embeds `sync.Mutex`, `rpcserver.go:585`, and `handleGetWorkRequest` takes it in
  four short sections at `:3890`, `:3919`, `:3959`, and `:3987`, holding it
  across neither the unbounded template wait nor the chain query that follows;
  the port's `handle_get_work_request` scopes it the same way), `subsidy_cache`
  (dcrd's `SubsidyCache` guards its own state with an internal `sync.RWMutex`,
  `blockchain/standalone/subsidy.go:100`, so the lock simply moved to the field
  holding it), and the help cacher's `method_help`/`usage` (dcrd's `helpCacher`
  embeds one `sync.Mutex` covering both fields, `rpcserverhelp.go:1058`, and
  holds it across generation in both `RPCMethodHelp` and `RPCUsage`; the port
  gives each cache its own lock. The `usage` lock is held across generation as
  dcrd holds its mutex across `RPCUsage` (`:1094-1127`), because the usage text
  depends on the websocket flag: the first caller to take the lock fixes the
  variant both transports receive, racing callers included (QK-0005). The
  `method_help` lock is released across generation, so two callers racing a
  cold entry can both generate; that text depends only on the method name, so
  they agree, and the second simply overwrites).

  The per-client state took one round of this too. dcrd's `wsClient` embeds a
  single mutex it takes a field at a time (`rpcwebsocket.go:1331`, `:303`,
  `:2333-2340`, `:2354-2356`); the port had been holding that lock across the
  whole request. The delivery thread locks each target client's state to build a
  notification, and `MempoolTx` targets every connected client, so one client's
  long call — a rescan, a `generate`, a `getwork` template wait — stalled fan-out
  to all of them. The handlers take it where they use it now, and `rescan` takes
  it per block rather than across the scan, which is where dcrd takes its own.
  Three check-then-act pairs survive that split because the client's own serving
  thread is the sole writer of the filter slot and the delivery thread only ever
  adds outpoints to an existing filter; the two that could be split without harm
  are kept under one guard anyway, so a later reader is not invited to assume
  otherwise. Pinned by `a_parked_rescan_does_not_hold_the_client_lock`, which
  measures whether the lock is free *while* a scan is parked — an earlier version
  asserted only that it came free eventually, and passed against the bug.

  Two pieces of serialization the coarse lock had been supplying by accident had
  to be put back explicitly, because dcrd has its own mechanism for each and the
  port had been leaning on the wrapper instead. First, `getwork`: dcrd serializes
  whole invocations — requests *and* submissions — through a single-item
  semaphore (`workState.workSem`, `rpcserver.go:571`, `makeSemaphore(1)` at
  `:595`, acquired `:4171`, released `:4175`), which the per-field `work_state`
  lock does not replace, since the request path deliberately releases that lock
  across the template wait. Without it two invocations can interleave their four
  critical sections and one can clear `wait_for_updated_template` after another
  re-armed it for a newer tip, leaving the next caller a template built on the
  stale parent. The port takes a `work_sem` at the same point. It is a
  condvar semaphore so a queued caller can give up when cancelled (see the
  getwork cancellation entry below for how far that reaches). Its release
  hands the permit straight to the longest-queued waiter that is still live,
  as the channel receive completes the sender at the head of the send queue
  (`rpcwebsocket.go:60-67`) and skips senders whose `select` already took
  `ctx.Done()`. Callers are therefore served in arrival order, and one that
  arrives as the permit comes free cannot overtake them. Pinned by
  `waiters_are_served_in_arrival_order` and
  `the_handoff_passes_over_a_cancelled_waiter` in `worksem.rs`.
  Second, the CPU miner: dcrd's `CPUMiner` embeds one `sync.Mutex` and holds it
  across the *whole* check-and-set in both `GenerateNBlocks`
  (`cpuminer.go:833-856`) and `SetNumWorkers` (`:697-706`), because the two
  guards are cross-checks — `generate` rejects on `normalMining`, `setgenerate`
  ignores on `discreteMining`. Splitting that pair across two atomics turns both
  into check-then-act, and two concurrent RPC threads can each pass; the port
  keeps them as one `MiningMode` struct behind one lock.

- **The RPC read is sliced for the same reason the peer read is.** dcrd's
  handlers are goroutines, so closing a connection makes the handler's `Read`
  return on any platform; the port's handlers are OS threads, and the
  pre-authentication pool's eviction depends on a victim's handler noticing the
  socket shut down under it and releasing its slot. A receive armed with the
  whole remaining budget does not come back when another thread shuts the socket
  down mid-flight under Winsock, so a victim slept out the ten-second
  authentication timeout, its entry stayed counted, the pool pinned at its
  ceiling, and every later arrival — an operator's `dcrctl` included — was
  refused with no reply. The receives are now issued in one-second slices, which
  is enough: only the receive already in flight survives the shutdown, and the
  next one fails. The ceiling still caps the pool, deliberately — an evicted
  entry holds its handler thread until that handler returns, so admitting past
  the ceiling on the strength of an eviction would let the thread count climb
  without bound, which is the exhaustion the budget exists to prevent. As on the
  peer side, a slice elapsing is not a timeout: `WouldBlock`/`TimedOut`
  continues, because otherwise any client that paused mid-request would be cut
  off. Pinned by `a_client_that_pauses_mid_request_is_still_served`, verified to
  fail when that arm is removed.

- **The port answers to its own name throughout.** Data directory
  `~/.dcroxide` (`$LOCALAPPDATA/Dcroxide`, `~/Library/Application
  Support/Dcroxide`), config file `dcroxide.conf`, and environment defaults
  `DCROXIDE_APPDATA` / `DCROXIDE_ALT_DNSNAMES` where dcrd uses `.dcrd`,
  `dcrd.conf`, and `DCRD_*`. The point is running beside a dcrd without
  either instance reaching into the other's state: a `DCRD_APPDATA`
  exported for dcrd would otherwise redirect this node's entire data
  directory at dcrd's, and a shared data directory is the one thing the
  exclusive database lock exists to prevent. Deliberately no fallback to
  the `DCRD_*` variables — honouring them would reintroduce exactly that
  collision. Everything else about the configuration surface is dcrd's,
  option names and error strings included, and the vectors stay faithful
  dumps of dcrd's own output: the comparisons normalize the port's names
  back to dcrd's (`tests/common/mod.rs`'s `as_dcrd_names`) rather than
  editing what dcrd produced. One consequence worth naming, since it
  cannot be normalized away: `DCROXIDE_APPDATA` is four characters longer
  than `DCRD_APPDATA`, which pushes the `[$VAR]` annotation onto its own
  line under go-flags' wrapping, so the *rendered* help differs from
  dcrd's by that reflow. The layout algorithm is still pinned byte for
  byte, by rendering the vector comparison with dcrd's names; what the
  production help advertises is pinned separately, so help that named a
  variable the daemon ignores would fail the suite.

- **A half-present RPC cert pair is not regenerated.** dcrd guards `genCertPair`
  with `!keyFileExists && !certFileExists` (`server.go` 3846), so with one file
  present it generates nothing and startup fails on the missing one. The port
  regenerated whenever either file was missing, which silently destroyed a
  private key an operator may have provisioned — on a path where nothing in the
  request said "replace my key". It now matches dcrd: both-absent to generate,
  otherwise an error naming the missing half with the surviving file untouched.
  Relatedly, neither dcrd nor the port inspects the mode of a file it did not
  create — dcrd contains no `Chmod`, `FileMode`, or `Mode()` call anywhere — so a
  key left group-readable by an earlier install stays that way and is the
  operator's to fix; tightening it would diverge for no gain against a threat
  model in which a local user who can read the datadir can read the key
  regardless. The certificate is written with an explicit 0644 (dcrd's mode)
  rather than `std::fs::write`'s 0666, which under a permissive umask would
  leave it group- and world-writable — an integrity question, since anyone able
  to write it can swap the identity the node serves. Pinned by
  `a_half_present_pair_is_not_regenerated_over_the_surviving_file`
  (`rpclisten.rs`).

- **The log file.** dcrd writes each log line to standard output and then to
  `dcrd.log` in the network's log directory, through `jrick/logrotate`
  v1.1.2's rotator (`log.go:32-42`, `:124-144`); it opens the file in
  `loadConfig` as soon as `--logsize` parses, not at all under
  `--nofilelogging` (`config.go:866-907`), and closes it after `Shutdown
  complete` (`dcrd.go:54-64`). The port writes `dcroxide.log`, in the same
  directory, in the same way (`logging.rs` `init_log_rotator`,
  `logrotate.rs`), and opens it at the same point, through a hook the
  configuration load calls there (`config.rs`
  `load_config_from_argv_with_home_refusal`). The directory is made as Go's
  `MkdirAll(logDir, 0700)` makes it, and the file is opened for appending,
  created `0644`, with its size counted toward the threshold. A write that
  ends in a newline rotates the file once the count reaches the threshold,
  1,000 bytes to the KiB (QK-0029). The roll is named one past the highest
  number beside the file; the compressed rolls from three behind it are
  deleted downwards, as Go's `os.Remove` deletes, a file or an empty
  directory, until one cannot be; and the roll is gzipped in the background
  and removed once its archive is written. A rotation that fails after
  closing the file leaves it taking nothing more until a restart. On Windows
  the files are opened with Go's share mode, which lets other handles read
  and write them but not delete or rename them.

  A directory or file that cannot be made ends the process with dcrd's
  standard error line and status 1, ahead of the `--debuglevel=show` exit
  and every later check, and under the service control manager before the
  service reports stopped. `--debuglevel=show`, and a configuration refused
  later, leave the file behind, empty, where dcrd's can hold the
  previous-testnet warnings (the daemon row above). One lock covers a line's
  two writes, as slog's backend mutex does, so the file has the log lines
  standard output has, in its order and as they are, the port's own lines
  among them. The startup banner, which dcrd does not print, stays out of
  the file. Five departures:
  - *The directory is listed, not globbed.* dcrd finds the rolls with
    `filepath.Glob` over a pattern made from the file's path
    (`rotator/rotator.go:181-186`), so a `*`, `?` or `[` in the log
    directory's path (or `\` off Windows) is read as a pattern. A `*` or `?`
    still matches itself, and can match sibling directories too, whose
    `dcrd.log.*` then count toward the numbering. A class or an escape does
    not match itself, so the rolls beside the file are never found: every
    roll takes the same number, one past the highest the pattern matches
    elsewhere, or 1 when it matches nothing; the first compresses, each
    later one fails on the archive the first left, and each rotation
    replaces the previous uncompressed roll. A pattern that does not parse,
    such as a `[` without its `]`, fails every rotation before the file is
    closed, so the file grows without bound. The rename and the deletions
    act on the real directory throughout. The port lists the directory, so
    such a path rotates as any other does.
  - *The compressed bytes.* An archive's ten-byte header and its trailer are
    Go's byte for byte, but the deflate stream between them is miniz_oxide's,
    through flate2, where dcrd's is Go's `compress/flate`, so a roll
    decompresses to the same bytes from an archive of other bytes and size.
  - *One compression thread.* dcrd starts a goroutine per roll, and they take
    turns on a mutex in no set order. The port queues the rolls to one
    thread, which gzips them in the order they were made, since a thread per
    roll piles up under a small `--logsize`. If that thread cannot be
    started, the roll is gzipped before the write returns, where a goroutine
    cannot fail to start.
  - *Closes.* std reports no error from a close, so a rotation never fails on
    closing the full file, where dcrd's can. dcrd's compression keeps the
    roll when the archive's close fails; the port syncs the archive before
    closing it instead, so a write the close would have reported, such as
    one an NFS server refused, fails the sync and keeps the roll as well. A
    write-back failure on a local disk, which dcrd's close does not report,
    keeps it too, and the archive is on the disk before its roll is removed,
    which dcrd does not wait for.
  - *The file-size limit.* Once the file reaches the process's file-size
    limit (`ulimit -f`, systemd's `LimitFSIZE=`), the next write to it raises
    `SIGXFSZ`, whose default action, which Rust leaves in place, ends the
    process. Go's runtime ignores the signal
    (`runtime/sigtab_linux_generic.go`), so dcrd's write fails with `EFBIG`
    and is discarded, and the node goes on, its file taking no more. The
    block files share this, but the log file is the first to reach a limit
    set below `--logsize`. With `unsafe` forbidden the port leaves the
    signal's action alone.

  Pinned by `logrotate::tests` (the threshold and its KiB, the newline rule,
  the roll names and the three kept, the numbering past the rolls already
  there and the gap that stops the deletion, rolls queued behind a
  compression under way, an empty directory in a roll's place, the gzip
  header and round trip, the append on restart, the failed rotation, the
  modes) and `tests/logfile.rs` (the file per network and under `--appdata`
  and `--logdir`, every log line of standard output in the file in order and
  appended at a restart, `--nofilelogging`, a log directory that cannot be
  made and a log file that cannot be opened, ahead of `--debuglevel=show`
  and a later refusal). Not run: a node that rotated its log over a sync,
  and anything on Windows, the share mode and a daemon under a real service
  control manager among it.

- **The mempool's and the template generator's lines below info.** dcrd
  points its `TXMP` logger at the mempool package and its `MINR` logger at
  the mining and CPU miner packages at init (`log.go:91-94`), so
  `--debuglevel=TXMP=debug,MINR=debug` shows each transaction the pool
  accepts, stages, prunes or refuses and each template the generator
  considers and builds.  The port's pool and template generation had no
  logger: a wallet run against dcrd 2.1.7 under that setting counted 7,017
  TXMP and 3,313 MINR debug lines from dcrd and none from the port.  The
  pool now logs every line `internal/mempool/mempool.go` logs (`policy.go`
  logs none), and the template generation, the background generator and
