  the CPU miner's solve core the lines of `mining.go`,
  `bgblktmplgenerator.go` and `cpuminer/cpuminer.go` the port lacked, each
  at dcrd's level and with dcrd's text, through
  `dcroxide_mining::log::Logger`, which the daemon binds to the two
  subsystems (`logging.rs` `txmp_logger`, `minr_logger`; the pool through
  `TxPool::set_logger`, the generation through `TemplateChain::logger`,
  the solve core through its `logger` argument).  A line's level is
  checked before the line is formatted, against the subsystem's level
  slot, so a line whose level is off costs an atomic load, as slog's
  check does, and the `debuglevel` RPC moves both at run time.  The solve
  core now also refuses compact bits whose sign bit is set where the
  exponent shifts the whole mantissa out, as `DiffBitsToUint256` does
  (`internal/staging/primitives/pow.go:45-87`), with dcrd's error line,
  where it searched for a target of zero until cancelled.

  Pinned by the `log` rows of the three pool replays, which
  `tools/mempoolvectors` writes from dcrd's pool logging at trace (227
  lines over the sessions' calls, compared in the order dcrd logged them
  but for the lines it logs while ranging over a Go map, which are
  compared sorted; a session in the transaction pool file reaches the
  `uses spent input` and `Moved ticket` lines);
  `the_expiry_scan_logs_dcrds_lines`, `the_prunes_log_dcrds_lines`,
  `a_failed_unstaging_is_logged_as_dcrd_logs_it` and the two QK-0030 tests
  in `pool.rs`; the tests of `dcroxide-mining`'s `log.rs` and
  `cpuminer.rs`, of `tests/newtemplate_vectors.rs`, whose replay also
  checks the count of revocations each automatic revocation template
  generates, and of `tests/review_bg_regen.rs`, each with the levels the
  lines show at and the ones they do not; and
  `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/review_txmp_minr_logs.rs`, whose three
  template lines, for the startup template and `generate 2` on a fresh
  simnet node, are the ones dcrd 2.1.7 logs for the same steps.  The
  pool's lines are checked against dcrd's output; the template
  generation's, but for those three, against texts written from dcrd's
  format strings, since the template vectors hold no lines.  Not run: the
  background generator's `NewBlockTemplate` trace line, which needs a
  build that fails in a running node, and the `TSPEND` form of the
  `maybeInsertStakeTx` line, whose call the `TADD` form shares.
  Reproduced quirks: QK-0030 and QK-0031.  What remains different:
  - *Order within a call.* Where dcrd ranges over a Go map, its lines come
    in an order that changes from run to run: the expiry and stake prunes
    (`mempool.go:2215`, `:2242`, `:2301`, `:2310`), the vote metadata
    pruning (`:389`), the orphan expiry scan (`:556`), the cascade of an
    orphan's removal into its redeemers (`:509`), the removal of a peer's
    orphans (`:536`) and of the orphans an accepted transaction double
    spends (`:664`), the trial of the orphans redeeming an accepted
    transaction's output (`:2162`), the template build's pass over the
    source, which dcrd takes from the pool's map (`:2501`), the ancestors
    of a bundled transaction (`internal/mining/tx_desc_graph.go:84`), and
    the dependents of a skipped template transaction
    (`mining_view.go:144`).  The port's come in hash order, but for the
    trial of orphans, which it shuffles; and the line of a random orphan
    eviction (`mempool.go:587-592`) names the orphan the port draws.
  - *Header serialization.* The solve core cannot fail to serialize a
    header, so `Unexpected error while serializing header`
    (`cpuminer.go:291-295`) has no counterpart.

- **`--maxsameip` keys on the exact IP**, so an IPv6 /64 can occupy every
  connection slot. Exact dcrd parity (`connmanager.go` 685); the rate limiter
  groups by /64 but the per-host permit does not. Pinned by the `tsip|` rows of
  `srvtargetout_vectors.rs`, which replay dcrd's own arithmetic for the flag.

- **`banned_hosts` grows without bound**, reclaimed only lazily on reconnect
  from that exact host. Exact dcrd parity (`server.go` 2201). The analogous
  structure at `ratelimiter.rs` is bounded at `MAX_GROUP_LIMITERS`; the same
  pattern applies here.

- **The median time source's set of seen sources grows without bound.** Each
  handshaken peer's address, port included, is remembered for the life of the
  process so that it contributes one clock sample at most, exactly as dcrd's
  `medianTime.knownIDs` does (`internal/blockchain/mediantime.go`). An inbound
  peer that reconnects from new ports adds an entry per connection. Exact dcrd
  parity; the same shape as `banned_hosts` above.

- **`gettxoutsetinfo`'s forced flush takes the chain lock where dcrd takes only
  its cache lock — deliberately, and this one is settled rather than pending.**
  The expensive half already matches: the walk of the UTXO set runs with no
  chain lock held, as dcrd's `backend.FetchStats()` (`utxobackend.go:529`) runs
  with neither `chainLock` nor `cacheLock`. Only the bounded forced flush ahead
  of it still holds the whole chain, where dcrd confines it to `cacheLock`
  (`utxocache.go:803`).

  Moving the port's flush to a cache-only lock would be a regression, not a
  fix. Both implementations connect a block as commit → flush → publish tip
  (dcrd `chain.go:717`/`:728`/`:735`; the port's `connect_block`:
  `commit_view`, `maybe_flush_utxo_cache`, then `best_chain.set_tip`), and
  dcrd reads `bestChain.Tip()` in `FetchUtxoStats` (`utxocache.go:1095`)
  with no `chainLock`. A stats call landing between the
  flush and the tip publication therefore force-flushes against the previous
  tip and leaves a `lastFlushHash` one block behind a backend that already
  holds the newer block. Neither implementation absorbs a re-applied block on
  the catch-up replay: both panic with `"view missing input"` (dcrd
  `utxoviewpoint.go:274-280` and `:342-348` since `e60cedc5`, reached from
  `utxocache.go:1024`, all at `382ff4dd`; the port's `UtxoView` connect
  functions), so the node stops. In the port the flush, the tip read and the
  tip publication are all `&mut Chain` and so all under one lock, which closes
  that window. Splitting the flush out would open it. The port is intentionally
  stronger than upstream here.

- **Peer teardown raises a flag where dcrd closes the connection.** dcrd ends a
  connection with `Peer.Disconnect`, which closes the `net.Conn` *and* CASes
  `p.disconnect` / closes `p.quit` in one method under one mutex, so every
  server-side teardown gets both halves and its reader unwinds immediately on
  every platform. The port cannot close a descriptor out from under a reading
  thread without `unsafe`, and `shutdown` does not reliably cut a pending
  `recv` under Winsock, so each connection carries a `transport::Cancel` the
  reader polls in `READ_POLL_INTERVAL` (1 s) slices. That much was already
  true; what was divergent was that the port wrote dcrd's one method out as two
  statements at each site, and three of them performed only the `shutdown`
  half. `transport::Teardown` now owns the socket handle and the flag together
  and exposes one `disconnect()`, and it has replaced the bare `TcpStream` in
  every field and parameter on a disconnect path, so a site with a lone socket
  to shut down no longer exists. The flag is minted at the accept and at the
  dial rather than inside `serve_peer`, so every holder shares one. Pinned at
  each of the six sites plus the clone-sharing invariant beneath them --
  `a_cloned_teardown_shares_one_flag_and_disconnect_does_both_halves` and
  `separately_minted_teardowns_do_not_share_a_flag`, both unit tests in
  `transport.rs` rather than in `tests/`. The residual cost is dcrd's own shape
  reproduced: one wake-up per second per idle
  connection (~125 at the default `--maxpeers`), against dcrd's blocking read
  with no poll at all.

- **External-address selection is ported and wired.** The subsystem follows the
  parity pin -- `ExternalAddrCandidate`, `ExternalAddrCandidateCache` over
  `dcroxide_containers::lru::Map`, `best_candidate`,
  `resolve_external_address`, `consider_reported_addr_outbound` and
  `consider_reported_addr` mirror `036b7090`'s `server.go:2373-2614` -- and it
  now has a live caller. `ServerPeerHandler` stores the address a peer reports
  for the connection from its version message (dcrd
  `serverPeer.reportedLocalAddr`, `server.go:1038`), `ServerContext` holds one
  candidate cache for the process behind its own mutex (dcrd's server field,
  `server.go:424-427`), and `on_connected` calls `consider_reported_addr` once,
  unconditionally, after the outbound-only advertise block and before the
  peer-state insert, which is where dcrd calls it (`server.go:2670`). The
  advertise block itself was already present, in `on_connected` rather than in
  `handle_add_peer` -- a placement difference, not a missing behaviour, and
  this entry previously claimed the port did not have it at all.
  Two gates read deliberately different sources, as upstream does: the
  advertise block reads the cfg `--simnet`/`--regnet` flags while the discovery
  gate one level down reads the active chain params name. Pinned end to end by
  `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/srvextaddr_wired.rs`, which drives a real
  handshake over a loopback socket through `run_peer_connection`, and row for
  row against the pin by `srvextaddr_vectors.rs`.

  `handle_add_peer` itself still has no daemon caller: the peer-state maps it
  maintains are the port's `SyncPeers` registry, populated elsewhere, so what
  remains of it is admission bookkeeping the runtime already does its own way.
  That is a separate question from external-address discovery, which is now
  live.
- **RPC TLS material reloads when the files change.** dcrd hangs a
  `GetConfigForClient` callback off the listener's `tls.Config`
  (`makeReloadableTLSConfig`, `server.go:3796-3822`, wired at `:3860`), so an
  arriving connection re-stats `rpc.cert`, `rpc.key` and, under
  `--authtype=clientcert`, the `--clientcafile` bundle -- at most once every
  five seconds -- and swaps in a freshly parsed config when any of them
  changed. The port now has the same subsystem: `ReloadableTlsConfig` in
  `node/src/rpcrun.rs` mirrors `reloadableTLSConfig`, `WatchedFile` mirrors
  `watchedFile`, and `config_for_client` stands exactly where dcrd's callback
  does, once the ClientHello is in (see the timing paragraph below). A
  rotated certificate is served without a restart, and
  under `--authtype=clientcert` a removed issuer stops being trusted on the
  first connection after the interval elapses -- which matters because editing
  that bundle is the whole revocation mechanism, neither implementation
  consulting a CRL or OCSP. The clientcert path is pinned end to end by
  `clientcert_auth_admits_the_bundle_and_follows_its_revocation`
  (`node/tests/rpclisten.rs`). Over TLS 1.3 and 1.2, a listed certificate and
  a leaf from a listed authority are served with no `Authorization` header,
  and a client with no certificate or an unlisted one gets no handshake.
  Once the bundle is edited, the authority's leaf is still served until a
  check has reloaded it and refused after.

  Three of dcrd's warts come across deliberately. `needsReload`'s `||` chain
  short-circuits, so a cert and key rotated together reload twice -- once when
  the cert is noticed, then again on the next check when the key finally
  stats against details that went stale -- and log twice; Rust's `||`
  short-circuits identically, so a direct translation inherits it, and
  statting all three would be tidier and would not be dcrd. A file that no
  longer exists reports changed without refreshing its remembered details, so
  a deleted certificate retries on every connection rather than latching. And
  the first check is a full interval out from startup, so a file rewritten in
  the first five seconds of uptime is not noticed until then.

  A failed reload keeps the working configuration and warns only when the
  error differs from the previous one, so replacing the files with malformed
  data or deleting them cannot take the RPC server down, and a repair is
  picked up on the next check.

  Resumption is no longer carried across a reload, because there is none to
  carry: the port disables stateful resumption outright, for reasons that
  belong to the separate entry below rather than to reloading. An earlier
  version of this entry argued for carrying the session cache and ticketer
  over, on the grounds that dcrd's resumption survives a rotation -- Go
  resolves ticket keys with `originalConfig.ticketKeys(configForClient)`
  (`handshake_server.go:177`), which falls through to the long-lived outer
  config's automatically rotated keys when the reloaded one sets none
  (`common.go:1095-1124`). That reasoning was sound and is now moot.

  The check runs when dcrd's does. Go calls the callback once it has read
  and parsed the ClientHello (`readClientHello`,
  `crypto/tls/handshake_server.go:166-176`), so only a peer that actually
  speaks TLS advances the five-second clock. The port used to choose the
  configuration as the connection was accepted, so a bare TCP connection
  consumed a check and, under port-scanner noise, a rotation could be served
  up to one interval late. The listener now reads the ClientHello through
  `rustls::server::Acceptor` before it chooses a configuration -- it has to,
  to pick the cipher suite and key exchange as Go does (next entry) -- and
  asks `config_for_client` at that point. The one sliver left is a
  ClientHello that rustls refuses before handing it over, for want of null
  compression or for a server name rustls finds malformed and Go's parser
  does not: Go would already have run the callback for it, the port does
  not. What is cached is now a set of configurations built from the same
  files, which reload together.

  Upstream since `488b8163` (2023-07-10), first carried by a dcrd release in
  2.0.0.
- **The RPC listener's TLS handshake chooses its key exchange and cipher
  suite, and reports a refusal, as Go's server does.** dcrd's RPC
  `tls.Config` sets the certificate, `MinVersion` and the client CAs and
  nothing else (`newTLSConfig`, `server.go:3685-3699`), so every
  negotiation choice is Go's default, which the port takes from the
  reference Go, 1.26.2 (ADR-0001's 2026-10-26 addendum on the reference
  Go). Go 1.27, which built dcrd 2.1.7's published linux-amd64 binary
  (go1.27.2), chooses its key exchange and cipher suite alike. The listener
  was rustls over the `ring` provider, and
  side by side with the 2.1.7 binary on loopback it differed three ways. It
  had no P-521 key exchange, so a client offering only secp521r1 -- curl
  with `--curves secp521r1`, `openssl s_client -groups P-521` -- got a
  `handshake_failure` alert where dcrd completed the handshake, and
  Decrediton 2.1.7 offers nothing else: it pins `ecdhCurve: "secp521r1"`
  on its requests to a remote daemon. It took the cipher suite in the
  client's order, so OpenSSL's default offer got TLS_AES_256_GCM_SHA384
  where dcrd answers TLS_AES_128_GCM_SHA256. And it logged nothing for a
  refused handshake, where dcrd logs each one.

  *The key exchanges.* `dcroxide_tlscrypto::kx_group::SECP521R1`
  (`crates/dcroxide-tlscrypto/src/kx.rs`, in the in-tree TLS provider of
  ADR-0015) adds ECDHE over P-521 as a rustls `SupportedKxGroup` on the
  `p521` crate's arithmetic, without its `ecdh` feature. The ephemeral
  scalar is drawn as Go's `GenerateKey` draws one, 66 bytes masked to 521
  bits and tried until they fall in `[1, n)`, from the kernel (see the
  `crypto/rand` row for why not the process generator). The peer's share is
  accepted only as an uncompressed point on the curve, as RFC 8446 and Go's
  `NewPublicKey` require, and the shared secret is the whole 66-byte
  x-coordinate. It reproduces the NIST CAVS 14.1 ECC CDH vector that Go's
  `crypto/ecdh` tests carry and Wycheproof's P-521 ECDH vectors, and gives
  Go's `crypto/ecdh` answer, through the oracle, on each of those vectors and
  on a generated set of malformed shares. The four groups are offered in
  Go's order, X25519, P-256, P-384, P-521 (1.26.2's
  `defaultCurvePreferences`, `crypto/tls/defaults.go:30-33`; 1.27's
  `curvePreferenceOrder` keeps it), less the hybrid post-quantum groups
  ahead of them (open entry below).

  *The choices.* Go picks a TLS 1.3 group in its own order among those
  the client supports, after moving the ones the client sent a key share
  for to the front, and asks for a share with a HelloRetryRequest when the
  winner has none (`crypto/tls/handshake_server_tls13.go:201-246`); in TLS
  1.2 it takes the client's first supported curve (`key_agreement.go:158-163`).
  It picks the suite in its own order, never the client's: AES-128-GCM,
  AES-256-GCM, ChaCha20-Poly1305 when the machine has AES-GCM hardware and
  the first suite in the client's list that Go knows is an AES-GCM one, and
  ChaCha20-Poly1305 first otherwise (`isAESGCMPreferred`,
  `cipher_suites.go:393-406`; the lists at `defaults.go:92-112` and
  `cipher_suites.go:283-335`). rustls picks the group in the client's order
  and retries for the client's first supported group (`server/hs.rs`
  `choose_suite_and_kx_group`, `server/tls13.rs:219-246`), and it offers no
  hook for either choice. So the listener reads each ClientHello through
  `rustls::server::Acceptor` and then serves it with one of ten
  configurations built from the same certificate, key and client verifier
  (`RpcTlsConfigs`): the two suite orders, each with `ignore_client_order`
  set, times one configuration per key exchange plus one offering all four,
  for TLS 1.2 and for a ClientHello with no group in common. A
  configuration offering only Go's group leaves rustls Go's choice,
  retry included. The key shares and offered versions, which rustls's
  `ClientHello` does not expose, are read from the raw records. The
  hardware test is Go's (`hasAESGCMHardwareSupport`,
  `cipher_suites.go:369-377`): AES, PCLMULQDQ, SSE4.1 and SSSE3 on x86, AES
  and PMULL on arm64, always on ppc64. Go's s390x test reads CPACF function
  codes that Rust's feature detection does not name, so an s390x build of
  the port takes the ChaCha20-first order where dcrd might not. On
  hardware with AES-GCM, OpenSSL's default offer now gets
  TLS_AES_128_GCM_SHA256 over TLS 1.3 and
  TLS_ECDHE_ECDSA_WITH_AES_128_GCM_SHA256 over TLS 1.2; a client listing
  ChaCha20-Poly1305 first gets it, as from dcrd. Pinned by
  `serves_a_client_offering_only_the_p521_key_exchange`,
  `negotiates_the_key_exchange_in_gos_order` and
  `negotiates_the_cipher_suite_in_gos_order`
  (`crates/dcroxide-node/tests/rpclisten.rs`), by the unit tests in
  `crates/dcroxide-node/src/rpctls.rs`, and for the P-521 group by those of
  `crates/dcroxide-tlscrypto`; not yet re-run side by side with the dcrd
  binary.

  *The refusals.* The handshake now runs to completion before the request
  head is read, as `conn.serve` runs it (`net/http/server.go:1924-1945`),
  under the same watchdog deadline as before. A refused handshake is logged
  as dcrd logs it, `[ERR] RPCS: http: TLS handshake error from <addr>:
  <err>`: net/http writes the line to the server's `ErrorLog` (`:1943`),
  which dcrd forwards at error level through the RPCS logger
  (`logForwarder`, `internal/rpcserver/rpcserver.go:5885-5892`, installed at
  `:5938`). `<err>` is Go's text wherever the two stacks refuse for the
  same reason: `EOF` for a close on a record boundary and `unexpected EOF`
  inside one (`crypto/tls/conn.go:625-637`); `read tcp4 <local>-><remote>:
  i/o timeout` at the deadline and `read tcp4 ...: read: connection reset
  by peer` for a reset; `tls: first record does not look like a TLS
  handshake`, an SSLv2 hello and an oversized first record, refused on the
  first record's header, with Go's alert where it sends one (`:641-676`);
  `client sent an HTTP request to an HTTPS server` for plaintext HTTP,
  which is also answered with net/http's `HTTP/1.0 400 Bad Request` on the
  bare connection, where the port used to send no HTTP answer
  (`net/http/server.go:1934-1939`); `remote error: tls: <alert>` under
  Go's alert names; and Go's own refusals for no suite or no key exchange
  in common, naming the suite first in TLS 1.3 as Go, which picks it
  first, does; versions it does not support; a missing client certificate
  or one signed by an unknown authority; an invalid key share in TLS 1.3
  and an invalid ClientKeyExchange in TLS 1.2; and a few more
  (`go_error_text`). Every refusal is logged, unthrottled, as dcrd logs
  it, like the authentication-failure warning and unlike the shed lines.
  Two closes are the port's own and are not logged: a connection the
  pre-authentication pool evicted, whose eviction has its own rate-limited
  line and which dcrd, having no such pool, would not have closed at all;
  and one force-closed at shutdown, which dcrd leaves to the process exit.
  Pinned over a running node by
  `refused_tls_handshakes_are_logged_as_dcrd_logs_them`
  (`crates/dcroxide-node/tests/rpctls_handshake_log.rs`), and the two
  unlogged closes by
  `eviction_prefers_a_connection_that_has_not_sent_a_request`
  (`crates/dcroxide-node/src/rpcrun.rs`). The refusals whose text still
  differs are an open entry below.
- **`generate 0` cancels an in-flight discrete mining call.** dcrd keeps a
  `generateCancelFn` while discrete mining is active and invokes it on the
  `n == 0` path (`internal/mining/cpuminer/cpuminer.go:159-163`, `:845-851`),
  so a concurrent `generate 0` stops the running solve, returns nothing
  itself, and leaves the cancelled caller with `ErrCancelDiscreteMining`.
  `MiningMode` now carries the same handle -- an `Arc<AtomicBool>` rather than
  a `context.CancelFunc`, since what it cancels is a wait on a channel and it
  is set from whichever connection ran `generate 0` while being read on the
  mining thread. It is published under the same lock that claims discrete
  mining and dropped by `DiscreteMiningGuard` alongside the flag, so a
  `generate 0` can neither cancel a call that has not started nor one that
  has already returned.

  The distinction between the two cancellations is the part worth checking.
  dcrd returns `ErrCancelDiscreteMining` for a `generate 0` *and* for a client
  that hung up, then tells them apart in `handleGenerate` by asking the
  request context first (`rpcserver.go:1850-1856`): a dead connection is
  `rpcConnectionClosedError`, everything else is `rpcCancelError`. The port
  carries that across in `GenerateFailure`'s two flags and checks them in
  dcrd's order, so `handle_generate`'s arms select the same way. This also
  retires the note that `is_cancel_discrete` had no reachable daemon producer
  and existed only for the vectors: it has one now.

  Two details came out of the port. The wait had to learn a second
  cancellation source, since the thread-local installed for a request cannot
  be reached from the connection running `generate 0`; `recv_with_timeout_until`
  takes the shared flag as an argument and defaults to ignoring it, so no test
  double changed. And the cancellation message is dcrd's
  `"discrete mining process canceled"` verbatim, because `rpcCancelError`
  formats it into the reply -- it is RPC surface, not an internal label. The
  port previously carried a paraphrase there, harmless only because no
  reachable path reached that arm.

  The cancellation reaches the solve worker as well as the loop, because
  dcrd's does: `solveCtx, cancel := context.WithCancel(genCtx)` makes the
  worker's context a child of the generate context, so cancelling the latter
  stops the former at once, and dcrd checks it both inside the hash loop and
  again after a solution is found, to suppress one discovered between the
  signal and the stop. The port passes the same flag into `SolveJob` and
  reads it at both points, alongside the per-solve flag that was already
  there. Without it the worker would keep hashing until the loop noticed and
  stopped it, and could submit in that window a block dcrd would have
  suppressed.

  That last half has no automated control, which is worth stating rather
  than implying otherwise. The window is bounded by one
  `CANCEL_POLL_INTERVAL`, and measured on this harness no block lands inside
  it either way -- solving a regnet template takes longer than 50ms -- so an
  assertion on chain height would pass with the guard removed and prove
  nothing. It is verified by construction against dcrd's two checks instead.

  What still differs is timing, bounded and small: dcrd's `select` wakes at
  once on `genCtx.Done()` while the port notices within one
  `CANCEL_POLL_INTERVAL` (50ms) of the template wait it is sitting in.

- **dcrd `New`'s version 3 test network cleanup pass is not ported, because it
  cannot fire.** At startup on testnet3, dcrd walks every chain tip. It
  invalidates, with notifications suppressed, any tip whose ancestor at height
  962928 (`testNet3MaxDiffActivationHeight`) is not `block962928Hash`
  (`chain.go:2266-2296`). This cleans up databases that stored the pre-reset
  branch before the maximum-difficulty checkpoint was enforced. No dcroxide
  database can hold such a branch. The only runtime block index insertion, in
  `maybe_accept_block_header`, runs the checks of
  `check_block_header_positional` first (in the form that takes the hash
  the header was looked up by), and that rejects any other header at that
  height with `ErrBadMaxDiffCheckpoint` whether or not the block is a fast
  add. That check (`edca020`) predates the port's first persisted chain
  state (`e497a38`). The omission is recorded on `Chain::open`'s doc comment
  beside the legacy migration and `upgradeDB` paths.

- **`addnode`, `node`, the reported protocol version and `debuglevel`'s
  subsystems follow dcrd master, not the 2.1.7 release.** The acceptance run
  of 2026-10-23 compared the port with the dcrd 2.1.7 release binaries, and
  these replies differed; each is what master gives at the parity pin.
  `addnode onetry` and `node connect` (with or without `temp`) dial before
  they reply and return the outcome: `-27` with master's texts when the
  attempt is cancelled or times out, and otherwise `-32603` with the error
  text, a refused dial's or `strconv.ParseUint`'s for a port above 65535
  (`internal/rpcserver/rpcserver.go:600-646`, `rpcadaptors.go:127-143`,
  `server.go:4481-4518`, `internal/connmgr/connmanager.go:1119-1160`).
  `addnode add` and `node connect perm` register the persistent entry and
  return null unless a gate refuses it (`connmanager.go:1822-1856`), and an
  `addnode remove` that finds nothing returns `-32603` "peer not found"
  (`rpcadaptors.go:147`, `:201-216`). 2.1.7 queued every connect, returned
  null without waiting for the dial, and wrapped its errors as `-8`; master
  changed that in `eb8c8c86`, "connmgr: Overhaul to use wrapped conns plus
  ctx.". The port's handlers are `handle_add_node`, `handle_node` and
  `connect_error` in `dcroxide-rpc`, over `rpc_connect` in `outbound.rs`;
  master's `-1` "Connection closed" reply, for a client that disconnects
  during a one-try dial, has no counterpart, as `connect_error` records.
  `getinfo` and `getnetworkinfo` report protocol version 12 where 2.1.7
  reports 11: master's `maxProtocolVersion` is `wire.AddrV2Version`
  (`server.go:80`), raised by `5f61e5aa` after the 2.1 branch point, while
  2.1.7's is `BatchedCFiltersV2Version`, although the wire module it builds
  against already says 12. The port now takes it, as dcrd does, from a
  server-level constant, `MAX_PROTOCOL_VERSION`
  (`crates/dcroxide-node/src/server.rs`, wire's `ADDR_V2_VERSION`), which is
  also the version every peer is offered, kept apart from wire's own
  `PROTOCOL_VERSION`. `debuglevel show` lists eighteen
  subsystems, master's (`log.go:103-122`; `logsubsys.rs` `SUBSYSTEM_IDS`),
  and its help no longer names ADXR (`internal/rpcserver/rpcserverhelp.go:25`):
  master's `9fa18b9c` removed 2.1.7's ADXR, a logger nothing had used since
  the address index was removed in 2022.

- **A `--connect` peer that cannot be reached is retried on dcrd master's
  backoff, not 2.1.7's.** Master runs each persistent peer in
  `runPersistent`: the first dial is immediate, and an attempt that ends
  within `RetryDuration` (5 s) of starting raises the retry count and waits
  `backoffWithJitter`, a uniform draw from b/2 up to b, with b = 5 s ×
  2^(n−1) capped at 5 minutes, so the waits fall in [2.5, 5), [5, 10),
  [10, 20), [20, 40), [40, 80) and [80, 160) s, and then [150, 300) s for as
  long as the entry stays; an attempt that lasted 5 s or more resets the
  count and redials at once (`internal/connmgr/connmanager.go:1881-1903`,
  `:1939-1969`). The backoff came with `f8640bc8`, "connmgr: Implement
  exponential backoff with jitter.". 2.1.7 builds against `connmgr/v3`
  v3.1.3, which waits the retry count times 5 s, without jitter, up to 5
  minutes, and resets the count only on a connection. The port carries
  master's constants, formula, reset rule and debug line (`dcroxide-connmgr`
  `backoff_with_jitter`, `outbound.rs` `handle_persistent_drop`). In the
  acceptance run of 2026-10-23 it retried an unreachable loopback
  `--connect` peer after 4.170, 7.373, 17.478, 25.695 and 70.953 s and was
  stopped 112.7 s into the sixth wait, each inside master's range, where
  2.1.7 waited 5, 10, 15 and so on to 40 s. The draw goes through
  `SystemCsprng::uint64n`, whose rejection sampling maps the stream to
  values differently from dcrd's Lemire reduction (the *Reduction of a draw
  to a range* row above), with the same distribution.

#### Open: the port does not match dcrd here

These are real divergences from the parity pin and someone should eventually
close them. Each says what upstream does, what the port does instead, and what
closing it would cost.

- **Websocket requests are still serviced on the reading thread, so one
  request blocks the next.** dcrd gives each websocket client three
  goroutines -- `inHandler`, `notificationQueueHandler` and `outHandler`
  (`rpcwebsocket.go:1998-2011`) -- and services a non-batched command on a
  fourth, acquiring a per-client semaphore of capacity `RPCMaxConcurrentReqs`
  and then spawning (`:1549-1553`); batches it services inline (`:1748`). The
  port now has two of those: a reading loop and a writer thread. Concurrent
  dispatch was evaluated separately and declined; re-evaluated once the
  writer thread existed, it is still declined, but on narrower ground than
  the earlier note gave, and two of that note's premises have since gone
  false. Replies already route through the outbound queue
  (`serve_ws_reads` handing each to `OutboundQueue::push_reply` in
  `websocket.rs`), as dcrd's spawned `serviceRequest` (`:1807`) ends on
  `SendMessage` (`:1925`) and so onto `sendChan` (`:1936`) -- that cost is
  paid, and measured below. Nor is the write half unsplit: reader and writer
  share the stream as a `Mutex<WsConn<S>>` (`serve_websocket` in
  `websocket.rs`).

  What is not paid is the wakeup, and that is the whole objection. dcrd's
  `outHandler` writes `c.conn` while `inHandler` is blocked reading it,
  neither taking a lock, so a reply enqueued while the reader waits goes out
  at once. The port cannot copy that, because a `rustls::StreamOwned` is one
  object serving both directions: the reader holds the shared mutex across
  `read_message` (the drain-then-read block in `serve_ws_reads`), whose
  socket timeout is `WS_POLL_INTERVAL` (`rpcrun.rs`, armed in
  `serve_rpc_connection` just before the upgrade). Since 96e9eb5
  whoever holds the stream drains the queue, and that is exactly what makes
  inline dispatch fast -- the handler runs with the lock free, so the reader
  writes its own reply microseconds later on the next pass of the loop. Move
  dispatch to a worker and the reader returns to the poll immediately,
  holding the mutex, so a reply finishing 1ms later waits out the remaining
  49. A standalone model of the loop -- shared mutex, drain and read under
  one guard, 50ms socket timeout, condvar writer -- measures 0.02ms per round
  trip inline against 51.2ms spawned, stable over three runs. That is a full
  poll interval added to every reply, spent closing a divergence that costs
  only pipelining, only for the client issuing the slow request, and only
  with admin credentials: weaker than dcrd on every reply to stop being
  weaker than dcrd on one.

  The prerequisite, if this is ever revisited, is therefore not splitting the
  stream but a readiness wait taken without the stream lock -- which under
  TLS must also account for plaintext already decrypted and sitting in
  `rustls::ServerConnection`, where the socket has nothing pending yet a
  whole message is available. Two invariants stated in comments today also
  assume one request at a time per connection and would have to move first:
  the thread-local carrying the cancellation token is sound only because
  "the transport ... processes one request at a time" (`worksem.rs`'s
  module doc), and ignoring mutex poisoning on the client state is
  justified by its serving thread being "its only writer" (`lock_client`'s
  doc in `rpc/websocket.rs`).

  The writer thread closes the largest consequence: a notification now
  reaches a client while one of that client's own requests is still running,
  which is what `outHandler` gives dcrd. Replies are queued rather than
  written inline, as dcrd queues its own through `SendMessage`
  (`:1925-1940`), so one writer drains one queue and output keeps its order.
  Measured, because the shape invites a latency regression and the first draft
  had one: thirty sequential round trips averaged 41.3ms here against 41.4ms
  before the change. Both figures were the delayed-ACK floor, not the loop.
  Each frame went out as two writes (header, then payload) on a socket with
  Nagle's algorithm on, so every payload waited for the client's delayed ACK
  of its header. Frames are now one write, as gorilla's server fast path is
  (`conn.go:775-785`). Every socket the node accepts or dials gets
  TCP_NODELAY, as Go's `newTCPConn` (`net/tcpsock.go`) gives every connection.
  That covers RPC accepts, peer accepts, direct peer dials, and dials through
  the SOCKS5 proxy (onion routes and Tor resolution included). The same thirty
  round trips now take 0.02ms each (0.016-0.054ms over three runs, against
  41.0-41.2ms before). `sequential_round_trips_do_not_wait_on_delayed_acks`
  pins the websocket latency, and `review_p2p_nodelay.rs` pins the peer
  sockets' option.

  `notificationQueueHandler` is ported with it (`:1837-1888`). Only one
  notification is ever handed to the writer; the rest wait in a held list,
  and each is promoted only once the one before it has been written --
  dcrd's `ntfnSentChan`, which `outHandler` signals after `WriteMessage`
  returns (`:1868-1880`, `:1902-1908`). A reply therefore queues behind at
  most one notification rather than behind the whole backlog, and one
  queued during that write goes out ahead of the next notification
  (`a_reply_queued_during_a_write_goes_ahead_of_the_next_notification`).
  That is the point of dcrd keeping two paths into one channel, and without
  it a client with a deep backlog would see its replies delayed by all of
  it.

  What remains, all latency rather than incorrectness, and all needing admin
  credentials -- `rescan` is in `rpcLimited` on both sides but `loadtxfilter`
  is not, so a limited client's rescan has no filter to match:

  * Two requests on one connection serialise where dcrd runs up to
    `RPCMaxConcurrentReqs`. Mining barely notices: `workState.workSem` is
    `makeSemaphore(1)` node-wide and serialises `getwork` anyway.
  * A client that hangs up mid-request goes unnoticed until it ends, since
    nothing is reading -- the other side of the cancellation gap above.

  Two hazards the shape does have, both handled and worth stating because
  they are what makes it work rather than incidental. A plain mutex is not
  fair, and the reader releases the stream only to ask for it again for
  another read, so it barges past a writer already waiting -- indefinitely,
  which starves notifications outright rather than delaying them. Making the
  writer wait its turn only narrowed that window; what closes it is that
  whoever holds the stream drains the queue, so the reader carries the
  pending output itself immediately before each read and the writer's
  fairness stops mattering. The reader drains until the queue is empty, not
  one batch. A batch carries at most one notification, so draining one per
  read delivered one notification per 50ms read interval to a client that
  only listens: twenty a second. A burst of N therefore arrived N x 50ms
  late, and any faster stream grew the held list without bound even though
  the client read everything. Every connected block during initial sync is
  such a stream, because neither dcrd nor the port gates blockconnected on
  `IsCurrent`. The drain stops after `READER_DRAIN_BUDGET` (one poll
  interval), so a client that takes a stream more slowly than it is
  produced still has its requests read between drains. Each promotion
  wakes the writer, which carries on as soon as the reader lets go of the
  stream. 200 queued notifications now reach an idle subscriber in about
  0.1s, against 10.3s before
  (`an_idle_subscriber_gets_a_backlog_at_socket_speed`,
  `the_reader_drains_a_whole_backlog_before_it_reads`,
  `the_reader_drain_stops_when_its_budget_is_spent`). What remains is the
  wait for the first notification of a burst, up to one read interval,
  which only the readiness wait described above would remove. And the
  writer parks on a condvar, so the queue is closed from a `Drop` guard
  rather than a trailing statement, for the reason `ClientRegistration`
  already documents: an unwind that skipped the close would leave the
  writer parked and `thread::scope` joining forever.
  Closing discards what is queued rather than draining it, because dcrd's
  `Disconnect` closes the socket out from under `sendChan` (`:1981-1990`) --
  draining would deliver more than dcrd does and could put a data frame after
  the close frame already sent. Discarding alone did not close that window:
  the reader writes its close frame and releases the stream before the queue
  closes, and a writer already waiting on the stream could still write in
  between. `WsConn` now fails every write once a close frame has gone out, as
  gorilla's `writeFatal(ErrCloseSent)` does (`conn.go:410-412`, `:482-484`).
  The writer's next write fails and it stops
  (`nothing_is_written_after_the_close_frame`).

  The interleaving itself has no end-to-end test. The only lever in the
  websocket harness that parks a handler is the chain mutex, and the
  notification builders that consult the chain take it too:
  `notify_for_new_tx` (`best_snapshot` and `is_treasury_agenda_active` for
  every transaction notification) and `notify_work`
  (`is_blake3_pow_agenda_active` and `best_snapshot`), both in
  `rpc/websocket.rs`, so holding it blocks those notifications along with
  the handler. `subscribed_clients` is not among them: it only matches each
  client's filter against the transaction and takes no chain lock.
  `notify_block_connected` consults no chain state either, so that reason
  covers transaction and work notifications, not block ones. The queue's
  contract is pinned by unit tests instead, one of which hangs the suite
  when the writer's exit is not accounted for.

  `rpcmaxconcurrentreqs` is parsed and validated against dcrd's `< 0` rule
  (`load_config_impl` in `config.rs`; `config.go:1070`) and then consumed
  nowhere, because there is still no per-client semaphore for it to size.
  That hides a divergence where the port is the safer of the two:
  `makeSemaphore` is `make(chan struct{}, n)` and `acquire` a bare send
  (`rpcwebsocket.go:62-67`), so at capacity 0 the channel is unbuffered and
  the send never completes. `--rpcmaxconcurrentreqs=0` wedges every
  websocket request in dcrd forever, and dcrd accepts the value because it
  checks only for negatives. The port serves them normally.

- **The RPC server issues no session tickets, so it never resumes.** Go's
  server resumes only from tickets, and dcrd leaves ticketing at its
  defaults. rustls's default ticketer is `NeverProducesTickets`
  (`server/builder.rs:116`), so the port issues none and every connection is
  a full handshake. That is stricter than dcrd rather than weaker, and costs
  handshake work rather than safety -- and costs nothing at all for Decred's
  own tooling, which never offers resumption in the first place: `rpcclient`
  builds its `tls.Config` with only `RootCAs` and `MinVersion`
  (`rpcclient/infrastructure.go:1234`, `:1265`), and Go's `loadSession`
  returns immediately when `ClientSessionCache` is nil
  (`crypto/tls/handshake_client.go:359`).

  Closing it means installing `rustls::crypto::ring::Ticketer`, and that was
  declined because it would trade this gap for a worse one. rustls restores a
  resumed session's stored client certificate chain with no verifier call and
  no expiry check, on the ticket path (`server/tls13.rs:371-376`) exactly as
  on the stateful one (`server/tls12.rs:289`), gated only by `can_resume`'s
  cipher-suite, extended-master-secret and SNI comparison
  (`server/hs.rs:39-58`). Go re-verifies against the current `ClientCAs`,
  rejects an expired certificate, and caps the ticket's age at seven days --
  on BOTH paths, `handshake_server_tls13.go:362-381` and
  `handshake_server.go:479-482`, `:508-526`, over `maxSessionTicketLifetime`
  (`common.go:998`).

  What that would cost is worth stating exactly, because
  `--authtype=clientcert` sets no Basic credentials and `check_auth` then
  returns admin rights with no header at all (`rpc/src/http.rs:102-113`, dcrd
  `rpcserver.go:5522-5527`): under that auth type the completed handshake IS
  the authentication, so a resumption that skips certificate validation is an
  unauthenticated admin session rather than merely an unwanted client.
  Closing it is possible, which an earlier version of this entry denied. It
  said rustls offers no resumption path that re-runs the client verifier, on
  the premise that `ServerSessionValue` and its decoding are crate-private.
  Neither half holds. The chain can be re-verified above rustls rather than
  inside it: `handshake_kind()` (`common_state.rs:188`), `peer_certificates()`
  (`:139`, documented at `:127` as available on resumed handshakes) and
  `ClientCertVerifier::verify_client_cert` (`verify.rs:219`) are all public,
  and the port already holds a client verifier exactly where it would need
  one (`GoClientCertVerifier`, which `build_server_config` in `rpcrun.rs`
  installs over webpki's). `serves_tls_with_a_generated_certificate` --
  the test this entry already cites as pinning the behaviour -- calls
  `handshake_kind()` itself (`rpclisten.rs:828`) to assert the second
  connection was `Full`, so the tree used the API the entry called absent.
  `ServerSessionValue` is reachable too, through `#[doc(hidden)] pub mod
  internal` (`lib.rs:466-470`, `:498`), though that is explicitly not stable
  interface and nothing should be built on it.

  It is declined on what closing it would cost, which is the stronger reason.
  Go rejects a stale ticket with `continue` and falls through to a full
  handshake -- every arm of `checkForResumption` does
  (`handshake_server_tls13.go:351`, `:365`, `:368`, `:371`, `:380`). rustls
  sets `peer_certificates` while handling the ClientHello and exposes it only
  after the handshake, so the port's only remedy is to tear down a connection
  already established. That is a new behavioural divergence, and it lands in
  the one operator-visible case: rotate `--clientcafile` and a `dcrctl` that
  dcrd would silently re-handshake gets a reset instead. That file is also
  the whole revocation mechanism, dcrd shipping neither CRL nor OCSP, and
  `reload` builds a fresh configuration per rotation carrying nothing across
  -- so a ticketer scoped to that configuration preserves no resumption at
  all, while hoisting one out to gain any is precisely what lets ticket keys
  outlive a CA rotation. Set against a client population that is empty, the
  measured benefit is a few microseconds of server CPU on an endpoint that
  caps at ten clients and sets `Connection: close` on every response.

  Two findings worth recording for anyone who revisits it. Installing a
  ticketer would *close* a hazard rather than open one: `early_data_configured`
  requires stateful resumption (`server/tls13.rs:671`), so 0-RTT is
  unreachable once a ticketer is installed and reachable only on the stateful
  path the port already disabled. And rustls applies no age cap whatever --
  `can_resume` compares cipher suite, extended master secret and SNI
  (`server/hs.rs:39-58`) and nothing else, while every resumption re-mints a
  ticket carrying the restored chain with a fresh creation time
  (`server/tls13.rs:1284-1294`, `server/tls12.rs:289-299`). Go's seven day cap
  has no rustls counterpart, so a ticketer would need that bound enforced
  above it as well.

  The related divergence in the other direction is closed:
  `build_server_config` sets `session_storage` to `NoServerSessionStorage`,
  so the port no longer resumes statefully where dcrd never does. It was not
  the TLS1.2 curiosity it first looked like -- with no ticketer configured,
  rustls's TLS1.3 "ticket" is itself a 32-byte key into `session_storage`
  with a 24 hour lifetime (`server/tls13.rs:1330-1338`), so the defect sat on
  the path a modern client actually takes. It was reachable by any client
  that offers resumption, which is third-party tooling rather than dcrctl or
  dcrwallet. Pinned end to end in `serves_tls_with_a_generated_certificate`,
  which reconnects on the same client config, in TLS1.3 and again in
  TLS1.2, and requires a full handshake each time. With the default store
  restored, the TLS1.2 reconnection reports `Resumed`, from the session id
  the store kept; the TLS1.3 one does only once rustls's default of two
  TLS1.3 tickets is restored as well, since `disable_stateful_resumption`
  also sets `send_tls13_tickets` to zero and a TLS1.3 client is then given
  no ticket to present. `stateful_resumption_is_disabled` in
  `rpctlsreload.rs` pins the store itself.

- **Over TLS1.2 a P-521 RPC key signs with SHA-512 where dcrd signs with the
  client's first ECDSA hash, and a client that never offers
  `ecdsa_secp521r1_sha512` gets no handshake.** This is what is left of
  `--tlscurve=P-521` being refused outright, which is closed.

  dcrd generates a P-521 RPC certificate on request (`config.go:1344`,
  `server.go:3582`) and serves whatever pair is on disk
  (`tls.LoadX509KeyPair`, `:3680`). The port used to refuse the flag during
  configuration, and a P-521 key found on disk, because its TLS stack is
  rustls over the `ring` provider, which signs with P-256 and P-384 only
  (`rustls/src/crypto/ring/sign.rs:45-65`). Both refusals are gone. rustls's
  signing is an open extension point: `SigningKey` and `Signer` are public
  (`crypto/signer.rs:59`, `:77`, re-exported at `lib.rs:685`), and ring's
  limit sits entirely in the key parser `with_single_cert` reaches through
  `CertifiedKey::from_der` (`crypto/signer.rs:159-174`). A P-521 key is
  therefore served by the ECDSA signer of `dcroxide-tlscrypto`, the in-tree
  provider ADR-0015 builds (`dcroxide_tlscrypto::sign`): the RPC server
  hands it every key that reads as P-521 by Go's `tls.X509KeyPair` rules,
  under whichever of the three PEM labels the server's reader takes
  (`PRIVATE KEY`, `EC PRIVATE KEY`, `RSA PRIVATE KEY`), and the provider
  loads it by those rules, signs with `p521` and offers
  `ECDSA_NISTP521_SHA512` (0x0603, `enums.rs:511`). The key it loads is
  installed with `with_cert_resolver`, and `keys_match` keeps the
  certificate-against-key check `from_der` makes. Every other key stays
  with ring's loader until ADR-0015's switch. `aws-lc-rs` stays out,
  since it would breach `unsafe_code = "forbid"` (`Cargo.toml:135`) for
  the reason `crates/dcroxide-node/Cargo.toml:59-63` gives.

  The nonce is what held it. `p521` 0.13.3 drew its ECDSA nonce straight from
  the RNG (`p521-0.13.3/src/ecdsa.rs:146`), where `ring` folds the message
  into the nonce to hedge against a faulty RNG
  (`ring/src/ec/suite_b/ecdsa/signing.rs:183-192`) and Go hedges on every
  curve, so serving through it would have given P-521 a weaker nonce than
  P-256 in the same binary. At 0.14.0 `p521::ecdsa::SigningKey` is
  `ecdsa::SigningKey<NistP521>` with SHA-512 (`p521-0.14.0/src/ecdsa.rs:62`,
  `:68-71`), and the handshake signer makes the computation of its hedged
  path: RFC 6979 with field-sized RNG output as additional data
  (`ecdsa-0.17.0/src/signing.rs:220-232`), calling
  `ecdsa::hazmat::sign_prehashed_rfc6979` (`hazmat.rs:102-123`) with 66
  bytes drawn from the process-wide generator rather than the kernel,
  because a handshake is a peer-paced path. The RFC 6979 core under it was
  checked rather than assumed. `p521` 0.14.0 still carries `TODO(tarcieri):
  debug why this is failing` above its A.2.7 vector test (`src/ecdsa.rs:80`),
  but its deterministic signature over that vector (P-521, SHA-512,
  `sample`) is the RFC's r and s byte for byte, and so is OpenSSL 3.6's with
  `pkeyutl -pkeyopt nonce-type:1`; `dcroxide-tlscrypto` pins it, beside
  A.2.5 and A.2.6 for P-256 and P-384, through its signer with no hedge
  (`ecdsa_answers_rfc_6979`, `src/sign.rs`). certgen's own P-521
  self-signature takes the deterministic `Signer` path (`certgen.rs:291`,
  the P-521 arm of `new_tls_cert_pair_parts`).

  Measured side by side against the pin binary on simnet, in four
  arrangements: dcrd serving the pair it generated under `--tlscurve=P-521`,
  the port serving the pair it generated, and each serving the other's pair.
  A Go client on `crypto/tls`, as dcrctl and dcrwallet are, completed a
  JSON-RPC call over TLS1.2 and TLS1.3 in all four; `openssl s_client
  -verify_return_error`, trusting the served certificate, reported
  `Verification: OK` over TLS1.2 and TLS1.3 in all four; and a TLS1.3
  client offering only `ecdsa_secp256r1_sha256` and `ecdsa_secp384r1_sha384`
  was refused with `handshake_failure` by both daemons alike. These runs,
  and the TLS1.2 ones below, predate the signer's move from `rpcrun.rs`
  into `dcroxide-tlscrypto` and have not been re-run since; the crate's
  signer makes the same RFC 6979 computation with the same 66-byte hedge,
  and offers the same one scheme. `serves_tls_with_a_p521_certificate`
  pins the port's side in-tree, on the crate's signer: over TLS1.3 and
  TLS1.2 a client verifies one P-521 signature per handshake, and a client
  offering only what ring verifies gets no handshake.

  TLS1.2 is where they part. An ECDSA scheme there names a hash and not a
  curve, so Go lets an ECDSA key of any curve sign with SHA-256, SHA-384 or
  SHA-512 (`crypto/tls/auth.go:193-201`; SHA-1 is excluded since Go 1.25,
  `common.go:1821-1833`) and picks in the client's order
  (`auth.go:263-267`). Against the pin binary `openssl s_client -tls1_2`
  reports `Peer signature type: ecdsa_secp256r1_sha256` on the P-521 key,
  where the port reports `ecdsa_secp521r1_sha512`. Go's own client lists
  `ecdsa_secp256r1_sha256` ahead of `ecdsa_secp521r1_sha512`
  (`crypto/tls/defaults.go:44`, `:52`), so dcrd signs a Go client's TLS1.2
  handshake with SHA-256 too, where the port uses SHA-512; both verify. A
  TLS1.2 client that never offers `ecdsa_secp521r1_sha512` is served by dcrd.
  The port refuses it, and without an alert: the key's scheme choice fails as
  a bare `Error::General` (`server/tls12.rs:384-385`), which openssl reports
  as `unexpected eof while reading`, whereas its TLS1.3 refusal carries
  `handshake_failure` as dcrd's does. The port cannot follow Go: rustls asks
  the key to
  choose from the client's list without saying which version was negotiated
  (`server/tls12.rs:384`, `server/tls13.rs:825`), the TLS1.3 list still holds
  `ecdsa_secp256r1_sha256` after `supported_in_tls13` (`server/tls13.rs:154`),
  and in TLS1.3 that scheme binds P-256, so a key that accepted it would
  mis-sign TLS1.3 handshakes with Go clients, which list it first. Go offers
  `ecdsa_secp521r1_sha512` in both versions (`crypto/tls/defaults.go:52`), as
  OpenSSL does by default, so dcrctl, dcrwallet and Decrediton never reach
  the refusal. The default P-256 key has the same shape and always has: `ring`'s
  signer offers only its own curve's scheme (`crypto/ring/sign.rs:284-294`)
  where Go would also sign with SHA-384 or SHA-512.

  One thing stays open past it. A P-521 *client* certificate is refused under
  `--authtype=clientcert`; it is recorded with the other client certificates
  webpki and ring refuse in the next entry.

- **Under `--authtype=clientcert` webpki and ring still decide part of a
  client chain: a few certificates dcrd accepts get no handshake, and some
  chains dcrd refuses verify.** The client chain
  is decided by `GoClientCertVerifier` (`build_server_config` in `rpcrun.rs`),
  which follows Go's `x509.Certificate.Verify` for
  `RequireAndVerifyClientCert` wherever webpki would decide otherwise. A
  presented certificate that is itself in the `--clientcafile` pool is its own
  chain (`opts.Roots.contains(c)`, `crypto/x509/verify.go:603-604`), so the
  self-signed CA:TRUE certificate that `gencerts` and certgen write
  authenticates, as it does against dcrd. A pooled certificate's own signature
  is never checked, so a SHA-1 self-signed one is accepted, as Go accepts it.
  A pool certificate anchors a chain only when Go would accept it at the top
  of the chain (`isValid(rootCertificate)` and `checkChainForKeyUsage`):
  inside its validity window, with no critical extension Go leaves unhandled,
  an extended key usage admitting clientAuth, and a `pathLenConstraint` the
  chain's intermediates stay within. webpki's trust anchors keep none of these
  properties.

  The pool loads with `AppendCertsFromPEM`'s skip rules over a port of
  `pem.Decode`, and fails only with dcrd's `no certificates found in %q`;
  `rpc.cert` follows `tls.X509KeyPair`'s block rules. A certificate enters the
  pool only when webpki's trust-anchor parser accepts it and so does a reader
  that follows Go's `ParseCertificate` for nothing after the certificate, the
  version, inner and outer signature algorithms that are the same, the
  validity times (cryptobyte's round-trip rules: no fractional seconds, zone
  hours up to 24), the signature's BIT STRING, the extension list (OID
  encoding, a DER critical flag, no OID twice), the critical marking Go
  forbids on the key identifiers and the authority information access, and
  the key identifiers, key usage, basic constraints, extended key usage,
  subject alternative name and name constraints the reader keeps. Go's
  remaining parse checks are not ported: the serial number, names, public
  key, and the contents of other extensions. A certificate malformed only
  there stays in the pool where dcrd skips it, and only the operator can place
  one there.

  webpki and ring still decide the rest. `GoClientCertVerifier` builds each
  chain it anchors with webpki's `EndEntityCert::verify_for_usage`, called
  directly, for which `rustls-webpki` is a direct dependency of
  `dcroxide-node`: one search over the roots Go would accept, with each path
  the search builds held to its root's `pathLenConstraint` in webpki's path
  callback and each certificate on it to Go's extended key usage rule
  (`GoClientAuthUsage`), so a chained leaf or intermediate whose extended key
  usage includes anyExtendedKeyUsage authenticates, as Go's
  `checkChainForKeyUsage` lets it (`verify.go:1011-1015`; review finding
  GAP06#4). A chain the constraint refuses fails with webpki's error for it,
  which the RPC log reports as Go's `x509: too many intermediates for path
  length constraint`, unless another path the search tried failed in a way
  webpki ranks higher, on a signature, a validity window or a usage, whose
  error is reported instead, where Go reports the constraint's. The alert
  rustls sends with it is `certificate_unknown`, where Go sends
  `bad_certificate`.

  The search keeps webpki's budgets, 100 signature checks, 200,000
  path-building steps and 250,000 name constraint comparisons, where Go keeps
  only its 100 signature checks, and the two count signature checks
  differently: webpki counts them over each whole path it verifies at a root,
  where Go counts one for each candidate issuer, whether or not it leads to a
  root. So a client can spend webpki's budget before Go's. Under a `pathlen:1`
  root, take a leaf whose issuer is sent twice under one key: first as a
  certificate issued by alternative intermediates that share one name and key
  and that the root signs, then as a certificate the root signs directly. With
  that first copy and 33 alternatives sent ahead of the second, the chain is
  refused, where by Go's count up to 48 would still verify. With an
  unconstrained pool that was so before this change as well, the verifier then
  being one webpki search too.

  The other way round, Go's budget is held by replaying Go's own search before
  webpki's (`go_build_chains`): Go's `buildChains` over the whole pool and the
  certificates sent, the candidates in `findPotentialParents`' order, each
  charged one of the 100 checks before anything else is asked of it, so one
  passed over by `alreadyInChain` or for a key Go leaves nil costs one too,
  then held to `CheckSignatureFrom` and `isValid`. That order is go1.25.14's,
  go1.26.2's and go1.27's, whose chain search is the same; go1.24.13, go1.25.4
  and go1.26.0 charged a candidate only after those two checks (go1.26.1 and
  the 1.25 point releases between were not checked). Certificates that match
  an issuer's name but lead to no root cost Go a check each and webpki none, so
  a replay that runs out before it builds a chain refuses the client with
  `x509: signature check attempts limit reached while verifying certificate
  chain`, where webpki alone would go on to a chain. Otherwise webpki searches
  only over the chains the replay built, the certificates sent that lie on one
  and the roots that end one, and accepts only a path that is one of them, so
  a chain past Go's budget, or through a certificate Go passes over, verifies
  only if Go built it. The narrower search matters for webpki's own budget: a
  path it builds and the callback then refuses still spends it, and it tries
  the certificates sent in the order sent, where Go puts the key identifiers'
  matches first, so intermediates the pool's roots signed that Go passes over
  (for a key usage without certificate signing, which webpki ignores), or
  forged copies of the right intermediate sent ahead of it, used to spend it
  before webpki reached the chain Go builds. When the replay builds no chain
  webpki still searches everything, for the error it ranks, and a budget it
  runs out of is reported as an unknown issuer, Go's search having ended for
  another reason. The replay holds a root to `CheckSignatureFrom` as well: a
  version 3 root without basic constraints, one whose basic constraints do not
  assert cA, and one whose key usage leaves out certificate signing anchor
  nothing, where webpki's anchors, which check none of this, anchored them;
  such a refusal is logged as Go's unknown issuer without the hint Go adds,
  `(possibly because of "..." while trying to verify candidate authority
  certificate "...")`. The replay verifies each signature with the ring
  provider's algorithms, as webpki does, and refuses, as Go's RSA verification
  does, an RSA key whose public exponent is above 2^31 - 1, which ring would
  take. A candidate whose signature Go verifies and ring cannot (among them an
  RSA key of 1024 to 2047 bits or over 8192, a P-224 or P-521 key, ECDSA with
  SHA-512, a signature algorithm identifier Go matches by its OID
  alone, such as ecdsa-with-SHA256 with NULL parameters, and a signature whose
  BIT STRING has unused bits, which Go right-aligns) counts as a failed check
  where Go would go on through it; such a chain does not verify here in any
  case, but the checks Go would spend beyond that candidate are not spent. An
  ML-DSA key is one Go 1.27 verifies and ring cannot, and is taken as 1.27
  takes it; 1.26.2, the reference, knows no ML-DSA algorithm, leaves the key
  nil (`crypto/x509/parser.go:1010`) and passes the candidate over
  (`verify.go:741`) at the same one check, so against the reference such a
  candidate's count and verdict are inferred to match (ADR-0001's 2026-10-26
  addendum on the reference Go).
  The verdicts are checked against Go's own `x509.Verify` through the oracle's
  `x509_verify_client` (`gos_signature_budget_charges_every_candidate`,
  `gos_candidate_order_and_checks_shape_its_budget`,
  `webpki_accepts_only_a_chain_gos_search_built`,
  `a_root_signs_only_as_gos_check_signature_from_allows`, and
  `a_candidate_already_on_the_chain_costs_a_check`, which compares only with a
  Go that charges first and otherwise expects the older count's verdict).
  webpki's signature check limit, and the replay's, are logged with that text,
  with the `certificate_unknown` alert where Go sends `bad_certificate`, and
  webpki's path-building limit with rustls's text, unless the search refused a
  path for its policies first (below).

  Each path is also held to Go's policy validation, a port of `policiesValid`
  (`verify.go`: RFC 5280's policy processing, as RFC 9618 replaces parts of it)
  run with the options Go's TLS server uses under dcrd's `tls.Config`, no
  initial policy set and none of the initial require or inhibit flags. A chain
  then fails it only when a certificate's policy constraints require an
  explicit policy and none is left valid, or when a certificate's mapping names
  anyPolicy, on either side. `CertFacts::parse` reads the four policy
  extensions, certificate policies, policy mappings, policy constraints and
  inhibit any policy, by Go's rules, and takes a length, there as everywhere it
  reads, only in the fewest octets, as cryptobyte does; so a certificate Go's
  parser refuses for one of them, such as one listing a policy twice or giving
  a length in more octets than it needs, is refused too. The port replays Go's
  own `TestPoliciesValid` table, with its options, over Go's vectors
  (`tests/data/x509-policy`). A path the validation refuses sends the search on
  to another, as Go drops a chain and keeps the rest. A path holding a
  certificate that Go's `alreadyInChain` takes for its root is refused before
  that, and not counted: Go passes over such a root before checking its
  signature, so a client that sends a copy of its root behind an intermediate
  makes no second chain to Go, where webpki, which keeps that rule for the
  intermediates it adds but not for the anchor, builds one, and the copy, held
  to the policy rules as an intermediate, could pass a chain Go refuses: a copy
  without a policies extension empties the policy graph before an
  intermediate's mapping of anyPolicy is read. webpki's rule for the
  intermediates compares the subject and key only, where Go's also compares the
  subject alternative names, so a path through an intermediate that shares a
  name and key with a certificate below it, but not its alternative names, is
  not built, where Go builds it. The search runs over one copy of each
  certificate the client sent, and the pool holds one copy of each certificate
  the client CA file lists, as Go's pools keep one (`addCertFunc`), so the
  CertificateRequest's certificate authorities name each certificate's subject
  once, as Go's `ClientCAs.Subjects()` does. When the callback has refused a
  path for its policies and the search fails, the refusal is logged as Go's
  `x509: no valid chains built: N candidate chains with invalid policies`, even
  when a budget then ran out. Go drops its signature limit once its search has
  built a chain, and a search in which Go runs out before it builds one is
  refused by the replay with Go's limit text, so a policy refusal always
  follows a chain Go built; but webpki's budgets can still run out before
  Go's, its signature budget included (above, where Go spends its own on a
  candidate ring cannot verify), and Go keeps none of webpki's other two, so
  its search would go on, to count more chains or to find one it accepts. The
  alert is `certificate_unknown` where Go sends `bad_certificate`. N counts the
  paths refused, which are, as far as both searches reach, the chains Go's
  search builds, but for those webpki refuses during its search, before the
  callback, for their key usage or a name constraint, both of which Go checks
  only after the policies. So a chain that fails the policies and one of those is a policy
  failure to Go and never reaches the policy check here: when every chain that
  fails the policies fails the key usage too, the port logs rustls's usage
  error, with `unsupported_certificate`, where Go logs the invalid policies; Go
  also names in the same error the chains it dropped for their key usage alone;
  and when a chain that passes the policies and the key usage breaks a name
  constraint, Go reports that, `x509: a root or intermediate certificate is not
  authorized to sign for this name: ...`, where the port logs the invalid
  policies of the others. When the four policy extensions are critical, webpki
  refuses them (`UnsupportedCriticalExtension`), where Go processes them. Go
  treats a critical subject alternative name with no DNS, email, IP or URI
  name, and a critical name constraints extension with a subtree of another
  form, as an unhandled critical extension, which webpki takes as understood;
  `GoClientCertVerifier` holds the certificates a client presents to it, as it
  holds the pool's, through `CertFacts::parse`. A leaf carrying one is refused
  before the search, as Go's `isValid(leafCertificate)` refuses it before its
  own, and logged as Go's `x509: unhandled critical extension`. A path through
  an intermediate carrying one is refused in the path callback, so the search
  goes on without it as Go's does. Which error a refused chain is then logged
  with follows webpki's ranking of the errors its search met, keeping the
  earlier of two that rank alike, where Go reports the error of the last
  candidate whose signature checked; they agree when that intermediate is the
  only path, and can differ when several paths fail. An intermediate on the
  path that carries a critical extension webpki does not support is refused by
  webpki itself, logged in rustls's words with `certificate_unknown`, where Go
  logs `x509: unhandled critical extension` with `bad_certificate`. Every
  certificate a client presents goes through that reader first, and the
  handshake is refused if one fails, used or not, as Go's
  `processCertsFromClient` refuses it when `ParseCertificate` fails; Go logs
  `tls: failed to parse client certificate: ...` with a `decode_error` alert,
  where the port reports rustls's bad encoding with `bad_certificate`. The
  reader covers the version, the validity times, the extension list and the
  extensions whose facts it keeps, but not the names inside a subject
  alternative name or name constraints extension beyond their forms, nor Go's
  other parse checks, so a certificate Go refuses to parse only for those, such
  as one with a negative serial number or an IP address of five bytes in its
  subject alternative name, can still verify.

  Every handshake signature goes to a `WebPkiClientVerifier`
  (`GoClientCertVerifier::webpki_over`), and client certificates of these
  kinds that dcrd accepts are refused, each failing closed. A P-521 client key
  is refused because the handshake signature is checked with the provider's
  verification algorithms (`webpki/client_verifier.rs:274-295`), and ring's
  stop at P-384 (`crypto/ring/mod.rs:109-112`). So is any chain whose issuer
  signs with ecdsa-with-SHA512, which ring cannot verify, including a P-256
  client certificate issued by a P-521 CA, such as an `rpc.cert` generated
  with `--tlscurve=P-521` and used with `gencerts -C`. So is an RSA client key
  below 2048 bits: ring accepts only `RSA_*_2048_8192`, where Go 1.24 and
  later accept 1024 bits and up. So is an X.509 v1 client certificate, pooled
  or chained: webpki parses only v3 end entities (`cert.rs` `version3`), and
  the handshake signature check parses the certificate the same way. So is a
  chained leaf whose basic constraints mark it a CA: webpki refuses a CA as an
  end entity (`CaUsedAsEndEntity`), where Go's `isValid` asks the CA flag only
  of the intermediates; a pooled one is accepted. So is a chain of more than
  six intermediates, which webpki's path building refuses
  (`MAX_SUB_CA_COUNT`), where Go's search has no such limit. And so is a
  chained leaf or intermediate whose extended key usage extension is present
  but lists nothing: webpki refuses it (`EmptyEkuExtension`, `verify_cert.rs`
  `check_eku`) before the usage rule is asked, where Go's
  `parseExtKeyUsageExtension` reads it as no extended key usage and accepts
  it; a pooled leaf carrying it is accepted. These cases need leaving ring or
  webpki.

- **The RPC listener's TLS key exchange is classical where dcrd's is hybrid
  post-quantum, and its TLS 1.2 offer lacks Go's CBC suites.** dcrd's
  `newTLSConfig` sets only `Certificates` and `MinVersion`
  (`server.go:3685-3688`), so `CurvePreferences` and `CipherSuites` stay nil
  and Go's defaults apply. Go 1.26.2, the reference (ADR-0001's 2026-10-26
  addendum on the reference Go), has three hybrid key exchanges for TLS 1.3 --
  X25519MLKEM768, on by default since Go 1.24, and SecP256r1MLKEM768 and
  SecP384r1MLKEM1024, on since Go 1.26 under the `tlssecpmlkem` setting
  (`crypto/tls/defaults.go:21-35`), as Go 1.27, which built dcrd 2.1.7's
  published linux-amd64 binary, has -- and sorts a hybrid the client supports
  ahead of every other group, even one the client sent a key share for
  (`handshake_server_tls13.go:201-246`). Which of them a dcrd binary offers
  is set by its `debug.go`. 2.1.7's has `//go:debug default=go1.25`, which
  restores Go 1.25's value of every setting changed since, `tlssecpmlkem=0`
  among them (`internal/godebugs/table.go`), so 2.1.7 offers X25519MLKEM768
  alone; at `3360ae4d`, the pin, as on master, it has `default=go1.26`, which
  leaves all three on. Every Go 1.24+ client offers X25519MLKEM768, dcrctl and
  dcrwallet included, so their sessions with dcrd use hybrid post-quantum
  key exchange. The port's listener now chooses among the classical groups
  as Go does, P-521 included (the accepted entry above), but the rustls
  ring provider has no ML-KEM (`crypto/ring/mod.rs:177-181`), which exists
  only under the aws-lc-rs provider the build excludes. So the same clients
  negotiate X25519 with the port, visible to them in
  `ConnectionState.CurveID`; a client that lists a hybrid without a share
  for it gets a HelloRetryRequest for the hybrid from dcrd and the
  classical choice from the port; and a client offering only hybrids that
  dcrd offers is refused. The protocol version and cipher suite are still
  dcrd's (TLS 1.3, TLS_AES_128_GCM_SHA256). The cost falls
  where `--rpclisten` is not local: recorded RPC sessions, the Basic
  credentials every request carries included, lose the
  harvest-now-decrypt-later protection dcrd gives them (review finding
  GAP06#6). ADR-0015 decides how it closes: the in-tree provider that
  replaces ring adds all three hybrids on RustCrypto's `ml-kem`, ordered
  as Go orders them and proven by interop with Go. None of it is built.

  Go's TLS 1.2 list also keeps four CBC suites after the AEADs,
  TLS_ECDHE_ECDSA_WITH_AES_128_CBC_SHA, TLS_ECDHE_RSA_WITH_AES_128_CBC_SHA and
  their AES-256 forms (`cipher_suites.go:289-291`; `disabledCipherSuites`,
  `rsaKexCiphers` and `tdesCiphers`, `:338-367`, leave them on), which rustls
  does not implement. A TLS 1.2
  client offering only CBC suites is served by dcrd and refused by the port
  with `handshake_failure`. No client the Decred tools ship is one: every
  one offers an AES-GCM suite, which both servers prefer.

- **The seeder TLS clients trust the compiled-in `webpki-roots` only.** dcrd's
  seeder client is a bare `http.Transport{DialContext: dialFn}` with a nil
  `TLSClientConfig` (`addrmgr/seed.go:127-131`), so it verifies against the
  platform trust store: on Linux the distro bundle plus the
  `SSL_CERT_FILE`/`SSL_CERT_DIR` overrides, and the platform verifier on macOS
  and Windows. Both port transports verify against the `webpki-roots` snapshot
  built into the binary: `ProxySeederTransport` through `seeder_tls_config`
  (`seeding.rs`), and the direct `ureq` transport through ureq's default
  `RootCerts::WebPki`. Behind a TLS-intercepting proxy whose root is installed
  system-wide, or with a private `SSL_CERT_FILE`, dcrd seeds while the port
  fails every seeder with `UnknownIssuer` and retries on the 1-10 s backoff,
  so a fresh node cannot bootstrap without `--connect` or `--addpeer`. In a
  container with no CA bundle it is the other way round: the port seeds and
  dcrd cannot. Roots the OS adds or distrusts reach the port only through a
  rebuild with a newer `webpki-roots`. Closing this needs
  `rustls-native-certs`, or `rustls-platform-verifier` on macOS and Windows,
  which is a new dependency, and a decision on which trust store the seeder
  uses, because a seeder MITM is the eclipse-bootstrap vector (review finding
  GAP06#5). Separately, the direct `ureq` path follows up to ten redirects as
  Go's client does, while the proxied path follows none (see the daemon row).

- **The RPC listener refuses an RSA `rpc.key` that ring cannot sign with, one
  outside 2048 to 4096 bits or with an exponent under 65537, where dcrd
  serves it.** dcrd loads the pair with `tls.LoadX509KeyPair`
  (`server.go:3680`) and serves any RSA key Go's `crypto/rsa` takes: 1024 bits
  and up, a smaller key being refused under the default `rsa1024min` setting
  (`crypto/rsa/rsa.go:280-293`), with an odd exponent from 3 to 2^31 - 1
  (`crypto/internal/fips140/rsa/rsa.go:343-362`). Go's own client, which
  dcrctl and dcrwallet use, takes a server key of up to 8192 bits
  (`crypto/tls/handshake_client.go:1085-1112`). Until ADR-0015's switch the
  port hands every key but a P-521 one to rustls's ring loader
  (`CertifiedKey::from_der` on ring's provider, `rpcrun.rs:4480`), whose RSA
  signer takes a modulus of 2048 to 4096 bits and an exponent of at least
  65537 (`ring-0.17.14/src/rsa/keypair.rs:268-285`,
  `ring-0.17.14/src/rsa.rs:35`) and which refuses every other key with
  "failed to parse private key as RSA, ECDSA, or EdDSA"
  (`rustls-0.23.46/src/crypto/ring/sign.rs:21-40`). So an RSA-1024, RSA-8192
  or e = 3 key that dcrd serves stops the port at startup, with an error
  carrying those words, and a certificate rotation to one keeps the
  configuration already served, with a warning (`rpcrun.rs:2085-2098`), where
  dcrd takes the new pair. `gencerts` and certgen write no RSA key, so only a
  pair made elsewhere reaches this. The node's Go interop matrix pins it:
  `go_clients_handshake_with_the_rpc_listener`
  (`crates/dcroxide-node/tests/tls_interop.rs`) has the listener refuse the
  test-key fixture's RSA-1024, RSA-8192 and e = 3 keys in ring's words while
  Go's server, under dcrd's GODEBUG, serves each to Go's client. ADR-0015's
  T6 refuses every RSA key at the switch, the in-tree provider having no RSA
  signer, so the switch widens this entry to every RSA key and, for the keys
  it lists now, changes only the refusal's text. Closing it would take an
  RSA signer, which ADR-0015 declines for the reasons under its T5 and T6,
  the `rsa` crate's timing advisory among them.

- **A `--maxpeers` inside the host-dependent band exhausts memory in dcrd
  and not the port.** `newServer` builds its relay and broadcast queues as
  `make(chan relayMsg, cfg.MaxPeers)` and `make(chan broadcastMsg,
  cfg.MaxPeers)` (`server.go:3931-3932`) before it computes anything from the
  flag, and Go takes a channel capacity as a signed `int`. Nothing validates
  the flag first -- `config.go:167` declares `MaxPeers int` with no range --
  and nothing on the startup path recovers.

  `makechan` refuses on two disjuncts of one `if` (`runtime/chan.go:87`):
  `size < 0`, and `mem > maxAlloc-hchanSize`. Both raise `panic: makechan:
  size out of range`, neither consults installed memory, and both are now
  reproduced by `max_peers_is_startable`. Between them lies a third regime
  that an earlier version of this entry mistook for the whole positive half:
  a capacity Go accepts and then cannot allocate, dying `fatal error:
  runtime: out of memory`. That threshold really is the host's, so it is not
  reproduced, and it is all that remains open here. dcroxide relays
  synchronously and allocates no such queue, so within that band it still
  reaches decisions dcrd never survives to make -- which is why QK-0014's two
  disagreeing outbound targets matter here and not upstream.

  The upper bound is derived rather than invented:
  `(maxAlloc - hchanSize) / sizeof(relayMsg) + 1`, which is `(2^48 - 104)/40
  + 1` = 7036874417764 on every 64-bit target dcrd ships. It does not track
  the Go release -- at 40 bytes an element any `hchanSize` in `[104, 136]`
  yields the same integer, covering the reference Go CI pins (`GO_VERSION`,
  1.26.2) and 1.27.1, which declare `hchan`, `waitq` and `mutex` alike. A
  32-bit build never reaches the regime by another route: `int` is
  32 bits there, so go-flags rejects the value while parsing, as it does past
  `int64` max on any build -- `--maxpeers=9223372036854775808` exits 1 without
  starting.

  Measured against the pin binary rather than a `makechan` probe, each input
  in its own subprocess: `125` starts, `7036874417763` dies out of memory
  asking for a 281TB block, and `7036874417764` and `9223372036854775807`
  both panic at `server.go:3931` before allocating a byte. The `mpchan|` rows
  of `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/data/srvtargetout_vectors.txt` carry them.

  What made the deterministic half worth reproducing was not the threshold
  but a contradiction inside the port's own rule. `uint32(-1)` and
  `uint32(2^63-1)` are the same value, 4294967295, so `--maxpeers=-1` and
  `--maxpeers=9223372036854775807` reach `MaxNormalConns` byte-identical and
  kill dcrd at the same line with the same message -- yet the port refused
  the first and booted the second into precisely the no-peer-limit state that
  first refusal exists to prevent. The counterexample was already in the
  fixture, and the assertion over those rows carried an `&& max_peers < 0`
  clause that exempted it, so a suppressed divergence read as an open one.
- **`getwork` cancellation is complete on every platform over HTTP; over a
  websocket a client hangup goes unnoticed.** dcrd cancels getwork in three
  places -- the semaphore queue (`rpcserver.go:4170-4174`) and both template
  waits inside the hold (`:3914`, `:3932`) -- and all three are ported. What
  was partial is the signal that drives them: detecting that the client has
  gone.

  A `MSG_PEEK` cannot answer it, because a TLS peer writes a `close_notify`
  before the FIN and the handler, blocked in the hold, never reads it, so the
  peek finds bytes rather than a zero-length read. The probe has to read the
