  peer's half-close state instead, and there is now one on every platform the
  port builds for: `POLLRDHUP` through rustix on Linux, FreeBSD and illumos;
  `EVFILT_READ`'s `EV_EOF` through nix's safe `Kqueue::kevent` on macOS and
  the other BSDs; and `WSAPoll`'s `POLLHUP`/`POLLERR` through rustix on
  Windows, with `FIONREAD` standing in for the peek that Winsock cannot
  express. None of it needed a new crate.

  An earlier version of this entry claimed no safe Windows probe existed,
  because "socket2's `WSAPoll` use is `pub(crate)` and rustix's poll is
  `cfg(not(windows))`". The first half is true; the second is false, and it
  was the load-bearing half. rustix exports `poll` unconditionally
  (`event/mod.rs:16`, `:32`, neither carrying a cfg, in visible contrast to
  the `#[cfg(bsd)]` on `kqueue`) and its libc backend aliases `WSAPoll as
  poll` on Windows (`backend/libc/winsock_c.rs:36`). Only the `RDHUP` flag is
  gated away, not the call. `ioctl_fionread` is likewise excluded only for
  espidf, horizon and vita.

  The load-bearing semantic -- whether the half-close is reported while data
  is still buffered -- is measured on both platforms rather than assumed.
  Driving a loopback pair through the four shapes that matter, on macOS
  26.6.2 and on Windows 11 build 26200:

  | case | kqueue `EV_EOF` | `WSAPoll` revents | `MSG_PEEK` |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | data then FIN (the TLS shape) | set, `data`=5 | `0x0102` (HUP+RDNORM) | 1 byte |
  | bare FIN | set, `data`=0 | `0x0002` (HUP) | 0 |
  | data, no FIN (pipelined) | clear | `0x0100` (RDNORM) | 1 byte |
  | idle | no event | `0x0000` | would block |

  Both probes report the half-close with bytes still queued, which is the
  case the peek cannot see -- note it returns a byte, not a zero-length read,
  in exactly that row. Both stay silent for a pipelined request, so neither
  can cancel a client that is merely mid-conversation. The kqueue result
  matches the kernel sources: FreeBSD's `filt_soread` computes `kn_data` and
  then takes the `SBS_CANTRCVMORE` branch setting `EV_EOF` without testing
  the byte count, and XNU's `filt_soread_common` does the same. Windows has
  no `RDHUP` in the WSAPoll vocabulary, but `POLLHUP` turns out to carry it.

  `a_hangup_behind_pending_bytes_is_seen` runs on the kqueue platforms and on
  Windows so CI keeps checking what these measurements established once. Both
  arms answer only a positive observation and decline otherwise, so a
  regression costs a red test rather than a silent behaviour change.

  The title said "one Windows semantic is unverified" until this commit.
  `838e3e7` wrote that while landing the Windows arm and never revised it,
  though the semantic it meant -- the `WSAPoll` row of the table above -- had
  already been measured in `76f33e8`, an ancestor of it, and has been checked
  by that test on every Windows CI run since. Nothing in the body ever
  supported the title; the word appeared nowhere else in the entry. What
  keeps this open is the websocket arm below.

  Two dead ends are recorded so nobody walks back into them. Detection by
  inspecting the peeked bytes cannot work: TLS 1.3 wraps an alert in outer
  content type 23, so an encrypted `close_notify` is indistinguishable from
  application data. And a pipelined request is deliberately not treated as a
  hangup. dcrd's JSON-RPC handler hijacks the connection, which ends
  `net/http`'s own background read. It then watches the connection with a
  goroutine that reads one byte and cancels only if that read *fails*
  (`rpcserver.go:5709-5731`). That is why every arm here reads half-close
  state rather than treating pending bytes as a signal in either direction.
  This paragraph was written into the entry by `ac6967b` and lost when
  `74caff7` rewrote it; that is the sort of deletion the ledger exists to
  prevent, so it is restored rather than silently reconstructed.

  The goroutine is one-shot, so one difference follows. Once a byte past the
  request arrives, the goroutine returns without cancelling, and dcrd no
  longer notices a later hangup at all. The port's probe keeps polling and
  does notice it. This is kept deliberately: noticing only frees a getwork
  slot held for a caller that is gone.

  The eventual platform-independent answer is not a better socket probe but
  `rustls::IoState::peer_has_closed` (`common_state.rs:841`), which sees the
  `close_notify` itself. It is unreachable today because the handler owns the
  `ServerConnection` and is parked in the hold, so nothing is driving
  `process_new_packets`; reaching it means restructuring who reads the TLS
  session during a getwork hold.

  The shutdown arm works everywhere, including over the websocket transport,
  which now installs a token of its own where it previously installed none.

  dcrd services a websocket command through the same handlers as HTTP:
  `serviceRequest` falls through to `standardCmdResult(ctx, r)` for any method
  not in its websocket-only table (`rpcwebsocket.go:1807-1819`), and getwork
  is not in that table. The context is the UPGRADE request's
  (`rpcserver.go:6045`), descending from the server's through `BaseContext`
  (`rpcserver.go:5925-5931`), so shutdown cancels it.

  dcrd reaches it on a client hangup too, which is worth stating carefully
  because the obvious derivation says otherwise. The upgrade hijacks the
  connection and Go's `hijackLocked` calls `abortPendingRead`
  (`net/http/server.go:318-322`), which does kill the background read that
  cancels an ordinary request. But `conn.serve` also calls `w.cancelCtx()`
  right after `ServeHTTP` returns, unconditionally and before the
  `c.hijacked()` check (`server.go:2137-2140`), and that is the cancel for
  `r.Context()` (`:1089`, `:1106`). A non-batched command runs in a goroutine
  (`rpcwebsocket.go:1550`) that `Run`'s `wg.Add(3)` does not cover
  (`:1998-2011`), so an in-flight getwork outlives the teardown and is still
  selecting on the context when that fires.

  Not on every arm, though. dcrd degrades to shutdown-only wherever it stops
  reading: a batched request, serviced inline in `inHandler`
  (`rpcwebsocket.go:1748`), and any request arriving once the per-client
  `serviceRequestSem` is exhausted, since it is acquired before the spawn
  (`:1549`) with capacity `RPCMaxConcurrentReqs`.

  The port is always in that second case. Its websocket loop dispatches
  synchronously, so while a request runs nothing is reading and a hangup
  cannot be observed at all -- no token could carry it. Installing the
  shutdown flag is therefore the whole of what is reachable without making
  websocket dispatch concurrent, which is a structural change well beyond
  this one. Recorded here rather than left implicit: the port matches dcrd's
  batched and saturated arms exactly, and diverges from its concurrent arm by
  not cancelling an in-flight websocket getwork when that client goes away.

  That one line has no automated test. Observing the flag through a websocket
  needs a request already in flight when shutdown is raised -- the loop checks
  shutdown before its next read, so anything sent afterwards is never
  dispatched -- and getwork is not callable in the websocket harness, which
  wires `block_templater: None`. The guard's per-request semantics are pinned
  by `the_cancel_scope_does_not_leak_across_requests`; the line installing it
  is verified by construction.

- **`getticketpoolvalue`, `ticketsforaddress` and `getstakeversions` hold the
  chain mutex for their whole walk, where dcrd holds its chain lock only to
  read the tip, or not at all.** dcrd's `TicketPoolValue` and
  `TicketsWithAddress` hold `chainLock` only to read the tip's stake node
  (`stakeext.go:140-143`, `:81-83`). They then fetch every live ticket's entry
  through the UTXO cache with the lock released. The port's RPC seams
  (`rpcrun.rs` `ticket_pool_value` and `tickets_with_address`) run the whole
  walk under the chain `Mutex`: about 41k entry fetches on mainnet, so a block
  waiting to connect waits for the call. The work itself matches dcrd, and
  each walk's cache misses share one read transaction. Only the lock scope
  differs. Closing it means moving the walk out of the single-writer lock
  ([ADR-0005](adr/0005-concurrency-model.md)). The UTXO cache is a
  `RefCell` inside `Chain`, so the walk can leave the mutex only through a
  backend read that bypasses the cache, which drops dcrd's cache-population
  side effect, or through a cache with its own lock. dcrd's `GetStakeVersions`
  takes no lock at all and shares each block node's `votes` slice
  (`chain.go:310-347`). The port walks up to the whole chain under the mutex
  and copies each node's votes: about 1.1M block nodes on mainnet for a count
  as large as the chain. Leaving the mutex there needs the votes shared in the
  block index and a walk that releases the mutex between bounded chunks, a
  `dcroxide-blockchain` change to the lock model under ADR-0005 that waits on
  a measurement: the mutex hold time and peak RSS of one max-count call on
  mainnet. The handler then builds the result as a tree of `GoValue`s
  (`handle_get_stake_versions` in `dcroxide-rpc`) where dcrd builds typed
  structs, so by estimate the call peaks at more than twice dcrd's memory;
  encoding large results straight from the typed rows would skip the tree, and
  the same measurement says whether that matters (review finding R1-p#3). The
  live-ticket walks no longer have the gap: `livetickets` and
  `existslivetickets` copy the tip's stake node under the mutex, the copy
  sharing its persistent ticket treaps, and walk the copy once the mutex is
  released, as dcrd's `LiveTickets` and `CheckLiveTickets` do
  (`stakeext.go:68-73`, `:121-131`); a later connect, made in place or not,
  copies every node the copy shares before it changes one, so the walk reads
  the tip it was taken at. `existsliveticket` makes a single lookup, which is
  cheaper than the copy, so it stays under the mutex. The treasury spend vote
  tally had the same gap and no longer does. The RPC and mining seams capture
  the voting window under the mutex, then read and tally its blocks after
  releasing it (`TSpendVoteWindow`), matching dcrd's `TSpendCountVotes` and
  `CheckTSpendHasVotes`, which take no `chainLock` (`treasury.go:1097`,
  `:1162`).

- **The version message's newest-block hook and the RPC server read the tip
  under the chain mutex, where dcrd reads a snapshot under a lock held only to
  publish it.** Every handshake, inbound or outbound, builds our `version`
  from the tip: dcrd's `serverPeer.newestBlock` (`server.go:744-747`) calls
  `BestSnapshot`, which takes only `stateLock.RLock()`
  (`internal/blockchain/chain.go:1609-1613`). The daemon's hook
  (`build_server` in `bin/dcroxide.rs`) locks the chain `Mutex` for
  `best_snapshot()` instead. That is the single-writer lock a block connect
  holds across any UTXO flush it triggers -- 528 to 2,590 ms a flush over the
  full mainnet replay (`docs/bench-ledger.md:298`) -- and `invalidateblock` or
  `reconsiderblock` holds across a whole reorganization. A handshake that
  arrives then waits the hold out, and one longer than the remote's 30 s
  negotiation timeout fails the connection on both sides, where dcrd answers
  at once. The RPC seams read the tip the same way:
  `NodeRpcChain::best_snapshot` (`rpcrun.rs`) for every handler that reads it,
  and `NodeChainQueryer::best` (`indexes.rs`) for each poll of the index sync
  wait, where dcrd's handlers call the same `BestSnapshot`. Those handlers
  wait the hold out too, and the three seconds `getrawtransaction`,
  `existsaddress` and `existsaddresses` give an index to catch up are no bound
  while it lasts. The wait checks its deadline only between polls, so it can
  outlast them and then answer from an index that caught up meanwhile; dcrd's
  races `time.After(syncWait)` and answers `<index>: index not synced` once
  the three seconds pass (`internal/rpcserver/rpcserver.go:1634-1648`,
  `:1691-1705`, `:2981-2995`). The block-index queries wait the same way.
  dcrd's `BestHeader`, `HeaderByHash`, `HeaderByHeight`, `BlockHashByHeight`,
  `MainChainHasBlock`, `MedianTimeByHash` and `ChainWork` take only the block
  index's or the chain view's own locks, where every `NodeRpcChain` seam takes
  the chain `Mutex`. So while a block is processed, dcrd answers
  getblockcount, getbestblock, getbestblockhash, getblockhash and the
  non-verbose getblockheader at once, and the port waits for the block. Those
  answers do not differ, only their latency. Closing it means publishing the
  snapshot outside the chain mutex where `Chain` installs it (`connect_block`,
  `disconnect_block`, `load_chain_state`), as dcrd's `stateSnapshot` is, or
  mirroring the tip in the daemon from the chain's connect and disconnect
  notifications, a second copy that must follow every tip change; either way
  the three seams read it. Which of the two is open. The block-index queries
  would also need a view of the block index shareable outside the mutex. Both
  change the single-writer model
  ([ADR-0005](adr/0005-concurrency-model.md)), so the change waits on a
  measurement: getblockcount polled at 10 Hz on both daemons during a mainnet
  IBD, comparing p50, p99 and maximum latency (review finding R1-p#2).

- **An unclean stop costs a full redb repair of the metadata store at the next
  open.** goleveldb recovers by replaying its journal. redb, finding no saved
  allocator state, verifies and rebuilds from the whole file, up to three full
  scans (redb-4.3.0 `db.rs:1473`). Flush commits are one-phase and save no
  allocator state, so every abort, SIGKILL, OOM kill or power loss is followed
  by that repair. The repair is logged under BCDB as "Detected unclean
  shutdown of the metadata store - Repairing..." and then "Metadata store
  repair N% complete" lines; this is port-specific text with no dcrd
  counterpart. `Database::close` ends with one quick-repair commit, so a store
  closed with it opens without the repair however long the handle's clones
  live, and then lets go of redb's handle, which closes the file: redb clears
  the recovery flag it keeps in the file's header while the file is open for
  writing (redb-4.3.0 `page_manager.rs:1458-1469`, `:2466-2486`). A clean
  daemon stop does not need the repair: the daemon closes its database on
  every exit once it is open, as dcrd's deferred `db.Close()` does. Until
  2026-10-23 the close left redb's handle to the drop of the last clone of
  the database handle, and the daemon's chain holds a clone in a reference
  cycle (its notification callback, the notification handler, the
  transaction pool, the chain), so the handle lived until the process
  exited and a cleanly stopped node's file kept the flag. Its next start
  then rewrote the header and loaded the saved allocator state, as a start
  after a clean stop now loads it, with no repair, and redb refused to open
  the file read-only ("Database repair aborted"); the file now opens read-only
  after a clean stop (`tests/shutdown_recovery_flag.rs`), unless a request
  from a peer or an RPC client was still reading the store as the process
  exited: redb closes the file only once the last transaction begun before the
  close has ended, so the flag then stays, and the next start loads the saved
  allocator state without a repair, as it did after every clean stop before.
  Closing the rest would take quick-repair on every flush commit (two-phase
  commit plus an allocator-state write, so a second fsync per flush). That is
  unmeasured, and so is the open-time cost after an unclean stop.

- **A failed block-index flush loses its rows, where dcrd retries them at
  the next flush.** dcrd's `blockIndex.Flush` clears the modified set only
  after a successful `db.Update` (`blockindex.go:1416-1432`), so a failed
  flush is retried by the next one. The port's `take_block_index_rows`
  drains the set through `BlockIndex::take_modified` before
  `flush_block_index` opens its write (`process.rs`), so a failed write
  loses those rows until something marks the nodes again: a strictly worse
  worst case than the revalidation dcrd's own NOTE describes
  (`chain.go:1503-1505`). It applies to every block-index flush, not only
  the warn-only ones. In practice a failed durable write also latches the
  store fatal (the "Metadata store after a failed durable write" row of the
  safety-controls table), which refuses every write that checks the latch
  after it, so the drained rows matter only for a write refused before it
  commits, on a closed handle or from inside its closure, which does not
  latch. In the daemon a commit that returned between a flush's hand-off
  and that flush's failure was not refused, and the rows it published,
  never acknowledged as durable, are lost at restart with the failed
  batch, which no retry would save either. This
  sat in the safety-controls table until the 2026-09-25 review; it is no
  safety control. Closing it means taking the modified set only once
  `db.update` has succeeded, and marking the nodes again on failure as the
  ticket-info failure path in `take_block_index_rows` already does.

- **`invalidateblock` and `reconsiderblock` flush the block index before
  their final reorganization, where dcrd flushes after it.** dcrd's
  `InvalidateBlock` goes from `MarkBlockFailedValidation` (`process.go:731`)
  straight to the isCurrent latch reset, and flushes next after its final
  `reorganizeChain` (`:776-777`); `ReconsiderBlock` flushes after its
  reorganization too (`:927-928`). The port's `invalidate_block` flushes
  warn-only right after `mark_block_failed_validation`, a flush with no dcrd
  counterpart, and `invalidate_block` and `reconsider_block` both flush
  before their final `reorganize_chain` rather than after it. dcrd's
  `connectBlock` and `disconnectBlock` flush the index during the
  reorganization (`chain.go:613`, `:825`), and so do the port's, so what
  differs is a status the final reorganization sets after its last connect
  or disconnect -- a candidate it marks failed validation, say: dcrd writes
  it before the RPC returns, the port at its next flush. The warning count
  on a failing store still matches dcrd's, one line, but through the drain
  in the entry above rather than by design: `mark_block_failed_validation`
  always leaves a row, so the extra flush always writes, and on a failed
  write it drains the set, so the pre-reorganization flush finds it empty
  and stays silent where dcrd's post-reorganization flush warns. Both are
  reachable through the `invalidateblock` and `reconsiderblock` RPCs, which
  the daemon serves since the 2026-09-23 review. This sat in the
  safety-controls table until the 2026-09-25 review. Closing it means
  flushing after the final `reorganize_chain` in both, as dcrd does, and
  dropping the extra flush after `mark_block_failed_validation`; with the
  entry above closed too, the warning count then matches for dcrd's reason.

- **Startup logs neither dcrd's STKE stake database line nor its UTXO
  database load lines.** On every start dcrd logs "Stake database version N
  loaded" under STKE (`blockchain/stake/tickets.go:445`, from
  `LoadBestNode`), and before the chain loads, `LoadUtxoDB` logs "Loading UTXO
  database from '<datadir>/utxodb'" and "UTXO database loaded" under CHAN
  (`internal/blockchain/utxobackend.go:361`, `:373`). The port's stake crate
  takes no log sink, and there is no separate UTXO database to load: the UTXO
  set lives in the block database
  ([ADR-0004](adr/0004-storage-backend.md)). The chain open's own CHAN
  lines are ported (see the `internal/blockchain` row). Closing the STKE half
  means a log sink on `dcroxide_stake::stakedb::load_best_node` that the chain
  hands the daemon's STKE sink. The UTXO database lines have nothing to report
  in this layout.

- **Some refused RPC TLS handshakes are logged with rustls's description where
  dcrd logs Go's.** The line is dcrd's (the accepted entry on the listener's
  TLS handshake), but its `<err>` is Go's text only where the two stacks
  refuse for the same reason and `rpctls::go_error_text` knows the
  correspondence. Elsewhere it is rustls's own description of its error. That
  covers a handshake message out of order, which Go reports as `tls: received
  unexpected handshake message of type <Go type> when waiting for <Go type>`;
  a malformed message, which Go reports as `local error: tls: error decoding
  message` and the port reports that way only when rustls classifies it as
  `InvalidMessage`; a TLS 1.2 ClientKeyExchange whose point is empty or runs
  past the message, which Go answers with `illegal_parameter` and reports as
  `tls: invalid ClientKeyExchange message`, where rustls sends no alert and
  the port reports `local error: tls: error decoding message` (with bytes
  after the point the text is Go's, but rustls's alert is `decode_error`); and
  a client certificate refused for any reason but an unknown issuer (without
  the hint Go adds when a candidate failed its checks, see the clientcert
  entry), a path length constraint, an unhandled critical extension on the leaf or of a
  subject alternative name or name constraints form, invalid policies, or the
  signature check limit, where Go prints x509's error with its times and
  names. It also covers the refusals dcrd never makes, because it would have
  served the client: a TLS 1.2 client without `ecdsa_secp521r1_sha512` against
  a P-521 key (`unexpected error: incompatible signing key`, see the P-521
  entry). A CBC-only or hybrid-only client is refused in Go's words for no
  suite or no key exchange in common, though dcrd would not refuse it at all.
  Socket errors take Go's Unix wording, `read: connection reset by peer`; on
  Windows Go names the call `wsarecv` or `wsasend` and keeps the system
  message's capital, where the port writes `read` or `write` and lower-cases
  it. Closing the rest is a longer table in `go_error_text`, case by case.

- **The RPC server does not log a websocket client's arrival or departure.**
  dcrd's `internal/rpcserver` logs through a package logger that the daemon
  binds to RPCS (`rpcserver.UseLogger(rpcsLog)`, `log.go:96`).
  `dcroxide_rpc::log` is that logger, installed by `bin/dcroxide.rs` through
  `logging::rpcs_log_sink`, and it now carries:

  * the Basic-auth failure warning with the client's address
    (`checkAuthMAC`, `checkAuth`);
  * every `rpcInternalErr` as `context: err`, with dcrd's context texts;
  * the reply marshal and create failures of `processRequest`,
    `jsonRPCRead`, `inHandler` and `serviceRequest`;
  * the websocket already-authenticated and unauthenticated warnings, and
    the debug received-command line;
  * the handler lines of getstakedifficulty, submitblock, the getwork
    submission, sendrawtransaction, verifychain, getnetworkhashps and
    `createVoutList`;
  * the notification paths' agenda, lottery-data, commitment and
    mix-encode failures.

  Not ported yet: `New websocket client`, `Max websocket clients exceeded`,
  `Failed to serve client` and `Disconnected websocket client`
  (`rpcwebsocket.go:112-133`); `Websocket receive error from` with its
  `shouldLogReadError` filter; the per-client handlers' Trace lines; and the
  notification marshal failures, which cannot occur for well-formed
  notifications. The lifecycle lines sit on the upgrade path, which needs no
  credentials, and there the port rate-limits its `Max RPC clients exceeded`
  shed line rather than log at a flood's rate. Porting them first needs a
  decision: verbatim like dcrd, or through the same limiter.

- **On macOS and Windows the autogenerated RPC certificate has no interface IP
  SANs.** dcrd's `certgen.NewTLSCertPair` adds every `net.InterfaceAddrs`
  address and names the host by `os.Hostname`. The port reads both from the
  kernel on Linux (the host name from procfs, the addresses over route
  netlink, as Go does), but has no safe source for them elsewhere in the
  current dependency set. There the certificate carries only 127.0.0.1, ::1,
  the name from `/etc/hostname` or `$HOSTNAME` (else `localhost`), and
  `--altdnsnames`. A remote client that pins `rpc.cert` and connects by a LAN
  address fails verification. Closing this needs a decision on a dependency
  feature: nix's `net` (`getifaddrs`) or rustix's `net`/`system`.

- **TCP keepalive is set on accepted RPC sockets only, and without Go's
  explicit probe count.** Go enables keepalive on every accepted and dialed
  TCP connection: 15 s idle, 15 s interval, 9 probes. Since the 2026-09-23
  review the RPC accept loop sets the idle time and the interval
  (`rpcrun::enable_keepalive`) but not the count. socket2's `with_retries`
  needs its `all` feature, so the count is the OS default: 9 on Linux, 8 on
  macOS and 10 on Windows. P2P sockets carry no keepalive. Pinned by
  `review_rpc_listen::accepted_connections_carry_gos_keepalive`.

- **A body the authenticated read cannot finish gets no answer.** dcrd's
  `jsonRPCRead` answers any `io.ReadAll` error with `http.Error(w, "400 error
  reading JSON message: %v", 400)`. That covers a body that ends before its
  Content-Length, one that stalls until the read deadline, and a chunked body
  whose stream ends or stalls below the limit. The port closes without an
  answer when that read fails or times out. A malformed chunked body below the
  limit is already answered as dcrd answers it, with Go's chunked-reader texts
  (`malformed chunked encoding`, `invalid byte in chunk length`). Matching
  dcrd for the rest needs Go's texts for the failed read: `unexpected EOF` and
  `read tcp A->B: i/o timeout`. Not changed.

- **The RPC `syncnode` flag still waits on the sync manager.** dcrd's
  `SyncPeerID` takes only the small `syncPeerMtx`.
  `NodeRpcSyncManager::sync_peer_id`, which feeds getpeerinfo's `syncnode`,
  takes the whole netsync manager mutex, and the dispatcher holds that mutex
  across block processing. Since the 2026-09-23 review, `sync_height` and
  `recently_confirmed_txn` read their own shared handles. Closing this needs a
  shared sync-peer handle exported from dcroxide-netsync.

- **`version` reports an empty `buildmetadata` on a live node.** dcrd's
  `handleVersion` builds the `dcrd` entry's build metadata from Go's
  `runtime.Version()` with its dots turned into dashes, after
  `version.BuildMetadata` and a dot when that is not empty
  (`internal/rpcserver/rpcserver.go:5203-5208`). `BuildMetadata` is what the
  version string carries after its `+`, and when it carries none and the
  binary has a VCS stamp, the commit's first nine hex digits, which `init`
  also appends to the version string (`internal/version/version.go`
  `vcsCommitID`, `init`). So the field is `go1-26-4` for a go1.26.4 build of
  the pin without a VCS stamp, `<commit>.go1-26-4` beside a `versionstring`
  of `2.2.0-pre+<commit>` for one built in a git checkout, and the version's
  own metadata before the runtime's for a release build. The port's
  `handle_version` makes the same computation over the RPC `Config`'s
  `runtime_version` and an empty `BUILD_METADATA` (`dcroxide-rpc`
  `version.rs`), and the frozen vectors inject the oracle's Go version, but
  the daemon has no Go runtime and sets the field to `String::new()`
  (`rpc_config` in `bin/dcroxide.rs`), so a live node answers
  `"buildmetadata":""` and `versionstring` `2.2.0-pre`, whatever it was built
  from. The field is informational. Closing it means a choice
  between a build script that captures a Rust toolchain identifier (rustc's
  version, normalized the same way), which is itself a value dcrd never
  reports, and keeping the field empty and moving this entry to Accepted as a
  settled departure. Either way a daemon-level test of the live `version`
  answer should pin the result (review finding R4-p#4).

- **Several descriptors per connection, and no block-file LRU.** The daemon
  raises its descriptor limit as dcrd does (see the daemon row), but it spends
  about five descriptors per inbound peer (the socket plus `try_clone` dups
  for the registry, the server handler, the writer and the timer thread's
  stall teardown; six outbound; one more on a connection that is read ahead
  of its block handler, for the handle its input thread ends it with), five
  per pre-authentication RPC connection
  and three per websocket. It also keeps a read handle open for every block
  file it has touched, where dcrd uses one descriptor per connection and caps
  open block files at 25 with an LRU (`maxOpenFiles`, `ffldb/blockio.go:43`).
  Under the usual hard limit (524288 under systemd) that leaves ample room.
  Under a hard limit set to a few thousand, a peer and RPC flood reaches it
  well before it would reach dcrd, and a failed block-file reopen when a flush
  syncs a file it has rolled over from (`SyncPlan::run`) then latches the
  database fatal. Closing it means sharing one `Arc<TcpStream>` per connection
  instead of dup'ing, and porting dcrd's block-file LRU. The `addblock` tool
  does not raise its limit either, where dcrd's `cmd/addblock` gets the Go
  runtime's raise and calls `limits.SetLimits` (exiting 1 silently on its
  error); `dcroxide_node::limits` has both steps ready for its `main`.

- **A one-request HTTP JSON-RPC connection spawns three OS threads where dcrd
  starts goroutines.** dcrd serves each connection on a goroutine and gives
  each request a close notifier, one more goroutine blocked in a one-byte read
  (`internal/rpcserver/rpcserver.go:5721-5731`). The port's accept loop arms a
  handshake watchdog thread per connection (`arm_handshake_watchdog` in
  `rpcrun.rs`), which stands down only at a websocket upgrade, once the body
  is read or just before an over-limit remainder is discarded, or when the
  handler returns. Dispatch then spawns an `arm_request_cancel` thread that
  polls the socket every 100 ms until the handler returns: with the handler
  itself, three threads for one request. Only getwork's queue
  (`WorkSem::acquire`), the template waits in `bgtemplate.rs` and `generate`'s
  loop in `cpuminer.rs` ever read the cancel flag, so every other method pays
  for a watcher nothing reads: about 50 microseconds of CPU per request by a
  scratch spawn-and-join benchmark, unmeasured on the daemon. Answers do not
  differ. Two independent changes would remove the extra threads. (a) Lazy
  cancellation: `request_cancelled()` (`worksem.rs` in `dcroxide-rpc`) probes
  the socket itself whenever a waiter reads it, and getwork's queue and the
  template waits already re-read it every 50 ms. That also changes `WorkSem`'s
  release, which passes over queued waiters whose flag is set: the flag would
  then be set only when a waiter polls, so a release could hand the permit to
  a client that has already gone. (b) One listener-wide deadline thread in
  place of the per-connection watchdogs, which would also let the 512-thread
  ceiling admit twice as many pre-authentication connections. Both change the
  RPC server's threading model, so both wait on a measurement: 10k sequential
  getblockcount calls over `--notls`, comparing server CPU per call before and
  after (review finding N1-p#3).

- **The command line on Windows takes only the POSIX option style.** dcrd
  builds go-flags without the `forceposix` tag, so on Windows
  `optstyle_windows.go` applies: `/name`, `/name:value` and `/?` are options
  beside `-x` and `--name=value` (a `/` argument longer than two bytes is a
  long option, so `/u:x` is ``unknown flag `u'``), a separate value starting
  with `/` is refused as an option (`isValidValue`), the Help Options group
  gains a `/?` entry, and the help and error texts render options as
  `/V, /version` and `/A, /appdata:` and environment variables as
  `[%DCRD_APPDATA%]`. The port's scanner (`flags::argument_is_option`,
  `scan_args_in`), help (`render_help_with`) and error texts (`opt_display`)
  are POSIX on every platform, so on Windows `dcroxide /?` or `/rpcuser:u` is
  a positional argument, and `-h` shows `-V, --version` and
  `[$DCROXIDE_APPDATA]`. The tools share the scanner and differ the same way.
  Closing it means porting `optstyle_windows.go` under `cfg(windows)`; the
  frozen go-flags and help vectors the Windows CI leg replays were dumped on
  Linux, with POSIX texts, and would need Windows counterparts from a dcrd
  built for Windows, which cannot be generated or checked off a Windows host.
  The config file side is ported: on Windows the INI parser knows dcrd's
  Service Options group as go-flags' `matchingGroups` and `optionByName` do,
  so a global `service=` (or `ServiceCommand=`, `s=`) line and a
  `[Service Options]` section are taken and, the service check having
  already run, ignored (`flags::parse_ini`).

- **Paths on Windows are joined with `/`.** dcrd builds its defaults with
  `path/filepath`, which on Windows joins with `\` and cleans with Windows
  rules (volume names, `/` turned into `\`), so its home is
  `C:\Users\u\AppData\Local\Dcrd` and every default under it uses `\`. The
  port's `gostd::filepath_join` and `filepath_clean` are the Unix functions on
  every platform, so on Windows the home is
  `C:\Users\u\AppData\Local/Dcroxide`, and the config file, data, log, RPC
  key, certificate and client CA defaults and the per-network directories
  carry mixed separators, in the `Home dir:` log line and the `-h` defaults
  among others. Windows file APIs accept `/`, so each file is the one dcrd
  opens. What differs is the text, and a backslash path typed to equal a
  default (`preCfg.ConfigFile == defaultConfigFile`, `config.go:695`, `:732`,
  `:745`) matches in dcrd and not in the port. Closing it means porting Go's
  Windows `Clean`, `Join` and `Abs` (`path/filepath`, `internal/filepathlite`)
  under `cfg(windows)`; the config and flags vectors the Windows CI leg
  replays are `/`-rooted dumps from Linux and would need per-platform
  expectations, which cannot be checked off a Windows host.

- **A socket `--piperx` on Linux without `pidfd_getfd` shuts the daemon
  down at startup.** dcrd adopts whatever descriptor it is given with
  `os.NewFile` (`ipc.go:45`, `:74`). The node crate forbids `unsafe`, so on
  Linux it duplicates the descriptor with `pidfd_getfd`, and where that call
  is unavailable (kernel < 5.6, or seccomp such as Docker's and podman's
  default profiles without `CAP_SYS_PTRACE`) it falls back to re-opening
  `/proc/self/fd/N`, which cannot serve a socket descriptor (`ENXIO`). A Node
  or Electron parent's `'pipe'` stdio slot is a socketpair, so there
  `--piperx` shuts the daemon down at startup and `--pipetx` sends nothing.
  Windows pipe handles are adopted through `dcroxide-winsvc` and are not
  affected.

- **A broken stdout pipe ends the daemon with exit status 141, not by
  `SIGPIPE`.** dcrd's `logWriter` ignores stdout write errors, except that
  Go's runtime kills the process with `SIGPIPE` on `EPIPE` for fd 1
  (`os.epipecheck`, `runtime.sigpipe`). The port ignores every other error too
  (`logging::write_stdout`). Rust starts with `SIGPIPE` ignored, and without
  `unsafe` the default action cannot be restored and raised, so on `EPIPE` it
  calls `process::exit(128 + 13)`. A shell sees the same status, but a
  supervisor that tells a signal from an exit code does not: systemd counts
  dcrd's `SIGPIPE` death as a clean stop and 141 as a failure.

- **Signals are not acknowledged in the log.** dcrd's `shutdownListener` logs
  `Received signal (%s).  Shutting down...` for the first SIGINT, SIGTERM or
  SIGHUP and `Received signal (%s).  Already shutting down...` for each repeat
  (`signal.go:34`, `:47-49`; `signal_syscall.go` adds SIGTERM and SIGHUP on
  every platform it builds for, Windows included). Apart from the Windows
  console close, logoff and shutdown events (see below), the port logs
  neither: ctrlc runs one handler for all three signals and does not say
  which arrived, so the line cannot name the signal. The shutdown itself is unaffected:
  `Server shutting down` still follows, and a later `stop` or pipe close logs
  the `Already shutting down` form. The unix fix is known and adds no crate.
  Block the three signals at the top of `main`, before any thread starts, and
  replace ctrlc with a thread that loops on `SigSet::wait`. The thread logs
  Go's names (`interrupt`, `terminated`, `hangup`) through the same
  first-or-repeat check a shutdown request uses. nix 0.31's
  `SigSet::thread_block` and `SigSet::wait` are safe, and ctrlc already builds
  nix 0.31 with `signal` on every unix target. The fix does need a direct `nix
  = { version = "0.31", default-features = false, features = ["signal"] }`
  under `[target.'cfg(unix)'.dependencies]` in
  `crates/dcroxide-node/Cargo.toml`, a manifest change not yet made. The crate
  names nix today only for the Apple and BSD kqueue targets. ctrlc re-exports
  nix's `Signal` enum but not `SigSet`. rustix's `kernel_sigprocmask` and
  `kernel_sigwait` are `unsafe`. On Windows, Go maps CTRL_C and CTRL_BREAK
  to `interrupt`, and the close, logoff and shutdown events to `terminated`
  (`runtime/os_windows.go` `ctrlHandler`). The close, logoff and shutdown
  events now reach `dcroxide-winsvc`'s console handler, which logs them as
  `terminated`; Ctrl-C and Ctrl-Break still go to ctrlc, which does not say
  which event arrived, so they cancel without a line.

- **Interface names in `addnode`, `node` and `startprofiler` resolve only on
  Linux.** dcrd's `normalizeAddress` dials an interface's first address
  whenever the host names a local interface (`net.InterfaceByName`,
  `rpcserver.go:540-558`), and the profile server's `normalizeAddresses`
  checks every address of it (`profiler.go:95`). The daemon's
  `SystemInterfaces` and `system_interface_by_name` ask the kernel over route
  netlink on Linux, as Go does. Elsewhere the dependency set has no safe
  interface source (`getifaddrs` needs `unsafe` or a new crate), so on those
  platforms the name is dialed as a host name, and `startprofiler` refuses it
  as one: not loopback, or with `allownonloopback` not an IP address.

- **Drained block maintenance still reads the chain at the end of the
  processing call.** dcrd runs each NTBlockConnected and NTBlockDisconnected
  handler with the chain lock released and the tip at the block that event
  produced (`internal/blockchain/chain.go:735-768`, `:917-937`). The port's
  chain callback runs under the chain mutex, and the pool reaches back into
  the chain, so the handlers queue and run after the whole `process_block`,
  `invalidateblock` or `reconsiderblock` call (`chainntfns.rs`
  `ChainNtfnHandler::drain_pending`).

  Three effects of the event's own tip are reproduced:

  * the recorded descriptor height (`TxPool::with_event_height`);
  * the transactions dcrd's chain still holds at each disconnect of a run:
    those of the blocks the run detaches next, and the fork block's regular
    tree when the first new block disapproves it (`DisconnectRun` through
    `TxPool::with_transient`). So a child mined a block after its parent is
    readmitted; a ticket and its split mined a block apart end with the
    split in the pool and the ticket staged; and a child of a fork-block
    transaction is readmitted before the new block returns its parent to
    the pool;
  * an orphan that a later connect of the batch mined is taken as accepted,
    without being pooled, when dcrd's chain or pool holds all its inputs at
    the earlier block (`TxPool::process_orphans_accepted_in_batch`). Its
    orphan redeemers, grandchildren included, are then accepted and
    announced at that block, as in dcrd. The mined orphan itself still
    differs: it is not announced, it is not fed to the fee estimator, and
    its policy checks (fees, standardness, scripts) are not run, where dcrd
    runs them there, announces the orphan when they pass, and discards it
    with its redeemers when they fail.

  Everything else is still read at the final tip:

  * every other acceptance check: expiry, finality, sequence locks, ticket
    price, the known-disapproved view, agenda flags and
    `tspend_mined_on_ancestor`;
  * the held transactions enter the pool's view as unmined outputs
    (`UNMINED_HEIGHT`, `NULL_BLOCK_INDEX`), where dcrd's view has them as
    chain outputs with their block height and index. A readmitted child of
    one gets unmined fraud-proof data where dcrd keeps the mined values,
    which `getrawtransaction` and relayed copies show; a spend of a held
    output under a maturity rule, such as ticket change mined one block
    earlier, is judged immature and dropped with its redeemers, where dcrd
    accepts it; and a relative lock on a held output counts from the next
    block;
  * the inputs that held transactions spend count as unspent. A
    disconnected transaction can double-spend one of them only when its
    block disapproved the held block. It is then readmitted, and the held
    transaction's own readmission or resurrection fails against it, where
    dcrd keeps the held transaction instead;
  * two consecutive disapprovals in one batch, which resurrect the older
    block's transactions against a tip without the newer block's
    conflicting ones;
  * a readmitted child whose parent, in the same block or in a held block,
    was double-spent by the new chain. The child stays in the pool without
    its parent, and templates skip it, where dcrd's `RemoveDoubleSpends`
    cascade removes it;
  * the fee estimator, which never re-tracks old-chain transactions that
    the new chain re-mines, because they fail as already mined.

  Carrying each held transaction's block height and index would not close
  the maturity and sequence-lock cases: after an `invalidateblock` the held
  blocks sit above the final tip, where neither check has an answer.
  Closing these needs each handler run at its own tip, with the
  reorganization yielding between steps to a caller that drops the chain
  mutex and drains. That is a change to the blockchain crate's processing
  loop.

- **The fee estimator is not persisted, so `estimatesmartfee` forgets its
  history on restart.** dcrd's `newServer` opens the estimator over
  `<datadir>/feesdb` (`server.go:3959-3977`). `NewEstimator` reloads the saved
  bucket statistics (`internal/fees/estimator.go` `loadFromDatabase`), and
  `ProcessBlock` writes them back after every mined block (`updateDatabase`).
  The port has the estimator and the pinned bucket row codec
  (`serialize_bucket`/`deserialize_bucket`) but not the store. The daemon's
  estimator starts empty on every run (`crates/dcroxide-node/src/fees.rs`).
  After a restart, dcrd answers `estimatesmartfee` from its saved history,
  while dcroxide returns `not enough transactions seen for estimation` until
  it has observed enough mined transactions again. Closing it means porting
  `loadFromDatabase`/`updateDatabase` over a store of the port's choosing,
  which is an on-disk format decision.

- **The fee estimator logs only its reorg warning.** dcrd's `internal/fees`
  logs through the daemon's `FEES` logger (`log.go:68`, `:89`). The port's
  estimator has no logger: `Estimator::process_block` returns the block it
  skips as at or below its best height, and the daemon logs dcrd's warning for
  it under `FEES` (`Trying to process mined transactions at block N when
  previous best block was at height M`, `estimator.go:885-887`;
  `crates/dcroxide-node/src/fees.rs`). The rest is not emitted: the debug
  lines `Updated moving averages into block %d` (`:537`), `Setting best height
  as %d` (`:743`), `Adding mempool tx %s using fee rate %.8f` (`:804`),
  `Removing tx %s from mempool` (`:828`) and `Processing mined tx %s (rate
  %.8f, delay %d)` (`:866`); the trace line `Processing previously unknown
  mined tx %s` (`:848`); the database lines (`:345`, `:459`, `:913`), which
  have no store to report on (see the entry above); and two errors that dcrd's
  own comments say should not happen in regular use, `Transaction count in
  bucket index %d and confirmation index %d became < 0` (`:630`) and `Mined
  transaction %s (%d) that was known from mempool at a higher block height
  (%d)` (`:859`). Only `--debuglevel=FEES=debug` or lower shows a difference
  in normal operation. Closing it means giving the estimator a log sink, like
  the one the indexers and the database take.

- **Some connection manager log lines are still missing.** The outbound driver
  logs dcrd's CMGR dial lifecycle lines, the once-per-pause
  `Max failed connection attempts reached` line and SRVR's
  `Marking address as attempted failed`. The two SRVR error lines dcrd
  logs after it for the same peer are present as well:
  `Setting services for address failed` from the version handler
  (`server.go:989`) and `Marking address as good failed` from the admission
  (`server.go:2664`). The three are pinned in dcrd's order by
  `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/review_peer_addr_logs.rs` (QK-0028). Still
  absent are
  `Rejected connection: %v` (`internal/connmgr/connmanager.go:894`);
  `Added persistent connection to %v (id: %d)` (`:1848`) and
  `Removed persistent connection to %v (id %d)` (`:1223`), which dcrd logs
  inside `AddPersistent` and `Remove`; and the inbound listener's
  `Accepted connection from` and `Disconnected from` (`:1739`, `:1728`), which
  belong in `runtime.rs`. All five are debug lines, so only
  `--debuglevel=CMGR=debug` or lower shows the difference. The port's dial
  cannot be interrupted, so when `Remove` cancels a pending dial,
  `Failed to connect to X: attempt manually canceled` is logged once the dial
  returns rather than at the cancel (review finding N8-p#2).
  Also absent: PEER's debug line `Negotiated protocol version %d for peer %s`
  (`peer/peer.go:2023`), which dcrd logs as it reads the remote version, just
  before the version callback; and SRVR's `New peer %s ignored - server is
  shutting down` (info, `server.go:2628`) with the `Marking address as
  connected failed: %v` error that can follow it (`server.go:2735`), both
  reached only for a peer that completes its handshake while the server is
  shutting down, a refusal the daemon does not have.
  `resolve_external_address` drops the five AMGR error lines of dcrd's
  `resolveExternalAddress` (`server.go:2439`, `:2447`, `:2456`, `:2462`,
  `:2488`); the first two cannot occur, because the candidate is a routable
  address literal, and whether `unable to parse listener: %v` can occur for a
  listener given by host name has not been checked.

- **Each transaction hash goes through a heap buffer, and some blocks'
  transactions are still hashed more than once.** dcrd memoizes the prefix
  hash in `dcrutil.Tx`, so sanity and connect share it and only `TxHashFull`
  recomputes it for the merkle root; its `mustHash` (`msgtx.go` 418-429)
  also streams the serialization straight into the BLAKE-256 hasher. The
  port's `MsgTx` caches nothing. Since 2026-10-11 one prefix pass and one
  witness pass are made over an arriving block
  (`BlockTxHashes::compute_with_merkle_leaves`), and the merkle check, the
  duplicate check, the ticket-purchase list, the block script check (as
  the prefix hash of every `SigHashAll` signature hash, which dcrd
  computes once per signature; see the `txscript` row), the connect and
  the node's connected-block maintenance read those hashes for a block
  connected by the call that delivered its data, where dcrd makes two
  prefix passes. Who makes the pass depends on how the block arrives. A
  block that arrives without hashes is hashed by the processing call:
  `Chain::process_block`, which is lent the block, or
  `Chain::process_shared_block`, which shares it and is the entry the
  daemon takes for a block its sync manager hands on without hashes
  (`process.rs:5207`, `:5224`). A block that a connection's `peer-reader`
  thread read (*Reading ahead of the block handler* under Deliberate
  divergences) is hashed on that thread, by `HashedBlock::new`
  (`blockhashes.rs:425`), which also builds the block's two merkle trees
  from those hashes and keeps their roots, and counts the block's signature
  operations once for each answer the treasury agenda can give and keeps the
  two sums, before it reaches the chain, and `Chain::process_hashed_block`
  (`process.rs:5266`) then makes no pass, builds no tree of its own and
  counts the block only where the sum its treasury agenda answer selects is
  over the limit. That holds for every block such a thread
  reads until its connection ends, ahead of the input thread or in turn. A
  side structure paired with the block instance stands in for
  `dcrutil.Block`'s cache: the hashes meet a block in five ways only, in
  the function that computed them, through a slot matched on the block
  mirror's own allocation (`Arc::ptr_eq`), on the block-connected event
  beside the block, inside a `HashedBlock`, which owns the `Arc` of the
  block it hashed, has private fields and one constructor and is not
  `Clone`, or in a batch of the script pool (`OwnedBatch`,
  `validate.rs:6130`), which holds the block and its hashes by reference
  count on helper threads and is checked by address against the block
  and the hashes being checked, in every build (the `SigHashAll` entry
  under Accepted); the side structure is never keyed by block hash. The
  ticket-purchase list is: it keeps a copy of the purchases' hashes under
  the block's hash (`Chain::ticket_purchases`), which the processing call
  files only after the block's merkle roots and its duplicate check have
  passed. netsync's `SyncChain::Block` and the node's `ArrivedBlock` only
  carry a `HashedBlock`, and the chain reads its block hash where the
  call starts, where a processing call otherwise hashes the header (the
  `internal/blockchain` row, *The block hash*), its transaction hashes and
  merkle roots at the one point where it otherwise computes them
  (`process.rs:5385`), after its duplicate, known-invalid and header checks,
  the verdict of its data sanity checks, where the reading thread made them
  with the values of the parameters the call processes it with, at the point
  where it otherwise makes them (`process.rs:5418`), and its counts of
  signature operations in the contextual check made as it is accepted, where
  it otherwise counts, through the same slot as the block hash
  (`Chain::accepted_block_sig_ops`, `process.rs:6392`): a block linked by a
  later block's data, or checked again as a reorganization attaches it, is
  counted by the check. A block refused there, or by the sync manager as
  unrequested, was hashed for nothing and is refused exactly as before.
  `Chain::arrivals_hashed` counts the arriving blocks the chain hashed
  itself, beside `Chain::tx_hash_recomputes`, and `Chain::arrivals_checked`
  those whose data sanity checks it made itself. Every consumer asserts one
  hash per transaction, and under debug assertions re-hashes and compares;
  each script engine likewise compares the hash it is handed with its
  transaction's under debug assertions. In a release build the
  `SigHashAll` signature hashes of a block being connected therefore rest
  on that pairing, as the keys of its new outputs do: one hash per
  transaction is all that is checked there for a tree checked on the
  calling thread or the scoped workers, and for a tree the script pool
  runs, also that each item's transaction and hash are the block's and
  the hashes' own at its position, by address.
  Still hashed again: every transaction in the contextual merkle check
  when the block is not among the recent merkle checks, which on regnet
  is every block until a vote tally has found the header commitments
  agenda's activation (simnet forces the agenda, so its blocks are proven
  at the data preconditions check and are not hashed again there); any
  block that is not the one being processed
  (a reorganization's other blocks, a block linked by a later block's
  data, a side chain block connected later, invalidate and reconsider),
  counted in `Chain::tx_hash_recomputes`; a disapproved parent's regular
  tree; every disconnect; the startup catch-up; the template checks; the
  later connects of a batch in the node's drain, only when an orphan
  tried during a connected block's maintenance fails as a duplicate or
  stays an orphan (`mined_by_later_connects`, at most once per connect);
  each treasury spend of a connected block, in `tspend_checks` on a
  treasury vote interval block and in `treasury_records_for_block`; the
  ticket purchases of the block `ticket_maturity` below when the
  ticket-purchase list holds no entry for it, counted in
  `Chain::ticket_purchase_misses`; and the public dcrd-named functions,
  which hash for themselves. The transaction index
  (`--txindex`) adds one more pass, now on the index thread: it hashes
  every transaction of each connected or disconnected block
  (`txindex.rs` `db_add_tx_index_entries`/`db_remove_tx_index_entries`),
  where dcrd passes the memoized `tx.Hash()` (`txindex.go:257`, and `:296`
  on disconnect); hashes carried on `IndexNtfn` would remove it. Three
  things differ in order only, on blocks that fail anyway: the
  processing calls and the public preconditions wrappers hash before the
  wire-size check and the agenda query; a block that a `peer-reader`
  thread read is hashed before the sync manager has checked that it was
  requested and before the chain's duplicate, known-invalid and header
  checks; and the DCP0005 root is computed
  as `calc_merkle_root` over the two tree roots, the value of dcrd's
  `CalcCombinedTxTreeMerkleRoot`. `BlockConnectedNtfnsData` carries the
  hashes and is built outside the crate only through its `new`. The block
  a peer delivered is kept in the `blocks` mirror as the allocation its
  message was decoded into, with or without hashes, as dcrd's
  `ProcessBlock` caches the pointer it is handed (`process.go:571`): the
  copy the chain made of every such block as it accepted the data is
  gone. A block from the RPC server, the CPU miner, `addblock` or the
  bench tool's replay is still copied once, and for the first two the
  sync manager makes that copy before the chain sees the block
  (`SyncManager::process_block`,
  `crates/dcroxide-netsync/src/manager.rs:3437`), so it is made for a
  block the chain then refuses as well. Pinned by
  `a_hashed_block_carries_the_hashes_of_its_own_block`,
  `a_duplicate_block_is_refused_before_its_hashes_are_read` and
  `a_hashed_block_is_processed_without_hashing_it_again`, which also
  counts the passes that build merkle leaves on their own, and
  `a_hashed_block_is_processed_without_building_its_merkle_trees`,
  `a_hashed_block_is_processed_without_counting_its_sig_ops`,
  `a_block_linked_by_its_parent_is_counted_by_the_check` and
  `the_battery_counts_a_hashed_block_only_over_the_limit`; by the
  full-block battery through each entry beside `process_block` on a twin
  chain, in memory (`crates/dcroxide-blockchain/tests/shared_block_battery.rs`,
  `hashed_block_battery.rs`) and on chains over a database
  (`the_battery_gives_the_same_outcomes_through_every_entry_on_stored_chains`);
  and in `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/dispatch.rs` by
  `a_delivered_block_reaches_the_chain_without_a_copy`,
  `a_delivered_block_is_known_to_the_peer_that_delivered_it` and
  `blocks_read_ahead_over_a_served_connection_are_hashed_once`. The
  buffer stays on purpose: streaming the serialization into the port's
  `Blake256` field by field measured 4-7% slower than allocating and
  hashing once, before the 2026-10-11 rewrite of its rounds. Block header
  hashes do not allocate, because `BlockHeader::serialize` writes
  straight into its fixed 180-byte array, as dcrd's `BlockHash` streams
  it. One default sync on m3 took 820.3 s with the single pass where the
  run before it took 893.7 s, and 836.6 s as first built, before the
  ticket-purchase list took its hashes from it (docs/bench-ledger.md,
  "Five changes on the block-processing thread").

- **An undecodable `xn--` label's error text keeps runes Go's `%q` escapes,
  and Go's `strconv.IsPrint` tables are kept in two copies.** When a non-ASCII
  host name or `--altdnsnames` entry holds an `xn--` label that does not
  decode, dcrd's certgen fails with `idna: invalid label %q`, and Go's
  `strconv.Quote` escapes every rune `strconv.IsPrint` rejects. The port's
  certgen quotes the label with an approximation (`punycode.rs` `go_quote`)
  that is exact for ASCII and otherwise escapes only control and space runes,
  so a soft hyphen U+00AD, a zero-width space U+200B, and private-use or
  unassigned runes print raw where Go writes `\u00ad`, `\u200b` and so on.
  Closing that needs only an internal dependency edge, `dcroxide-certgen` on
  `dcroxide-dcrjson` (which itself depends only on `dcroxide-chainhash`), so
  that `puny_error` calls the exact `dcroxide_dcrjson::gojson::go_quote` and
  the approximation goes. Separately, `dcroxide-txscript`'s `stdaddr` carries
  a second copy of Go 1.26.5's `IsPrint` tables beside `dcroxide-dcrjson`'s
  `gojson/isprint.rs`, because it is no_std and cannot depend on dcrjson;
  `dcroxide-node`'s `rpcrun::go_quote_bytes` repeats `stdaddr`'s byte-slice
  quote on top of dcrjson's; and `dcroxide-peer`'s `netaddress::go_quote`,
  which quotes an unparsable port in `Peer::new_outbound`'s errors, is exact
  only below U+0100 and makes certgen's approximation above it. Until a small
  no_std leaf crate holding `is_print`, the tables and a byte-slice quote
  replaces them, which is a crate-layout decision, both table copies must be
  re-copied when the parity toolchain moves (review finding RG17#2).

- **An address's own timestamp is compared on the wall clock, where dcrd's
  `Connected` stamp is monotonic.** dcrd's `Connected` sets
  `na.Timestamp = time.Now()` (`addrmgr/addrmanager.go:1022-1027`), which
  carries a monotonic reading, so for an address connected in this process the
  20-minute refresh test there and `isBad`'s from-the-future and month-old
  tests (`knownaddress.go:97-103`) measure running time. The port's
  `NetAddress.timestamp` holds only Unix nanoseconds, so those three tests use
  the wall clock and a wall-clock step moves them. The attempt and success
  times and `pickOutboundAddr`'s recency test do carry the monotonic reading
  (`dcroxide_addrmgr::GoTime`). Closing this means giving the wire-facing
  `NetAddress` an optional monotonic reading (review finding GAP07#4).

- **`lru::Map` runs item expiry and the expiry-scan schedule on one clock.**
  dcrd's `container/lru` stores and compares item expiry as `UnixNano`, on
  the wall clock (`map.go:175`, `:228`), but schedules its lazy expiry scans
  with `now.After(nextExpireScan)` over `time.Now()` readings, on the
  monotonic clock (`map.go:297`). `dcroxide-containers`' `lru::Clock` drives
  both. Every production LRU with a TTL passes the wall clock: per-peer
  known inventory, recently advertised, the inbound rate limiter and the
  mixpool's recently-removed cache. So lookups expire exactly as in dcrd,
  but a backward wall step of D postpones the physical sweep of expired
  items by D. Only memory reclamation timing differs, within each cache's
  capacity bound. Closing it needs a second, monotonic, injectable clock for
  `next_expire_scan` (review finding C2-p#1).

- **`Peer::new_outbound` keeps dcrd 2.1's string-address constructor.** At the
  pin, dcrd's `NewOutboundPeer` takes the dialed `net.Addr`, parses nothing
  and cannot fail (`peer/peer.go:2468-2472`), and an outbound peer's
  `HostToNetAddress` is consulted in `localVersionMsg` (`:2132-2150`). The
  port's constructor still takes a `host:port` string, splits, parses and
  resolves it through `Config::host_to_net_address`, and can fail with Go's
  parse errors, while `local_version_msg` never consults the resolver. Nothing
  observable differs: the daemon sets no resolver and overwrites the
  constructor's address through `associate` with the one it built for the
  dial, so each dial pays for a parse whose result is thrown away, and the
  `outbound` vector rows pin a pre-2.2 code path the daemon never uses.
  Closing it means making `new_outbound` take the dialed address and return
  `Peer`, applying the resolver in `local_version_msg` for outbound peers as
  dcrd does, and retiring those vector rows: one public API change across the
  daemon's dial path and its tests (review finding P2-p#3).

- **`gettxout` decodes a ticket's minimal outputs, where dcrd's never does.**
  dcrd decodes them only where `TicketMinimalOutputs` is called
  (`internal/blockchain/utxoentry.go:231`): the vote and revocation input
  checks, auto-revocation generation, and on the RPC side `createrawssrtx`
  alone (`internal/rpcserver/rpcserver.go:1091`). `handleGetTxOut` (`:3658`)
  reads only the entry's amount, script, height and flags. The daemon's
  `RpcChain::fetch_utxo_entry` (`crates/dcroxide-node/src/rpcrun.rs`) fills
  `RpcUtxoEntry::ticket_minimal_outputs` for every ticket submission it
  returns, so `gettxout` decodes them too. For a well-formed row that costs a
  decode and nothing else. For a corrupt ticket-submission row whose stored
  output count is 2^63 or more, which the UTXO loader accepts as dcrd's does
  (see the `internal/blockchain` row), the port's `gettxout` panics in
  `deserialize_to_minimal_outputs`' capacity reservation, an abort in release
  builds, where dcrd's answers; `createrawssrtx` panics in both. Before the
  loader matched dcrd the same row aborted at the fetch, so only the site has
  moved. Closing it means decoding lazily: `RpcUtxoEntry` carries the
  serialized outputs and only `handle_create_raw_ssrtx` decodes them, where
  dcrd calls `TicketMinimalOutputs`. `dcroxide-rpc` does not depend on
  `dcroxide-blockchain`, so the decoder has to come through the `RpcChain`
  seam (arithmetic-lint sweep,
  [ADR-0008](adr/0008-clippy-lint-policy.md)).

- **`getstakeversions` from a block at height 2^31 - 1 or above walks the full
  count, where dcrd panics or returns nothing.** dcrd's `GetStakeVersions`
  clamps the count against `int32(startNode.height+1)` over an `int64` height
  (`internal/blockchain/chain.go:327`). From height 2^31 - 1 through 2^32 - 2
  that truncates negative, the count takes the negative value, and
  `make([]StakeVersions, 0, count)` panics (`:331`); at height 2^32 - 1 it
  truncates to 0 and dcrd returns an empty list. `Chain::get_stake_versions`
  (`crates/dcroxide-blockchain/src/process.rs`) clamps in `i64` and returns
  the blocks. Nothing reaches it: the start block needs about 2^31 headers
  below it. Closing it means clamping at `i32` as dcrd does, which makes the
  answer in that range dcrd's panic or empty list. The cast was outside the
  arithmetic-lint sweep that found it
  ([ADR-0008](adr/0008-clippy-lint-policy.md)).

- **`is_treasury_vote_interval` answers false for a zero interval, where dcrd
  divides by zero.** dcrd's `IsTreasuryVoteInterval` computes
  `height%tvi == 0 && height != 0` (`blockchain/standalone/treasury.go:23`),
  and Go's integer remainder panics on a zero divisor. The port's
  `is_treasury_vote_interval` (`crates/dcroxide-standalone/src/treasury.rs`)
  uses `u64::is_multiple_of`, which is defined for a zero divisor: given a
  zero interval it answers false, and its chain, mining and RPC callers and
  `calc_tspend_window` take their not-an-interval branch where dcrd panics.
  Every caller passes a network's `TreasuryVoteInterval` (288, 60, 48 or 4),
  so no reachable input differs. Closing it means computing the remainder with
  `%`, which panics on zero as Go's does, under allows for
  `clippy::manual_is_multiple_of` and `clippy::arithmetic_side_effects` whose
  reason says a zero interval panics as in dcrd (arithmetic-lint sweep,
  [ADR-0008](adr/0008-clippy-lint-policy.md)).

- **The script tokenizer's offsets are `usize` where dcrd's are `int32`, so a
  script of 2 GiB or more parses differently.** dcrd's `ScriptTokenizer` keeps
  its offset in an `int32`, stops at `int32(len(script))`
  (`txscript/tokenizer.go:34`, `:43`), advances in `int32` (`:88`, `:130`) and
  checks a push against the remaining length truncated the same way (`:122`).
  `removeOpcodeByData` keeps `prevOffset` and `fullPushLen` in `int32`
  (`txscript/script.go:264-285`), and `ExtractMultiSigScriptDetailsV0` tests
  `int32(len(script)) - ByteIndex()` (`txscript/stdscript/scriptv0.go:342`).
  The port's tokenizer and its consumers use `usize`. For a script of 2^31
  bytes or more, dcrd tokenizes only `int32(len)` bytes: none when that is
  zero or negative, otherwise up to the length modulo 2^32, with an
  `OP_DATA_N` push begun just before that point allowed to run past it. So
  dcrd's `ExtractMultiSigScriptDetailsV0` accepts as a 16-of-16 multisig a
  (2^32 + 547)-byte script whose first 547 bytes are a 16-of-16 compressed-key
  multisig script and whose last byte is `OP_CHECKMULTISIG`, which the port
  rejects, and a push that carries dcrd's offset past 2^31 - 1 wraps it
  negative, so the next `Next` panics on a negative index. No source can
  deliver such a script: a P2P message is capped at 32 MiB
  (`wire.MaxMessagePayload`), an RPC request body at 8 MiB (16 MiB over a
  websocket), and a block by its maximum size. Closing it means carrying
  dcrd's `int32` arithmetic through the tokenizer, `remove_opcode_by_data` and
  `extract_multi_sig_script_details_v0` for inputs nothing can supply, which
  is why the sweep that found it left it
  ([ADR-0008](adr/0008-clippy-lint-policy.md)).
